# PHASE 3, PART 1: Frontend Foundations (HTML and CSS)

**Contents**
- **3.1 HTML Fundamentals:** document structure, semantics, script loading, forms and validation, accessibility, SEO and meta tags, responsive images, interactive elements, SVG, Canvas, Web Components
- **3.2 CSS Styling:** cascade and specificity, box model, positioning and stacking, Flexbox, Grid, centering and layout recipes, responsive design, modern CSS, animation, preprocessors and CSS Modules, architecture, Tailwind and CSS-in-JS, dark mode, performance
- Machine-coding drills, rapid-fire Q&A, readiness checklist

Part 2 covers JavaScript Core. Part 3 covers TypeScript, browser internals, performance, accessibility in depth, build tools, and Web APIs.

**Why this matters:** Frontend and full stack interviews often open with "center a div," "explain the box model," or "build a modal." Weak CSS and HTML fundamentals are among the most common reasons strong React developers fail frontend rounds.

---

# 3.1 HTML FUNDAMENTALS

## 3.1.1 Document Structure and Parsing
🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: Phase 2.1

**Definition:** The skeleton every HTML page needs, and how the browser turns markup into the DOM.

**Why interviewers ask:** It checks whether you know what the browser does with your markup, which underpins performance, SEO, and bug-hunting.

**Minimal correct document**
```html
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>
  <meta charset="utf-8">
  <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1">
  <title>Page title (unique per page)</title>
  <meta name="description" content="150-160 char summary for search results">
  <link rel="stylesheet" href="/styles.css">
  <script src="/app.js" defer></script>
</head>
<body>
  <header>…</header>
  <main id="main">…</main>
  <footer>…</footer>
</body>
</html>
```


**Prerequisites:** None — this is the entry point of the HTML group. The render pipeline it names is developed in 3.5.1, and event/loop behaviour in 3.5.3.
**Key concepts**
- **`<!DOCTYPE html>`** triggers **standards mode**. Without it the browser uses **quirks mode**, which emulates old buggy behavior (e.g., a different box model).
- **`lang`** on `<html>` helps screen readers pick pronunciation, helps translation tools, and aids SEO.
- **`charset` must be within the first 1024 bytes** and match the HTTP header (UTF-8).
- **Viewport meta:** without it, mobile browsers render at about 980px and scale down, so your media queries won't behave. Don't set `user-scalable=no`, because it breaks zoom accessibility.
- **Parsing pipeline:** bytes → characters → tokens → nodes → **DOM**. CSS becomes the **CSSOM**. DOM + CSSOM → **render tree** → layout → paint → composite.
- **The parser is forgiving.** It auto-closes tags, relocates misplaced elements, and never throws. Invalid HTML therefore fails silently in odd ways. Validate with the W3C validator.
- **Render-blocking and parser-blocking:** CSS blocks rendering. A classic `<script>` blocks the parser (it must run first because it might call `document.write` or read styles).
- **Lifecycle events:** `DOMContentLoaded` fires when HTML is parsed and deferred scripts have run (no waiting for images). `load` fires after all resources (images, styles) finish.
- **Head order matters for performance:** charset, viewport, `<title>`, preconnects, critical CSS, then scripts.
- **Block vs inline elements:** block (`div`, `p`, `section`) start on a new line and take full width. Inline (`span`, `a`, `strong`) flow in text, and width and height don't apply. Inline-block mixes both.

**Questions**
1. What does the doctype do? What is quirks mode?
2. Why is the viewport meta tag needed?
3. `DOMContentLoaded` vs `load`?
4. What happens when the parser meets a `<script>` without `defer` or `async`?
5. What's the difference between the DOM and the HTML source? *(The DOM is the parsed, corrected, live object tree. JS changes it, and "view source" shows the original text.)*
6. What does the browser do with invalid HTML like an unclosed `<div>`?
7. Block vs inline vs inline-block?

**Model answer, Q4:** "The parser stops building the DOM, downloads the script, executes it, then resumes. That's why scripts at the top without `defer` delay first render. `defer` downloads in parallel and runs after parsing, in order. `async` runs as soon as downloaded, in any order, which suits independent scripts like analytics."


**Trade-offs:**
- **Standards mode vs legacy quirk workarounds:** declare the doctype and fix the handful of real layout regressions, versus omitting it locally to reproduce an old-IE-era bug. Ship standards mode; use quirks mode only as a diagnostic.
- **`DOMContentLoaded` vs waiting for `load`:** wire app code to `DOMContentLoaded` (or a deferred script) so it runs at the earliest safe moment; wait for `load` only when you genuinely need images, iframes or fonts, because a single slow image can push `load` seconds past interactivity.
- **`<script defer>` in `<head>` vs a `<script>` at the end of `<body>`:** both avoid parser-blocking; `defer` keeps execution order explicit, starts the fetch earlier and works for modules with `import`, whereas end-of-body relies on the parser reaching the bottom and can't reach code that runs before it.
- **Server-rendered HTML vs a client-rendered shell:** streamed HTML gives first paint and indexable content with no main-thread work; a client shell needs a render-blocking bundle, delays LCP, and shows social scrapers an empty page — buy SSR only where the content actually deserves it.

**Common wrong answers:** "The DOM is just the HTML file"; "the doctype is only an IE validator"; "invalid HTML throws in the console — so my unclosed `<div>` is a parser bug"; "CSS blocks the HTML parser"; "a classic `<script>` is `async` by default"; "`defer` scripts run before the DOM exists".

**Probe:** "Your LCP element sits behind a 40KB stylesheet and a `<script>` at the top of `<head>` with no attributes — what happens on first load, in what order, and what would you change?" (Expect: stylesheet fetch blocks first paint not parsing; the script blocks the parser until it downloads and runs; both must settle before first paint; fix with `defer`/module and inlined critical CSS.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Whether they can narrate the pipeline in order** — bytes → characters → tokens → nodes → DOM, DOM + CSSOM → render tree → layout → paint → composite — and, crucially, which stages *block* which: a stylesheet blocks rendering but not parsing, a classic script blocks parsing, and a script after a stylesheet waits for the CSSOM.
- **Doctype as a mode switch, not decoration.** Standards vs quirks mode changes the box model, table and image behaviour, so it explains real cross-browser bug reports instead of sounding like trivia.
- **Whether they distinguish the DOM from the source.** That the parser silently repairs malformed markup, that the DOM is live, and that "view source" and DevTools show you two different documents.
- **Calibration.** A strong candidate volunteers the edges they know: `document.write` blowing away the document, a long task freezing the parser, `readyState`, why `DOMContentLoaded` can fire before a late `async` script runs.
**Mistakes:** forgetting the viewport meta, multiple `<h1>`-style structure chaos, scripts in `<head>` with no `defer`, missing `lang`.

**Mini task:** Write a page, remove the doctype, and observe the difference in rendering (e.g., table cell sizing and `box-sizing`). Then `console.log` the timing of `DOMContentLoaded` vs `load` with a large image.


**Self-check:** Explain, in order, everything that happens between the first response byte and the first painted pixel — then name precisely which step a missing doctype, a missing viewport meta, and an un-deferred head script each break.

**Resources:** MDN "Quirks Mode and Standards Mode" and the `<!DOCTYPE>` reference page; WHATWG HTML spec §13.2 "Parsing" (tokeniser and tree construction); web.dev "Rendering on the Web" and "Browser rendering priority"; MDN `Document.readyState`, `DOMContentLoaded` and `window.load`.
---

## 3.1.2 Semantic HTML
🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Choosing elements for their *meaning* (a nav is a `<nav>`, not a `<div class="nav">`), so browsers, assistive tech, and search engines understand the page.

**Why interviewers ask:** It's a proxy for accessibility awareness and craftsmanship. "Div soup" is a red flag.


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.1 — the document skeleton has to exist before element choice means anything.
**Key concepts**

| Element | Purpose |
|---|---|
| `<header>` | Intro or navigational aids, for the page or a section |
| `<nav>` | Major navigation links (label multiple navs with `aria-label`) |
| `<main>` | Primary content, **one per page** (skip-link target) |
| `<article>` | Self-contained, independently distributable (post, card, comment) |
| `<section>` | Thematic grouping, **should have a heading** |
| `<aside>` | Tangentially related content (sidebar, callout) |
| `<footer>` | Footer for page or section |
| `<figure>`/`<figcaption>` | Media with a caption |
| `<time datetime="">` | Machine-readable dates |
| `<address>` | Contact info for the nearest article/body |
| `<mark>`, `<abbr>`, `<code>`, `<kbd>`, `<blockquote>`, `<cite>` | Inline semantics |

- **Landmarks:** `header`, `nav`, `main`, `aside`, `footer`, and `section`/`form` with a name map to ARIA landmarks. Screen reader users jump between them.
- **Heading hierarchy:** one logical outline (`h1` → `h2` → `h3`), no skipping levels for styling. Style with CSS, not heading level.
- **Buttons vs links:** `<a href>` **navigates**, `<button>` **performs an action**. A `<div onclick>` has no keyboard focus, no Enter/Space activation, and no role. You'd have to re-implement all of that (the first rule of ARIA is to use native elements first).
- **Lists** (`ul/ol/dl`) for list content, **tables** for tabular data only (with `<caption>`, `<th scope>`), and never for layout.
- **`<div>` and `<span>`** are fine when no semantic element fits.
- **Benefits:** accessibility for free, SEO, readable code, default behaviors (focus, keyboard, form submit), and reader modes.

```html
<!-- ❌ -->
<div class="button" onclick="save()">Save</div>
<!-- ✅ -->
<button type="button" onclick="save()">Save</button>
```

**Questions**
1. Why use semantic HTML? Give concrete benefits.
2. `<section>` vs `<article>` vs `<div>`?
3. Why is `<div onclick>` a bad button? What do you lose?
4. How should headings be structured?
5. When is a `<table>` appropriate?
6. `<b>` vs `<strong>`, `<i>` vs `<em>`? *(strong/em carry meaning and emphasis for screen readers. b/i are presentational.)*
7. How do you make multiple `<nav>` elements distinguishable? *(`aria-label="Primary"`, `aria-label="Footer"`)*

**Model answer, Q3:** "A `div` has no role, so screen readers don't announce a button. It isn't focusable via Tab, doesn't activate with Enter or Space, has no disabled state, and doesn't submit forms. A native `<button>` gives all of that for free. To fix a div you'd need `role="button"`, `tabindex="0"`, key handlers, and focus styles, which is just reimplementing `<button>` badly."


**Mistakes:**
- **Choosing heading level by font size** instead of nesting depth, producing an outline that jumps h1 → h4 and breaks screen reader navigation.
- **Interactive elements inside an `<a>` (or inside another button):** invalid HTML, and the inner control becomes unreachable or double-fires.
- **Positive `tabindex`** to "fix" ordering, which desynchronises focus order from DOM and reading order.
- **`<table>` for layout:** it breaks at narrow widths, floods the accessibility tree with meaningless cells, and destroys responsive behaviour.
- **Several `<nav>` or several `<form>` landmarks with no distinguishing name,** leaving screen reader users with a list of identical "navigation" items.
- **`<main>` used more than once** on a page, which invalidates the skip link target.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`<section>` vs `<article>` vs `<div>`:** `<section>` for a genuine thematic grouping that has a heading, `<article>` only when the content is independently distributable (a post, a review, a syndicated card), `<div>` for everything else. `<article>` implies an `aria-landmark` and an entry in the accessibility outline, so misusing it pollutes navigation.
- **Native controls vs ARIA-ified `<div>`s:** `<dialog>`, `<details>`, `<select>` and `<button>` give you role, keyboard handling, focus management and state for free and are the paths assistive tech is best tested against; ARIA is the right answer only for gaps the HTML vocabulary genuinely lacks — and a wrong role actively misleads users.
- **A single `<h1>` per page vs one `<h1>` per section:** the practical convention is one `<h1>` and a non-skipping outline, because heading navigation in real screen readers is noisy when every card has an `<h1>`. Pick by how users navigate, not by which is technically valid.
- **Semantic markup vs utility-class speed:** `<nav>`/`<ul>`/`<li>` with a handful of classes is faster to build, test and audit than 40 divs with per-element roles, and it keeps focus order equal to reading order instead of fighting it.

**Common wrong answers:** "Semantic HTML is mostly for SEO"; "adding `role="button"` to a div makes it a button"; "wrap every component in `<section>` for structure"; "use `<table>` to lay out a grid"; "a `<label>` placeholder is fine if the contrast is OK".

**Probe:** "You have a clickable product card `<div>` that navigates to the product page, and inside it an 'Add to cart' button." (Expect: nesting interactive controls inside a div-with-button-role breaks the keyboard and accessibility contract; fix by making the card's title a real `<a>` inside an `<article>`, keeping the cart action a separate sibling button, and stopping propagation deliberately rather than trapping the key event.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Meaning-driven element choice, defended from behaviour rather than looks.** Why `<button>` and not a styled `<div>`, and why `<a href>` and not a `<button>` for navigation (deep-linkable, middle-click, open in new tab).
- **Landmarks and heading outline as an accessibility mechanism** — that screen reader users navigate by landmark and by heading level, so a correct outline is a navigation feature, not typography.
- **Restraint.** Knowing `<section>` is not a generic `<div>`, that `<div>`/`<span>` are correct when nothing fits, and that over-landmarking a page with ten `<section>`s is its own accessibility failure.
- **Pragmatism with ARIA:** labelling multiple identical landmarks (`<nav aria-label="Footer">`), and the first rule of ARIA being "don't use ARIA if a native element exists".
**Mini task:** Take a div-soup page (copy any), refactor with semantic landmarks, run Lighthouse accessibility before and after, and navigate with VoiceOver, NVDA, or the browser's accessibility tree.


**Self-check:** Take a page you have actually shipped: name its landmarks, recite its heading outline, and point at every place a `<div>` is doing a semantic element's job — including one you would fix.

**Resources:** MDN's element reference for `<article>`, `<section>`, `<main>`, `<nav>` and `<address>`; WHATWG HTML spec §4.10 "Main elements"; WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices Guide "Landmark regions" and "ARIA labelling"; web.dev's "Use semantic HTML" plus Lighthouse's accessibility audits for before/after evidence.
---

## 3.1.3 Script Loading and Resource Hints
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

```
<script>              ── HTML parsing ▮▮▮ pause ▮ fetch ▮ execute ▮ resume ▮▮▮
<script async>        ── parsing ▮▮▮▮▮▮ (fetch in parallel) ▮ pause + execute when ready ▮▮▮
<script defer>        ── parsing ▮▮▮▮▮▮▮▮▮▮ (fetch in parallel) ▮ execute after parse, in order
<script type="module">── like defer by default; strict mode; own scope; import/export
```

| | Blocks parsing | Order preserved | Runs when | Use for |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| plain | Yes | Yes | Immediately | Rarely (tiny inline critical code) |
| `async` | Only during execution | No | ASAP after download | Independent scripts (analytics) |
| `defer` | No | Yes | After parsing, before `DOMContentLoaded` | App code that needs the DOM |
| `module` | No | Yes (deferred) | After parsing | Modern apps; `async` also allowed on modules |

**Resource hints**
- `<link rel="preload" href="font.woff2" as="font" crossorigin>`: fetch this *current-page* resource early at high priority (fonts need `crossorigin` even same-origin).
- `<link rel="preconnect" href="https://api.example.com">`: do DNS, TCP, and TLS early.
- `<link rel="dns-prefetch">`: DNS only, a cheaper fallback.
- `<link rel="prefetch" href="/next-page.js">`: low-priority fetch for a *likely future* navigation.
- `<link rel="modulepreload">`: preload ES modules.
- `fetchpriority="high"` on the LCP image, `loading="lazy"` for below-the-fold images, `<link rel="stylesheet" media="print">` tricks for non-critical CSS.
- **Overusing preload** hurts, because it competes with other critical resources.


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.1 (what blocks parsing versus rendering), 3.1.7 (`fetchpriority` and the LCP image, which these hints compete with).

**Why interviewers ask:** Load strategy is where real-world performance actually lives; it is the difference between a 1s and a 5s interactive time on a mid-range phone, and it is directly visible in a network waterfall. It also tests whether you understand the browser's scheduler and priority model rather than a bundler's config file.

**Definition:** The execution semantics the HTML spec gives each `<script>` type — parser-blocking, `defer`, `async` and `type="module"` — together with the `<link rel>` hints that let you start or prioritise a fetch before the browser would discover it. The hints are advisory: they change scheduling, not correctness.
**Questions**
1. `async` vs `defer`? When would each be wrong? *(async for scripts that depend on DOM or on each other can break. defer is the default choice.)*
2. Why can a `<script>` in `<head>` hurt performance?
3. `preload` vs `prefetch` vs `preconnect`?
4. Why must font preloads include `crossorigin`?
5. How are ES modules loaded differently from classic scripts?


**Mistakes:**
- **A `<script>` in `<head>` with no attributes,** which serialises download, execute and parse and delays first paint by the full network round trip plus compile time.
- **`preload` for the LCP image *and* `loading="lazy"` on it,** or `fetchpriority="high"` on several resources — it is a relative priority signal, not a guarantee.
- **Preloading a URL that redirects, 404s or is served from a different CDN path,** burning a high-priority slot; browsers warn about unused preloads.
- **Mixing `async` with a script that patches a global another script depends on,** which is the classic "worked in dev, broke in prod" ordering bug.
- **Treating `type="module"` as synchronous,** or reaching for `nomodule`-style dual tags without checking what your bundler actually emits.
- **Preloading a stylesheet as if it were a font,** without `as`/`type`/`crossorigin`, so the browser discards the hint and re-fetches.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`defer` vs `async` vs bundling:** `defer` keeps a guaranteed order and a single execution point for the price of one round trip; `async` executes at the earliest opportunity but requires order-independent code, which is why `<script async src="…/lit" type="module">` is the idiomatic third-party pattern; bundling collapses the request waterfall entirely at the cost of a build step and a cache-busting story.
- **Inlined critical CSS vs preloaded stylesheet:** inlining a few KB of above-the-fold CSS removes a round trip and beats any hint, at the cost of caching across pages and an extra build step; preloading the sheet keeps the cache but still pays the round trip. Split it — inline critical, preload the rest.
- **Prefetch on hover or intent vs on page load:** intent-based prefetch warms exactly what's needed and wastes nothing, at the cost of hooking navigation correctly in an SPA; load-time prefetch is simpler and burns bandwidth and data on users who never navigate.
- **Third-party scripts async vs facade / Partytown:** `async` keeps the parser unblocked and costs nothing to adopt, but the script still eats main-thread time, which is exactly what INP measures; a facade or off-main-thread execution buys real isolation for a UX and integration cost.
- **Few large hints vs many small ones:** connection limits and the preload scanner mean that bandwidth can only be bought once — optimising for the critical path usually beats hinting everything.

**Common wrong answers:** "`async` scripts execute in document order"; "`async` is faster so use it everywhere"; "`defer` blocks first paint"; "`preload` guarantees the resource isn't downloaded twice"; "`prefetch` is for resources this page needs"; "modules execute as soon as they download".

**Probe:** "You've preloaded a font, the hero image, three scripts and your API origin, and LCP got worse. Explain what happened and what you'd remove first." (Expect: over-preloading raises the priority of everything at once so it contends with CSS and the LCP image; unused preloads produce console warnings; the right fix is one preload for the LCP image at most, plus `preconnect` for cross-origin APIs, and nothing preloaded that the parser would discover anyway.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **"Blocks parsing" versus "blocks rendering."** Confusing these two is the most common error in this area and is worth more than any resource-hint trivia.
- **Modules as defer-by-default with `async` allowed,** and understanding that the graph is fetched in parallel while execution order still follows the dependency tree.
- **Resource-hint discipline:** they can say when preload *hurts*, how many preloads a page should have (one to three), and how to verify with the Network priority column and the Coverage panel.
- **The `crossorigin` on font preloads:** font fetches are always CORS-mode, so a font preloaded without `crossorigin` is fetched twice and the preload is wasted.
- **Honesty about the limits:** hints are ignored on slow connections and in data-saver mode, and `prefetch` on a 3G connection mostly costs the user data.

**Model answer, Q3:** "All three are bets about the future, and they differ in how expensive it is to be wrong. `prefetch` is a low-priority hint that you *may* need this resource on the next navigation, so a wasted fetch costs almost nothing. `preload` is a high-priority fetch of something you know this page needs — because misusing it (more than a handful, or preloading a resource that is about to be discovered anyway) steals bandwidth from your CSS and your LCP image and makes things worse. `preconnect` fetches nothing; it just moves DNS, TCP and TLS forward for an origin, which makes it the safe one to use liberally. So: preconnect liberally, preload sparingly and only what the HTML doesn't discover early, prefetch only for the next navigation."
**Mini task:** Load three scripts with dependencies using the three modes and log the execution order. Make a table of what you observed.


**Self-check:** Look at one waterfall with three critical bars — HTML, one stylesheet, one chunk — with budget for exactly one extra request: which do you attack, which do you hint, and which do you simply inline?

**Resources:** MDN `<script>` reference, especially the "Script type" and "Module scripts" sections; WHATWG HTML spec §4.12 "Scripting" for the `defer`/`async` algorithm; web.dev "Preload your resources" and "Render-blocking resources"; Chrome DevTools docs on the Network panel's priority column, plus Lighthouse's "Eliminate render-blocking resources" audit.
---

## 3.1.4 Forms and Validation
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Collecting user input with native controls and validating it in the browser (UX) and on the server (security).

**Why interviewers ask:** Forms are the main way users send data. Questions expose your knowledge of native validation, accessibility, UX, and the client/server trust boundary.


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.1 (labels need real `id`/`for` structure), 3.1.2 (native controls over div-soup), 3.1.5 (the accessible-error contract: naming, describing, announcing, focus).
**Key concepts**
- **Labels:** every control needs a programmatic label, via `<label for="id">` or by wrapping. Placeholders are *not* labels (they disappear and have poor contrast). A label also increases the click target.
- **Input types** give mobile keyboards and built-in validation: `email`, `tel`, `url`, `number`, `date`, `password`, `search`, `range`, `file`, `checkbox`, `radio`. Plus `inputmode="numeric"` for numeric keypads without `number`'s quirks (use it for OTP and card numbers, not `type=number`).
- **Constraint attributes:** `required`, `minlength`, `maxlength`, `min`, `max`, `step`, `pattern`, `type`.
- **Constraint Validation API:** `input.validity` (`valueMissing`, `typeMismatch`, `patternMismatch`, `tooShort`, `rangeUnderflow`, `customError`…), `checkValidity()`, `reportValidity()`, `setCustomValidity(msg)` (an empty string clears it), `:valid`/`:invalid`, and `:user-invalid` (only after user interaction, a newer and better UX hook).
- **`novalidate`** on the form disables built-in bubbles so you can render custom messages while still using the API.
- **`autocomplete` tokens** (`email`, `current-password`, `new-password`, `one-time-code`, `given-name`, `street-address`) enable autofill and password managers (also a WCAG requirement).
- **Submit behavior:** a `<button>` inside a form defaults to `type="submit"`, so always set `type="button"` for non-submit buttons. Enter submits when a form has a submit button.
- **`FormData`** collects values: `new FormData(form)`, then `Object.fromEntries(fd)`. Unchecked checkboxes aren't included. Multiple values per name need `getAll`.
- **File uploads:** `<input type="file" accept="image/*" multiple>` and `enctype="multipart/form-data"`. Validate type and size on the *server* (the `accept` attribute is only a hint).
- **Accessible errors:** associate with `aria-describedby`, set `aria-invalid="true"`, announce with `role="alert"` or `aria-live="polite"`, move focus to the first invalid field on submit, state the error in text (not by color alone), and don't clear user input.
- **Client vs server validation:** client validation is for UX. **Server validation is mandatory** because the client can be bypassed with curl or DevTools. Validate type, length, range, format, and authorization on the server, and return a field-level error structure (`422` with `{errors: {email: "..."}}`).
- **Fieldset/legend** group related controls (radio groups).
- **CSRF** (Phase 7) applies to cookie-authenticated form posts.

```html
<form id="signup" novalidate>
  <div>
    <label for="email">Email</label>
    <input id="email" name="email" type="email" required
           autocomplete="email" aria-describedby="email-err">
    <p id="email-err" class="error" hidden></p>
  </div>

  <div>
    <label for="pw">Password (min 12 chars)</label>
    <input id="pw" name="password" type="password" required minlength="12"
           autocomplete="new-password" aria-describedby="pw-err">
    <p id="pw-err" class="error" hidden></p>
  </div>

  <button type="submit">Create account</button>
</form>
```
```js
const form = document.querySelector('#signup');

form.addEventListener('submit', (e) => {
  let firstInvalid = null;
  for (const input of form.elements) {
    if (!(input instanceof HTMLInputElement)) continue;
    const err = document.getElementById(`${input.id}-err`);
    const ok = input.checkValidity();
    input.setAttribute('aria-invalid', String(!ok));
    err.hidden = ok;
    err.textContent = ok ? '' : input.validationMessage;
    if (!ok && !firstInvalid) firstInvalid = input;
  }
  if (firstInvalid) { e.preventDefault(); firstInvalid.focus(); }
});

// custom rule example
pw.addEventListener('input', () => {
  pw.setCustomValidity(/\d/.test(pw.value) ? '' : 'Include at least one number');
});
```

**Questions**
1. Why is client-side validation not enough?
2. How do you make form errors accessible?
3. `<input type="number">` problems? *(scroll wheel changes value, allows `e`, leading zeros lost, bad for IDs/phones/OTP, so use `text` + `inputmode`/`pattern`.)*
4. What's the Constraint Validation API?
5. What's the default `type` of a `<button>` in a form, and why does it matter?
6. Controlled vs uncontrolled inputs (React, covered in Phase 4)?
7. How would you validate an email? *(`type=email` for basic shape, then send a confirmation email. A regex can't prove the address exists or is deliverable.)*
8. How do you prevent double submission? *(disable the button while pending, use idempotency keys server-side)*
9. How do file uploads work in HTML? What are the security concerns? *(multipart, MIME sniffing, size limits, malware, path traversal, storing outside the web root, Phase 7)*
10. How do you preserve user data on validation failure?


**Trade-offs:**
- **Native bubbles vs custom error rendering:** `reportValidity()` is free, localised, accessible and impossible to style; custom rendering gives design control, inline summaries and per-field placement at the cost of your own ARIA wiring — the way to get both is `novalidate` plus the same API as the source of truth.
- **Validate on submit vs on blur vs on keystroke:** submit is predictable and cheapest; blur catches each field once at a moment the user has finished with it; keystroke feedback is the most responsive and the most hostile, since it flags an email as invalid at `a` and demands debouncing and sequence-guarding on any async rule.
- **`type="number"` vs `text` + `inputmode`:** number gives spinners, locale-dependent parsing, silent coercion of `1e3`, and lost leading zeros — fatal for IDs, card numbers and OTPs; text with `inputmode="numeric"` and a `pattern` gives a numeric keypad plus full control, at the price of parsing it yourself.
- **Synchronous rules vs an async availability check:** sync is race-free; async (debounced, abortable, guarded by a sequence number) avoids a wrong "email already taken" verdict on a slow connection, at the cost of staleness and out-of-order response bugs.
- **Native constraint attributes vs `pattern` regexes for complex rules:** attributes are declarative, localised and instantly accessible, but limited to one `pattern` per field and awkward for cross-field rules; JS plus `setCustomValidity` handles cross-field logic (confirm password, start before end) but is code you must test.

**Common wrong answers:** "Client-side validation prevents bad data"; "`type="email"` guarantees a real, deliverable address"; "the `accept` attribute stops malicious uploads"; "the client is enough because our API is internal"; "clear the form and re-render it on error".

**Probe:** "The user submits with an empty email and a too-short password. Walk me through what they hear and where focus lands, in order." (Expect: submit handler prevents default, loops fields, sets `aria-invalid`, reveals and fills linked error text, focuses the first invalid control, and the summary announces politely — and crucially, nothing they typed is discarded.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **The client/server trust boundary stated without hedging,** with a concrete attack attached rather than "it's insecure".
- **Whether accessibility is part of the form design, not a retrofit** — linked errors, an announcement path, focus moved on submit, user input preserved.
- **Real fluency with the API surface:** `ValidityState`, `setCustomValidity`, `novalidate` + `reportValidity`, `:user-invalid`, `FormData.getAll`.
- **Awareness of UX traps:** validating on every keystroke, blocking paste, losing input on failure, treating `accept` as enforcement, and double-submitting.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Because the client is untrusted by definition — anyone can replay a request with curl, flip a hidden field in DevTools, or skip the JS entirely, so client-side validation is a UX feature and never a security control. Its real jobs are instant feedback, avoiding a network round trip for obviously wrong input, and driving keyboard and autofill behaviour well. Everything that matters has to be enforced again on the server: type, length, range, format, uniqueness, permissions and CSRF, returning a `422` with a per-field error structure that you map back to the same `aria-describedby` nodes you used on the client."
**Mistakes:** placeholder as label; relying only on client validation; `type="submit"` by accident on plain buttons (page reload); error text only in red; blocking paste in password fields; regex-validating emails to death.

**Mini task:** Build a registration form with custom error rendering, `aria-describedby`, focus-first-error, async username-availability check (debounced), and a server stub that returns `422` with per-field errors that you display.


**Self-check:** Pick one form in a codebase you know and, for a single field, name its label link, its error link, and where focus lands on a failed submit — if you can't answer all three, that's your next commit.

**Resources:** MDN "Constraint Validation API", "Client-side form validation" and `HTMLFormElement.reportValidity()`; WHATWG HTML spec §4.10.5 "Form control infrastructure" (states and the constraint-validation algorithm); MDN's `autocomplete` token list; WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices for error-summary and field-error patterns; web.dev "Preventing spam submissions and bot attacks".
---

## 3.1.5 Accessibility Standards (HTML-level)
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** (deep dive: Phase 3 Part 3)

**Definition:** Building so people with disabilities (visual, motor, hearing, cognitive) can perceive, operate, and understand your UI.

**Why interviewers ask:** It's legally relevant (ADA, EAA, Section 508), widens your audience, and signals senior-level care.


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.2 (native elements first — rule 1 of ARIA), 3.1.4 (labels and the accessible-error pattern), 3.1.8 (`<dialog>`, popover and `inert` supply the modal behaviour).
**Key concepts**
- **WCAG** principles, **POUR:** Perceivable, Operable, Understandable, Robust. Levels A, AA (the usual legal target), and AAA. Current version is 2.2.
- **Text alternatives:** meaningful `alt` for informative images, `alt=""` for decorative ones (so screen readers skip them), and an `aria-label` or visually hidden text for icon-only buttons. Never omit `alt` entirely.
- **Contrast (AA):** 4.5:1 for normal text, 3:1 for large text (18pt+ or 14pt bold+) and UI components.
- **Keyboard:** everything operable via Tab, Shift+Tab, Enter, Space, arrows, and Esc. **Visible focus indicator** (never `outline: none` without a replacement), and use `:focus-visible`. Logical tab order follows DOM order, and avoid positive `tabindex`. A **skip link** to `#main`. Don't trap focus (except in modals, deliberately).
- **Target size:** WCAG 2.2 sets a 24×24 CSS px minimum (AA), with about 44×44 as a good mobile target.
- **ARIA:** it only changes what assistive tech *hears*, not behavior.
  - **Rule 1:** use native HTML first.
  - Roles (`role="dialog"`), states (`aria-expanded`, `aria-checked`, `aria-selected`), properties (`aria-label`, `aria-labelledby`, `aria-describedby`), and live regions (`aria-live`, `role="status"`, `role="alert"`).
  - **Bad ARIA is worse than no ARIA.** Incorrect roles mislead users.
- **Don't convey info by color alone**; respect `prefers-reduced-motion`; support zoom to 200% and reflow at 320px width; use relative units.
- **Forms:** labels, grouped controls, and error association (3.1.4).
- **Dynamic content:** announce changes (toasts, route changes in SPAs) with live regions, and manage focus on route change and modal open and close.
- **Testing:** keyboard-only pass, screen reader (VoiceOver, NVDA), axe DevTools, Lighthouse, the browser accessibility tree panel, and zoom and high-contrast testing. Automated tools catch only about 30-40% of issues.

```html
<a class="skip" href="#main">Skip to content</a>
<button aria-label="Close dialog"><svg aria-hidden="true">…</svg></button>
<button aria-expanded="false" aria-controls="menu">Menu</button>
<div id="status" role="status" aria-live="polite"></div>
```
```css
.sr-only { position:absolute; width:1px; height:1px; margin:-1px; padding:0;
           overflow:hidden; clip-path:inset(50%); white-space:nowrap; border:0; }
:focus-visible { outline: 3px solid #1a73e8; outline-offset: 2px; }
```

**Questions**
1. What are the first steps to make a page accessible?
2. What's ARIA and when should you not use it?
3. `alt=""` vs missing `alt` vs descriptive alt?
4. How do you test accessibility?
5. How do you make a custom dropdown or modal accessible? *(roles, focus management, keyboard handling, Esc, `aria-expanded`, return focus. Better: use native `<dialog>` and `<select>`.)*
6. `display:none` vs `visibility:hidden` vs `aria-hidden` vs `.sr-only` for screen readers? *(none and hidden remove from the accessibility tree. `aria-hidden` removes it from AT but not visually. `.sr-only` is visual-only hidden but announced.)*
7. How would you announce an async result (e.g., "Saved") to screen readers?
8. What contrast ratio does WCAG AA require?


**Mistakes:**
- **`outline: none` with nothing put back:** removing the focus ring without replacing it with a `:focus-visible` outline or box-shadow at 3:1 against adjacent colours leaves keyboard users with no cue about where they are. It is a WCAG 2.4.7 failure, and in forced-colors mode the indicator disappears entirely.
- **`role="button"` + `tabindex="0"` on a `div`, and stopping there:** ARIA changes what assistive tech *announces*, not what the element *does*. Without Space/Enter key handling and synchronised `aria-expanded`/`aria-checked`, you have shipped something that says "button" and does nothing — the exact case where the native `<button>` wins.
- **Creating the live region in the same tick you write its message:** `role="status"` or `aria-live` must be in the DOM and observed *before* the text changes, so injecting `<div role="alert">Saved</div>` as one new node is announced unreliably at best and silently at worst. Render the empty region on mount, then write to it.
- **Fixing tab order with a positive `tabindex`:** it overrides DOM order for the entire document, so every focusable element after that number is reordered as a side effect, for everyone. Reorder the DOM; `tabindex="0"` and `-1` exist for programmatic focus (a skip-link target, a dialog heading, a roving-tabindex stop), not for ordering.
- **Hiding an off-canvas menu or overlay with `display: none`, `hidden` or `visibility: hidden`:** all three remove it from the accessibility tree, so its links can never be focused or announced. Use visually-hidden CSS (`clip-path: inset(50%)`) or a closed `popover`. `aria-hidden="true"` on an element containing focusable content is the same bug in disguise — reachable by keyboard, invisible to AT.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Native elements vs ARIA:** native gives role, keyboard, focus, state and platform conventions, and is what AT vendors test against first; ARIA covers the gaps the HTML vocabulary lacks but a wrong role is worse than no role, so it should be the considered fallback rather than the default.
- **`role="application"` vs plain semantics:** `application` makes a screen reader hand keystrokes straight to your widget instead of reading normally. It rescues a grid, code editor or canvas surface and wrecks browse mode everywhere else, so scope it to the smallest subtree that needs it — usually after a native option has failed.
- **Automated tooling vs manual testing:** tooling is cheap, repeatable, CI-enforceable and catches the mechanical failures; manual testing with a screen reader and the keyboard finds broken flows, meaningless names and focus bugs no linter can see. The trade-off isn't choosing one — it's knowing which defect class each one owns.
- **Icon-only controls vs visible labels:** an icon-only button needs an accessible name, but a visible label serves everyone including users with cognitive disabilities; visually hidden text is the compromise when the design forbids the label, and it must never be used to justify removing the focus indicator.
- **Auto-focus in modals vs leaving focus on the trigger:** trapping focus inside a dialog is correct, but which element receives it is a judgement call — the first input for a form, the heading or the least destructive action for a confirmation.

**Common wrong answers:** "Lighthouse said 100, so it's accessible"; "ARIA can replace semantic HTML"; "`aria-label` on a `<div>` makes it a button"; "colour-only errors are fine if the contrast is high enough"; "screen reader users don't use hover or the mouse".

**Probe:** "The design team wants `outline: none` on `.btn:focus` because the ring is ugly." (Expect: replace with `:focus-visible` and an outline or box-shadow with at least 3:1 contrast against adjacent colours, offset so it isn't clipped, tested in forced-colors and on Windows High Contrast. There is no version of this answer where the indicator is simply removed — keyboard users navigate by that ring.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Native-first discipline,** and the ability to name one case where ARIA is genuinely the answer (a grid, a tablist, a combobox with listbox popup) rather than sprinkling roles everywhere.
- **Concrete thresholds rather than adjectives.** "High contrast" or "good focus states" score zero; 4.5:1, 3:1, 24×24, 200% are what they're listening for.
- **That ARIA changes the accessibility tree, not behaviour** — `role` and `aria-expanded` describe; they don't add key handling, focus management or disabled behaviour.
- **A testing method with both halves,** plus an honest statement of what automated tools miss.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Native HTML first. Every control is a real element, every image has correct `alt` — descriptive for informative, `alt=""` for decorative — every field has a programmatic label, and the page has one `<main>`, a heading outline that starts at `h1` and never skips, and a skip link. Then keyboard: every action reachable with Tab, Shift+Tab, Enter, Space, arrows and Esc, in DOM order, with a visible `:focus-visible` indicator and no focus trap outside a deliberate modal. Then the automatable numbers: 4.5:1 contrast for normal text, 3:1 for large text (24px regular or 18.66px bold) and for the boundaries of UI components, 24×24px minimum targets, reflow at 320px and zoom to 200%, and `prefers-reduced-motion` respected. Then test it manually with a screen reader, because tooling catches roughly a third of real issues."
**Mini task:** Unplug your mouse and complete a purchase flow on a real site. Write down every barrier. Then fix a small demo app until axe reports zero issues *and* it works by keyboard alone.


**Self-check:** Name the accessible name, the role and the keyboard contract for the three custom widgets in your last project, and say honestly where each was tested and where it wasn't.

**Resources:** WCAG 2.2 (W3C Recommendation) — success criteria 1.4.3, 1.4.11, 2.1.1, 2.4.7, 2.5.8, 3.3.8; WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices Guide, patterns for Dialog and Disclosure; MDN "ARIA", "ARIA states and properties" and the `prefers-reduced-motion` page; axe DevTools documentation; the "Accessible Names" material in the WebAIM/Deque curriculum.
---

## 3.1.6 SEO, Meta Tags, and Open Graph
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know** (**Must** for content and e-commerce roles)


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.1 (the head, and the fact that these tags must exist in the initial HTML), 3.1.2 (semantics, headings and link text are what search actually consumes).

**Why interviewers ask:** Because it is the boundary between code and business — the cheapest surface on which to demonstrate judgement about caching, staleness and content strategy — and because it tests whether you understand what a crawler can and cannot execute. In content and e-commerce roles it is frequently a first-round question on its own.

**Definition:** The head-of-page metadata layer: `<title>` and description, robots directives, canonical URLs, `hreflang`, JSON-LD structured data, and the Open Graph / Twitter card tags that shape link previews. It is document metadata read by crawlers and scrapers, most of which will not execute your JavaScript.
**Key concepts**
- **Crawl → render → index → rank.** Googlebot does execute JavaScript but in a deferred rendering queue. For critical content prefer **SSR/SSG** (Phase 4). Make sure content isn't behind client-only fetches or blocked by `robots.txt`.
- **Essentials**
  - Unique `<title>` (about 50-60 chars) and `<meta name="description">` (about 150-160 chars) per page
  - One clear `<h1>`, a logical heading outline, semantic HTML, descriptive link text (not "click here"), and `alt` text
  - Clean, human-readable URLs, and **canonical** tags: `<link rel="canonical" href="…">` to resolve duplicate content
  - `robots.txt` (crawl rules, not security) and `<meta name="robots" content="noindex">` (index rules), plus `sitemap.xml`
  - Proper status codes: 301 for moves, a real 404 (avoid "soft 404"), and a 410 for permanently gone
  - `hreflang` for localized variants
  - **Core Web Vitals** (LCP, INP, CLS) are ranking signals, and mobile-first indexing applies
  - **Structured data (JSON-LD)** enables rich results (Product, Article, FAQ, BreadcrumbList)
- **Open Graph / Twitter cards** control link previews (not ranking):

```html
<meta property="og:title" content="Title">
<meta property="og:description" content="Summary">
<meta property="og:image" content="https://example.com/og.png">  <!-- ~1200×630, absolute URL -->
<meta property="og:url" content="https://example.com/page">
<meta property="og:type" content="article">
<meta name="twitter:card" content="summary_large_image">

<script type="application/ld+json">
{ "@context":"https://schema.org", "@type":"Product", "name":"Mug",
  "offers": { "@type":"Offer", "price":"12.00", "priceCurrency":"USD" } }
</script>
```
- **SPA SEO pitfalls:** the same `<title>` on every route, content loaded only after login or interaction, hash-based URLs, and missing server-side 404s.
- **Social crawlers don't run JS**, so OG tags must be in the server-rendered HTML.

**Questions**
1. How would you make a React SPA SEO-friendly? *(SSR/SSG with Next.js or similar, per-route metadata, canonical, sitemap, real links `<a href>`, structured data, performance.)*
2. `noindex` vs `robots.txt` disallow? *(A disallowed URL can still be indexed from links without content. To remove it from results, allow crawling and use `noindex`.)*
3. What's a canonical URL and why?
4. What are Open Graph tags? Why do they sometimes not update? *(scraper caches. Use the Sharing Debugger)*
5. What's structured data?
6. Why does link text matter?


**Mistakes:**
- **Using `Disallow` in `robots.txt` to take a page out of the index:** a blocked URL can still be indexed from inbound links without ever being fetched, leaving a result with no snippet. Allow the crawl and serve `<meta name="robots" content="noindex">` instead — and note there is no `noindex` directive *in* `robots.txt` at all.
- **Relative `og:image` and `og:url`:** a scraper has no base URL to resolve `/og.png` against, so the preview renders blank or falls back to a stale cached asset. Absolute URLs on a publicly fetchable host, plus `og:image:width`/`og:image:height` and a ~1200×630 file.
- **Setting `<title>`, description or OG tags from JavaScript after hydration:** crawlers and every social scraper read the initial HTML response, so both the search snippet and the link preview come from the root document and every route looks identical. Per-route metadata has to exist in the server response — SSR/SSG or edge injection.
- **An SPA catch-all returning `200` with "no results found" body copy:** that is a soft 404, and it stays indexed carrying the wrong snippet. Deleted or moved pages need a real `404` (or `410` when permanently gone), and client-side `router.replace()` cannot produce a status code at all.
- **Pointing `rel="canonical"` at a URL that redirects or 404s, or canonicalising a paginated set to page 1:** the canonical is a hint, not a redirect. A broken target is ignored, or worse, consolidates ranking signals onto nothing, and folding `?page=2` into page 1 hides deep content from users as well as crawlers. Keep canonicals absolute, self-referencing on the canonical page, and use a real `301` for genuine moves.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Client-side routing vs SSR/SSG:** SPA routing is cheaper to build and fine for authenticated application surfaces, but crawlers and every social scraper see a shell; SSR/SSG buys indexability, link previews and per-route metadata for the price of server work and hydration. Hybrid — static or streamed shell, partial hydration — is where most teams end up.
- **`<meta name="robots" content="noindex">` vs `robots.txt` Disallow:** `noindex` requires the crawler to be able to fetch the page in order to see the tag, so it works; Disallow prevents exactly that and can leave a bare, indexed URL with no snippet. Use Disallow to conserve crawl budget, `noindex` to remove from results, and neither as access control.
- **Static tags vs edge/CDN-injected metadata:** injection lets a content team change titles and OG copy without a deploy and can vary by crawler, but adds a caching layer that is easy to get wrong behind a CDN (a stale `og:image` is the classic symptom). Static tags are boring, cacheable and verifiable in `curl`.
- **Hand-written JSON-LD vs generated from the API response:** schemas hand-written into templates drift out of sync with the rendered price and availability within weeks; generating them from the same payload that renders the page is the only way they stay correct at scale.

**Common wrong answers:** "Meta keywords still matter"; "robots.txt removes a page from Google"; "set `og:image` to `/og.png` and it works everywhere"; "put 20 keywords in the title"; "SSR is only for SEO — users don't care"; "SPA routing is fine because Googlebot runs JavaScript now".

**Probe:** "Your 20-route React SPA gets indexed as a single page with the first route's title. What do you change, and what changes if it's 5,000 products?" (Expect: per-route SSR or SSG metadata, real URLs and real status codes, sitemap plus internal `<a>` linking so crawlers can discover routes, a canonical per route, JSON-LD generated from the same data; at 5,000 pages, static generation or edge-injected metadata rather than anything client-side.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Where the metadata has to live.** Social scrapers and most crawlers read the server response, not the hydrated DOM, so a client-set `<title>` is invisible.
- **Robots directives vs `robots.txt`** — a genuinely common trap where a confident wrong answer disqualifies a candidate for content roles.
- **Duplicate-content strategy:** canonical vs 301 vs parameter handling vs `hreflang`, and how they interact.
- **That Open Graph is a preview, not SEO,** and that OG tags can be injected at the edge from a cache so marketing can change copy without a deploy.

**Model answer, Q3:** "A canonical URL is the single address you declare as authoritative via `<link rel="canonical" href="…">`. It exists because one page has many addresses — trailing slash or not, `?utm_*`, `?page=1`, uppercase paths, http vs https, www vs apex, print views — and without a canonical the ranking signals get split across duplicates. Three caveats: it is a hint, not a directive, so if canonicals conflict or contradict the sitemap Google ignores them; it never redirects, so duplicates are still crawled and can still win; and a canonical pointing at a page that returns 404 or a redirect is a bug, not an optimisation. Use a real `301` for genuine moves, keep canonicals absolute and self-referencing on canonical pages, and never canonicalise two genuinely different pages to the same URL."
**Mini task:** Audit a page with Lighthouse and view-source, then write the missing meta, canonical, OG, and JSON-LD. Validate with the Rich Results Test.


**Self-check:** For the last page you shipped, describe exactly what a crawler and a chat app's scraper see in the first response — then name the single change that would make it invisible.

**Resources:** Google Search Central docs on `robots.txt`, `noindex`, canonicalisation and "JavaScript SEO basics"; the Open Graph protocol specification and the X (Twitter) Cards documentation; Schema.org plus Google's structured data guidelines, Rich Results Test and Schema Markup Validator; Bing Webmaster Tools documentation for `IndexNow`.
---

## 3.1.7 Responsive Images and Media
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** (for performance)


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.1 (what blocks first paint, and why head ordering matters), 3.1.3 (priority hints that compete with the image for bandwidth), 3.2.7 (breakpoints, since `sizes` is expressed in the same media conditions).

**Why interviewers ask:** Images are usually the largest share of page bytes on content sites and are the LCP element on most pages, so this is where Core Web Vitals are won or lost. It is also one of the few areas where the correct answer is "let the browser decide, but tell it the constraints" — which reveals whether you understand layout well enough to write `sizes`.

**Definition:** The markup and attributes that let the browser choose an image file, crop or format for the actual viewport and pixel density: `<img srcset>` with `w` or `x` descriptors, `sizes`, `<picture>` with `<source>`, plus `width`/`height`, `loading`, `decoding`, `fetchpriority`, and the `<video>` equivalents (`preload`, `poster`, `<track>`).
**Key concepts**
- **Resolution switching:** let the browser choose a file by viewport and DPR.
```html
<img src="photo-800.jpg"
     srcset="photo-400.jpg 400w, photo-800.jpg 800w, photo-1600.jpg 1600w"
     sizes="(min-width: 1024px) 33vw, 100vw"
     width="800" height="600" alt="Team at the 2024 offsite"
     loading="lazy" decoding="async">
```
  `w` descriptors say how wide each file is, and `sizes` says how wide the image will *display*. The browser combines these with DPR to pick one. (`x` descriptors suit fixed-size images.)
- **Art direction and formats with `<picture>`:**
```html
<picture>
  <source type="image/avif" srcset="hero.avif">
  <source type="image/webp" srcset="hero.webp">
  <source media="(max-width: 600px)" srcset="hero-crop.jpg">
  <img src="hero.jpg" width="1200" height="600" alt="…">
</picture>
```
- **Prevent CLS:** always set `width` and `height` (or CSS `aspect-ratio`), and the browser reserves space before the image loads.
- **Lazy-load below the fold; never lazy-load the LCP/hero image.** Give it `fetchpriority="high"`.
- **Formats:** AVIF (best compression) → WebP → JPEG/PNG fallback. SVG for icons and logos. Prefer video over GIF (`<video autoplay muted loop playsinline>`).
- **`object-fit: cover|contain`** controls cropping in a box.
- **Video/audio:** `<video controls preload="metadata" poster>` with `<track kind="captions">` for accessibility.
- **Image CDNs/frameworks** (`next/image`, Cloudinary, imgix) automate resizing and format negotiation.

**Questions**
1. `srcset` vs `sizes` vs `<picture>`: what does each do?
2. How do images cause layout shift? How do you prevent it?
3. Why shouldn't the hero image be lazy-loaded?
4. Which format would you choose and why?
5. How do you serve an appropriately cropped image on mobile? *(`<picture>` with `media` sources)*


**Mistakes:**
- **Using `w` descriptors with no `sizes`:** the spec default is `sizes="100vw"`, so a 400px card in a three-up grid downloads the full-width candidate on every device. `sizes` is a claim about your CSS and the browser has no way to correct you — this is the single largest source of wasted bytes on a media-heavy page.
- **Mixing `w` and `x` descriptors in one `srcset`:** that is invalid, and the browser discards the entire attribute and falls back to `src` — the precise opposite of what you wanted. Pick one scheme per `srcset`: `w` for fluid images, `x` only for fixed-size assets where `sizes` is irrelevant.
- **Setting `width`/`height` to the rendered size:** those attributes describe the *intrinsic* dimensions, which the browser turns into an aspect-ratio placeholder before the bytes arrive. Put your CSS display size on them and you reserve the wrong box (CLS) while telling the browser the file is smaller than it is, so it upscales instead of picking a larger candidate.
- **`loading="lazy"` on the hero or LCP image:** lazy images are only fetched once layout has finished, at deliberately low priority, so the largest paint element on the page is discovered last. Load it eagerly with `fetchpriority="high"` and a `<link rel="preload">`, and keep `lazy` for below-the-fold content.
- **Putting a format `<source>` above the crop `<source>` in `<picture>`:** the first matching `<source>` wins outright, so a media-less `<source type="image/avif">` at the top swallows every `media` crop below it — and every browser without AVIF loses the art direction entirely. Order most specific to least.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`<picture>` with `media` sources vs one flexible crop:** art direction ships only what each viewport needs and lets you compose for the screen, at the cost of more files, more markup and duplication that frameworks make awkward; a single crop is simpler but on mobile the subject frequently ends up cropped out — a brand problem that no amount of compression fixes.
- **AVIF/WebP with fallbacks vs JPEG only:** AVIF is typically 30–50% smaller than JPEG at matched quality and WebP ~25–35%, but AVIF encoding is slow enough to belong in a build step, and every route through negotiation — `<picture>` or `Accept` header — needs a fallback anyway. JPEG-only is universally supported and simple, and leaves that saving on the table.
- **Image CDN/service (`imgix`, Cloudinary, `next/image`) vs an in-repo build step:** a CDN gives on-the-fly resizing, format negotiation, automatic `srcset` from one URL and no build time; it also adds a vendor, per-request cost and a caching configuration to get wrong. A build step keeps derivatives in git, reviewable and cache-busted by hash, but bloats the repo and lengthens CI.
- **`<video>` vs an animated image:** `<video autoplay muted loop playsinline>` is typically an order of magnitude smaller than the equivalent GIF and gets composited on the GPU; it costs codec negotiation, requires `muted` and `playsinline` for autoplay on mobile, and adds a heavier element.
- **Serve a smaller file vs a bigger file at lower quality:** below roughly 2–4KB the request overhead dominates, so a tiny SVG or an inline data-URI icon beats any raster candidate.

**Common wrong answers:** "`sizes` is a list of breakpoints the browser switches between"; "`x` descriptors are the modern approach to responsive images"; "lazy-load everything that isn't above the fold — including the hero"; "set `width` to the rendered size so the browser doesn't upscale"; "put `?w=800` on the URL and let the CDN sort it out, no markup needed".

**Probe:** "Your hero is a 3000×1200 JPEG shown at 1200px on desktop and 375px on mobile. Write the markup and the pipeline that produces the files." (Expect: `srcset` candidates at roughly 400/800/1200/1600w, `sizes="(min-width: 1024px) 1200px, 100vw"`, `<picture>` with AVIF and WebP sources plus a JPEG fallback, `width`/`height` set, no `loading="lazy"`, `fetchpriority="high"`, and a build step (`sharp`, `squoosh`) or an image CDN doing the resizing.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **That the browser, not the developer, picks the file** — and that the developer's job is to describe layout accurately.
- **`sizes` as a layout assertion,** and the specific cost of over-estimating it.
- **CLS discipline:** intrinsic `width`/`height` or CSS `aspect-ratio`, always, on every image and video.
- **Priority judgement:** which element is LCP on this page, and that lazy-loading it is a bug rather than an optimisation.
- **Format and dimension trade-offs** grounded in real numbers, not "WebP is better".

**Model answer, Q1:** "`srcset` is the menu and `sizes` is the promise. Each candidate in `srcset` is declared with its intrinsic width (`400w, 800w, 1600w`); `sizes` states how wide the image will actually be laid out in CSS pixels using media conditions. The browser takes `sizes`, multiplies by `devicePixelRatio`, and picks the smallest candidate that covers the result. Get `sizes` wrong — `sizes="100vw"` on a 400px-wide card is the classic failure — and you download a file far larger than you need. `x` descriptors suit fixed-size icons and never involve `sizes` at all. `<picture>` is the wrapper that lets you change the source itself: a different crop per breakpoint or a different format per browser, its `<source>` elements are evaluated in order, and the `<img>` is the mandatory fallback."
**Mini task:** Take a 3MB hero image and get it under 150KB on mobile using AVIF/WebP, `srcset`, and proper dimensions. Compare LCP before and after in Lighthouse.


**Self-check:** For the last page you shipped, name the LCP element and state which candidate file the browser selected for it on a 390px, DPR-3 phone — and how you'd prove it from the Network panel.

**Resources:** MDN `<img>` reference (the `srcset` and `sizes` sections), `<picture>`, and the responsive images guide; web.dev "Responsive images" and "Use images responsibly", including preloading the LCP image and `fetchpriority`; web.dev's `sizes` visualiser; the `sharp` documentation if you generate derivatives in the build.
---

## 3.1.8 Native Interactive Elements: `<dialog>`, `<details>`, Popover
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

- **`<dialog>`:** `dialog.showModal()` opens it in the top layer, with a `::backdrop`, **focus trapping**, inert background, and **Esc to close**, all built in. `dialog.show()` opens non-modally. `dialog.close(returnValue)`, and `<form method="dialog">` closes it on submit. Focus is restored to the previously focused element in modern browsers.
```html
<button id="open">Delete…</button>
<dialog id="confirm" aria-labelledby="t">
  <form method="dialog">
    <h2 id="t">Delete item?</h2>
    <button value="cancel">Cancel</button>
    <button value="ok">Delete</button>
  </form>
</dialog>
<script>
  open.onclick = () => confirm.showModal();
  confirm.addEventListener('close', () => console.log(confirm.returnValue));
</script>
```
- **`<details>`/`<summary>`:** an accessible, JS-free disclosure. With a shared `name` attribute, several details form an exclusive accordion. It's searchable and keyboard-operable.
- **Popover API:** `<button popovertarget="tip">` + `<div id="tip" popover>` gives light-dismiss, top-layer rendering, and Esc handling. It's good for menus and tooltips (anchor positioning is arriving in CSS).
- **`inert` attribute:** makes a subtree non-interactive and non-focusable (handy for modals you build yourself).
- **Why it matters:** these replace hundreds of lines of JS and ARIA, with better accessibility by default.


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.2 (semantics, and rule 1 of ARIA), 3.1.5 (the dialog focus and keyboard contract), 3.2.3 (positioning, stacking contexts and `overflow` clipping — which the top layer overrides).

**Why interviewers ask:** "Build a modal" is the classic take-home and frontend-round question, and it cleanly separates people who know the platform from people who have a `z-index: 9999` `<div>`, a hand-written focus trap and a document-level keydown listener. It also signals that you keep up with the platform instead of reaching for a dependency for every overlay.

**Definition:** Disclosure, dialog and overlay behaviour that is now a platform feature rather than DIY JavaScript: `<dialog>` with `showModal()`/`show()` rendered into the top layer, `<details>`/`<summary>` (optionally exclusive via a shared `name`), and the Popover API with light dismiss and top-layer rendering.
**Questions**
1. What do you get for free from `<dialog>.showModal()`?
2. How would you build an accessible modal from scratch (if you couldn't use `<dialog>`)? *(role=dialog + aria-modal, labelled, focus moves in, trapped inside, Esc closes, backdrop click closes, focus returns, background inert/scroll lock)*
3. `<details>` vs a custom accordion?


**Mistakes:**
- **Adding `role="dialog"` or `aria-modal="true"` to a `showModal()` dialog:** both are already implicit, and an explicit non-modal `role="dialog"` overrides the platform's modality — you keep the top layer but lose the inertness and focus behaviour you opened it for.
- **Shipping `<dialog open>` in the markup, or calling `show()` when you meant a modal:** the `open` attribute and `show()` both give a non-modal dialog — no inert background, no Esc, no focus trap, no `::backdrop`, and it stays in normal flow so it scrolls away with the page. Only `showModal()` promotes it to the top layer.
- **Opening the dialog with no accessible name:** `showModal()` supplies role and modality, not a name. Without `aria-labelledby` on the heading or an `aria-label`, it is announced as a bare "dialog" and the user has to go hunting for what it is.
- **Assuming `showModal()` centres itself and that clicking the backdrop closes it:** it does neither. You still write `margin: auto` with `max-width`/`max-height` for centring and internal scrolling, and `::backdrop` is not a dismiss target — closing on backdrop click needs a `click` listener that checks `event.target === dialog`.
- **Using `popover` for a tooltip or menu without `popover="manual"`:** light dismiss closes it the instant the pointer crosses from trigger to popup, and any click inside kills it, so the tooltip never appears. `manual` restores Esc-only dismissal and you own hover/focus intent with a delay.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`<dialog>` + `showModal()` vs a hand-built ARIA modal:** native gives inertness, top layer, focus trap, Esc, backdrop and scroll lock with zero JS; custom is still the fallback where `<dialog>` is unavailable, or where you need exit animations and portals across frames — at which point you own every behaviour above plus the focus-return bug, which is where most implementations leak.
- **`<dialog>` vs popover for the same UI:** `showModal()` is modal (background inert, no dismiss by click) which is right for a confirmation; `popover` is non-modal (light dismiss, background stays interactive) which is right for a tooltip, a non-critical popover and menus — use `popover="manual"` for tooltips so a click can't strand it open, and add your own hover/focus handlers with a delay.
- **`<details>` vs a custom accordion:** native is correct for free and keeps content in the DOM, with limited animation hooks and one `name`-based exclusivity model; custom allows deep-linking, controlled state and arbitrary animation at the cost of `aria-expanded` bookkeeping, key handling and focus management.
- **Native behaviour vs a library:** libraries win when you need portals, transitions, virtualisation and consistent cross-browser edge cases; native wins on bundle size, correctness guarantees and the fact that focus and Esc semantics were designed rather than patched.

**Common wrong answers:** "`<dialog>` is a `<div>` with a class"; "I still add `role="dialog"` and `aria-modal` to a `showModal()` dialog"; "popover traps focus, so it's a modal"; "we use `<details>` for dropdown menus"; "`showModal()` scrolls the page to the dialog".

**Probe:** "Your confirm dialog lives inside a scrollable container that also has a sticky header. What does `<dialog>` fix, and what CSS do you still write?" (Expect: the top layer removes all stacking and ancestor clipping — `overflow: hidden` no longer clips it — and `::backdrop` covers the viewport; you still write `margin: auto` for centring, `inset`, `max-width`, internal scrolling for a tall dialog, and `::backdrop` styling such as `background: rgb(0 0 0 / 0.4)`.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Platform knowledge:** top layer, inertness, light dismiss, and that `showModal()` is a behaviour, not an attribute — many candidates still reach for `role="dialog" aria-modal="true"`, which is redundant on a modal dialog.
- **What remains your job** — naming, initial focus, content, and verifying focus return rather than assuming it.
- **The `returnValue` pattern for confirms,** which is the clearest "I actually ship this" signal.
- **Restraint:** where these elements stop working — multi-step wizards, deeply nested modals, animated height on `<details>`, tooltips that need hover intent.

**Model answer, Q1:** "`showModal()` gives you the entire ARIA modal pattern from the platform, with no attributes. The dialog is promoted into the top layer, so no `z-index`, transform or `overflow: hidden` on an ancestor can put it behind or clip it; everything outside becomes `inert` — unfocusable, non-clickable, and no longer scrollable; focus moves inside on open and is trapped while open, and is restored to the previously focused element on close; Esc closes it; and `::backdrop` is styleable. What is still yours: the name (`aria-labelledby` on the heading, or `aria-label`), initial focus (usually `autofocus` on the first field or the least destructive action), and the contents. With `<form method="dialog">` a button's `value` becomes `dialog.returnValue` on close, so a confirm dialog is `showModal()` plus a `close` event listener — no promises, no state machine."
**Mini task:** Build a confirm modal, an FAQ accordion, and a dropdown menu using only native elements.


**Self-check:** Delete the focus trap, scroll lock and z-index from your modal component and replace it with `showModal()` — what breaks, and what exactly do you still have to write yourself?

**Resources:** MDN `<dialog>` reference, including `showModal()`, `::backdrop` and the top-layer explanation, and MDN's Popover API and `popover` attribute reference; CSS anchor positioning on MDN for tooltips and menus; the W3C WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices "Dialog (Modal)" pattern, which is still the contract you must satisfy for naming; Scott Dom's write-ups on `<dialog>` and popover as the canonical practical examples.
---

## 3.1.9 SVG
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

- **Definition:** XML-based vector graphics, resolution-independent and scriptable.
- **Usage options**

| Method | CSS-stylable internals | Cached | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `<img src="x.svg">` | No | Yes | Simple, no scripting |
| Inline `<svg>` | Yes (`currentColor`, CSS vars) | No (part of HTML) | Best for icons you theme and animate |
| CSS `background-image` | No | Yes | Decorative |
| `<use href="#id">` sprite | Partial (via CSS vars) | Yes | Reuse many icons |

- **`viewBox="0 0 24 24"`** defines the internal coordinate system, and the SVG scales to fit its CSS size. `preserveAspectRatio` controls fit.
- **Theming:** `fill="currentColor"` makes icons inherit text color.
- **Accessibility:** decorative → `aria-hidden="true"`. Meaningful → `<title>` + `role="img"` + `aria-labelledby`.
- **Performance:** optimize with SVGO, avoid huge path data, and avoid thousands of nodes (use Canvas then).
- **SVG vs Canvas vs DOM:** SVG has a retained scene graph (each shape is a DOM node, so it's easy to attach events and accessible, but slow with many thousands of elements). Canvas is immediate mode (fast for many objects, but you manage hit-testing and accessibility).
- **Security:** inline untrusted SVG can contain scripts, so sanitize user-uploaded SVGs (DOMPurify) or serve via `<img>`.


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.2 (inline markup, and `aria-hidden` versus a name for meaningful graphics), 3.1.7 (raster image sizing, CLS and format trade-offs — the same rules govern the `viewBox` and dimensions problem).

**Why interviewers ask:** It sits exactly on the boundary between "design asset" and "DOM", and choosing wrongly shows up in both performance reviews (a 200KB icon repeated 30 times) and security reviews (an unsanitised user-uploaded SVG). Icon theming is the concrete test: if you can't recolour an icon from a design system, you don't own your assets.

**Definition:** An XML dialect for describing resolution-independent graphics as a retained scene graph of paths, shapes and text that you can query, style and script like DOM; or, when referenced as a file, as an immutable image asset loaded through `<img>`, CSS or `<use>`.
**Questions:** inline SVG vs `<img>`? How do you recolor an icon with CSS? What's `viewBox`? SVG vs Canvas for 10k points? *(Canvas, or WebGL.)* Security risk of user-uploaded SVG? *(XSS through `<script>` or event handlers.)*


**Self-check:** Given two delivery options for an icon set, state which one lets you change colour, hover state and stroke width from CSS without touching the asset — and name the case where each one breaks.

**Resources:** MDN `<svg>` reference (`viewBox`, `preserveAspectRatio`), MDN `<use>`, and MDN's "Using SVG"; the W3C SVG 2 specification for the coordinate and rendering model; SVGO's option documentation for safe optimisation; DOMPurify's README for the default sanitisation policy and what it deliberately allows; CSS-Tricks' guide to `currentColor` in inline SVG for the theming pattern.

**Mini task:** Build five 24×24 line icons as a single inline symbol sprite. Consume them with `<use>`, make them inherit `currentColor`, scale via `width`/`height` with no viewBox edits, and animate one path's `stroke-dashoffset` on hover. Then inline one of them 30 times and diff the HTML bytes against the `<use>` version. Finish by sanitising a deliberately hostile user SVG with DOMPurify and listing what it stripped.

**Mistakes:**
- **`<svg>` with a `viewBox` but no `width`/`height` (or vice versa),** which either collapses the icon to 300×150 default or distorts it when the ratio disagrees with the container.
- **Hard-coded `fill="#000"` in an exported asset,** so theming requires a fork of the design source; the fix is `fill="currentColor"` or a CSS variable in the file.
- **Inconsistent `viewBox` units across a set** (16 vs 24 vs 32), so icons render at different sizes until every instance is patched with CSS.
- **Pasting a design tool's output straight into the page** with editor metadata, `xmlns` soup, thousands of unnecessary path nodes and no SVGO pass.
- **Using `<img alt="">` for a meaningful icon,** which silently removes it from screen readers; and conversely shipping a decorative icon without `aria-hidden`, which makes screen readers announce "image" for chrome.
- **Deep-nesting SVG in SVG** for a sprite hit, which breaks `currentColor` inheritance and inflates node count.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Inline SVG vs `<img src="…svg">`:** inline is stylable, targetable and animatable per path but is not separately cacheable and repeats markup; `<img>` is cached, immutable, one DOM node, and sandboxed against script and external fetches, but it's a black box you can only size and (awkwardly) recolour with `mask` or `filter`.
- **`<use>` sprite vs inline copies:** a sprite defines each icon once, is themable through inherited properties and CSS custom properties, and repetition is nearly free; the cost is that styling *inside* a `<use>` shadow tree is limited to inheritance (fill, stroke, CSS variables) — you cannot target inner paths — and cross-document `<use href="file.svg#id">` has weaker support outside Chromium. Inline copies give full styling control at the price of bytes per instance.
- **`<img>` vs inline or `<object>` for untrusted SVG:** `<img>` puts the graphic in a restricted mode with no scripting, no external resource loading and no interaction, which neutralises XSS; inline and `<object>` execute script and load external resources, so anything user-supplied must go through DOMPurify with a strict allowlist first.
- **SVG vs Canvas vs CSS for icon work:** CSS masks and `currentColor` are cheapest for monochrome UI icons but offer no path-level control and no animation beyond transforms; SVG wins when shapes are dynamic or themed per state; Canvas only wins at thousands of moving elements, and it discards accessibility entirely.

**Common wrong answers:** "SVG is just a fancy image format"; "SVG is always smaller than PNG"; "`viewBox` sets the width and height"; "`aria-label` on an `<svg>` is all the accessibility you need"; "you can inline any user-uploaded SVG safely because it's just a picture".

**Probe:** "The design system ships 400 icons; the app needs them themed, hover-animated and used ~30 times per page. How do you deliver them?" (Expect: one symbol sprite inlined once per document (or per route bundle) and referenced with `<use href="#icon-id">`, `fill="currentColor"` with `stroke-width` normalised, styled from the app's CSS, and note that 30 `<use>` references to one definition are cheap while 30 inlined copies are not — plus a build-time SVG-to-sprite step or an existing icon package.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Whether the choice is driven by requirements** — theming, animation, interaction, repetition count, cacheability — rather than by preference or habit.
- **That inline SVG is DOM and therefore stylable:** `currentColor`, CSS variables, per-path selectors, and the fact that `fill="none"` icons need `stroke="currentColor"` too.
- **`viewBox` versus `width`/`height`:** the coordinate system versus the rendered box, and that the ratio between them must match or the graphic distorts.
- **Security judgement** — inline or `<object>` SVG is active content; `<img>` is not.
- **Accessibility precision:** decorative `aria-hidden="true"`, meaningful `role="img"` with a name, not just `aria-label` sprinkled on.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Inline it if the icon needs to be themed, animated or targeted; reference it as a file if it doesn't. Inline SVG is part of the HTML document, so its paths are real DOM: `fill="currentColor"` makes it inherit text colour, CSS custom properties reach into it, `:hover` and `transition` work per path, and you can attach listeners. The cost is that it's not separately cacheable — it repeats in every page and every instance, and 30 copies of a 3KB icon is real bytes. `<img src="icon.svg">` is the opposite trade: cached once by URL, one node per instance, and loaded in a restricted mode where scripts don't run and no external resources load, which is also what makes it the safe delivery for untrusted SVG. So: themed or animated icons inline (usually via `<use>` against one sprite), decorative or user-supplied graphics through `<img>`, and full-colour brand logos through `<picture>`/`<img>` so you can serve them responsively."
---

## 3.1.10 Canvas API
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know** (appears in games, charts, image editing, drawing apps)

**Definition:** A bitmap drawing surface controlled by JavaScript (`2D` context, plus `WebGL`/`WebGPU` for GPU graphics).


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.7 (`drawImage`, and the same dimension and format rules that govern backing-store sizing), 3.1.9 (SVG — the retained-mode counterpoint you must justify canvas against), 3.1.1 (raster versus vector, and `devicePixelRatio` as a window property).

**Why interviewers ask:** It is the take-home staple — charts, editors, games, drawing tools — and the one place where the browser's retained-mode model disappears. They want to see whether you understand that you have traded the DOM's free hit-testing, accessibility and event handling for pixel control and speed, and whether you know precisely what you now own. It is also a clean test of engineering hygiene: HiDPI handling and frame-rate independence are visible in any code review.
**Key concepts**
