- **Immediate mode:** once drawn, pixels are just pixels. There are no objects. To "move" something you clear and redraw the scene each frame.
- **Setup and HiDPI fix:** the canvas has a *bitmap size* (`width`/`height` attributes) separate from its *CSS size*. If you don't scale for `devicePixelRatio`, drawings look blurry on retina screens.
- **Animation loop:** use `requestAnimationFrame` (synced to display refresh and paused in background tabs), and make motion **time-based** (delta time), not frame-based.
- **API surface:** paths (`beginPath`, `moveTo`, `lineTo`, `arc`, `fill`, `stroke`), shapes (`fillRect`, `clearRect`), text, images (`drawImage`), transforms (`translate`, `rotate`, `scale`), state (`save()`/`restore()`), compositing, and `getImageData`/`putImageData` for pixel manipulation.
- **Hit-testing and events:** the canvas is one element, so you compute hits yourself (distance, bounding boxes, or an offscreen color-picking trick).
- **Accessibility:** the canvas is opaque to assistive tech, so provide fallback content inside the tag, ARIA labels, or a parallel DOM and data table.
- **Performance:** batch draw calls, avoid per-frame allocations, cache static layers on an offscreen canvas, and use `OffscreenCanvas` in a Web Worker, WebGL for heavy scenes, and integer coordinates for crisp lines.

```js
const canvas = document.querySelector('canvas');
const ctx = canvas.getContext('2d');
const dpr = window.devicePixelRatio || 1;
const { width, height } = canvas.getBoundingClientRect();
canvas.width = width * dpr;  canvas.height = height * dpr;
ctx.scale(dpr, dpr);                       // draw in CSS pixels

let x = 0, last = performance.now();
function frame(now) {
  const dt = (now - last) / 1000; last = now;   // seconds since last frame
  x = (x + 120 * dt) % width;                   // 120 px/second regardless of FPS
  ctx.clearRect(0, 0, width, height);
  ctx.fillStyle = '#1a73e8';
  ctx.beginPath(); ctx.arc(x, height / 2, 20, 0, Math.PI * 2); ctx.fill();
  requestAnimationFrame(frame);
}
requestAnimationFrame(frame);
```

**Questions**
1. Canvas vs SVG: when to use each?
2. Why does canvas content look blurry on retina screens? Fix?
3. Why `requestAnimationFrame` and not `setInterval`?
4. How do you detect a click on a drawn shape?
5. How do you make canvas content accessible?
6. How would you build a drawing app with undo? *(store strokes as data, redraw from the list, a command stack, or snapshots with `getImageData` for small canvases)*


**Mistakes:**
- **Ignoring `devicePixelRatio`,** or scaling the context every frame instead of once — the second compounds and renders at wildly wrong sizes.
- **Frame-based physics** (`x += 5`), which runs at different speeds on 60Hz and 144Hz displays, plus unclamped `dt` that teleports objects after a tab switch.
- **Leaking the render loop** — no `cancelAnimationFrame` on unmount, so multiple loops run and CPU sits at 100%.
- **`save()`/`restore()` and `beginPath()` mismatches,** so `fillStyle`, `globalAlpha` or `lineWidth` leak between frames and paths connect to previous subpaths; bugs that appear only in certain draw orders are almost always this.
- **Converting click coordinates without the transform,** so hit-testing breaks under CSS scaling and on retina; subtract `getBoundingClientRect()` and account for the DPR scale.
- **`toDataURL` per frame,** which is synchronous, allocates megabytes and drops frames; use `toBlob` and only on export.
- **No accessibility fallback,** leaving a drawing app or chart that is completely unusable with a screen reader — the most common and most defensible-seeming failure.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Canvas vs SVG vs DOM:** SVG/DOM gives a retained scene graph with free hit-testing, CSS, events and accessibility and degrades gracefully at low node counts; Canvas gives a fast draw loop and thousands of moving objects but every hit test, focus ring, tooltip and screen-reader affordance is code you write. Decide per scene, not per project.
- **`getImageData` snapshots vs a model-plus-redraw undo stack:** snapshots are trivially implemented and cost O(width × height × 4) bytes each — fine for a small scribble pad at 20–30 steps, ruinous for a 4K canvas; storing strokes as data is cheap and hit-testable but needs a full redraw per undo and an occasional compaction pass.
- **Main-thread 2D context vs `OffscreenCanvas` in a worker vs WebGL:** a worker keeps heavy rendering off the main thread, which is what INP measures, at the cost of transferring frames or transferring canvas control; WebGL/WebGPU is the only sane answer past tens of thousands of primitives, and it buys you shaders, buffers and a much larger surface to maintain.
- **Reallocating the backing store on every resize vs throttling:** resizing clears the bitmap, so doing it per `resize` event visibly hitches while dragging a window; throttle to `requestAnimationFrame` or debounce with `ResizeObserver`, and don't allocate more buffer than `maxTextureSize` allows.
- **`Path2D` and cached static layers:** offscreen canvases and `Path2D` objects trade memory for CPU, which is the right call for a chart that redraws on every hover.

**Common wrong answers:** "Canvas is always hardware accelerated, so it's faster"; "canvas content is accessible if you add `role="img"` and an `alt` attribute (there is no `alt` on canvas)"; "use `setInterval(fn, 16)` for a game loop"; "keep a `toDataURL` snapshot for every frame of undo"; "use `getImageData` for hit-testing".

**Probe:** "Your chart looks sharp at DPR 1 and blurry the moment the user drags the window to a second monitor. What happened, and what's the fix?" (Expect: the backing store was sized once at startup; `devicePixelRatio` changed when the display changed, so the buffer is now stretched. Re-read DPR in a `resize`/`matchMedia` handler, resize the bitmap, reset and re-apply the transform, and redraw — with the redraw debounced to one per frame.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Immediate mode understood,** so the candidate explains that animation means clear-and-redraw and that they, not the browser, hold the scene state.
- **HiDPI correctness** — the most common real bug, easy to spot in a review, and a strong discriminator between people who have shipped canvas and people who have only read about it.
- **Frame-rate independence:** delta time from the rAF timestamp, clamped after a tab switch, rather than per-frame constants.
- **Awareness of the trade they made:** no DOM, no accessibility, no free hit-testing — and a concrete plan for each, not silence.

**Model answer, Q2:** "The canvas has two independent sizes. The `width`/`height` attributes define the bitmap buffer; CSS `width`/`height` define how many CSS pixels that buffer is displayed across. On a DPR-2 display those differ, so leaving the attributes at the layout size gives you one buffer pixel per CSS pixel stretched over two device pixels — that's the blur. The fix is to size the backing store to `round(cssWidth * devicePixelRatio)` and `round(cssHeight * devicePixelRatio)`, then call `ctx.scale(dpr, dpr)` once and draw in CSS pixels, so all your coordinates stay device-independent. Two things people miss: you have to reset the transform (`setTransform(1,0,0,1,0,0)`) before re-applying the scale, or it compounds; and DPR changes when a window moves between monitors or the user zooms, so re-read it in a `resize` handler and on `matchMedia(\`(resolution: ${dpr}dppx)\`)` firing."
**Mini task:** Build a bouncing-balls simulation with click-to-add and time-based motion at 60 and 144 Hz, then a scribble pad with undo and export to PNG (`canvas.toBlob`).


**Self-check:** Point at your last canvas component and say, out loud, how a user clicks a drawn shape, how a screen reader finds it, and how it survives both a Retina display and a 120Hz monitor.

**Resources:** MDN `<canvas>` and the "Canvas tutorial"; MDN `CanvasRenderingContext2D` (`save`/`restore`, `setTransform`, `Path2D`); MDN `OffscreenCanvas`, `HTMLCanvasElement.toBlob` and `window.devicePixelRatio`; Chrome for Developers' rendering-performance guidance on canvas, draw-call batching and avoiding state changes.
---

## 3.1.11 Web Components and Shadow DOM
🔴 Advanced · **Good-to-know** (increasingly asked for design-system roles)

**Definition:** A set of browser-native standards for creating reusable, encapsulated custom elements that work in any framework.

**Three pillars**
1. **Custom Elements:** `customElements.define('user-card', UserCard)`. The name must contain a hyphen.
2. **Shadow DOM:** an encapsulated DOM subtree with scoped styles, via `attachShadow({mode:'open'})`.
3. **HTML templates and slots:** `<template>` (inert markup to clone) and `<slot>` (placeholders for light-DOM children).

**Lifecycle callbacks**

| Callback | Fires when |
|---|---|
| `constructor` | Element created (don't read attributes or children here) |
| `connectedCallback` | Added to the DOM (set up listeners, fetch, render) |
| `disconnectedCallback` | Removed (clean up listeners, timers, observers) |
| `attributeChangedCallback(name, old, new)` | An attribute in `static observedAttributes` changes |
| `adoptedCallback` | Moved to another document |

**Encapsulation rules**
- Styles inside the shadow root don't leak out, and page styles don't leak in.
- **Inherited properties and CSS custom properties pierce** the boundary (the theming mechanism).
- Expose styling hooks with `::part(name)` and CSS variables. Style from inside with `:host`, `:host(.x)`, and `::slotted(span)`.
- **Events:** are retargeted at the boundary. Dispatch custom events with `{bubbles: true, composed: true}` to cross out of the shadow root.
- `mode: 'open'` allows `el.shadowRoot` access. `closed` hides it (rarely worthwhile).
- **Form participation** needs `static formAssociated = true` plus `ElementInternals`.
- **SSR:** historically hard, and **Declarative Shadow DOM** (`<template shadowrootmode="open">`) now allows server-rendered shadow roots. Libraries like **Lit** add reactive templates.
- **Trade-offs vs React components:** framework-agnostic, long-lived, and native, but no built-in reactivity or state primitives, a verbose API without Lit, accessibility across shadow boundaries needing care (`aria-labelledby` can't reference across roots), and SSR and React interop quirks (React 19 improved custom-element props and events).
- **Used by:** design systems intended for multiple frameworks (Shoelace/Web Awesome, Salesforce Lightning, GitHub elements) and micro-frontend widgets.

```js
class UserCard extends HTMLElement {
  static observedAttributes = ['name'];

  constructor() {
    super();
    this.attachShadow({ mode: 'open' });
    this.shadowRoot.innerHTML = `
      <style>
        :host { display: block; border: 1px solid var(--card-border, #ddd); padding: 1rem; }
        h3 { margin: 0; }
      </style>
      <slot name="avatar"></slot>
      <h3 part="title"></h3>
      <slot></slot>`;
    this.title = this.shadowRoot.querySelector('h3');
  }

  connectedCallback() { this.#render(); }
  attributeChangedCallback() { this.#render(); }

  #render() { this.title.textContent = this.getAttribute('name') ?? 'Anonymous'; } // textContent: no XSS
}
customElements.define('user-card', UserCard);
```
```html
<user-card name="Ada">
  <img slot="avatar" src="ada.png" alt="">
  <p>Mathematician</p>
</user-card>
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.2 (semantics and the first rule of ARIA, which shadow DOM does not exempt you from), 3.1.5 (the accessibility contract you must still satisfy across the boundary), 3.1.10 (where the platform has no encapsulation at all — the inverse case).

**Why interviewers ask:** Because it is the honest test of whether you understand encapsulation: the same reasoning that makes you reach for closures and module boundaries in JavaScript applies to DOM and CSS. It is disproportionately asked for design-system and platform roles, where shipping one component that works in four frameworks is the actual job, and it separates people who have read the docs from people who have shipped a versioned, themeable, SSR-safe element.
**Questions**
1. What are the three main technologies behind Web Components?
2. What does Shadow DOM encapsulate? What still leaks in? *(Inherited CSS properties and custom properties.)*
3. How do you style a web component from outside? *(CSS variables, `::part`)*
4. What is a slot?
5. Why must custom events set `composed: true`?
6. Web Components vs React components: when would you choose Web Components? 
7. What's the lifecycle, and where would you register listeners vs clean them up?
8. What are issues with Shadow DOM and accessibility, forms, or SSR?


**Mistakes:**
- **Reading attributes or light-DOM children in the constructor:** for an element parsed after `define`, the constructor runs before its attributes and children exist, so `getAttribute('name')` is `null` and any state you read is fiction. Do the reads and setup in `connectedCallback`, and render once there and again from `attributeChangedCallback`.
- **Setting a JS property and waiting for `attributeChangedCallback`:** that callback only fires for attributes listed in `static observedAttributes`. Properties and attributes are two separate channels, which is exactly why anything structured (`Date`, a range, a `Set`) can only be a property — needing a setter that upgrades the attribute and an `attributeChangedCallback` that downgrades it.
- **Dispatching custom events without `composed: true`:** the event is destroyed at the shadow boundary and never reaches the host or the document, so the consumer's `el.addEventListener('change', …)` silently never fires. You need `composed: true` *and* `bubbles: true`, and you listen on the host.
- **Pointing `aria-labelledby`, `aria-describedby` or `getElementById` at an id inside the shadow root:** ids in a shadow tree live in a separate ID space, so the reference resolves to nothing and the element is announced with no name at all. Name the host, or use `aria-label` / `ElementInternals.ariaLabel` on the internals.
- **Registering `window`/`document` listeners, observers or timers in `connectedCallback` with no teardown in `disconnectedCallback`:** every reparent leaks a handler and every re-entry double-registers one, so the element renders twice and eventually takes the page down with it. For the same reason, building shadow markup with `innerHTML` and interpolated values is XSS inside your own root — `textContent` is deliberate.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Web components vs framework components:** native gives framework independence, long-lived versioning, zero runtime and real platform APIs (`ElementInternals`, slots, parts, `adoptedCallback`); it gives up built-in reactivity, a declarative template story without Lit, ergonomic SSR and mostly-seamless framework interop. Choose by distribution: multi-framework consumers or embeddable widgets justify it, single-app internals don't.
- **Shadow DOM vs a light DOM plus a prefixed class/BEM convention:** shadow gives genuine style isolation and encapsulation for free; light DOM keeps global selectors, theming, devtools inspection and CSS-in-JS/utility pipelines working, at the cost of a naming discipline you have to enforce. Many mature design systems ship shadow with a "no shadow" flag for exactly this reason.
- **`mode: 'open'` vs `mode: 'closed'`:** open exposes `el.shadowRoot`, so consumers and devtools can inspect and even style into the tree — the pragmatic default; closed hides it and buys essentially nothing security-wise while permanently breaking debugging and any test that pokes at internals.
- **`<slot>` (composition) vs `::part` (configuration):** slots let a consumer inject arbitrary content into fixed places and keep owning that content's DOM; `::part` and custom properties let you restyle the component's own internals without owning its structure. Use both, and don't use `::part` as a substitute for a slot.
- **Declarative Shadow DOM vs client-side `attachShadow`:** DSD keeps the shadow root in the server response so there's no hydration mismatch and no flash of unstyled content; client-side attachment is simpler and fine in CSR-only apps, and it is the wrong choice the moment you SSR.
- **Lit vs hand-written elements:** Lit gives reactive properties, template caching and declarative SSR at the cost of a runtime dependency and a build step; raw custom elements keep the bundle at zero bytes and force you to write diffing, batching and lifecycle plumbing yourself — fine for one component, painful for a system.

**Common wrong answers:** "Shadow DOM is full encapsulation — page styles can never reach in" (inherited properties and CSS custom properties still do); "custom events bubble out of shadow DOM automatically" (they don't without `composed: true`); "`mode: 'closed'` is more secure"; "React can use custom elements the same way it uses React components"; "the constructor is where you read attributes".

**Probe:** "Your design system needs a `<date-range-picker>` consumed by four frameworks. What do you expose as attributes, what as properties, what as events, and what do you do about forms and SSR?" (Expect: attributes for primitive, serialisable, HTML-authored configuration and `observedAttributes` to react to changes; properties for anything structured (Date objects, ranges) that you upgrade from attributes in the setter; a single composed, bubbling, `retargeting`-clean `change` event with a `detail` payload; `formAssociated` + `ElementInternals` for value and validity so it submits with a normal form; and Declarative Shadow DOM or a build step for SSR so the shadow root exists before hydration.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Whether the encapsulation reasoning is precise:** what shadow DOM scopes (CSS and DOM lookup) and what still crosses — inherited properties, custom properties, font and form-control styling from the UA sheet.
- **That event retargeting happens at the boundary,** and therefore that `composed: true` and `bubbles: true` are both needed to escape, and that listening on the host is how you expose an API.
- **Lifecycle discipline:** doing work in `connectedCallback`, cleaning up in `disconnectedCallback`, and never touching attributes or children in the constructor (which runs before the element is upgraded and populated).
- **Knowledge of the real ergonomics:** `mode: 'closed'` costs you debuggability, slots versus `::part`, `observedAttributes` for properties versus attributes, and the fact that properties are not attributes.
- **Restraint about the platform's gaps:** form association, SSR, accessibility labelling across boundaries and framework interop are the actual reasons this is still "good to know".

**Model answer, Q6:** "I reach for a web component when the consumer's framework is unknown or when the component must outlive any particular one: a design system published to product teams on React, Vue, Angular and server-rendered HTML, a widget embedded by a third party, or a micro-frontend boundary where two teams on different stacks have to agree on one contract. The value is real: framework-agnostic by construction, versioned like a package, cacheable, no runtime, and `ElementInternals` plus CSS custom properties give it real styling and form-participation APIs that framework components lack. I do not choose it for a single app's internals: without Lit, you are hand-rolling reactivity, lifecycle, SSR and accessibility, and the ergonomics lose to a component library. The decision rule is distribution, not preference — if it will only ever be consumed by one React app, it is a React component."
**Mini task:** Build `<star-rating>` as a form-associated custom element that emits a `change` event, is keyboard-accessible, and themes via CSS variables. Use it from plain HTML and from a React page.


**Self-check:** Describe your last custom element's public API precisely — which attributes it observes, which properties it exposes, which events it emits and whether they are `composed`, and how it participates in a form.

**Resources:** MDN "Web Components" plus the Custom Elements, Shadow DOM, `<template>` and Popover-adjacent reference pages, and `ElementInternals`; the WHATWG/W3C Custom Elements v1 and Shadow DOM v1 specifications; the W3C WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices on labelling and the Declarative Shadow DOM explainer on web.dev; the Lit documentation and `webcomponents.org`'s resources; and Chrome's "Custom Elements v1" / styling `::part` documentation for the theming contract.
---

## 3.1 HTML Interview Q&A (mixed)

| Question | Strong answer outline |
|---|---|
| Why does semantic HTML matter beyond SEO? | Accessibility, built-in behavior, maintainability, reader modes, and testing via roles |
| What happens if you omit `alt`? | Screen readers may read the filename. Use `alt=""` for decorative, and descriptive text for informative |
| `localStorage` vs cookies? | Storage details in Phase 3 Part 3 (browser internals). For HTML: cookies are sent with every request |
| What is `data-*`? | Custom attributes readable via `element.dataset` (kebab → camelCase) |
| What's an HTML entity and why escape? | `&lt;`, `&amp;`: prevents markup injection (XSS) |
| `<link>` vs `@import`? | `@import` serializes downloads and blocks. Prefer `<link>` or bundling |
| How do you embed third-party content safely? | `<iframe sandbox>`, `allow`, `referrerpolicy`, `loading="lazy"`, CSP `frame-ancestors` on the embedded side |
| `target="_blank"` risk? | Reverse tabnabbing, historically. Modern browsers imply `noopener`, but be explicit with `rel="noopener noreferrer"` |
| `<button>` vs `<input type="button">`? | `<button>` can contain markup and is more flexible |
| What's the `hidden` attribute? | Equivalent to `display:none` by default (author CSS can override it) |

---

# 3.2 CSS STYLING

## 3.2.1 The Cascade, Specificity, and Inheritance
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The algorithm that decides which declaration wins when several rules target the same property.

**Why interviewers ask:** Almost every "why isn't my style applying?" bug is cascade-related. Candidates who understand it debug fast.

**Cascade order (high to low priority)**
1. **Origin and importance:** transitions → `!important` user-agent → `!important` user → `!important` author → animations → normal author → normal user → normal user-agent
2. **Cascade layers** (`@layer`): for normal declarations, later layers win, and unlayered styles beat layered ones. The order *reverses* for `!important`.
3. **Specificity**
4. **Order of appearance:** the last rule wins ties

**Specificity** (compare as (IDs, classes/attributes/pseudo-classes, elements/pseudo-elements), left to right, not as a base-10 number):

| Selector | Specificity |
|---|---|
| `*`, combinators (`>`, `+`, `~`) | 0,0,0 |
| `p`, `::before` | 0,0,1 |
| `.card`, `[type=text]`, `:hover` | 0,1,0 |
| `#nav` | 1,0,0 |
| `nav ul li a.active` | 0,1,4 |
| inline `style=""` | beats all selectors (just not `!important`) |
| `:is(a, .b)` | the highest specificity among its arguments |
| `:where(...)` | **always 0**, great for resets and defaults |
| `:not(.x)` | the specificity of its argument |

- **Inheritance:** some properties inherit by default (`color`, `font-*`, `line-height`, `visibility`, `cursor`), and many don't (`margin`, `padding`, `border`, `background`, `width`). Control it with `inherit`, `initial`, `unset`, `revert`.
- **Strategies:** keep specificity low and flat (single classes), avoid IDs in CSS, avoid `!important` (except utilities and overriding third-party), use `@layer` to order resets → base → components → utilities, and use `:where()` for default styles.
- **Debugging:** DevTools → Elements → Styles panel (struck-through rules lost), and the Computed tab shows the winner and traces it.

```css
@layer reset, base, components, utilities;
@layer components { .btn { padding: .5rem 1rem; } }
@layer utilities  { .p-0 { padding: 0; } }   /* wins even with lower specificity, as it's a later layer */
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.1, 3.1.2.
**Questions**
1. How does CSS decide between conflicting rules?
2. Specificity of `#a .b p` vs `.x .y .z .w`? *(1,1,1 beats 0,4,0, since an ID beats any number of classes.)*
3. What does `!important` do, and why avoid it?
4. Which properties inherit?
5. What are `@layer` and `:where()` for?
6. `initial` vs `inherit` vs `unset` vs `revert`?
7. Why might your style not apply? *(Overridden by a more specific rule, typo, wrong selector, not a valid value, an inherited vs non-inherited property confusion, the rule loads earlier, a layer order issue, a shadow DOM boundary.)*

**Model answer, Q1:** "First by origin and importance, so `!important` flips priorities. Then cascade layers, then specificity, comparing IDs, then classes/attributes/pseudo-classes, then elements. If still tied, the later declaration wins. I keep selectors flat and low-specificity and use layers to make ordering explicit."


**Mistakes:**
- Adding another `!important` to win the fight instead of finding which rule actually won in the Styles panel.
- Assuming the last rule in the file wins, when a later normal rule loses to an earlier `!important` one.
- Forgetting that `!important` **reverses** the layer order, so an important declaration in an earlier layer beats one in a later layer.
- Counting specificity arithmetically instead of comparing IDs, then classes, then elements.
- Debugging by reading the first matching rule in the Elements panel rather than the Computed value and the source it names.

**Trade-offs:**
- **ID selectors vs flat single classes:** an ID is unbeatable at matching, which is precisely why nobody can override it later; one class per component is trivially overridable and composes into a layered system.
- **`!important` vs `@layer`:** `!important` settles an argument instantly but turns the stylesheet into a graph of fights you cannot reason about; layers order styles at zero specificity cost, and `!important` in an *earlier* layer still beats a later one.
- **`:is()` vs `:where()`:** `:is()` takes the highest specificity among its arguments, which suits reusable component selectors but poisons every override; `:where()` is always 0, which is the right tool for resets and default styles.
- **Inherited properties vs explicit per-component tokens:** inheriting `color` is less markup and automatically consistent, but it couples a component to its context; explicit tokens keep components portable at the cost of repetition.

**Common wrong answers:** "Specificity is one number, so `#a` is 100 and `.b.c.d.e.f` is 60" — wrong, there is no carry between columns, so `#a .b p` (1,1,1) beats `.x .y .z .w` (0,4,0). Or "`!important` wins because it's more specific" — importance is compared *before* specificity.

**Probe:** "Your `.btn--primary` override stopped working the week the team added `@layer` — why, and what do you change?" (Expect: unlayered author declarations rank above all layered ones, so the old global rule now wins; move the override into a later-declared layer or lift it out of the layer system, and declare layer order up front with `@layer a, b;`.)

**What they evaluate:**
- Whether they recite the cascade order with **origin and importance before specificity**, and know `!important` inverts the origin order rather than simply "winning".
- Specificity handled as a **tuple compared column by column**, not a base-10 number.
- Whether they know **layers beat specificity**, and that unlayered normal author declarations outrank every layer.
- That they separate **inheritance** (per-property, from the parent's computed value) from the **cascade** (per-element, among matching declarations).
**Mini task:** Create five conflicting rules for one element and predict the winner before checking DevTools. Then reorganize a messy stylesheet with `@layer` and remove all `!important`.


**Self-check:** Can you state the full cascade order, explain why `#a .b p` beats `.x .y .z .w`, and say what changes when both rules sit in `@layer`s?

**Resources:** MDN "The cascade" and "Specificity"; the CSS Cascading and Inheritance Level 5 spec (`drafts.csswg.org/css-cascade-5/`) for the layer and `revert` rules; web.dev's "Learn CSS: the cascade"; Chrome's `@layer` explainer for the layer-inversion detail.
---

## 3.2.2 Box Model, Display, Margin Collapsing, BFC, Units
🟢 → 🟡 · **Must-know**

**The box model:** every element is a box: **content → padding → border → margin**.
```
┌────────────── margin ──────────────┐
│ ┌──────────── border ────────────┐ │
│ │ ┌────────── padding ─────────┐ │ │
│ │ │          content           │ │ │
```
- **`box-sizing: content-box` (default):** `width` is *content only*, so the total = width + padding + border. **`border-box`:** `width` includes padding and border, which is far more predictable.
```css
*, *::before, *::after { box-sizing: border-box; }
```
- **Margin collapsing:** adjacent *vertical* margins of block elements combine into the larger one (not the sum). It happens between siblings, between a parent and its first or last child (if no border, padding, or clearfix separates them), and for empty blocks. It does **not** happen horizontally, in flex or grid containers, with floats, absolutely positioned elements, or in a new BFC.
- **Block formatting context (BFC):** an isolated layout region. Created by `overflow` other than visible, `display: flow-root` (the clean way), floats, absolute/fixed positioning, flex/grid items, and `contain: layout`. A BFC contains its floats (the clearfix replacement) and prevents margin collapse with children.
- **`display` values:** `block`, `inline`, `inline-block`, `flex`, `grid`, `none`, `contents`, `flow-root`, `table`.
- **Hiding techniques**

| Technique | Layout space | Accessibility tree | Events |
|---|---|---|---|
| `display: none` | Removed | Removed | No |
| `visibility: hidden` | Kept | Removed | No |
| `opacity: 0` | Kept | **Kept** | **Yes** (still clickable) |
| `hidden` attribute | Removed (UA `display:none`) | Removed | No |
| `.sr-only` | ~None | Kept | Focusable |

- **Units**

| Unit | Relative to | Use |
|---|---|---|
| `px` | CSS pixel | Borders, shadows, hairlines |
| `rem` | Root font-size (user preference) | Font sizes, spacing (respects user zoom settings) |
| `em` | The element's own font size (and for `font-size`, the parent's) | Component-relative padding (compounds when nested) |
| `%` | Parent's relevant dimension | Fluid widths |
| `vw`/`vh` | Viewport width/height | Full-bleed sections |
| `dvh`/`svh`/`lvh` | Dynamic/small/large viewport height | Mobile browsers with a collapsing URL bar (`100vh` overflows) |
| `ch` | Width of "0" | Readable line length (`max-width: 65ch`) |
| `fr` | Fraction of free space (grid) | Grid tracks |
| `lh`, `cqw` | Line height, container width | Modern spacing, container-based sizing |

- **Width/height details:** `width: auto` for block = fill container. `min-width`/`max-width` override. Percent heights need a parent with an explicit height. `aspect-ratio: 16/9` keeps proportions. `overflow: auto|hidden|scroll|clip`.
- **Inline elements** ignore vertical `margin` and `width`/`height`. Images are inline by default, causing a small baseline gap beneath them (`display:block` or `vertical-align: middle` fixes it).
- **`outline` vs `border`:** the outline doesn't take layout space and can overlap.
- **Text overflow**
```css
.truncate { white-space: nowrap; overflow: hidden; text-overflow: ellipsis; }
.clamp-3 { display: -webkit-box; -webkit-line-clamp: 3; -webkit-box-orient: vertical; overflow: hidden; }
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.1.

**Why interviewers ask:** Almost no layout bug is "hard CSS" — it is a box-model misunderstanding wearing a costume. What this section separates is people who debug a broken page from people who start changing numbers and hoping.

**Definition:** The rules governing the four nested boxes every element generates (content, padding, border, margin), which `display` types those boxes, how vertical margins interact, what a block formatting context isolates, and what each length unit resolves against.
**Questions**
1. Explain the box model. `content-box` vs `border-box`?
2. What is margin collapsing? When doesn't it occur?
3. What's a BFC and what creates one? What problem does it solve?
4. `rem` vs `em` vs `px`? Why prefer `rem` for fonts? *(Respects the user's browser font setting for accessibility.)*
5. `display: none` vs `visibility: hidden` vs `opacity: 0`?
6. Why does `100vh` break on mobile? Fix? *(`dvh`/`svh`)*
7. Inline vs block vs inline-block?
8. A parent containing only floated children collapses to 0 height. Why and how do you fix it? *(`display: flow-root`, clearfix, or just use flex.)*
9. How do you truncate text with an ellipsis (single and multi-line)?
10. How are `margin: 0 auto` and `auto` margins computed? *(Distribute the free horizontal space equally. They only center blocks with a defined width and, in flex, absorb free space.)*


**Mistakes:**
- Fixing parent/child collapse with padding or `overflow: hidden`, which works but silently changes the layout and reintroduces the problem the moment that padding becomes conditional.
- Using `%` heights under a parent with `height: auto` and concluding percentages "don't work".
- Sizing images, iframes and embeds without `width`/`height` or `aspect-ratio`, so they arrive at zero height and shift the page (3.6.4).
- Setting `width: 100%` plus horizontal padding without `box-sizing: border-box`, then chasing the resulting overflow with `overflow: hidden`.
- Forgetting `min-height: 0` on a flex item that is supposed to scroll with `overflow: auto`, so the whole page scrolls instead.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`content-box` vs `border-box`:** `border-box` makes declared widths mean what the design says and stops padding from breaking a grid; `content-box` is the spec default and is what every percentage calculation in older code assumes, so mixing the two in one project is the actual hazard.
- **`overflow: hidden` vs `display: flow-root`:** both establish a BFC, but `hidden` also clips content and makes the box a scroll container, which changes how `position: sticky` descendants behave; `flow-root` isolates layout and nothing else.
- **`rem` vs `em`:** `rem` is predictable and honours the user's root font size, but ignores a component's own type scale; `em` scales with the element and compounds when nested, so nested `em` padding grows quietly at every level.
- **`dvh` vs `svh` vs `100vh`:** `dvh` tracks the collapsing URL bar so full-height sections look right but the layout shifts as the bar hides; `svh` is stable but leaves a gap when the bar is collapsed; `100vh` is the largest viewport height, which is exactly why it overflows behind mobile browser chrome.
- **Fluid `clamp()` vs fixed steps:** fluid sizing removes breakpoints and stays zoom-accessible, but it is harder to hold to an exact design spec.

**Common wrong answers:** Treating collapsed margins as a sum, so a 20px heading margin above a 30px paragraph margin produces a 50px gap. Or naming `overflow: hidden` as the cleanest BFC fix with no mention that it clips and creates a scroll container.

**Probe:** "A flex child with `flex: 1 1 0` and a long unbroken URL overflows the row and the ellipsis never appears, even though the parent has `overflow: hidden`. Why?" (Expect: flex items have an automatic minimum size (`min-width: auto`) equal to their min-content size, so the item refuses to shrink below the URL and `overflow: hidden` merely clips it; fix with `min-width: 0` on the item, then `overflow: hidden; text-overflow: ellipsis; white-space: nowrap` on the element holding the text.)

**What they evaluate:**
- Arithmetic on the box: `width` semantics in both box-sizing modes, and that margins sit outside the box either way.
- Collapsing described as **max, not sum**, with the preconditions named: no separating border, padding, inline content, clearance or `min-height`, and no new formatting context.
- Whether they reach for `display: flow-root` over the `overflow: hidden` hack, and say why.
- Unit literacy: `rem` tracking the user's root font size, `em` compounding when nested, `dvh` on mobile, and why pure `vw` type fails zoom.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Every element generates content, padding, border and margin boxes. In the default `content-box`, `width` sets the content box only, so a 200px element with 20px padding and a 2px border occupies 244px — which is exactly how padding quietly breaks a percentage grid. `border-box` makes `width` the outer edge, so it covers padding and border but never margins. I set `*, *::before, *::after { box-sizing: border-box }` in the reset and then think in border-box everywhere."
**Mini task:** Reproduce a margin collapse (parent and child), fix it three ways (padding, `flow-root`, flex parent), and write what each does to layout.


**Self-check:** Given `width: 200px; padding: 20px; border: 2px; margin: 10px; box-sizing: border-box`, what horizontal space does it occupy — and what under `content-box`?

**Resources:** MDN "The box model", "Margin collapsing", "Block formatting context" and "CSS values and units"; CSS 2.1 §8.3.1 for the collapsing rules and §9.7 for BFC; the `dvh`/`svh`/`lvh` viewport-unit docs.
---

## 3.2.3 Positioning and Stacking Context
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 · **Must-know**

**`position` values**

| Value | Behavior |
|---|---|
| `static` (default) | Normal flow, and `top/left/z-index` ignored |
| `relative` | Normal flow, offset visually, and becomes the **containing block** for absolute children |
| `absolute` | Removed from flow, positioned relative to the nearest **positioned ancestor** (else the initial containing block) |
| `fixed` | Relative to the viewport (unless an ancestor has `transform`/`filter`/`perspective`, which makes *that* the containing block) |
| `sticky` | Hybrid: acts relative until a scroll threshold, then sticks within its **scroll container and parent** |

- **Sticky gotchas:** needs a threshold (`top: 0`). It fails if any ancestor has `overflow: hidden|auto|scroll` (that ancestor becomes the scroll container). The sticky element can't leave its parent's bounds. Also check for `height` constraints on the parent.
- **Stacking context:** an isolated 3D layering scope. `z-index` only compares siblings *within the same stacking context*. A child with `z-index: 9999` can't escape a parent whose context sits below another.
  - **Created by:** the root element; `position` absolute/relative with `z-index` ≠ auto; fixed/sticky; `opacity < 1`; `transform`, `filter`, `perspective`, `clip-path`, `mask`; `isolation: isolate`; `will-change` on those properties; flex/grid children with `z-index` ≠ auto; `contain: layout|paint`; and top-layer elements.
  - **Paint order inside a context (low → high):** the context's background → negative z-index children → in-flow blocks → floats → inline content → `z-index: 0/auto` positioned → positive z-index.
- **Practical fix for z-index wars:** create intentional contexts with `isolation: isolate` on components, use a small z-index scale via CSS variables (`--z-dropdown: 100; --z-modal: 1000`), and render modals and tooltips in the **top layer** (`<dialog>`, popover) or a React **portal** attached to `body`.
- **Floats (legacy):** originally for text wrap. Layout is done with flex/grid now. `clear` and `flow-root` contain them.
- **`inset: 0`** is shorthand for `top/right/bottom/left: 0`, and `position: absolute; inset: 0` fills the positioned parent.

```css
.card { position: relative; }
.card .badge { position: absolute; top: .5rem; right: .5rem; }      /* corner badge */
.overlay { position: fixed; inset: 0; background: rgb(0 0 0 / .5); } /* full-screen scrim */
.header { position: sticky; top: 0; z-index: var(--z-header); }
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.1, 3.2.2.

**Why interviewers ask:** "Why is my dropdown behind the header?" and "why is my fixed header scrolling with the page?" are the two most common CSS escalations in real teams, and both are answered by naming an *ancestor*, not a number. The interviewer is checking whether you can reason about which ancestor is the containing block and which one created the stacking context.

**Definition:** The rules that place a box relative to its containing block (`static`, `relative`, `absolute`, `fixed`, `sticky`) and the paint-order rules that decide which of several overlapping boxes ends up visible.
**Questions**
1. `relative` vs `absolute` vs `fixed` vs `sticky`?
2. My `position: sticky` isn't sticking. Why? *(ancestor overflow, no `top`, parent too short)*
3. Why doesn't `z-index: 9999` put my dropdown above another element? *(a lower stacking context, no positioning, or an ancestor with transform/opacity)*
4. What creates a stacking context?
5. How do you ensure a modal always overlays everything? *(top layer via `<dialog>`, or a portal to `body` at the end of the DOM)*
6. Why does `position: fixed` sometimes behave like absolute? *(an ancestor with `transform`, `filter`, or `perspective`)*
7. How do you make an absolutely positioned child fill its parent? *(parent `relative`, child `inset: 0`)*

**Model answer, Q3:** "`z-index` only orders elements within the same stacking context. If my dropdown lives inside a parent that creates its own context (e.g., it has `transform` or `opacity < 1`) and that parent is below the other element's context, no z-index value inside can escape. I'd inspect the ancestors for context-creating properties, restructure the DOM, or render the dropdown in a portal or the top layer."


**Mistakes:**
- Setting `z-index` on a `static` element, then concluding the property is broken.
- Bumping `z-index` on the child while the parent itself sits in a lower stacking context — nothing inside can escape.
- Reaching for a bigger number instead of finding the ancestor that created the context; `z-index: 99999` inside a `transform`ed card still loses to an untransformed sibling.
- Assuming `overflow: hidden` is harmless for sticky descendants: it becomes the scrollport, so the sticky element stops at that boundary.
- Forgetting `position: relative` on the parent, so the absolute child anchors to the initial containing block and lands somewhere unrelated.
- Deleting a `transform` that was there for a reason while chasing a `fixed` bug, and silently re-breaking a parent that contained its descendants.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`position: absolute` vs `fixed`:** absolute scrolls with its containing block and is safe inside transformed ancestors; fixed is viewport-anchored but hijacked by any transformed or filtered ancestor — good for page-level overlays, risky for anything inside a modal or an animated card.
- **A component owning a `z-index` vs `isolation: isolate`:** a component that sets its own `z-index` can be repositioned by callers and can never be pushed below a page-level context; `isolation: isolate` caps it so its internals stop fighting the page, at the cost of losing the ability to sit above it.
- **An in-place dropdown vs a portal or top layer:** in-place keeps focus, events and DOM order local and is simpler; a portal (or `<dialog>`/`popover`) escapes every `overflow` and `z-index` constraint at the price of rendering outside the component's subtree.
- **`position: sticky` vs a scroll listener:** sticky is compositor-friendly, needs no scroll handler and survives virtualised lists poorly; JS gives dynamic thresholds and pin/unpin states at the cost of a scroll callback that must be throttled with `requestAnimationFrame`.

**Common wrong answers:** "Raise the `z-index` to 99999." Or "`position: sticky` is broken in this browser", when the real cause is that a `transform` on an ancestor also creates a containing block for sticky, or that the element's own parent has no height to stick within.

**Probe:** "A `position: fixed` header starts scrolling with the content the moment a modal opens — why?" (Expect: something in the open path adds `transform`, `filter`, `perspective`, `contain` or `will-change` to an ancestor, and any of those makes that ancestor the containing block for fixed descendants instead of the viewport.)

**What they evaluate:**
- The containing block named precisely: the **padding box** of the nearest positioned ancestor for `absolute`, and the specific list of properties (`transform`, `perspective`, `filter`, `backdrop-filter`, `contain: paint|size|strict`, `will-change`) that hijack `fixed`.
- That `z-index` only orders siblings inside the same stacking context, and does nothing at all on a `position: static` element.
- Whether the z-index fix is **structural** — `isolation: isolate`, a portal to `body`, or the top layer via `<dialog>`/popover — rather than a larger integer.
- That they can say why sticky fails: no inset threshold, an ancestor whose `overflow` is not `visible`/`clip` becoming the scrollport, or a containing block too short to travel.
**Mini task:** Build a sticky header, a card with a corner badge, a dropdown that gets clipped by an `overflow: hidden` parent (then fix via portal or `position: fixed`), and a z-index conflict caused by a `transform` ancestor. Diagnose each in DevTools.


**Self-check:** Given an element with `z-index: 5` inside an ancestor with `opacity: 0.99`, and a sibling of that ancestor with `z-index: 100` — which paints on top, and why?

**Resources:** MDN `position`, `position: sticky` and `z-index`; the CSS Positioned Layout Level 3 spec for the containing-block and `z-index` rules; the "Stacking context" and "Painting order" figures in that spec; every-layout.dev's article on stacking contexts.
---

## 3.2.4 Flexbox
🟢 → 🟡 · **Must-know**

**Definition:** A one-dimensional layout model (a row *or* a column) for distributing space and aligning items.

**Terminology:** the container has a **main axis** (set by `flex-direction`) and a **cross axis** (perpendicular).

| Container property | Controls |
|---|---|
| `display: flex` | Enables flex |
| `flex-direction` | `row` / `column` (+ `-reverse`) |
| `flex-wrap` | `nowrap` (default) / `wrap` |
| `justify-content` | Distribution along the **main** axis (`flex-start`, `center`, `space-between`, `space-around`, `space-evenly`) |
| `align-items` | Alignment of items along the **cross** axis (`stretch` default, `center`, `flex-start`, `baseline`) |
| `align-content` | Distribution of *lines* on the cross axis (only with wrapping, multiple lines) |
| `gap` | Spacing between items (prefer over margins) |

| Item property | Controls |
|---|---|
| `flex-grow` | Share of leftover space (default 0) |
| `flex-shrink` | Share of overflow to give up (default 1) |
| `flex-basis` | Starting size before grow/shrink (default `auto`, which uses `width`/content) |
| `flex` shorthand | `flex: 1` = `1 1 0%`, `flex: auto` = `1 1 auto`, `flex: none` = `0 0 auto` |
| `align-self` | Per-item cross-axis override |
| `order` | Visual order (**does not change tab or screen-reader order**, so it's an accessibility pitfall) |
| `margin: auto` | Absorbs free space (`margin-left: auto` pushes an item to the far end) |

**Key concepts and gotchas**
- **`flex: 1` vs `flex: auto`:** `1` starts every item from a 0 basis, so items become *equal width*. `auto` starts from content size, so items grow *proportionally* from their content.
- **Items won't shrink below their content:** the automatic minimum size is `min-width: auto`. Long text, URLs, or `<pre>` overflow the container. **Fix: `min-width: 0`** on the flex item (and `overflow: hidden`/`text-overflow` for truncation).
- **`justify-content` vs `align-items`** swap axes when you change direction. The mnemonic is "justify = main, align = cross."
- **`align-items` vs `align-content`:** `align-items` positions items within each line. `align-content` distributes lines, and does nothing on single-line containers.
- **`flex-basis` vs `width`:** `flex-basis` wins on the main axis when not `auto`, and the main axis flips in columns, where it acts like height.
- **Flex items are blockified**, and margins don't collapse in flex.
- **Use flex for** navbars, toolbars, centering, button groups, card internals, and media objects. **Use grid for** two-dimensional page layouts.
- **Sticky footer:** `body { min-height: 100dvh; display:flex; flex-direction:column } main { flex:1 }`.

```css
.navbar { display: flex; align-items: center; gap: 1rem; }
.navbar .push-right { margin-left: auto; }           /* logo | links ........ avatar */

.cards { display: flex; flex-wrap: wrap; gap: 1rem; }
.cards > * { flex: 1 1 250px; }                      /* wrap at ~250px, grow to fill */

.row { display: flex; gap: .5rem; }
.row .text { min-width: 0; flex: 1; }                /* allows ellipsis to work inside flex */
.row .text p { overflow: hidden; text-overflow: ellipsis; white-space: nowrap; }
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.1, 3.2.2.

**Why interviewers ask:** Flexbox is the layout model most interview code actually gets written in, and the questions target the two things candidates memorise wrongly: the main/cross axis when `flex-direction` flips, and the automatic minimum size. A strong answer reaches for `min-width: 0` unprompted and can expand every form of the `flex` shorthand.
**Questions**
1. What's the difference between the main and cross axis? `justify-content` vs `align-items`?
2. What does `flex: 1` expand to? What's the difference from `flex: auto`?
3. Why is my flex child overflowing its container? *(`min-width: auto`, so set `min-width: 0`)*
4. `align-items` vs `align-content`?
5. `flex-basis` vs `width`?
6. How do you push one item to the right in a navbar? *(`margin-left: auto`)*
7. How would you build an equal-height card row, with the button pinned at the bottom of each card? *(cards are flex columns, the content `flex: 1`, or `margin-top: auto` on the button)*
8. Why can `order` be problematic? *(visual order ≠ DOM/tab order, which breaks accessibility and WCAG 2.4.3)*
9. Flexbox vs Grid: how do you choose? *(1D content-driven vs 2D layout-driven)*
10. How do `flex-grow` and `flex-shrink` calculations work? *(Free space distributed by the grow ratio. Overflow removed proportionally to `shrink × basis`.)*


**Mistakes:**
- Setting `flex: 1` and expecting equal *height* — `flex` only controls the main axis.
- Using `justify-content` for cross-axis alignment on a row, or `align-items` for main-axis distribution, and then changing values at random.
- Using `width: 100%` on children instead of `flex: 1`, which overflows as soon as the content exceeds the basis.
- Expecting `align-content` to do anything on a single-line container.
- Using `order` to fix a visual mismatch instead of fixing the DOM, leaving keyboard and screen-reader order wrong.
- Forgetting `min-height: 0` (not just `min-width`) on a column-direction item that should scroll.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Flex vs Grid (1D vs 2D):** flex is content-driven and ideal for a toolbar or a row of unknown-width items; grid is layout-driven and better when you define the tracks first and place into them, and it aligns items across rows for free.
- **`gap` vs margins:** `gap` spaces siblings only, needs no `:last-child` exception and never leaks outside the container; margins also work in inline-axis layout and let you collapse intentionally, at the cost of manual edge handling.
- **`margin: auto` vs `justify-content`:** auto margins absorb free space before justification, which is ideal for pushing one item to the far end; `justify-content` distributes all items and cannot single one out on its own.
- **Zero basis vs content basis:** `flex: 1 1 0%` gives equal columns and predictable wrapping; `flex-basis: auto` or a percentage lets rows adapt to content, which produces ragged widths and unequal card text.
- **`space-between` vs `space-evenly`:** `space-between` pins the first and last item to the edges, which is right for navbars and toolbars; `space-evenly` adds equal space at both ends, which keeps a group optically centred.

**Common wrong answers:** "Add `overflow: hidden` to the container" — it clips, it does not explain why the item refuses to shrink. Or "`order` reorders the tab order too", or "`align-items: flex-end` doesn't work on my column".

**Probe:** "A two-pane layout sets `flex: 1` on both panes and the left one still overflows when its content grows — what's the fix, and what else does that declaration change?" (Expect: `min-width: 0` on the flex item so the automatic minimum size stops pinning it to min-content; it also lets a nested `overflow: auto` actually scroll instead of pushing the sibling, which is why it is the default reflex in any scrollable flex layout.)

**What they evaluate:**
- `flex: 1` = `1 1 0%` said unprompted, with the consequence for sizing explained rather than asserted.
- `min-width: auto` named as the cause of flex overflow, and `min-width: 0` as the fix.
- Correct use of `align-content` only with wrapping, and `order` flagged as visual-only — a WCAG 2.4.3 Focus Order and 1.3.2 Meaningful Sequence failure.
- Whether they know `flex-basis` wins over `width` on the main axis, and that `margin: auto` absorbs free space *before* `justify-content`.

**Model answer, Q1:** "The main axis is the one `flex-direction` names, and `justify-content` distributes along it; the cross axis is perpendicular, and `align-items` works on it. The mnemonic holds — justify main, align cross — but flip to `flex-direction: column` and the same two properties swap what they control: `justify-content` then controls vertical. That's exactly why centring vertically on a row container fails when you reach for `justify-content`."
**Mini task:** Build (a) a responsive navbar with a right-aligned section, (b) a card list with equal heights and bottom-pinned buttons, (c) a chat layout with a header, scrollable messages (`flex:1; overflow:auto; min-height:0`), and a footer input. Note the role `min-height: 0` plays.


**Self-check:** Can you expand every form of the `flex` shorthand — `1`, `auto`, `none`, `initial`, `2 3 200px` — and say which axis each component acts on?

**Resources:** MDN "Basic concepts of flexbox", "Understanding flex sizing", and the `flex` shorthand reference; the CSS Flexible Box Layout Level 1 spec for the free-space algorithm and automatic minimum size; every-layout.dev; Josh Comeau's flexbox guide.
---

## 3.2.5 CSS Grid
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** A two-dimensional layout system for placing items in rows and columns.


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.2, 3.2.4.

**Why interviewers ask:** Grid replaced most page-level layout hacks, so the interesting question is usually "when would you *not* use it", plus the two facts people get wrong: `fr` carries an implicit `auto` minimum, and `auto-fit` and `auto-fill` diverge once there are fewer items than tracks. Asking for a media-query-free card grid exposes whether they understand intrinsic sizing or have memorised the snippet.
**Key concepts**
- **Defining tracks:** `grid-template-columns`/`-rows`, with units `px`, `%`, `fr`, `auto`, `minmax(min, max)`, `repeat(n, size)`, `min-content`/`max-content`/`fit-content()`.
- **`fr` units** divide the *remaining* space after fixed and content-sized tracks. `1fr` actually means `minmax(auto, 1fr)`, so content can blow out a track (use `minmax(0, 1fr)` to prevent this).
- **Placement:** `grid-column: 1 / 3`, `grid-row: span 2`, `grid-area`, negative line numbers (`-1` = the last line), and named lines and areas.
- **`grid-template-areas`:** readable page layouts:
```css
.page {
  display: grid;
  grid-template-columns: 240px 1fr;
  grid-template-rows: auto 1fr auto;
  grid-template-areas:
    "header header"
    "sidebar main"
    "footer footer";
  min-height: 100dvh;
}
.page > header { grid-area: header; }
.page > aside  { grid-area: sidebar; }
.page > main   { grid-area: main; }
.page > footer { grid-area: footer; }
```
- **Explicit vs implicit grid:** items beyond your defined tracks create implicit tracks, sized by `grid-auto-rows`/`-columns` and ordered by `grid-auto-flow` (`row` | `column` | `dense`).
- **Alignment:** `justify-items`/`align-items` (inside cells), `justify-content`/`align-content` (the whole grid in its container), `place-items: center`, `place-content: center`, and `gap`.
- **Responsive without media queries (the RAM pattern):**
```css
.grid { display: grid; gap: 1rem;
        grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit, minmax(min(250px, 100%), 1fr)); }
```
  **`auto-fit` vs `auto-fill`:** both create as many columns as fit. With few items, `auto-fill` keeps the empty tracks (items stay at their minimum size), whereas `auto-fit` collapses the empty tracks so items stretch to fill the row.
- **Subgrid:** `grid-template-columns: subgrid` lets nested grids align to the parent's tracks (now supported in all major browsers). It's good for card layouts where titles, bodies, and footers must align across cards.
- **Masonry:** not fully standardized (native support is still in progress), so use JS libraries or CSS columns.
- **Grid items:** margins don't collapse, and `z-index` works without `position`. Items can overlap by sharing cells.
- **Grid vs Flexbox:** use Grid when you define the layout first and put content into it (page layouts, dashboards, galleries, forms). Use Flex when content size drives the layout in a single dimension. They nest together.

**Questions**
1. Flexbox vs Grid?
2. What does `fr` mean? Why use `minmax(0, 1fr)`?
3. `auto-fit` vs `auto-fill`?
4. How would you build a responsive card grid with no media queries?
5. How do you create the "holy grail" layout (header, footer, sidebar, main)? *(`grid-template-areas`)*
6. Explicit vs implicit grid?
7. What is subgrid for?
8. How do you center something with Grid? *(`display:grid; place-items:center`)*
9. How can grid items overlap? *(same `grid-area`/line placement)*


**Mistakes:**
- Using `1fr` on a text-heavy track and blaming the browser when a long word overflows.
- Writing `grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit, 100px)` — `auto-fill`/`auto-fit` require a flexible or intrinsic track size, so the whole declaration is dropped and the container ends up with one implicit column.
- Confusing `align-content` (the tracks within the container) with `align-items` (the items within their cell).
- Fixing equal-height cards with `grid-template-rows: 1fr 1fr`, which fights the content instead of stretching items.
- Assuming grid items behave like blocks: their margins never collapse, and `z-index` works on them without `position`.
- Relying on fixed pixel columns and shipping horizontal scroll at 320px.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`auto-fit` vs `auto-fill`:** `auto-fit` stretches a small number of items to fill the row, which looks deliberate; `auto-fill` preserves the track structure so items keep a consistent width and a lone item does not become absurdly wide.
- **Fixed `1fr 1fr 1fr` vs `repeat()`:** fixed tracks are dead simple and match a design spec exactly; `repeat(auto-fit, minmax(…, 1fr))` needs no breakpoints but makes the column count content-driven, which is harder to hold to a specified breakpoint.
- **`grid-template-areas` vs explicit line placement:** areas document the layout as ASCII and refactor safely; `grid-column: 2 / 4` is terser for a small stable placement but breaks silently when the template changes around it.
- **Nested grid vs `subgrid`:** a nested grid has to restate the parent's track sizes by hand and drifts the moment the parent changes; `subgrid` inherits the tracks exactly, at the cost of requiring the parent to define them first.
- **Grid vs flex for a card row:** grid aligns cards across rows automatically because the tracks are shared; a flex row with wrap needs `flex: 1 1 250px`, and equal heights come from `align-items: stretch` rather than from flex itself.

**Common wrong answers:** "`1fr` is exactly one fraction of the container", which forgets the auto minimum entirely. Or using `align-content` to centre a grid whose rows already fill the container, then concluding grid "can't centre".

**Probe:** "One of your grid columns has a long unbroken filename and pushes the whole layout wide — what's the one-token fix, and why is `1fr` letting that happen?" (Expect: `minmax(0, 1fr)`; `1fr` implies an automatic minimum, and the automatic minimum size is the min-content contribution, so the track cannot shrink below the longest unbreakable token.)

**What they evaluate:**
- `minmax(0, 1fr)` offered unprompted as the fix for track blow-out, with the `1fr == minmax(auto, 1fr)` reason attached.
- `auto-fit` vs `auto-fill` distinguished by what happens to the *empty tracks*, not by "one is responsive".
- Correct alignment vocabulary: `justify-items`/`align-items` place items in their cells, `justify-content`/`align-content` position the whole grid inside its container.
- `subgrid` named as the answer to aligning a nested component's tracks to its parent's, and named areas as the readable alternative.

**Model answer, Q2:** "`fr` distributes whatever space remains after fixed and content-sized tracks are resolved. The trap is that `1fr` is really `minmax(auto, 1fr)`, so the track's automatic minimum — its min-content contribution — still applies, and one long unbreakable word can blow the whole column out. `minmax(0, 1fr)` removes that floor and lets the track shrink properly; I use it whenever a track holds text I don't control."
**Mini task:** Build a dashboard layout with named areas that collapses to a single column on mobile (redefine areas inside a media query), plus a photo gallery with a featured 2×2 tile (`grid-column: span 2; grid-row: span 2` + `grid-auto-flow: dense`).


**Self-check:** Can you build a responsive card grid, a dashboard with named areas, and an overlay spanning two cells — and justify every track-sizing function you used?

**Resources:** MDN "CSS grid layout", "Basic concepts of grid layout" and `grid-template-columns`; the CSS Grid Layout Level 1 spec for the track-sizing algorithm and the `1fr == minmax(auto, 1fr)` rule; every-layout.dev's grid recipes; Ahmad Shadeed's grid guides.
---

## 3.2.6 Centering and Common Layout Recipes
🟢 · **Must-know** (the classic warm-up)

**Centering cheat sheet**

| Goal | Solution |
|---|---|
| Center horizontally (block with width) | `margin-inline: auto` |
| Center text/inline content | `text-align: center` |
| Center both axes (modern) | `display: grid; place-items: center;` |
| Center both axes (flex) | `display:flex; justify-content:center; align-items:center;` |
| Center an absolutely positioned element | `position:absolute; inset:0; margin:auto; width:200px; height:100px` |
| Center with transform (works with unknown size) | `position:absolute; top:50%; left:50%; transform:translate(-50%,-50%)` |
| Single line vertically | `line-height` equal to the container height (legacy) |
| Center an item in the viewport | `min-height: 100dvh` + grid |

**Layout recipes to know cold**
```css
/* Sticky footer */
body { min-height: 100dvh; display: grid; grid-template-rows: auto 1fr auto; }

/* Constrained, centered content wrapper */
.container { width: min(100% - 2rem, 70rem); margin-inline: auto; }

/* Equal-width columns that wrap */
.cols { display: grid; grid-template-columns: repeat(auto-fit, minmax(16rem, 1fr)); gap: 1rem; }

/* Media object (image + text) */
.media { display: flex; gap: 1rem; align-items: flex-start; }
.media img { flex: none; width: 64px; }

/* Aspect-ratio box (video embed) */
.video { aspect-ratio: 16 / 9; width: 100%; }

/* Overlay text on image */
.hero { position: relative; }
.hero::after { content:""; position:absolute; inset:0; background: linear-gradient(transparent, rgb(0 0 0/.6)); }
.hero .caption { position:absolute; inset-inline:1rem; bottom:1rem; color:#fff; z-index:1; }

/* Holy-grail: header, footer, sidebar, main -> see Grid section */

/* Toggle switch (accessible: real checkbox) */
.switch { appearance: none; width: 2.5rem; height: 1.5rem; border-radius: 99px;
          background:#ccc; position:relative; transition: background .2s; }
.switch::after { content:""; position:absolute; inset:2px auto 2px 2px; aspect-ratio:1;
                 border-radius:50%; background:#fff; transition: transform .2s; }
.switch:checked { background:#1a73e8; }
.switch:checked::after { transform: translateX(1rem); }
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.2, 3.2.3, 3.2.4, 3.2.5.

**Why interviewers ask:** This is the warm-up drill and the first screen share of the interview, and it is not about snippets. It is a proxy for whether you can construct the boring 90% of a design from first principles, and — crucially — whether you know *why* each recipe works well enough to adapt it when the requirement changes.

**Definition:** The composable, no-JS layout patterns worth having memorised — centring on one or both axes, sticky footers, constrained content wrappers, equal-width wrapping columns, media objects, aspect-ratio boxes and full-height scrolling shells — assembled from the box model, positioning, flex and grid.
**Questions:** center a div in at least 4 ways; build a sticky footer; make a square responsive box (`aspect-ratio: 1`); two columns where the sidebar is fixed-width and main fills the remaining space (`grid-template-columns: 250px 1fr`, or flex with `flex:1`); a full-height layout where only the middle scrolls.


**Self-check:** Can you centre a div in four ways and, for each, name the one precondition that would make it fail?

**Resources:** MDN "Centering" and `margin-inline`; every-layout.dev's recipe index; Josh Comeau's centering guide; the CSS Box Alignment Level 3 spec for `place-items` and auto-margin centring.

**Mini task:** Build a full-page app shell — sticky header, a `1fr` main that is the only scroll container, a footer, and a centred modal overlay — then rebuild the same shell with flex instead of grid and note which one was harder to reason about. Done-condition: the footer stays visible at 320px wide, at 200% browser zoom, and with a mobile keyboard open, and the overlay centres when its content wraps to three lines.

**Mistakes:**
- Forgetting `box-sizing: border-box` when combining `width: 100%` with padding, then chasing the overflow with `overflow: hidden` instead of fixing the box.
- Using `100vh` for a full-height shell and losing the footer or submit button behind mobile browser chrome.
- Applying `transform: translate(-50%, -50%)` to a non-absolute element, which offsets it from wherever it already sits.
- Using `justify-content` to centre vertically on a row container.
- Reaching for `position: absolute` for overlay centring inside a scroll container and being surprised when it scrolls away.
- Building a "full-height" layout with `height: 100%` up to `<body>` and discovering the chain breaks at the first ancestor with `height: auto`.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Grid `place-items` vs flex centring:** grid centring is one declaration and never needs to know the child's size; flex needs both axes named and is safer when the container is already a flex row for unrelated reasons.
- **Absolute + `margin: auto` vs absolute + `translate`:** the `inset`/`margin` version is pure centring with no transform, so it adds no stacking context and no containing block, but requires a definite width and height; `translate` handles any size but creates a stacking context and a containing block for the element's own descendants, which can break `position: fixed` inside it.
- **`min()` wrapper vs `max-width` plus padding:** `width: min(100% - 2rem, 70rem)` adapts at both ends with no risk of overflow; `max-width` plus padding can exceed the viewport under `content-box` and produce a horizontal scrollbar.
- **Only the middle scrolls vs a normal scrolling page:** a fixed `100dvh` grid with `1fr` middle and `overflow: auto` gives a stable app shell and a pinned header, at the cost of losing natural document scroll — which matters for mobile URL-bar behaviour and for anchors.

**Common wrong answers:** Setting `top: 50%; left: 50%` without the compensating `transform`, which offsets the element down and right by half its own size. Or `line-height: 100vh`, which breaks the moment there is more than one line of inline content.

**Probe:** "Your `position: absolute` overlay centres fine at one content length and drifts at another — which of your centring methods depends on a known size, and what breaks it?" (Expect: `inset: 0; margin: auto` requires a definite width *and* height, because auto margins can only distribute leftover space once the insets resolve to a definite one; with `height: auto` the free space is zero and the margin is zero. Use grid or flex centring on the overlay instead, or the `top/left: 50%` plus `translate(-50%, -50%)` method, which needs no known size.)

**What they evaluate:**
- At least four **different mechanisms**, each with the reason it works, not four variations on flex.
- The `margin-inline: auto` vs `text-align` distinction, and knowledge that auto margins need a definite size on a block.
- The absolute-centring trick's **preconditions** (resolved insets, non-auto size) rather than the snippet alone.
- `dvh` over `vh` for anything full-height, and `min()` in a content wrapper so the gutter doesn't double-count.
- Whether they can build a full-height shell where only the middle scrolls, and say what that costs on mobile.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Horizontally, a block with a definite width centres with `margin-inline: auto`, because auto margins in the inline axis split the free space; text centres with `text-align: center`. For both axes, `display: grid; place-items: center` is the shortest and most predictable, since it never needs to know the child's size. Flex is identical in effect (`justify-content` plus `align-items`) and is the right choice when the container is already flex for other reasons. For an absolutely positioned element, `inset: 0` with `margin: auto` and a definite width and height — auto margins resolve against the resolved insets. And when the size is genuinely unknown, `top: 50%; left: 50%` with `transform: translate(-50%, -50%)`."
---

## 3.2.7 Responsive Design
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Designing layouts that adapt to any viewport, input method, and user preference.


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.1, 3.1.7, 3.2.2, 3.2.5.

**Why interviewers ask:** This is a systems question wearing a layout question's clothes. The interviewer is checking whether you treat responsiveness as intrinsic sizing plus user preferences, or as three device widths that must be fixed each quarter. Container queries and logical properties are the tell — someone who only knows `max-width` breakpoints has never tried to drop a component into a narrow slot.
**Key concepts**
- **Mobile-first:** write base styles for small screens, then layer enhancements with `min-width` queries. It yields simpler CSS and better mobile performance.
- **Viewport meta** (3.1.1) is required.
- **Media queries:** `@media (min-width: 48rem) { … }`. Use **content-driven breakpoints** (where the layout breaks), not device models. Using `rem`/`em` in breakpoints respects user zoom settings. Range syntax: `@media (width >= 48rem)`.
- **Feature queries:** `prefers-color-scheme`, `prefers-reduced-motion`, `prefers-contrast`, `hover: hover` and `pointer: coarse` (touch vs mouse), `orientation`, and `@supports (display: grid)`.
- **Fluid techniques** reduce the number of breakpoints:
```css
h1 { font-size: clamp(1.75rem, 1.2rem + 2.5vw, 3rem); }       /* fluid type with min/max */
.section { padding-block: clamp(2rem, 6vw, 6rem); }
.container { width: min(100% - 2rem, 70rem); margin-inline: auto; }
```
  Keep a `rem` component in fluid type so users' browser zoom still scales it (pure `vw` text fails zoom accessibility).
- **Container queries:** a component responds to *its container's* size, not the viewport. This is key for reusable components in design systems:
```css
.card-wrap { container-type: inline-size; container-name: card; }
.card { display: grid; gap: 1rem; }
@container card (min-width: 30rem) {
  .card { grid-template-columns: 8rem 1fr; }
}
```
  Supported in all modern browsers. Related: container query units (`cqw`, `cqi`), and style queries (newer).
- **Logical properties:** `margin-inline`, `padding-block`, `inset-inline-start`, `border-inline-end`, `text-align: start`, `inline-size`/`block-size`. They adapt automatically to writing mode and direction (**RTL** for Arabic and Hebrew), so you don't maintain separate left/right overrides.
- **Responsive images and media** (3.1.7) and **responsive tables** (wrap in an `overflow-x: auto` container, or restack on small screens).
- **Touch:** targets of 44px or more, no hover-only functionality (`@media (hover: hover)`), and `touch-action`.
- **Layout tools:** Grid (`auto-fit/minmax`), Flex wrap, `gap`, `aspect-ratio`, `min()`/`max()`/`clamp()`.
- **Testing:** DevTools device mode (not a real device substitute), real devices, resizing from 320px up to 2560px, zoom to 200%, landscape, and slow networks.
- **Mobile viewport units:** use `dvh`/`svh`, and account for safe areas (`env(safe-area-inset-bottom)`) on notched phones.
- **Avoid:** fixed pixel widths, horizontal scroll at 320px, hiding vast content with `display:none` on mobile (it still downloads), and `user-scalable=no`.

**Questions**
1. What is mobile-first and why use it?
2. Media queries vs container queries: when would you use each? *(Media: page-level layout and user preferences. Container: reusable components placed in varying contexts.)*
3. How do you choose breakpoints? *(Content-based, not by device)*
4. What does `clamp()` do? Fluid typography without media queries?
5. How do you support RTL languages? *(logical properties, `dir="rtl"`, mirrored icons)*
6. How would you make a data table responsive?
7. How do you handle hover on touch devices?
8. How do you reduce layout shift in a responsive layout?
9. Your design works at 375px and 1440px but breaks at 900px. Why? *(device-model breakpoints. Test the continuum and use fluid and intrinsic layout.)*


**Mistakes:**
- Sizing type purely in `vw`, so browser zoom stops working and WCAG 1.4.4 fails.
- Hiding content with `display: none` on small screens and calling it done — the markup, images and JS are all still fetched.
- Writing hover-only affordances without `@media (hover: hover)`, leaving them unusable on touch.
- Testing only DevTools device presets, never resizing continuously, never zooming to 200%, and never checking landscape.
- Using `100vh` for a full-height section and losing the CTA behind mobile browser chrome.
- Using fixed pixel widths and shipping horizontal scroll at 320px.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Viewport media queries vs container queries:** media queries are right for page scaffolding and user-preference features; container queries are right for a component reused in a sidebar, a main column and a modal, but they need `container-type` on the parent and cannot respond to viewport size directly.
- **Breakpoints vs fluid sizing:** `clamp()`, `min()` and `minmax()` remove breakpoints and give continuous behaviour, at the cost of being harder to hold to an exact design spec — you can't force a specific layout at exactly 900px.
- **Physical vs logical properties:** logical properties give RTL and vertical writing modes for free; physical properties are shorter and familiar, and every `left`/`right` then needs a second rule or ships backwards in Arabic.
- **Mobile-first vs desktop-first:** mobile-first keeps CSS smaller and starts from the constrained case; desktop-first matches how designs arrive and is easier when the primary audience is desktop, at the cost of more overrides.
- **Horizontal scroll for a wide table vs restacking:** scrolling preserves every column and one source of markup; restacking reads far better on a phone but needs per-cell labels and loses column alignment for comparison.

**Common wrong answers:** Designing at three widths and calling it responsive. Or adding `user-scalable=no` to hide an overflow bug — that is an accessibility failure and iOS Safari ignores it anyway.

**Probe:** "Your layout is right at 375px and 1440px and breaks at 900px — what did you do, and what would have prevented it?" (Expect: device-model breakpoints with content that has an awkward intermediate size; prevention is intrinsic sizing plus testing a continuous range rather than presets, and container queries so the component responds to its slot instead of the viewport.)

**What they evaluate:**
- Breakpoints justified by **where the content breaks**, not by device names, and written in `rem`/`em` so user zoom changes them.
- Whether they reach for intrinsic sizing — `auto-fit`/`minmax`, `min()`, `clamp()`, `aspect-ratio` — to *remove* breakpoints rather than add them.
- Container queries correctly scoped to components and viewport queries to page scaffolding and user preferences, with `container-type` on the parent named.
- `prefers-reduced-motion`, `prefers-contrast`, `hover: hover` / `pointer: coarse` and ~44px targets treated as requirements, plus `env(safe-area-inset-*)`.
- Logical properties chosen so RTL works without a second stylesheet.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Mobile-first means the base styles describe the smallest case and `min-width` queries layer enhancements on top. It's better for three reasons: the base CSS stays simple instead of accumulating `max-width` overrides that fight each other, the browser only pays for queries that actually match, and it forces you to prioritise content, because the narrow layout is the constrained one. The one argument against is that designs are usually handed over desktop-first, so the desktop case has to be reconstructed."
**Mini task:** Build one pricing-card component that works inside a sidebar (narrow), main column (wide), and modal, with container queries only, no viewport media queries. Then convert a physical-property stylesheet to logical properties and flip `dir="rtl"`.


**Self-check:** Can you name a breakpoint you'd choose, justify it from content rather than a device, and say how a container query would remove it?

**Resources:** MDN "Responsive design", "Media queries", "CSS container queries" and "Logical properties"; web.dev's "Learn CSS: media queries" and "Learn CSS: container queries"; the WCAG 1.4.4 Resize Text and 1.4.10 Reflow success criteria.
---

## 3.2.8 Modern CSS Features
🟡 → 🔴 · **Must-know** (signals currency)

**Custom properties (CSS variables)**
```css
:root { --brand: #1a73e8; --space: 1rem; }
.btn { background: var(--brand); padding: var(--space, .5rem); }  /* fallback value */
.btn.danger { --brand: #d93025; }                                  /* scoped override */
```
- They are **runtime, cascade-aware, and inherited**, unlike Sass variables, which compile away. JS can read and write them (`el.style.setProperty('--x', v)`, `getComputedStyle`), they work in media queries only indirectly (you can't use `var()` inside a media condition), and they're the basis of theming and design tokens.
- `@property` registers typed variables, enabling animation of custom properties (e.g., gradients).

**Selectors and logic**
- **`:has()`**, the "parent selector": `.card:has(img)`, `label:has(input:checked)`, `form:has(:invalid) button { opacity:.5 }`.
- **`:is()`/`:where()`**: grouping, with `:where` at zero specificity.
- **`:not()`, `:focus-visible`, `:focus-within`**, `:user-invalid`, `::marker`, `::selection`, `::placeholder`.
- **Native nesting:**
```css
.card { padding: 1rem;
  & h2 { margin: 0; }
  &:hover { box-shadow: 0 2px 8px rgb(0 0 0/.15); }
  @media (width >= 48rem) { padding: 2rem; } }
```
