**Layout and sizing helpers:** `gap`, `aspect-ratio`, `inset`, `min()/max()/clamp()`, `object-fit`, `scroll-snap-type`, `position: sticky`, `contain`, and `content-visibility: auto` (skip rendering of offscreen sections to improve load performance), `text-wrap: balance|pretty` for nicer headings, and `scrollbar-gutter: stable`.

**Color and visuals:** `rgb(0 0 0 / .5)` space-separated syntax, `hsl()`, `color-mix(in srgb, red 40%, white)`, `oklch()` (perceptually uniform), `light-dark()`, `accent-color`, `filter`/`backdrop-filter`, `mix-blend-mode`, `clip-path`, and `@supports`.

**Newer, check support before using in production:** anchor positioning, scroll-driven animations, View Transitions API, `@starting-style` (animate from `display:none`, with `transition-behavior: allow-discrete`), and `field-sizing`. Use progressive enhancement via `@supports` and check **Baseline** status on MDN or caniuse.


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.1, 3.2.7.

**Why interviewers ask:** It is the cheapest currency signal available: it tells you whether you read release notes and check support tables, or whether you are still writing 2016 CSS. The follow-up is always "how would you ship it safely", which is really a question about Baseline, `@supports` and progressive enhancement.

**Definition:** The platform features that changed what CSS can express without a build step — custom properties and `@property`, `:has()`/`:is()`/`:where()`, native nesting, `clamp()`/`min()`/`max()`, container-query units, `content-visibility`, `text-wrap: balance`, `light-dark()`, `oklch()`, `@starting-style` and View Transitions — together with the Baseline status that decides when each is safe to ship.
**Questions**
1. CSS variables vs Sass variables?
2. What does `:has()` enable that was previously impossible?
3. What is `@layer` for? (3.2.1)
4. How do you animate between `display: none` and visible? *(`@starting-style` and `transition-behavior: allow-discrete`, or a class toggle with opacity and `visibility`.)*
5. How do you adopt a new CSS feature safely? *(Check Baseline, use `@supports`, provide a fallback, progressive enhancement.)*
6. `:focus` vs `:focus-visible` vs `:focus-within`?
7. What does `content-visibility: auto` do? Trade-offs? *(Skips layout/paint of off-screen content, which is a large rendering win, but needs `contain-intrinsic-size` to avoid scrollbar jumps.)*


**Self-check:** Can you name three features you'd ship today with no fallback, one you'd feature-detect, and one you'd gate behind a flag — and say how you'd decide?

**Resources:** MDN "CSS custom properties", `@property`, `:has()` and CSS nesting; the MDN "Baseline" indicator and the Baseline widget on every feature page; web.dev's "Newly available CSS features" and "Baseline newly available"; caniuse.com for per-browser support.

**Mini task:** Take one dialog and one menu. Replace their JS class-toggling with `:has()`/`:is()` where you can, register a custom property with `@property` and interpolate it inside a gradient transition, and add `@starting-style` for the entry animation. Done-condition: all three animations run in Chrome, Safari and Firefox, and the dialog still opens, traps focus and closes on `Esc` in a browser with no `allow-discrete` support.

**Mistakes:**
- Using `var(--x)` inside a media query condition, where custom properties are not allowed; that needs a custom-media PostCSS plugin or a real `@media` per value.
- Animating a custom property that hasn't been registered with `@property`, so it jumps at 50% instead of interpolating.
- Treating `@supports` as proof a feature *works* — it only proves the browser recognises and parses it.
- Using `oklch()` with no fallback declaration, so the whole rule drops in an older Safari rather than degrading.
- Using `:has()` on `body` or a huge container to style a descendant, which is a performance trap for no benefit.
- Reaching for `!important` to force a new feature, when `@supports` or a fallback declaration is the actual mechanism.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Custom properties vs Sass variables:** custom properties survive to the runtime, inherit and cascade, and are themeable from JS; Sass variables are author-time, work inside media-query conditions, and vanish on compile — so the sensible split is Sass for a breakpoint map, custom properties for theme tokens.
- **`:has()` vs a JS-toggled class:** `:has()` removes state from JS entirely and works on server-rendered markup with no hydration concern, at the cost of selector-matching work in large trees and a specificity bump you must account for.
- **Native nesting vs Sass nesting:** native nesting ships as written and needs no build step, but it gives no mixins, maps or loops; a deep `&` chain still compounds specificity silently, and flattening to a low-specificity compiled selector is often the better output.
- **Fluid functions vs media queries:** `clamp()` and `min()` are continuous and breakpoint-free, but they cannot express a *discrete* switch — a row that becomes a column — which a media query makes explicit.

**Common wrong answers:** Treating custom properties as compile-time like Sass, so a "themed" brand colour can't change at runtime. Or claiming `content-visibility: auto` removes content from the accessibility tree — it does not; it skips rendering work while keeping the content exposed.

**Probe:** "A design-system dialog needs to animate in from `display: none`. How, and what do you do where `allow-discrete` isn't supported?" (Expect: `@starting-style { dialog { opacity: 0 } }` plus `transition: opacity .2s, display .2s allow-discrete`, so `display` flips at the *end* of the transition and the element stays `none` while hidden — without `allow-discrete` a discrete property jumps instead of interpolating. Fallback: toggle a class driving `opacity` and `visibility`, or accept no entry animation and keep the exit.)

**What they evaluate:**
- Whether they check **Baseline** and reach for `@supports`, and know a fallback must be a working layout, not a blank page.
- The custom-property vs Sass-variable distinction argued on runtime, inheritance and the cascade rather than preference.
- `@property` named as the reason a custom property can be interpolated, and `@starting-style` with `transition-behavior: allow-discrete` named as the fix for animating `display`.
- `:has()` used for a real case, with awareness that it takes the specificity of its most specific argument.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Sass variables are substituted at build time, so they can't change at runtime and they don't inherit — `$x: 1px` in a parent file is just text pasted at the call site. CSS custom properties are real properties on the DOM tree: they inherit, the cascade applies to them, JS can read and write them with `setProperty` and `getComputedStyle`, and they can be scoped to a subtree and overridden there. The catch is they're untyped by default, they aren't allowed inside a media query condition, and you have to register one with `@property` before you can give it an initial value or transition it."
---

## 3.2.9 Transitions, Animations, and Animation Performance
🟡 → 🔴 · **Must-know**

**Techniques**

| Tool | Use for |
|---|---|
| `transition` | State changes (hover, focus, class toggle) between two values |
| `@keyframes` + `animation` | Multi-step, looping, autonomous animation |
| Web Animations API (`el.animate()`) | JS-controlled playback (pause, reverse, finish promises) |
| `requestAnimationFrame` | Custom per-frame logic (canvas, physics, scroll-linked effects) |
| View Transitions API | Animated page or state transitions |
| Libraries | Framer Motion/Motion, GSAP, React Spring, Lottie for complex sequences |

```css
.btn { transition: transform .2s ease, background-color .2s ease; }
.btn:hover { transform: translateY(-2px); }

@keyframes spin { to { transform: rotate(360deg); } }
.spinner { animation: spin 1s linear infinite; }

@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce) {
  *, *::before, *::after { animation-duration: .01ms !important; animation-iteration-count: 1 !important; transition-duration: .01ms !important; }
}
```

**Performance, the key interview topic**
- The pipeline: **JS → Style → Layout → Paint → Composite**. Animating a property triggers the work from its stage onward.
  - **Layout-triggering** (expensive): `width`, `height`, `top/left`, `margin`, `padding`, `font-size`
  - **Paint-triggering:** `color`, `background`, `box-shadow`, `border-radius`
  - **Composite-only (cheap, GPU-friendly):** **`transform`** and **`opacity`** (also `filter` in many cases)
- **Rule:** animate `transform` and `opacity`, not `top/left/width/height`. Move with `translate`, resize with `scale`.
- **`will-change: transform`:** a hint to promote a layer *before* animating. Use it sparingly and remove it afterward, since each layer costs memory.
- **FLIP technique** (First, Last, Invert, Play): to animate layout changes cheaply, measure before and after, apply an inverse `transform`, then transition it to none.
- **Avoid layout thrashing:** interleaving DOM reads (`offsetHeight`) and writes in a loop forces repeated synchronous layouts. Batch reads, then writes.
- **Timing:** about 100-300ms for UI feedback. Use easing (`ease-out` for entering, `ease-in` for leaving), and keep motion purposeful.
- **Accessibility:** respect `prefers-reduced-motion`, avoid flashing (more than 3 flashes per second), and provide pause controls for autoplay content longer than 5 seconds.
- **60fps budget:** about 16.7ms per frame (and about 8.3ms for 120Hz).
- **Debug:** DevTools Performance panel (frames and long tasks), Rendering panel → Paint flashing, Layer borders, and Layers.


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.1, 3.5.1.

**Why interviewers ask:** "Why is this dropdown animation janky?" is a real production question and the answer is almost always a property choice. This is where a candidate proves they understand that `transform` and `opacity` skip layout and paint because they run on the compositor, and that `will-change` is a memory trade rather than a free win.

**Definition:** The declarative and scripted ways to interpolate property values over time — `transition`, `@keyframes` with `animation`, and the Web Animations API — plus the rendering-pipeline rules that decide whether an animation is cheap or expensive.
**Questions**
1. Transition vs animation?
2. Which CSS properties are cheap to animate, and why? *(Composite-only: `transform` and `opacity`, which skip layout and paint and run on the compositor thread.)*
3. Why is animating `left` worse than `transform: translateX`?
4. What does `will-change` do, and what are the risks?
5. What's layout thrashing?
6. How would you animate an element's height from 0 to `auto`? *(`grid-template-rows: 0fr → 1fr` trick, `interpolate-size: allow-keywords` where supported, or measure with JS, or `max-height` hack with its caveats)*
7. What is the FLIP technique?
8. `requestAnimationFrame` vs CSS animations?
9. How do you handle users who prefer reduced motion?
10. How do you build a skeleton loader? *(gradient with animated `background-position` or a transform on a pseudo-element)*


**Mistakes:**
- Animating `top`, `left`, `width`, `height` or `margin` and blaming the hardware.
- Leaving `will-change` permanently applied "just in case", exhausting memory on low-end devices.
- `transition: all`, which animates properties you did not intend — including ones that trigger layout.
- Reading `offsetHeight` or `getBoundingClientRect()` inside a scroll or resize handler without batching reads before writes, forcing a synchronous layout per iteration.
- Expecting a transition on an element coming from `display: none` to run its entry, without `@starting-style` and `allow-discrete`.
- Animating `height: 0 → auto` with a `max-height` hack and calling it done, when the timing curve is wrong for any height other than the assumed maximum.
- Ignoring `prefers-reduced-motion` for parallax, autoplay or anything above a few pixels of movement.

**Trade-offs:**
- **CSS transitions vs the Web Animations API:** CSS is declarative, tiny, survives framework re-renders and needs no JavaScript; WAAPI gives `finished` promises, playback rate, reverse and scrubbing from JS, at the cost of more code and a runtime.
- **CSS animations vs FLIP:** CSS alone is simpler but forces you to fake a layout change with `transform`; FLIP measures and inverts so the *real* layout changes, at the price of JavaScript and manual inverted-state management.
- **`will-change` vs letting the browser decide:** the hint removes first-frame jank on user-initiated animations; the browser's own heuristics cost nothing but occasionally promote a frame late.
- **A blanket `prefers-reduced-motion` override vs a considered variant:** the blanket rule (`*, *::before, *::after { animation-duration: .01ms !important }`) is cheap, total and kills meaningful feedback; querying the preference in JS lets you swap a large movement for a short fade and keep the state change legible.
- **`scale()` vs animating real dimensions:** `scale()` is compositor-only but leaves the layout box at its original size, so a growing card overlaps its neighbours; animating `width`/`height` reflows correctly but costs a layout per frame.

**Common wrong answers:** "`transform` is faster because the GPU is faster." Or "leave `will-change: transform` on everything to be safe", which is how a page runs out of GPU memory on a mid-range phone.

**Probe:** "A list of 500 rows animates 200px on scroll and drops frames; the same animation at 20 rows is smooth. Where is the time going, and what do you change?" (Expect: if it animates `top` or `left`, every frame is a main-thread layout, and the more rows the worse, which is exactly the scaling seen; switch to `transform`, add `will-change` before and remove it after, verify in the Performance panel that the animation no longer produces purple Layout bars, and if the drop persists with `transform` alone then the cost is elsewhere — a large paint area or main-thread work in the scroll handler, and the fix is `contain` or virtualisation.)

**What they evaluate:**
- `transform` and `opacity` called composite-only **because** they skip layout and paint and run on the compositor thread, not merely "because the GPU is fast".
- Whether they know `will-change` costs memory and creates a stacking context, and that it is applied before and removed after.
- Transitions requiring a before-and-after state while animations are self-running, and animations overriding transitions in the cascade.
- FLIP as the correct answer for animating layout changes, and `prefers-reduced-motion` handled rather than ignored.

**Model answer, Q1:** "A transition reacts to a state change: the browser computes the new value of a property that already has one and interpolates between the two, once, over the duration. An animation runs on its own from `@keyframes` — it can have multiple steps, per-keyframe easing, loops, alternation, and it can be paused and scrubbed. Practically: transitions for hover, focus and class-driven UI; animations for anything autonomous. And because animations sit above normal author declarations in the cascade, a running animation wins over a transition on the same property."
**Mini task:** Animate 500 elements by `left` and again by `transform`, record both in the Performance panel, and compare frame times. Then implement a FLIP animation for a reordering list.


**Self-check:** Can you name the four pipeline stages an animated property triggers work in, and say which two properties skip two of them?

**Resources:** MDN `transition`, `@keyframes`, `will-change`, the Web Animations API and `prefers-reduced-motion`; web.dev's "Animations guide" and "Stick to compositor-only properties"; the CSS Will Change spec's note on layer memory; 3.5.1 for the pipeline and forced-layout rules this builds on.
---

## 3.2.10 Preprocessors, CSS Modules, and Architecture
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Preprocessors (Sass/SCSS, Less, Stylus)**
- **Features:** variables, nesting, mixins, functions, partials, loops/maps, and math. Sass modules use `@use`/`@forward` (the old `@import` is deprecated).
- **Compile-time:** output is plain CSS.
- **Native CSS now covers much of it:** custom properties (runtime), nesting, `color-mix`, `calc()`, and `@layer`.
- **Still useful for:** loops and generated utility classes, complex mixins and functions, legacy codebases (Bootstrap source), and team conventions. **PostCSS** (autoprefixer, nesting polyfills, Tailwind pipeline) is the other commonly used tool, and **Lightning CSS** is the fast modern alternative.
```scss
@use 'sass:map';
$breakpoints: (sm: 40rem, md: 48rem, lg: 64rem);
@mixin up($bp) { @media (min-width: map.get($breakpoints, $bp)) { @content; } }
.card { padding: 1rem; @include up(md) { padding: 2rem; } &__title { font-weight: 600; } }
```

**CSS Modules**
- Each `.module.css` file is **locally scoped**: class names are transformed (hashed) at build time, e.g. `.title` → `Card_title__x7Gh2`. No collisions, and dependencies are explicit via imports.
```css
/* Card.module.css */
.card { padding: 1rem; }
.title { font-weight: 600; }
.title.active { color: var(--brand); }
```
```jsx
import styles from './Card.module.css';
export const Card = ({ active }) =>
  <div className={styles.card}><h2 className={`${styles.title} ${active ? styles.active : ''}`}>…</h2></div>;
```
- **Pros:** zero runtime cost, real CSS (tooling works), predictable scoping, and it works with Server Components. **Cons:** dynamic styles need CSS variables or conditional classes, global concerns need `:global`, class naming (`composes`) can be awkward, and theming requires variables.

**Naming and architecture methodologies**

| Method | Idea |
|---|---|
| **BEM** | `.block__element--modifier` (`.card__title--large`), flat specificity and explicit naming, but verbose |
| **SMACSS / ITCSS** | Layered organization (settings → tools → generic → elements → objects → components → utilities), going from low to high specificity |
| **OOCSS** | Separate structure from skin, favoring reusable objects |
| **Utility-first** | Compose single-purpose classes (Tailwind) |
| **CUBE CSS** | Composition, Utility, Block, Exception |
| **CSS Modules / CSS-in-JS** | Tool-enforced scoping instead of naming conventions |

**Scaling CSS:** design tokens (variables for color, spacing, type, radii), a reset or normalize layer, `@layer`, component-colocated styles, consistent spacing scale, linting (Stylelint), a dead CSS check (coverage tab), and documented patterns (Storybook).


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.1, 3.2.8, 3.8.1.

**Why interviewers ask:** The question is how you keep CSS maintainable at scale, so the strong answer is architectural: tokens as runtime custom properties, `@layer` making cascade order explicit, scoping enforced by the toolchain rather than by discipline, and a real mechanism for deleting dead CSS. "Use BEM" on its own is not an answer.

**Definition:** The tooling and conventions around stylesheets: Sass/Less for author-time abstraction, PostCSS and Lightning CSS as build-time transforms, CSS Modules for build-enforced scoping, and the naming and layering systems — BEM, ITCSS, utility-first, `@layer` — that keep a large codebase navigable and overridable.
**Questions**
1. What do preprocessors offer that plain CSS doesn't (and vice versa today)?
2. What's BEM and why use it? Drawbacks?
3. How do CSS Modules achieve scoping?
4. How do you structure CSS in a large app?
5. CSS Modules vs BEM vs Tailwind vs CSS-in-JS? *(see 3.2.11 table)*
6. How do you override third-party component styles without `!important`? *(layers, a wrapper's higher specificity, the library's CSS variables, the `::part`, or `:where`)*
7. How do you find and remove unused CSS?


**Self-check:** Can you explain why design tokens must be custom properties rather than Sass variables, and how `@layer` removes the need for `!important`?

**Resources:** MDN on CSS Modules conventions and `@layer`; Sass's `@use`/`@forward` documentation and the Dart Sass deprecation list; the PostCSS plugin directory and Lightning CSS's documentation; Stylelint's rules reference; 3.2.11 for the tooling trade-off table.

**Mini task:** Convert one existing component to a token-driven CSS Module. Define colour, spacing and radius tokens as custom properties on `:root`, move the reset into `@layer reset`, put the component in `@layer components`, and delete every `!important` and ID selector. Done-condition: the production build shows hashed class names and a smaller stylesheet than before, changing one custom property still re-themes the component, and no rule in the file targets anything outside the component's own markup.

**Mistakes:**
- Using Sass variables for theme values and only discovering at release time that a theme needs a recompile.
- Reaching for `!important` or an ID to beat a third-party class, starting a cascade war the next team inherits.
- Depending on Sass's deprecated `@import` instead of `@use`/`@forward`, so every member is global and load order silently matters.
- Building one enormous global stylesheet keyed on `body` and `#app`, which makes scoping and dead-code removal both impossible.
- Using `@apply` heavily, reintroducing an abstraction layer that hides the actual styling from the next reader.
- Copy-pasting a `!important` override "temporarily" and never removing it, so the component can never be restyled again.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Sass vs plain CSS with custom properties:** Sass still wins for loops, maps, mixins and author-time computation; native CSS wins because custom properties are runtime and cascade-aware, and it removes a language and a build step from the critical path.
- **CSS Modules vs `@layer` plus `:where()`:** modules give build-enforced scoping with zero runtime cost, at the price of hashed class names and a `:global` escape hatch; global CSS with a naming convention needs no tooling but depends on everyone obeying the convention, which is exactly what a convention cannot guarantee.
- **PostCSS vs Lightning CSS:** PostCSS is a plugin ecosystem you can bend to anything — autoprefixing, custom media, nesting transforms — at the cost of a JS plugin chain; Lightning CSS is one fast Rust implementation that handles prefixes, nesting and minification, with far fewer hooks.
- **BEM-style naming vs a layers-first approach:** naming is free and greppable but verbose and unenforced; layers plus flat single-class selectors remove the specificity fight entirely, at the cost of needing to agree on layer order before writing anything.
- **A monolithic stylesheet vs per-component modules:** one file is easy to search and impossible to scope; per-component files are scoped and deletable, at the cost of jump-around and needing a documented token layer so they agree.

**Common wrong answers:** "Use Sass variables for theming", which cannot change after compilation. Or "`!important` to beat the library", which escalates rather than resolves.

**Probe:** "Your design system's `--brand` must change at runtime per tenant, and a library's `.btn` keeps beating your override. What do you reach for, in order?" (Expect: expose `--brand` as a custom property and set it on a wrapper or `:root` per tenant; for the override, put your rules in a later `@layer` than the library's, or wrap the component in a class with deliberately higher specificity, or consume the library's own CSS custom properties, or use `::part` if it's a web component with shadow parts exposed.)

**What they evaluate:**
- Tokens implemented as **runtime custom properties**, not Sass variables — this is the single most important architectural point and the most commonly missed one.
- `@layer` used to make cascade order explicit (reset → base → components → utilities) instead of relying on file order and specificity.
- A real answer on scoping: CSS Modules, or `:where()` and layers, rather than "be careful with names".
- A concrete dead-CSS strategy: coverage analysis, route-level splitting, or atomic output — not "we're disciplined".
- Honest cost accounting: naming conventions are free but unenforced; build-enforced scoping costs a build step and hurts debuggability.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Sass gives me what CSS still doesn't: loops over maps to generate a scale or utility set, real mixins and functions, placeholders with `@extend`, and computed values at author time — genuinely useful for a breakpoint map or a generated palette. What native CSS gives me instead, and increasingly replaces it: custom properties for runtime theming, native nesting, `color-mix()`, `calc()`, `min()/max()/clamp()`, and `@layer`, which removes most of the original justification for preprocessor architecture. So I still reach for Sass for loops, maps and complex mixins, and I wouldn't introduce it just for variables and nesting."
---

## 3.2.11 Tailwind CSS and CSS-in-JS: Trade-offs
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** ("which would you choose and why?")

**Tailwind (utility-first)**
```html
<button class="rounded-lg bg-blue-600 px-4 py-2 font-medium text-white hover:bg-blue-700
               focus-visible:outline-2 focus-visible:outline-offset-2 md:px-6">
  Save
</button>
```
- **How it works:** it scans your source files for class names at build time and generates only the CSS you use (a small production stylesheet). Variants (`hover:`, `md:`, `dark:`, `group-hover:`) compose. Recent versions (v4) moved configuration into CSS and use a faster engine, and the core mechanics are the same.
- **Pros:** fast iteration, no naming, design-system constraints (spacing and color scale), tiny output, colocated styles, and consistent look.
- **Cons:** noisy markup, a learning curve (memorizing classes), harder to read complex states, dynamic class names must be complete strings (`"bg-" + color` **won't** be detected), and you still need CSS knowledge. Extract components (not `@apply` everywhere) to avoid duplication.

**CSS-in-JS**
- **Runtime libraries (styled-components, Emotion):** styles are authored in JS, scoped, and dynamic via props. **Costs:** runtime style injection overhead, bundle size, and **friction with React Server Components and streaming SSR**, which is why many teams are moving away.
- **Zero-runtime / build-time (vanilla-extract, Panda CSS, Linaria, StyleX):** typed JS or TS authoring that is extracted to static CSS at build time, so there is no runtime cost.

| | CSS Modules | Tailwind | Runtime CSS-in-JS | Zero-runtime CSS-in-JS | BEM + Sass |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Scoping | Build-hashed | Utilities (global but atomic) | Generated names | Generated names | Convention |
| Runtime cost | None | None | Yes | None | None |
| Dynamic styles | Variables/classes | Classes/variables | Easy (props) | Variables/recipes | Classes |
| RSC-friendly | Yes | Yes | Problematic | Yes | Yes |
| Learning curve | Low | Medium | Medium | Medium-high | Low-medium |
| Best for | Component apps wanting real CSS | Fast product teams | Legacy/dynamic theming apps | Typed design systems | Traditional sites |

**Choosing, a good interview answer:** "It depends on team and constraints. For a new React/Next app with a modern setup I'd pick Tailwind for speed and consistency, or CSS Modules if the team prefers authoring real CSS. For a design system consumed by many apps, I'd lean on CSS variables and tokens with zero-runtime styling. I'd avoid runtime CSS-in-JS in new Server Component codebases because of runtime cost and RSC friction. Whichever I pick, the fundamentals of the cascade, layout, and accessibility don't change."


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.1, 3.2.10.

**Why interviewers ask:** This is the "which would you pick and why" question, and at senior level it is asked because the answer is about team, build pipeline and server rendering rather than taste. Failing it usually means defending a tool instead of stating a constraint-driven decision.

**Definition:** The two strategies competing to replace global stylesheets — Tailwind's atomic utilities generated by build-time static analysis, and CSS-in-JS's JS-authored styles, split into runtime injection (styled-components, Emotion) and zero-runtime extraction (vanilla-extract, Panda, Linaria, StyleX) — plus the criteria for choosing between them.
**Questions**
1. Why might a team choose Tailwind? Concerns about it?
2. Why is runtime CSS-in-JS falling out of favor?
3. Why doesn't `` `bg-${color}-500` `` work in Tailwind? *(Static analysis needs complete class names, so use a lookup map or safelist.)*
4. CSS Modules vs Tailwind?
5. How do you theme a Tailwind app? *(CSS variables mapped into the theme and `dark:` variants.)*
6. How do you prevent a 40-class button from being copy-pasted everywhere? *(Extract a component, use variant helpers such as `cva`/`tailwind-variants`.)*


**Mistakes:**
- Building class names dynamically (`` `bg-${color}-500` ``), which the scanner cannot see, so the rule is silently never generated.
- Using `@apply` to re-create a component class inside another, duplicating the abstraction and hiding the styling from reviewers.
- Adding a runtime CSS-in-JS library to an App Router codebase and then debugging a flash of unstyled content on first load.
- Assuming Tailwind's preflight removes the need for a design system, so every product reinvents its own spacing.
- Reaching for a bigger `z-` index or `!important` when the real problem is cascade order or a stacking context (3.2.1, 3.2.3).
- Concluding that markup-heavy utilities are inherently unwieldy for screen readers, when the accessibility problem is a missing label, not the class attribute.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Tailwind vs CSS Modules:** Tailwind optimises iteration speed, output size and colocation; CSS Modules optimises authoring familiarity, reviewability and the full power of CSS, at the cost of naming decisions and more files.
- **Utility markup vs an extracted component class:** utilities show exactly what they do at the point of use, at the cost of a 40-class button nobody can scan; an extracted class is concise but hides the styling, so you need a documented variant system such as `cva` or `tailwind-variants` or the class list gets copy-pasted.
- **Runtime vs zero-runtime CSS-in-JS:** runtime gives prop-driven dynamic styling with no build step, at the cost of bundle size, injection work and RSC/streaming friction; zero-runtime gives typed authoring and static output, at the cost of a build step and less dynamic flexibility.
- **Global conventions vs build-enforced scoping:** BEM and ITCSS cost nothing to adopt and are greppable, but depend on discipline a compiler can't check; CSS Modules is enforced and collision-free, at the cost of hashed names in DevTools and in stack traces.

**Common wrong answers:** "Tailwind is faster, always" — faster to iterate, not to render. Or "CSS-in-JS is dead", which ignores vanilla-extract and StyleX. Or "styled-components is fast because it runs in the browser".

**Probe:** "Your team is building a Next.js App Router app with a design system consumed by four apps. Which do you pick, and what breaks if you choose wrong?" (Expect: tokens as CSS variables plus either Tailwind's CSS-first `@theme` config or a zero-runtime extraction layer; runtime CSS-in-JS breaks because client components are needed for injection and streaming SSR needs styles before first paint — and you end up shipping a styling runtime that four apps must each stay compatible with.)

**What they evaluate:**
- Constraint-driven reasoning (RSC, streaming SSR, design-system ownership, team familiarity) rather than tool preference.
- Whether they know **zero-runtime** CSS-in-JS exists and can place it between "runtime libraries" and "CSS Modules".
- The dynamic class-name failure named without prompting, with the mitigation (a complete lookup map or `safelist`).
- Honest cost accounting: markup noise and a learning curve on the Tailwind side, a build step and build-time hashing on the CSS Modules side.

**Model answer, Q2:** "Three reasons. First, runtime cost: styled-components and Emotion inject and manage `<style>` tags in the browser, so every mounted styled component is work the browser does that a static stylesheet would not need. Second, Server Components: a runtime library has to execute during render to collect its styles, which pulls it into the server render path and breaks the model where only client components may hold state and effects. Third, streaming SSR: styles collected during render either block the stream while you inject them, which costs TTFB, or the user sees a flash of unstyled content. That's also why zero-runtime libraries exist — same authoring, extracted to static CSS at build time, so they are compatible with RSC and streaming."
**Mini task:** Build the same card + button + responsive grid in CSS Modules and Tailwind. Compare time taken, CSS output size, and readability. Write a one-paragraph recommendation.


**Self-check:** Can you state the one condition under which you'd reject your preferred styling tool, and name the zero-runtime alternative you'd reach for instead?

**Resources:** MDN on `@layer` and `!important` for the cascade reasons underneath; the Tailwind documentation on theme variables and content detection; the styled-components and Emotion server-side rendering guides; the vanilla-extract documentation on zero-runtime extraction; caniuse for support levels.
---

## 3.2.12 Dark Mode and Theming
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

- **Detect the OS preference:** `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)`. **Tell the browser** your supported schemes with `color-scheme: light dark` (this styles native controls, scrollbars, and defaults), and `<meta name="color-scheme" content="light dark">`.
- **Token approach (variables):**
```css
:root { color-scheme: light dark;
        --bg: #fff; --fg: #111; --surface: #f5f5f5; --brand: #1a73e8; }
@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark) { :root:not([data-theme="light"]) { --bg:#0f1115; --fg:#e8eaed; --surface:#1a1d23; --brand:#8ab4f8; } }
[data-theme="dark"]  { --bg:#0f1115; --fg:#e8eaed; --surface:#1a1d23; --brand:#8ab4f8; }
body { background: var(--bg); color: var(--fg); }
/* or: color: light-dark(#111, #e8eaed);  (modern browsers) */
```
- **User toggle:** three states (system, light, dark), saved in `localStorage` and applied as `data-theme` on `<html>`.
- **Avoid the flash of wrong theme (FOUC):** apply the stored theme in an **inline `<script>` in `<head>`** before first paint, because waiting for React hydration flashes light mode. (Next.js: `next-themes` handles it, with `suppressHydrationWarning` on `<html>`.)
- **Design details:** don't just invert colors. Use dark gray rather than pure black (`#000` on white text causes halation), reduce saturation, re-check contrast ratios, lower image brightness or provide dark variants, and elevate surfaces with lighter tones rather than shadows.
- **Images and SVG:** use `currentColor`, or `<picture>` with `media="(prefers-color-scheme: dark)"`.
- **Related preferences:** `prefers-contrast`, `forced-colors` (Windows High Contrast, so don't rely on backgrounds for meaning, and use `outline` over `box-shadow` for focus).


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.7, 3.2.8, 3.2.10.

**Why interviewers ask:** It's a short question with a lot of correct answers, and it distinguishes candidates who have actually shipped dark mode from someone who once added a `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` block. The FOUC fix and the halation/elevation design points are what separate a good answer from a checklist.

**Definition:** Theme switching driven by `prefers-color-scheme`, the `color-scheme` property and `light-dark()`, with every colour expressed as a semantic custom-property token so light, dark and brand variants are one token map — plus the small inline script that applies the stored preference before first paint.
**Questions:** How do you implement dark mode with a user toggle and system default? Why does the page flash light on load and how do you fix it? What does `color-scheme` do? How do you structure tokens for multi-theme (brand A/B) support?


**Self-check:** Can you explain the three-state model, say what `color-scheme` does beyond cosmetics, and state exactly where the anti-flash script has to run?

**Resources:** MDN `prefers-color-scheme`, `color-scheme` and `light-dark()`; web.dev's "prefers-color-scheme" guide; the CSS Color Adjustment Module for the light/dark default surfaces; the WCAG 1.4.3 Contrast and 1.4.11 Non-text Contrast criteria; 3.7.5 for `forced-colors`.

**Mini task:** Build a three-state theme toggle on semantic tokens. Done-condition: with `dark` stored, a reload shows no flash — verify with CPU throttling and the Rendering panel's paint flashing — the native scrollbar and a `<select>` follow the theme, inline SVG icons flip via `currentColor`, and every text/background pair meets 4.5:1 in both themes (3:1 for large text and UI boundaries).

**Mistakes:**
- Using `#000` with `#fff` text in dark mode, which causes halation and measurable eye strain — use dark greys with off-white text and re-check every pair against WCAG 1.4.3 and 1.4.11.
- Hard-coding dark values on individual components instead of re-pointing the tokens, so a fourth theme or a per-tenant brand means touching every rule.
- Applying elevation with `box-shadow` in dark mode, where shadows are nearly invisible — lift surfaces with lighter tones instead.
- Forgetting `color-scheme`, so native form controls, scrollbars and the default canvas stay light inside a dark page.
- Ignoring `prefers-contrast` and `forced-colors`, so Windows High Contrast users get light-on-white; in forced-colors mode backgrounds are overridden by the system, so meaning has to come from `border` and `outline`.
- Gating dark mode behind `prefers-color-scheme` only, so there is no way to opt out of it.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`color-scheme` alone vs full tokens:** `color-scheme: light dark` gives correct native widgets and a matching default canvas almost free; explicit tokens give brand-controlled colour, which is what a design system needs, at the cost of defining and maintaining the token map.
- **Inline script before paint vs waiting for JS:** the inline script eliminates the flash but adds a render-blocking script in `<head>` and reads the stored value twice — once there, once in the app; setting the class during hydration is cleaner but flashes unless the server also knows the preference.
- **System default only vs system plus an explicit toggle:** honouring the OS alone is respectful and needs no code; a toggle is what users expect, and it costs persistence, an SSR-safe initial state and a hydration story.
- **`light-dark()` vs a duplicated token block:** `light-dark()` halves the declarations and keeps each pair adjacent, but needs a recent browser and a `color-scheme` to resolve against; duplicated blocks work everywhere and diff cleanly.

**Common wrong answers:** Inverting with `filter: invert(1) hue-rotate(180deg)`, which inverts images and any already-dark brand asset and produces wrong brand colours. Or treating `@media (prefers-color-scheme: dark)` on its own as the whole feature, with no tokens, no toggle and no `color-scheme`.

**Probe:** "Your page flashes white before going dark on reload. Why, and what's the smallest fix?" (Expect: the theme attribute is only applied after hydration, so first paint has already used the light tokens; read the stored preference and set `data-theme` on `<html>` from a blocking inline script in `<head>` that runs before the stylesheet is applied, and keep the server-rendered `<html>` consistent — otherwise hydration complains about the attribute mismatch, which is exactly what `suppressHydrationWarning` on `<html>` is for.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Semantic tokens** (`--bg`, `--fg`) rather than colour names, so a theme is a token map and not a rewritten stylesheet.
- `color-scheme` set, with a reason: native controls, scrollbars, form widgets, the default canvas, and `light-dark()`.
- The three-state model, and the `:root:not([data-theme='light'])` guard so an explicit override beats the OS preference.
- The inline-script-before-paint fix, plus design judgement: no pure `#000` behind pure `#fff` (halation), reduced saturation on large fields, elevation via lighter surfaces rather than shadows, images and SVG via `currentColor` or `<picture>`.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Three states: system, light, dark. I define semantic tokens — `--bg`, `--fg`, `--surface`, `--border`, `--brand` — on `:root` with `color-scheme: light dark`, so the browser also renders native controls, scrollbars and the default canvas to match. Then the media query sets the dark values, but I scope it to `:root:not([data-theme='light'])` so an explicit user choice beats the OS. `[data-theme='dark']` declares the same values. In JS a three-way toggle persists to `localStorage` and sets `data-theme` on `<html>`; no explicit choice means the attribute is absent and the media query governs. Modern browsers also have `light-dark()`, which collapses the two value lists into one declaration — `color: light-dark(#111, #e8eaed)`. And the flash: apply the stored theme from a tiny inline script in `<head>` that runs before first paint, because waiting for hydration is too late."
---

## 3.2.13 CSS Performance and Debugging
🟡 → 🔴 · **Good-to-know**

**Performance**
- **CSS is render-blocking:** the browser won't paint until the CSSOM is built. Keep critical CSS small (inline above-the-fold CSS), defer non-critical CSS, remove unused CSS, and minify.
- **Selector cost rarely matters** in modern browsers. Avoid micro-optimizing selectors, and focus on payload size, render-blocking, layout cost, and paint cost.
- **Layout cost:** large DOMs, expensive selectors with `:has()` on huge trees (rare), forced synchronous layout in JS, and `content-visibility: auto` for long pages.
- **Fonts:** `font-display: swap|optional`, preload key WOFF2, subset fonts, use variable fonts, and match fallback metrics (`size-adjust`) to reduce CLS.
- **Paint cost:** large blurs and shadows, `backdrop-filter`, and big `filter` effects are expensive.
- **`contain`** (`layout`, `paint`, `size`, `content`) isolates subtrees so changes inside don't invalidate the whole page.

**Debugging playbook**
1. Inspect the element → Styles panel for struck-out rules and the winning rule → Computed tab for the final value and where it comes from.
2. **Layout:** toggle Flex/Grid overlays, check the box model diagram, and look for unexpected `min-width: auto`, margin collapse, or a missing positioned parent.
3. **Overflow:** run `* { outline: 1px solid red }` to find what causes horizontal scroll (usually a fixed width or 100vw plus a scrollbar).
4. **Not applying:** is the selector matching (check the Elements tab)? Is the property valid (a yellow warning icon appears)? Is it inherited vs non-inherited? Is it overridden? Is there a typo?
5. **Mobile only:** device emulation, `dvh` vs `vh`, hover states, and safe areas.
6. **z-index problems:** find the stacking contexts (the Layers panel or inspect ancestors).
7. **Rendering panel:** paint flashing, layout-shift regions, and prefers-* emulation.


**Prerequisites:** 3.2.1, 3.2.9, 3.5.1.

**Why interviewers ask:** This is the senior section. They are checking whether you own a codebase's CSS health and debug systematically, rather than minifying harder or reaching for a bigger number. Good answers name measurable levers — bytes before first paint, recalculated style time, paint area — and reach for coverage tooling.

**Definition:** The two halves of CSS maintenance — keeping CSS off the critical path and out of the paint and layout budget (inline critical CSS, delete dead rules, contain work with `contain` and `content-visibility`), and the DevTools-driven procedure for diagnosing why a declaration is not applying, what causes overflow, and where the time is going.
**Questions**
1. How do you reduce render-blocking CSS?
2. What's critical CSS?
3. How do you find the element causing horizontal scroll?
4. How do you avoid layout shift from web fonts?
5. A 40KB stylesheet vs 400KB: what do you do? *(split by route, purge unused with coverage, and the utility or atomic approach)*


**Self-check:** Can you take a 400KB stylesheet, name the four changes you'd make in order, and say which DevTools panel proves each one worked?

**Resources:** web.dev's "Eliminate render-blocking resources", "Optimize CSS" and "Content-visibility" pages; MDN "Critical CSS" and `content-visibility`; the Chrome DevTools documentation for the Rendering, Layers and Coverage panels; 3.6.4 for eliminating CLS and 3.6.6 for fonts, images and CSS payloads.

**Mini task:** Take a real page and get a 400KB stylesheet under 60KB: record first paint, inline the above-the-fold CSS, `preload` the rest, run the Coverage tab and delete or split what never applies, then add `content-visibility: auto` with `contain-intrinsic-size` below the fold. Done-condition: LCP and FCP improve under throttled mobile, no route regresses, scrolling produces no layout-shift entries in the Performance panel, and the outline trick shows no unintended horizontal overflow at 320px.

**Mistakes:**
- Optimising selector specificity for performance when the real cost is bytes, layout and paint area.
- Inlining an entire stylesheet "to make it render sooner", which bloats the HTML and can delay the rest of the page.
- Adding `content-visibility: auto` without `contain-intrinsic-size` and shipping a page whose scrollbar jumps as you scroll.
- Preloading every font, which contends for bandwidth with the CSS and the LCP image and makes each one arrive later.
- Debugging by editing source and reloading instead of reading the Computed panel's winning declaration and its origin.
- Assuming the horizontal scrollbar proves a CSS problem when it is a long string in a form input that needs `min-width: 0`.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Inlining critical CSS vs loading everything render-blocking:** inlining removes a round trip and protects LCP, but duplicates CSS into every HTML response, has to be per-route, and needs a size budget; external CSS caches once and is simpler, at the cost of blocking first paint.
- **`content-visibility: auto` vs rendering everything:** it skips layout and paint for offscreen sections, which is a large win on long pages, but without `contain-intrinsic-size` the scrollbar jumps as you scroll, and it interacts with find-in-page and fragment navigation — `hidden="until-found"` exists precisely because of this class of problem.
- **`contain: content` vs careful scoping:** containment stops style and layout changes inside a subtree from invalidating the rest of the page, but it establishes a containing block and a stacking context and can break layouts that depended on the outside, so it needs measurement before and after.
- **Preloading vs `font-display: optional`:** preloading gets the font in early so the `swap` is imperceptible; `optional` removes CLS by never swapping late, but the user may never see the brand font on a slow first visit.
- **Coverage-based purging vs a stricter build:** purging catches dead rules in any architecture but breaks if class names are built dynamically; a utility or atomic pipeline eliminates the problem by construction at the cost of markup.

**Common wrong answers:** "Optimise the selectors" — selector cost is not the bottleneck in modern engines. Or "add `overflow-x: hidden` to `html`", which masks the overflow, breaks `position: sticky`, and leaves the layout wrong.

**Probe:** "A 400KB stylesheet is halving your LCP. What do you do, in order, and how do you verify each step?" (Expect: record which CSS actually applies before first paint; inline critical, `preload` the rest, drop `@import`; open the Coverage tab, delete or route-split what never applies; then re-measure with Lighthouse or a Performance trace and confirm no route regressed. Selector micro-optimisation would change nothing, and that is the point of the question.)

**What they evaluate:**
- The right levers: bytes before first paint, `@import`'s extra round trip, route splitting, coverage-based deletion — not "minify harder" or "shorten selectors".
- A correct view that selector matching is no longer the bottleneck, while layout, paint area and Recalculate Style time are.
- `content-visibility: auto` used with `contain-intrinsic-size`, with the scrollbar-jump caveat understood.
- A real debugging order: Elements → Styles (struck-through losers) → Computed (winning value and its source), then Layers and the Rendering panel for stacking, paint flashing and layout-shift regions.
- Fonts handled with `font-display`, preloading one or two faces, subsetting, and metric overrides rather than `font-display: optional` alone.

**Model answer, Q1:** "CSS is render-blocking because the browser won't paint until it has the CSSOM, so every byte on the critical path delays first paint. I inline the above-the-fold CSS in the head, then load the rest with `<link rel=\"preload\" as=\"style\" onload=\"this.rel='stylesheet'\">` plus a `<noscript>` fallback, and never use `@import`, which adds a second round trip. Then I delete the bytes nobody needs: route-level splitting and the Coverage panel or PurgeCSS to find rules that never match anything. Minify and compress at the server, and set `Cache-Control` so the non-critical part is free on repeat visits."
---

# MACHINE-CODING DRILLS (frequently asked live)

Build each in 20-30 minutes, with semantic HTML, keyboard support, and responsive behavior.

| # | Task | What it tests |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Center a div 5 ways | Flex, grid, absolute + transform, margin auto |
| 2 | Holy-grail layout | Grid areas, sticky footer, responsive collapse |
| 3 | Responsive navbar with a hamburger | Flex, media queries, `aria-expanded`, focus management |
| 4 | Card grid with equal heights | Grid `auto-fit`, flex column, `margin-top: auto` |
| 5 | Modal | `<dialog>`, focus return, backdrop click, Esc |
| 6 | Accordion (no JS, then with JS) | `<details name>`, ARIA, animations |
| 7 | Tabs | Roles `tablist/tab/tabpanel`, arrow-key navigation |
| 8 | Dropdown menu | Popover or portal, outside-click close, z-index |
| 9 | Tooltip | Positioning, `aria-describedby`, hover and focus |
| 10 | Toggle switch | Real checkbox, `:checked`, transition |
| 11 | Star rating | Radio inputs, `:has()`/sibling selectors, accessibility |
| 12 | Image carousel (CSS scroll-snap) | `scroll-snap-type`, controls, reduced motion |
| 13 | Skeleton loader | Gradient animation, `aria-busy` |
| 14 | Pure-CSS sticky table header | `position: sticky` + overflow constraints |
| 15 | Progress bar and spinner | `transform`-based animation |
| 16 | Multi-step form with validation | Constraint API, focus management, `aria-live` |
| 17 | Masonry-like gallery | CSS columns or grid + JS |
| 18 | Dark-mode toggle | Variables, `data-theme`, no flash |
| 19 | Responsive data table | Overflow wrapper or stacked cards |
| 20 | Text truncation (1 line and 3 lines) with "show more" | `line-clamp`, `aria-expanded` |

**Evaluation rubric:** semantics (2) · accessibility/keyboard (2) · responsiveness (2) · clean CSS (2) · edge cases such as long text, empty state, and RTL (2).

# PHASE 3.1-3.2 RAPID-FIRE (under 30 seconds each)

| # | Question | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Doctype purpose? | Standards mode vs quirks |
| 2 | `async` vs `defer`? | Unordered ASAP vs ordered after parse |
| 3 | Semantic HTML benefits? | A11y, SEO, built-in behavior, maintainability |
| 4 | Placeholder as label? | No. Use `<label>` |
| 5 | Why server validation? | Client can be bypassed |
| 6 | `alt=""` means? | Decorative, so it's skipped by screen readers |
| 7 | First rule of ARIA? | Use native elements first |
| 8 | AA contrast ratio? | 4.5:1 text, 3:1 large text and UI |
| 9 | `srcset` + `sizes`? | Browser picks the file by width and DPR |
| 10 | Prevent image CLS? | `width`/`height` or `aspect-ratio` |
| 11 | Don't lazy-load which image? | The LCP/hero image |
| 12 | `<dialog>.showModal()` gives? | Top layer, focus trap, Esc, backdrop |
| 13 | Canvas vs SVG? | Immediate pixels vs retained DOM nodes |
| 14 | Custom element name rule? | Must contain a hyphen |
| 15 | What pierces Shadow DOM? | Inherited props and custom properties |
| 16 | Specificity order? | Inline > ID > class/attr/pseudo-class > element |
| 17 | `:where()` specificity? | Always 0 |
| 18 | `border-box`? | Width includes padding and border |
| 19 | Margin collapse? | Adjacent vertical margins merge into the larger one |
| 20 | Create a BFC? | `flow-root`, overflow, float, abs/fixed, flex/grid items |
| 21 | `rem` vs `em`? | Root-relative vs element-relative |
| 22 | `100vh` problem on mobile? | The URL bar changes it, so use `dvh`/`svh` |
| 23 | Sticky not working? | Ancestor `overflow`, missing threshold |
| 24 | z-index not working? | Different stacking context |
| 25 | What creates a stacking context? | transform, opacity<1, positioned+z-index, isolation, filter… |
| 26 | `flex: 1` means? | `1 1 0%` |
| 27 | Flex child overflow fix? | `min-width: 0` |
| 28 | `align-items` vs `align-content`? | Items per line vs distribution of lines |
| 29 | `auto-fit` vs `auto-fill`? | Collapses empty tracks vs keeps them |
| 30 | Center with grid? | `place-items: center` |
| 31 | Mobile-first means? | Base = small, `min-width` queries upward |
| 32 | Container queries are for? | Components responding to their container's size |
| 33 | Logical properties? | `inline`/`block` instead of left/right, which makes RTL work |
| 34 | Cheap properties to animate? | `transform` and `opacity` |
| 35 | `will-change` risk? | Memory from extra layers, so use sparingly |
| 36 | CSS Modules scoping? | Build-time hashed class names |
| 37 | Why avoid runtime CSS-in-JS in RSC? | Runtime cost and server component friction |
| 38 | Why does dynamic Tailwind `bg-${x}` fail? | Static scanning needs full class names |
| 39 | Dark-mode flash fix? | Inline script in `<head>` before paint |
| 40 | Opacity 0 vs display none? | Still clickable and accessible vs removed |

**HTML**
- [ ] I can write a correct document skeleton and explain doctype, viewport, charset, and `lang`
- [ ] I can choose semantic elements and explain why `div`/`span` would be wrong
- [ ] I can explain `defer`/`async`/`module` and use preload, preconnect, and prefetch correctly
- [ ] I can build an accessible, validated form (labels, constraint API, aria-describedby, server errors)
- [ ] I know WCAG POUR, contrast ratios, keyboard and focus rules, and the first rule of ARIA
- [ ] I can set up SEO metadata, canonical, OG, and JSON-LD, and explain SPA SEO pitfalls
- [ ] I can implement responsive images with `srcset`/`sizes`/`<picture>` and prevent CLS
- [ ] I can use `<dialog>`, `<details>`, and popover natively
- [ ] I can write a Canvas animation loop with DPR scaling and time-based motion
- [ ] I can build a Web Component with Shadow DOM, slots, lifecycle, and events

**CSS**
- [ ] I can predict cascade winners and use specificity, `@layer`, and `:where()` deliberately
- [ ] I can explain the box model, margin collapse, BFC, and choose units confidently
- [ ] I can diagnose sticky and z-index failures (overflow, stacking contexts)
- [ ] I can build layouts with Flexbox and Grid, and explain `flex` shorthand, `min-width: 0`, `fr`, and `auto-fit`
- [ ] I can center anything at least five ways and build the common layout recipes from memory
- [ ] I can build mobile-first, fluid, container-query-driven, and RTL-safe layouts
- [ ] I can use variables, `:has()`, nesting, `clamp()`, and judge feature support safely
- [ ] I can explain which properties are cheap to animate and why, and respect reduced motion
- [ ] I can compare Sass, CSS Modules, BEM, Tailwind, and CSS-in-JS and defend a choice
- [ ] I can implement dark mode without a flash
- [ ] I completed at least 10 machine-coding drills with semantic, accessible, responsive results

**Resources for the whole phase:** javascript.info (primary), MDN, *You Don't Know JS Yet* (Kyle Simpson), *JavaScript: The Hard Parts* (Will Sentance), Jake Archibald's article "Tasks, microtasks, queues and schedules", Philip Roberts' talk "What the heck is the event loop anyway?", and 2ality / *Exploring JS* (Axel Rauschmayer).

# 3.3.1 Variables, Hoisting, and the TDZ
🟢 Beginner → 🟡 · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: 2.4.1


**Definition:** A *binding* is a name-to-cell link the parser creates; *hoisting* is the creation phase of an execution context instantiating every lexical declaration in scope before a single statement runs. `var` and function declarations arrive already initialised (`undefined` and the function respectively), while `let`, `const` and `class` arrive as bindings in an *uninitialised* state — touching one before its declaration throws, and that span is the TDZ.
**Why interviewers ask:** It's the standard opener. Answers reveal whether you understand the creation phase of execution contexts.

| | `var` | `let` | `const` |
|---|---|---|---|
| Scope | Function (or global) | Block | Block |
| Hoisted? | Yes, initialized to `undefined` | Yes, but **uninitialized** (TDZ) | Yes, but uninitialized (TDZ) |
| Redeclare in same scope | Allowed | SyntaxError | SyntaxError |
| Reassign | Yes | Yes | No (binding is fixed, the object is still mutable) |
| Global object property (script scope) | Yes (`window.x`) | No | No |
| Per-iteration binding in `for` loops | No (one shared binding) | Yes | Yes (but can't increment it) |

**Hoisting, precisely:** declarations are processed during the *creation phase*, before any code runs.
- **`var`:** the binding is created and initialized to `undefined`, and the assignment stays where you wrote it.
- **Function declarations:** the binding is created and initialized to the *whole function*. They're the most hoisted, and they win over a `var` of the same name until reassigned.
- **`let`/`const`/`class`:** the binding exists in scope from the start of the block but is **uninitialized**. Touching it before the declaration line throws `ReferenceError`. That span is the **Temporal Dead Zone (TDZ)**. This proves they *are* hoisted. If they weren't, an outer variable of the same name would be read instead, and it isn't:
```js
let x = 'outer';
{
  console.log(x);   // ReferenceError (the inner x shadows it, but is in TDZ)
  let x = 'inner';
}
```
- **Function expressions and arrows** follow their variable's rules: `var f = () => {}` hoists `f` as `undefined`, so calling it early gives `TypeError: f is not a function`.
- **`typeof` isn't safe in the TDZ:** `typeof undeclared` is `'undefined'`, but `typeof tdzVar` throws.
- **Default parameters** have their own TDZ: `function f(a = b, b) {}` throws when called as `f()`.

**Best practice:** default to `const`, use `let` when you reassign, never use `var`. Turn on ESLint's `prefer-const` and `no-var`.


**Prerequisites:** none — this is the entry point to JavaScript Core. 3.3.2 then formalises the two-phase context model this section depends on, and 3.3.3 explains the loop consequence.
**Questions**
1. `var` vs `let` vs `const`?
2. What is hoisting? Are `let` and `const` hoisted? *(model below)*
3. What is the Temporal Dead Zone?
4. Is `const` immutable? How would you make an object truly immutable? *(`Object.freeze` is shallow, so use a recursive freeze or Immer)*
5. Why does `var` in a loop with `setTimeout` print the same value? *(see 3.3.3)*
6. What's the difference between `undefined` and "not defined"? *(`undefined` is a value, "not defined" is a ReferenceError for an undeclared identifier)*
7. What happens when a function declaration and a `var` share a name?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Hoisting means the engine registers declarations when it creates the execution context, before running code. `var` is hoisted and initialized to `undefined`. Function declarations are hoisted with their full body. `let`, `const`, and `class` are hoisted too, meaning the name is reserved in the block, but they stay uninitialized until the declaration executes. Accessing them early throws a ReferenceError, and the window between block start and declaration is the TDZ."


**Trade-offs:**
- **`var` vs `let`/`const`:** `var` is function-scoped, so it leaks out of the block you wrote it in, one shared loop binding, and redeclaration is legal — forgiving in decade-old code, a bug factory in new code; `let`/`const` are block-scoped and per-iteration, so there is nothing to trade.
- **`const` vs `let`:** pure intent and lint (`prefer-const` catches the accident), no performance difference — pick `const` until you need to reassign.
- **`const` vs `Object.freeze`:** `const` prevents rebinding of one name, one level deep; `freeze` seals own properties, shallowly. Deep immutability needs a recursive freeze over a clone, or an immutable library like Immer. A `let x;` declared "for safety" is the worst of both: it trades a loud `ReferenceError` for a silent `undefined` that can propagate into logic.
- **Classic script scope vs module scope:** top-level `var` becomes a `window` property in a classic script but not in a module, and modules are always strict with real block scope — use modules for the scoping hygiene even in a no-bundler setup.
- **Named function declarations vs `const f = function`:** declarations are fully hoisted and therefore callable above their definition, which lets you order a file top-down; the `const` form is not hoisted, so ordering matters and you get a `TypeError` if you get it wrong. Prefer declarations for functions, `const` for everything else.

**Common wrong answers:** "Only `var` and functions are hoisted — `let`/`const` aren't." "Hoisted means initialised to `undefined`." "`const` makes objects immutable." Or describing the TDZ as an engine bug rather than the mechanism that makes `let` safe.

**Probe:** "What does `console.log(x); let x = 1;` print, and now what about `console.log(typeof y); let y = 1;` — why doesn't `typeof` save me the way it does for an undeclared name?" (Expect: `ReferenceError` both times; `typeof` only guards against *unresolvable* names, whereas a TDZ binding is resolvable-but-uninitialised, so the engine has to throw.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Binding vs value.** Strong answers talk about environment records and bindings, not "the declaration moves to the top". Only the declaration is hoisted; the assignment stays where you wrote it, which is why `console.log(x); var x = 1` logs `undefined`.
- **Hoisted and initialised treated as two separate facts.** They must say `let`/`const`/`class` *are* hoisted — that is the entire reason the TDZ exists — and that the TDZ is deliberate: it converts a silent wrong-value bug into a loud error at the declaration site.
- **Exact error types.** Reading before the declaration line is `ReferenceError`; *calling* a hoisted-but-unassigned function expression or `var` function is `TypeError: x is not a function`. Conflating the two signals pattern-matching.
- **That `typeof` is not TDZ-safe.** `typeof undeclared` is `'undefined'`; `typeof tdzVar` throws. Almost nobody volunteers this.
- **Block scoping and per-iteration bindings.** `let` in a `for`/`for...of`/`for...in` header creates a fresh binding per iteration, which is why the `setTimeout`-in-loop bug disappears without an IIFE — and that `const` freezes the binding, not the value (`const o = {}; o.x = 1` is legal).
**Mistakes:** saying "let/const aren't hoisted"; thinking `const` makes objects immutable; relying on function hoisting for readability.

**Mini task:** write 6 snippets that each demonstrate a different hoisting outcome (`undefined`, ReferenceError, TypeError, working call, shadowing in TDZ, `typeof` in TDZ), and predict each before running.


**Self-check:** Prove that `let` is hoisted using a shadowing example — outer `x`, inner block, read before the inner declaration — and explain what the `ReferenceError` proves.

**Resources:** MDN "Let" (lexical declarations, including `const`'s limitations), MDN "Hoisting" (the `var`/function/lexical behaviour table), MDN "Temporal dead zone"; ECMA-262 §8.2 "Declarative Environment Records" (`CanBeUninitialized`/`BindingInitialization`) and §14.3.1 "Runtime Semantics: Evaluation".
---

# 3.3.2 Execution Context, Call Stack, and Scope Chain
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** An *execution context* is the environment in which code is evaluated. The *call stack* tracks active contexts. The *scope chain* is how identifiers are resolved by walking outward through lexical environments.


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.1 (the creation phase *is* hoisting), 3.3.5 (the global environment and `Object.prototype` are what the chain ends at, and module wrapping is what makes module scope a function scope).

**Why interviewers ask:** This is the mental model every other JavaScript question hangs off: almost every "why does this print X" puzzle is this model plus one twist. It also separates people who memorised trivia from people who can reason about a language they haven't seen, and because it's platform-agnostic it tests whether they think about how code runs rather than about a framework.
**Key concepts**
- **Types:** global (created once, `this` = global object or `undefined` in modules), function (one per call), and eval (avoid).
- **Two phases per context:**
  1. **Creation:** build the environment (variable and function bindings, hoisting), determine `this`, and link the outer environment reference.
  2. **Execution:** run line by line and assign values.
- **Lexical environment** = an *environment record* (identifier → value map) + a reference to the *outer* environment. A function's outer reference is fixed **where the function is defined**, not where it's called. That is **lexical (static) scoping**.
- **Scope chain lookup:** resolve a name in the current environment, then its outer, and so on to global. If not found, a `ReferenceError` is thrown (or, in sloppy mode, an assignment creates an accidental global, one more reason to use strict mode and modules).
- **Scopes:** global, module, function, block (`let`/`const`/`class` inside `{}`), and catch-clause scope.
- **Shadowing:** an inner binding hides an outer one with the same name.
- **Call stack:** LIFO. `main()` → `a()` → `b()`. Each call pushes a frame and each return pops one. Exceeding the limit throws `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`. Async callbacks run later on an **empty** stack, which is why stack traces from async code can lose context.
- **Global object:** `globalThis` is the portable name (`window`, `self`, or `global`). Top-level `var` in a classic script becomes a property of it, but not in ES modules or Node CommonJS modules (which are function-wrapped).
- **Strict mode** (`'use strict'`, automatic in modules and classes): no accidental globals, `this` is `undefined` in plain function calls, duplicate parameter names are errors, and assigning to read-only properties throws.
- **Dynamic scoping contrast:** JS `this` behaves dynamically (depends on the call site), but variable lookup is lexical. Interviewers love this contrast.

```js
const a = 'global';
function outer() {
  const b = 'outer';
  function inner() {
    const c = 'inner';
    console.log(a, b, c);   // found via scope chain: inner → outer → global
  }
  return inner;
}
outer()();        // 'global outer inner'

function whereDefined() { return a; }
function caller() { const a = 'local'; return whereDefined(); }
caller();         // 'global': lexical scoping, not the caller's scope
```

**Questions**
1. What is an execution context? What are its phases?
2. What is the scope chain? Lexical vs dynamic scope?
3. What's the difference between scope and context? *(Scope = variable visibility and is lexical. Context = the value of `this`, determined at call time.)*
4. How does the call stack work? What is a stack overflow?
5. What is the global object? What's `globalThis`?
6. What does strict mode change?
7. What is shadowing? Is it a problem? *(Legal, but it can hide bugs. Linters can warn with `no-shadow`.)*
8. Why is the call stack empty when a `setTimeout` callback runs?


**Mistakes:**
- **Saying scope is dynamic.** The most damaging single error here; every follow-up about `this`, `call` or closures then compounds it.
- **Equating the call stack with the scope chain.** One is runtime frames with return addresses, the other a chain of environment records fixed at definition — mixing them makes async reasoning impossible.
- **Expecting an async callback to keep its caller on the stack,** so they put `try/catch` around `setTimeout` and wonder why the error escapes.
- **Forgetting that `var` in a nested block belongs to the enclosing function,** so they predict block behaviour and get function behaviour.
- **Omitting the third context type and `arguments`.** `eval` creates its own context, and sloppy-mode functions get an `arguments` object that aliases the parameters — naming both is what a careful answer does. Related: the global environment record is itself an object, which is why `globalThis` is the portable name for it.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`var` scoping vs block scoping:** function scope is forgiving — a declaration above its use still works, which is why huge ES5 codebases read top-down — but it leaks past `{}` and shares one loop binding; block scope gives you a TDZ that catches the bug and a fresh binding per iteration, at the cost of reordering code that used to rely on hoisting.
- **Bare `{}` blocks vs IIFEs for scoping:** a block is free and gives a scope plus a TDZ boundary, and it's readable; an IIFE also creates a private scope but costs a function call and a wrapper, and is the only option pre-ES2015 — reach for it when you need `var` isolation in legacy code or a deliberate module pattern.
- **Strict mode vs sloppy:** sloppy's implicit globals and default `this` are convenient in a console and actively dangerous in a library, since a typo silently corrupts global state; modules and class bodies are strict for free, so opting out has no upside.
- **Recursion vs an explicit stack:** recursion reads better on tree-shaped data but borrows one call frame per level, so a 10k-deep list throws `RangeError` and a cyclic one hangs; an array-as-stack moves that cost to the heap and gives you a bound and a resume point you control.
- **Function declarations vs arrows in this context:** declarations are hoisted so they can be called above their definition, which makes file order forgiving; arrows are not hoisted and bind `this` from the enclosing scope, so they're wrong for anything acting as a method or a constructor.

**Common wrong answers:** "Scope is dynamic, so a function sees its caller's variables." "A function shares one execution context and re-entering just reuses it." "Async code runs on a separate stack/thread inside JS." Or conflating *scope* (lexical name resolution) with *context* (`this`, fixed at call time).

**Probe:** "A name isn't found anywhere in the chain. What happens when you read it, and what's different about assigning to it in a sloppy classic script versus a module?" (Expect: `ReferenceError` on read either way; in sloppy code a bare assignment creates an implicit property on the global object, in a module or strict code it throws — which is the actual reason to use `'use strict'`, modules and class bodies.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Correct decomposition:** environment record + outer reference + `this` binding, the two phases, one context per call. They should say the global context is created once and that a script or module body is evaluated in it.
- **Lexical, not dynamic — stated and demonstrated.** The function-defined-here/called-there example is the whole test; candidates who get it wrong fail everything downstream, including `this`.
- **Consequences rather than slogans:** shadowing resolves to the inner binding, `var` in a nested block still belongs to the enclosing *function* scope, and an unresolved name in strict mode or a module throws instead of creating a global.
- **Module awareness:** modules are strict and function-wrapped, so top-level `this` is `undefined` and a top-level `var` is not a property of the global object — the detail that distinguishes people who have shipped code.
- **Empty stack for host-entered callbacks:** they explain it as the host re-entering the engine, not as "async runs on another thread".

**Model answer, Q1:** "An execution context is the environment a piece of code is evaluated in, and the spec tracks three things per context: the variable environment, which is an environment record mapping names to bindings; the outer reference, used for scope-chain lookups; and the `this` binding that code gets. Each context is built in two phases — creation, where every declaration is instantiated and hoisted and `this` is decided, then execution, where the statements run and values get assigned. Every function call pushes a new context onto the call stack; a return pops it, and a script or module evaluation starts one from nothing."
**Mini task:** draw the stack and the scope chain at the `console.log` in the example above, listing each environment record's contents.


**Self-check:** Draw the stack frames and the environment records at the `console.log(a, b, c)` above, naming each record's contents, the `this` value in each frame, and the order the name lookups happen in.

**Resources:** MDN "Execution context" and MDN "Lexical scope"; the scope-and-hoisting section of the V8 blog post "What the heck is a closure?" for an executable scope-chain walkthrough; ECMA-262 §10.2 "Environment Records" (Declarative, Object, Global, and the `HasBinding`/`GetBindingValue`/`CreateMutableBinding` operations) plus §9.4 "Jobs and Job Queues" for how a host-entered callback becomes a job.
---

# 3.3.3 Closures
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** (almost guaranteed)

**Definition:** A closure is a function bundled with references to the lexical environment where it was created, so it keeps access to those variables even after the outer function has returned.

**Why interviewers ask:** Closures underpin callbacks, modules, memoization, React hooks, and event handlers. They also explain the most famous JS gotchas.


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.2 (a closure *is* a retained outer environment reference), 3.3.1 (the per-iteration `let` binding is what makes the loop fix work without an IIFE).
**Key concepts**
- Every function in JS forms a closure. It becomes *interesting* when the function outlives its creator.
- Closures capture **variables (bindings), not values**. A closure sees later changes to the variable.
- **Uses:** private state and encapsulation, function factories and partial application, memoization and caching, once/debounce/throttle, the module pattern (IIFE), event handlers and callbacks, and iterators/generators.
- **Memory:** captured variables stay alive as long as the closure is reachable. A closure capturing a large object keeps all of it alive, so null out references or restructure when that matters.
- **React tie-in:** a **stale closure** happens when an effect or callback captures old state or props. Fixes are correct dependency arrays, functional updates (`setN(n => n + 1)`), and refs for latest-value access (Phase 4).

```js
// Private state
function createCounter() {
  let count = 0;                                  // not accessible from outside
  return { inc: () => ++count, get: () => count };
}
const c = createCounter(); c.inc(); c.inc(); c.get();   // 2

// Function factory
const multiplier = factor => n => n * factor;
const double = multiplier(2);

// Classic loop bug
for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) setTimeout(() => console.log(i), 0);   // 3 3 3 (one shared i)
for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) setTimeout(() => console.log(i), 0);   // 0 1 2 (new binding per iteration)
for (var j = 0; j < 3; j++) ((k) => setTimeout(() => console.log(k), 0))(j);  // 0 1 2 (IIFE copies the value)

// Stale closure (React-style)
function makeLogger() {
  let msg = 'a';
  const log = () => console.log(msg);
  msg = 'b';
  return log;
}
makeLogger()();   // 'b': captured the variable, not the value 'a'
```

**Questions**
1. What is a closure? Give a practical example. *(model below)*
2. Why does the `var` loop print 3 3 3? Three ways to fix? *(`let`, an IIFE, or passing the value via `bind` or a parameter)*
3. How do you create private variables in JS? *(closures, `#private` fields, `WeakMap`, and the underscore convention only as a hint)*
4. Can closures cause memory leaks? How? *(long-lived listeners or caches holding large captured scopes)*
5. What's a stale closure in React and how do you fix it?
6. Implement `once(fn)` and `memoize(fn)`. *(3.3.17)*
7. What's the module pattern? How did ES modules replace it?
8. Do closures capture by value or by reference? *(Bindings, i.e. by reference to the variable.)*

**Model answer, Q1:** "A closure is a function plus the lexical environment it was defined in, so it can read and write the outer function's variables after that function has returned. For example, `createCounter` returns `inc` and `get`. Both close over `count`. Nothing else can touch `count`, so it's private state with no class needed. It's how callbacks remember context, how memoize keeps its cache, and why React hooks can hold state between renders."


**Trade-offs:**
- **Closure-based privacy vs `#private` fields:** a closure works for any factory function and composes freely with partial application, but it's invisible to tooling, unfalsifiable from outside, and unusable on plain objects; `#x` is enforced by the engine, shows up in stack traces and DevTools, and only works on class instances.
- **Memoisation vs no cache:** caching converts repeated computation into a lookup, but you inherit cache invalidation, unbounded growth, and a closure allocation per call — for expensive pure functions with bounded keys it's a clear win, otherwise it's a leak.
- **Per-iteration `let` vs IIFE vs `bind`:** `let` in the header is the answer, with no allocation and value semantics preserved; an IIFE adds a call and an argument per iteration and `bind` allocates a bound function per iteration, so both exist only for ES5 targets — and note the async-iteration analogue, where a shared `var`-style cursor gives you the bug again.
- **One listener per node vs one delegated listener:** per-node closures are the obvious code and cost memory proportional to the list plus explicit teardown; a single listener on a stable ancestor is cheaper, survives nodes being replaced, and needs `event.target`/`closest` dispatch logic instead.
- **Closure state vs framework state (useState/ref):** closure state is renderer-agnostic, trivially unit-testable and fast, but invisible in devtools and not serialisable; framework state is inspectable, persisted and time-travelled, but coupled to the renderer and carries its own scheduling cost. A cache hoisted to module scope is shared by every caller and lives for the process lifetime, which is either the point or a multi-tenant leak.

**Common wrong answers:** "A closure is a function defined inside another function." "Closures capture by value" (or "by reference" in the sense of the object's *contents*, which is a different and wrong claim). "Closures cause memory leaks" with no reachability condition. Or naming only the loop example, with no production pattern behind it.

**Probe:** "A debounce helper closes over the search results array, and the debounced function is stored on `window` for a session-long widget. What exactly stays alive, what happens if a later query replaces that array, and how would you release it?"
