(Expect: the *binding* stays alive, so a later reassignment is visible to the old closure — that's why stale; the retained environment keeps the whole scope alive including anything large it references, and it can only be released by removing the reference from `window`, nulling the captured variable, or scoping the state so it isn't captured.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **"Function plus the environment it was defined in",** not "a nested function". Every function is a closure; it becomes interesting only when the function outlives the call that created it, which is also the precise condition for retention.
- **Bindings, not values.** The `makeLogger()` result printing `'b'` is the proof, and it's the question they'll use to check it — an answer of "it copies the value" guarantees the `var` loop bug comes out wrong.
- **Reachability as the memory model.** They can explain a leak as "a long-lived listener or cache keeps its captured environment, and that environment holds every variable in the scope chain above the closure, including the big one" — not "closures leak".
- **Range of patterns:** private state, partial application/currying, memoisation, `once`/`debounce`/`throttle`, the module pattern, event handlers, and generators/iterators each holding per-instance state.
- **Awareness of the React consequence:** a stale closure is an effect or callback reading a stale binding; the fixes are a correct dependency array, functional updates (`setN(n => n + 1)`) and refs for latest-value reads (Phase 4).
**Mistakes:** saying "a closure is a function inside a function" (incomplete); thinking closures copy values; creating closures in hot loops without considering memory.

**Mini task:** build a `createStore(initial)` with `getState`, `setState`, and `subscribe` using only closures (about 15 lines), then a `rateLimit(fn, n, ms)` that allows at most `n` calls per window.


**Self-check:** Explain, in terms of environment records rather than intuition, why `for (let i = 0; i < 3; i++) setTimeout(() => i)` prints 0 1 2 while the `var` version prints 3 3 3.

**Resources:** MDN "Closures" and the "Closures" section of MDN "Function"; the V8 blog post "What the heck is a closure?" (the `var`-loop walkthrough plus how to inspect retained scopes in DevTools); MDN "Memory management" for the reachability framing; `node:v8` `getHeapSnapshot()` / Chrome's "Retainers" view for proving a leak rather than guessing.
---

# 3.3.4 `this` Binding
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** `this` is determined by *how a function is called*, not where it's defined (except arrow functions, which capture it lexically).

**The five rules, in order of precedence**
1. **`new` binding:** `new Foo()` makes `this` the newly created object.
2. **Explicit binding:** `fn.call(obj)`, `fn.apply(obj, [args])`, `fn.bind(obj)` (a bound function can't be re-bound, except by `new`).
3. **Implicit binding:** `obj.method()` makes `this` the object left of the dot at call time.
4. **Default binding:** a plain `fn()` call gives `this` as the global object in sloppy mode, or `undefined` in strict mode, modules, and classes.
5. **Arrow functions:** no own `this`. They use the enclosing scope's `this` at *definition*, and `call`/`bind` can't change it.

**Details**
- **Losing `this`:** extracting a method (`const f = obj.method; f()`) or passing it as a callback (`setTimeout(obj.method)`, `arr.map(obj.method)`) drops implicit binding. Fix with `bind`, an arrow wrapper, or class-field arrows.
- **DOM event handlers:** a regular function gets `this === event.currentTarget`. Arrows don't.
- **`call` vs `apply` vs `bind`:** `call(ctx, a, b)` invokes now, `apply(ctx, [a, b])` invokes now with an array, and `bind(ctx, a)` returns a new function (supports partial application).
- **Arrows aren't suitable** as object methods needing `this`, as constructors (no `new`), as prototype methods, or when you need `arguments`.
- **Class fields with arrows** (`handle = () => {...}`) bind per instance, which is convenient for React class components but costs memory per instance and prevents prototype-level overriding.
- **`this` in nested functions:** a regular function nested inside a method has default binding, which is a classic bug. Use an arrow or `bind`.
- **In modules:** top-level `this` is `undefined`. In CommonJS it's `module.exports`.

```js
const user = {
  name: 'Ada',
  regular() { return this?.name; },
  arrow: () => this?.name,                 // `this` of the enclosing module scope, not user
  later() { return [1].map(() => this.name); },        // arrow keeps method's `this`
  laterBroken() { return [1].map(function () { return this?.name; }); } // default binding
};
user.regular();               // 'Ada'
const f = user.regular; f();  // undefined (strict: `this` is undefined, hence `?.`)
user.arrow();                 // undefined
user.later();                 // ['Ada']
user.laterBroken();           // [undefined]
user.regular.call({ name: 'Bob' });   // 'Bob'

function Person(n) { this.n = n; }
const bound = Person.bind({ n: 'ignored' });
new bound('Eve').n;           // 'Eve': `new` beats bind
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.2 (default binding, strict mode, and why an unbound call sees `globalThis` or `undefined`), 3.3.1 (why calling a hoisted-but-unassigned `const f = () => {}` is a `TypeError`, not a `ReferenceError`).

**Why interviewers ask:** `this` is the one part of JavaScript whose value you cannot see in the code that produces it — it lives in the *call expression*, not the declaration. That's a clean test of runtime reasoning versus pattern-matching, and it's the number one source of real bugs in React, Node callbacks and event handlers, so a strong answer comes with a bug story attached.
**Questions**
1. How is `this` determined? List the rules and their precedence. *(model below)*
2. `call` vs `apply` vs `bind`?
3. Why do arrow functions not have their own `this`? When should you not use them?
4. What's the output of `const f = obj.method; f()`? Why?
5. How does `this` work in event handlers? In classes? In `setTimeout`?
6. How would you fix `this` being lost in a React class component's handler? *(bind in the constructor, class-field arrow, or arrow in JSX with performance caveats)*
7. What does `new` do step by step? *(Create an object with `Ctor.prototype` as its prototype, call the constructor with `this` bound to it, and return the object unless the constructor returns another object.)*
8. Can you `bind` an arrow function? *(You can call it, but `this` doesn't change.)*

**Model answer, Q1:** "`this` is set at call time. If the call uses `new`, it's the new object. Otherwise, if the function was created with `bind` or invoked via `call`/`apply`, it's the specified object. Otherwise, if it's called as `obj.fn()`, it's `obj`. Otherwise it's the global object in sloppy mode or `undefined` in strict mode. Arrow functions are the exception: they don't get their own `this`, they use the one from where they were defined, so none of the other rules apply to them."


**Trade-offs:**
- **Prototype method + `bind` vs class-field arrow:** the field is shorter and always correctly bound but allocates per instance and removes prototype-level overriding and `Object.create`-based subclassing; prototype methods are shared, overridable and polyfillable, at the cost of remembering to bind. Libraries take the second; class components take the first for convenience.
- **Inline arrow in JSX vs `useCallback` vs `useRef`:** an inline arrow creates a new function every render and defeats `React.memo` on children; `useCallback` fixes identity but costs a hook and a deps array that must be right; a `useRef` holding the latest handler avoids both. Justify with profiling, not vibes.
- **`call`/`apply` vs `bind`:** `call`/`apply` invoke now against one receiver and keep a single function identity; `bind` returns a new function, which is what you need for partial application and for handing a bound callback to an API — but it allocates, and since ES5 it also changes `name` and `length`, which can confuse some libraries.
- **Strict mode vs sloppy default binding:** sloppy silently binds the global object, so a bug mutates global state instead of failing; strict surfaces it as `undefined` and gives you `undefined`-safe `?.` fallbacks. Modules and class bodies are strict for free — there's no scenario where opting out wins.
- **Object method shorthand vs a property holding a function:** identical at runtime, but shorthand gives a real `name` and a non-enumerable property, which matters for `JSON.stringify` and spread — a real trap when tests assume either behaviour. Where you don't control the caller, a per-call `fn.call(ctx)` at the boundary is more explicit than storing `const self = this` and hoping you remembered it in the callback.

**Common wrong answers:** "`this` comes from where the function is written." "Arrow functions have their own `this`, which is `undefined`." "`bind` still wins when you `new` the bound function." Ordering `bind` above `new`. Or "arrows are fine everywhere as long as you don't use them as constructors" — forgetting they also can't be prototype methods and have no `arguments`.

**Probe:** "`const { handler } = obj; setTimeout(handler, 0)` — what is `this` inside `handler`, why, and what's the smallest fix that doesn't allocate a closure per instance?" (Expect: `undefined` under strict/module code, because the call expression has no receiver; fix with `setTimeout(obj.handler, 0)` plus one `bind` at setup time — free, no per-instance property, and `obj.handler` keeps its original identity for every other consumer — versus an arrow class field, which is shorter but costs a closure and a property on every instance and makes the method impossible to override on the prototype.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Exact precedence, in order:** `new` → explicit (`call`/`apply`/`bind`) → implicit → default, with arrows exempt from all four. They must say `new` beats a prior `bind`, and that a second `bind` on a bound function is ignored.
- **The receiver is read at call time,** so extracting a method (`const f = obj.method`), passing it to `setTimeout`/`addEventListener`/`Array#map`/`forEach`/a promise `.then`, or destructuring it, all drop the receiver — with the arrow-wrapper, `bind`, and class-field fixes named.
- **Why arrows differ and where they break:** `this` is resolved lexically from the enclosing *definition* context; arrows have no own `this`, no `arguments`, no `prototype`, cannot be constructed, and `call`/`apply`/`bind` on one is a silent no-op — so they're wrong as object methods, prototype methods and constructors.
- **Environment specifics:** top-level `this` is `undefined` in a module and `module.exports` in CommonJS, a DOM handler's `this` is `currentTarget` (not `target`), class bodies are always strict so an unbound method gets `undefined` rather than the global, and arrow-wrapped handlers give you the module-scope `undefined`.
- **Cost awareness:** class-field arrows bind per instance — a closure and a property per object, unoverrideable from the prototype — versus a shared prototype method plus an explicit `bind` at the registration site.
**Mistakes:** assuming `this` is where the function was *written*; using arrows for object methods; forgetting that callbacks lose their receiver.

**Mini task:** implement `call`, `apply`, `bind`, and `new` from scratch (3.3.17), then write a table of 10 snippets with predicted `this` values.


**Self-check:** Given ten call expressions — bare, `obj.m()`, extracted, `m.call(x)`, `new C()`, inside an arrow, inside a nested `function`, in a class field, in a module top-level, in a DOM handler — state the receiver for each and name which of the rules decided it.

**Resources:** MDN "this" (including the `new`-over-`bind` note and arrow-function caveat); MDN "Function.prototype.bind"; TC39's "Arrow Function Definitions" proposal rationale (lexical `this`, no `arguments`, non-constructible, no `prototype`); the HTML spec's event-handler processing model for `this === currentTarget` versus `event.target`; MDN "Strict mode" for the sloppy/strict `this` differences.
---

# 3.3.5 Prototypes, Inheritance, and Classes
🟡 → 🔴 · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Every object has an internal `[[Prototype]]` link to another object (or `null`). Property lookup walks this *prototype chain*. Inheritance in JS is delegation along this chain.


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.2 (environment records versus objects on the heap; module scope and strictness), 3.3.4 (`new` is the implicit-binding rule, and `super` needs it), 3.3.1 (`class` declarations have a TDZ).

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the bridge between "I use classes" and "I know what a class *is*", and it carries three production concerns in one place: shared mutable state on a prototype, hidden-class deoptimisation, and prototype pollution — the last of which is a security question they like to put to front-end engineers.
**Key concepts**
- **`obj.__proto__`** (legacy accessor) = `Object.getPrototypeOf(obj)`. **`Fn.prototype`** is a property on *functions*: the object that becomes the `[[Prototype]]` of instances created with `new Fn()`. Mixing these up is the classic mistake.
```
const d = new Dog();
d ──[[Prototype]]──▶ Dog.prototype ──▶ Animal.prototype ──▶ Object.prototype ──▶ null
```
- **Lookup:** read `d.speak` → own properties → each prototype upward → `undefined`. **Writes** create *own* properties (shadowing), except for setters and non-writable inherited properties.
- **`Object.create(proto)`** builds an object with that prototype. `Object.create(null)` gives a dictionary with no prototype (safe from key collisions such as `"toString"` and from prototype pollution).
- **Own vs inherited:** `Object.hasOwn(obj, 'k')` (modern, instead of `obj.hasOwnProperty`), `Object.keys` (own enumerable), `for...in` (own and inherited enumerable, so filter or avoid).
- **Constructor functions:**
```js
function Animal(name) { this.name = name; }
Animal.prototype.speak = function () { return `${this.name} makes a sound`; };

function Dog(name) { Animal.call(this, name); }                  // super constructor
Dog.prototype = Object.create(Animal.prototype);                  // link chain
Dog.prototype.constructor = Dog;                                  // repair constructor
Dog.prototype.speak = function () { return `${this.name} barks`; };
```
- **ES6 classes are syntactic sugar over this**, with differences: `class` declarations have TDZ, class bodies are strict, methods are non-enumerable, calling a class without `new` throws, and `extends` also links the *constructors* (`Dog.__proto__ === Animal`) so statics inherit.
```js
class Animal {
  #energy = 10;                          // private field (truly private)
  static count = 0;                      // static field
  constructor(name) { this.name = name; Animal.count++; }
  speak() { return `${this.name} makes a sound`; }
  get energy() { return this.#energy; }
  static create(n) { return new this(n); }
}
class Dog extends Animal {
  constructor(name) { super(name); }     // must call super before using `this`
  speak() { return `${super.speak()}, barks`; }
}
```
- **Methods live on the prototype (shared)**, while class-field arrow functions live on each instance (not shared).
- **`instanceof`** checks whether `Ctor.prototype` appears in the object's chain. `isPrototypeOf` is similar. Customize it with `Symbol.hasInstance`. It can fail across realms (iframes), so prefer `Array.isArray`.
- **Inheritance vs composition:** deep hierarchies are brittle ("gorilla/banana problem"). Prefer small factories, mixins, and composition: `const canFly = s => ({ fly: () => ... })`.
- **Performance:** engines optimize objects with stable "shapes" (hidden classes). Adding properties in different orders or deleting them can degrade inline caches. Initialize all fields in the constructor in the same order.
- **Prototype pollution (security):** `obj.__proto__.isAdmin = true` or a malicious merge of `{"__proto__": {...}}` can alter *all* objects. Defenses: validate keys, use `Object.create(null)` or `Map`, `Object.freeze(Object.prototype)` in hardened environments, and keep merge libraries patched (Phase 7).
- **Property enumeration order:** integer-like keys ascending, then string keys in insertion order, then symbols.
- **Mixins:** `const Serializable = Base => class extends Base { toJSON() {…} }`.

**Questions**
1. Explain the prototype chain. What's the difference between `__proto__` and `prototype`? *(model below)*
2. How does inheritance work without classes? Show with `Object.create`.
3. Are ES6 classes "real" classes? What differs from Java? *(Sugar over prototypes. No static typing and no true multiple inheritance. Private fields are the exception, being truly private.)*
4. What does `new` do? Implement it.
5. What does `super` do in constructors and methods?
6. How do you check if a property is own or inherited?
7. `Object.create(null)` use cases?
8. Class method vs arrow class field: trade-offs?
9. Composition vs inheritance: how do you choose?
10. What is prototype pollution and how do you prevent it?
11. What's `Object.setPrototypeOf` and why is it discouraged? *(It deoptimizes performance.)*
12. How can you extend built-ins like `Array` or `Error`? *(`class MyErr extends Error` works natively, set `name`, and use `Error.captureStackTrace` in V8)*

**Model answer, Q1:** "Every object has a hidden link to a prototype object, and property reads walk that chain until found or until `null`. `prototype` is a property on constructor *functions*. When you call `new Fn()`, the new object's `[[Prototype]]` is set to `Fn.prototype`. `__proto__` (or `Object.getPrototypeOf`) is how you read an object's actual link. So `dog.__proto__ === Dog.prototype`. Methods put on the prototype are shared by all instances, which is memory-efficient, and `class` is just nicer syntax for that."


**Trade-offs:**
- **Inheritance vs composition:** a hierarchy is terse for genuine is-a relationships and gives you polymorphism through `super`, but subclasses couple to base internals and the fragile-base-class problem appears at depth three; composition and mixins keep the graph flat and let you attach behaviour per instance, at the cost of more delegation plumbing.
- **Prototype methods vs instance fields:** prototype methods are shared and cheap but need a correct receiver; instance fields (especially arrows) are self-contained and pre-bound but multiply memory per instance, hide the method from prototype overriding, and defeat `Object.create`-based cloning.
- **`#private` vs `_underscore` vs `WeakMap`:** `#` is enforced by the engine, invisible outside the class body and shows up in stack traces, but only exists on classes; `WeakMap` gives equivalent encapsulation for plain objects and factory functions (entries vanish with the key) at the cost of a lookup per access; the underscore is a convention with zero runtime protection.
- **`Object.create(null)` vs `{}`:** a null-prototype dictionary cannot collide with `Object.prototype` members and is immune to prototype pollution, but it has no `toString`/`hasOwnProperty`, so any library that assumes an object prototype will need `Object.prototype.hasOwnProperty.call` or your own helpers — great for user-supplied key/value maps, wrong for general DTOs.
- **`instanceof` vs brand checks:** `instanceof` walks a chain, fails across realms (iframes) and is spoofable via `Symbol.hasInstance`; `Array.isArray`, `Number.isNaN` and `Object.prototype.toString.call(x)` are internal-brand checks that survive realm boundaries and proxies. Note that hand-rolled `Object.create(Animal.prototype)` with a repaired `constructor` is exactly what `extends` compiles to, minus `super`, static inheritance and private fields.

**Common wrong answers:** "`__proto__` and `prototype` are the same thing." "Classes copy methods onto every instance." "Assigning to an inherited property changes the object on the prototype." "`instanceof` is the reliable array check." Or "private fields are enforced by naming convention."

**Probe:** "`class Dog {}; const d = new Dog(); d.bark = () => {}` — what does `for...in` show, what changes if `bark` is a `class` method, and what breaks if I put `log = []` on `Dog.prototype`?" (Expect: the assigned function becomes an own *enumerable* property and appears in `for...in`; class methods are non-enumerable so they never appear; and a field initialised on the prototype is one object shared by every instance, so `d.log.push(1)` is visible from every other dog — the classic prototype-pollution-of-your-own-class bug.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **`__proto__` vs `prototype`, precisely:** `[[Prototype]]` is an internal slot on every object; `Fn.prototype` is an ordinary property on a *function*; `Object.getPrototypeOf(o)` reads the slot, `Object.getPrototypeOf(Fn)` reads the property. `new` copies the *value* of `Fn.prototype` into the new object's `[[Prototype]]`, and never copies properties.
- **Read vs write semantics:** reads walk the chain upward and stop at the first hit; writes create own properties (shadowing), with two exceptions — a setter on the prototype is invoked instead, and a non-writable inherited data property silently fails in sloppy mode and throws in strict. Also `Object.defineProperty` as the deliberate way to write through.
- **That `class` is sugar with real differences:** TDZ, always-strict bodies, non-enumerable methods, `TypeError` without `new`, `extends` linking constructors via `[[Prototype]]` so statics and `super`-static calls inherit, and `#private` being genuinely private rather than conventional.
- **Performance and tooling awareness:** stable hidden classes, initialise fields in a fixed order and don't `delete`, methods shared on the prototype versus arrow fields copied per instance, `Object.setPrototypeOf` deoptimising existing objects, `in`/`for...in`/`Object.keys` walking different sets, and why `Array.isArray` beats `instanceof` across realms.
- **Security:** they can name the vectors (`__proto__` assignment, `constructor.prototype` in a recursive merge, `JSON.parse` with a reviver) and the defences (reject `__proto__`/`constructor`/`prototype` keys, `Object.create(null)` dictionaries or `Map`, `Object.freeze(Object.prototype)`, and patched merge libraries).
**Mistakes:** confusing `prototype` and `__proto__`; putting mutable arrays or objects on the prototype (shared across instances); arrow functions as prototype methods; forgetting `super()` before `this`.

**Mini task:** implement the same hierarchy three ways (constructor+prototype, `Object.create`, `class`), then demonstrate shadowing and shared-state bugs. Write a `deepMerge` that is safe against `__proto__`.


**Self-check:** Given `class Dog extends Animal {}`, explain why `Dog.__proto__ === Animal`, what `Object.getPrototypeOf(new Dog())` is, and where a static method defined on `Animal` becomes reachable from.

**Resources:** MDN "Inheritance and the prototype chain" and MDN "Object.prototype"; MDN "Private properties" (`#x` semantics and the brand-check failure `TypeError`); the V8 blog posts "Optimizing property access with prototypes" and "Fast properties" for hidden classes and inline caches; the OWASP "Prototype Pollution" cheat sheet and DOMPurify/`Object.create(null)` guidance; ECMA-262 §10.1.1 for `[[Prototype]]`, `OrdinarySetPrototypeOf` and the `Set`/`DefineOwnProperty` receiver rules.
---

# 3.3.6 The Event Loop: Tasks and Microtasks
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** (guaranteed at most companies)

**Definition:** The mechanism that lets single-threaded JavaScript handle asynchronous work by queuing callbacks and running them when the call stack is empty.

**The model**
```
          ┌───────────────┐   Web APIs / OS / libuv (timers, network, I/O, DOM events)
          │  Call Stack   │ ◀──────────────────────────────┐
          └──────┬────────┘                                │ completed work enqueues callbacks
                 │ empty?                                  │
     ┌───────────▼────────────┐   ┌─────────────────┐     │
     │ 1. Run ONE macrotask   │──▶│ 2. Drain ALL    │─────┘
     │   (script, timer, I/O, │   │   microtasks    │
     │    UI event, message)  │   │   (promises,    │
     └────────────────────────┘   │   queueMicrotask)│
                 ▲                └────────┬────────┘
                 │                         ▼
                 │          3. (Browser) maybe RENDER: rAF callbacks → style → layout → paint
                 └─────────────────────────┘ repeat
```

**Rules**
- **Macrotasks (tasks):** the initial script, `setTimeout`/`setInterval`, UI/DOM events, network callbacks, `MessageChannel`, and `setImmediate` (Node).
- **Microtasks:** Promise reactions (`.then/.catch/.finally`, and the continuation after `await`), `queueMicrotask`, and `MutationObserver`.
- After each macrotask, the engine runs **all** microtasks until the queue is empty, *including newly added ones*, before the next macrotask or a render.
- **Consequences**
  - A promise callback always runs **before** a `setTimeout(0)` callback scheduled in the same turn.
  - **Microtask starvation:** an endless chain of microtasks blocks rendering and timers.
  - **Long tasks (over 50ms)** block input and rendering. That is jank, and INP suffers.
- **`setTimeout(fn, 0)` isn't immediate:** it's the *minimum* delay. Browsers clamp nested timers to about 4ms after 5 levels, throttle background tabs, and the callback only runs when the stack is free. Timer drift is normal.
- **Rendering:** the browser may render between tasks (typically at display refresh rate). `requestAnimationFrame` callbacks run right before paint, so use them for visual updates.
- **Breaking up long work:** chunk with `setTimeout` or `scheduler.postTask`/`scheduler.yield()` (where supported), `requestIdleCallback` for low-priority work, Web Workers for CPU-heavy work, and virtualization to reduce DOM work.

**Node.js event loop (libuv) phases**
```
┌─▶ timers (setTimeout/setInterval callbacks)
│   pending callbacks (some system errors)
│   idle, prepare (internal)
│   poll (retrieve new I/O events; run I/O callbacks; may block waiting)
│   check (setImmediate callbacks)
└── close callbacks (socket.on('close'))
```
- Between each callback (Node 11+), Node drains **`process.nextTick` queue first, then promise microtasks**.
- **`setImmediate` vs `setTimeout(0)`:** from the main module the order is non-deterministic (depends on process performance). *Inside an I/O callback*, `setImmediate` always runs first.
- **`process.nextTick` vs `Promise.then`:** in CommonJS, nextTick wins. In **ES modules** the top-level code itself runs inside a promise job, so promise callbacks run *before* nextTick.
- **Blocking the loop:** a sync `for` over millions of items, `JSON.parse` of a huge payload, sync crypto, or a catastrophic regex stalls *all* requests. Fix with `worker_threads`, streaming and chunking, a job queue, or another service.
- **libuv thread pool** (default 4 threads) handles file system, DNS lookup (`dns.lookup`), crypto (`pbkdf2`, `scrypt`), and zlib. Network I/O uses OS async primitives (epoll/kqueue/IOCP), not the pool.

```js
console.log('1 script start');
setTimeout(() => console.log('7 timeout'), 0);
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('4 promise 1'))
                 .then(() => console.log('6 promise 2'));
queueMicrotask(() => console.log('5 microtask'));
(async () => { console.log('2 async start'); await null; console.log('?? after await'); })();
console.log('3 script end');
// Actual: 1 script start, 2 async start, 3 script end,
//         4 promise 1, 5 microtask, ?? after await, 6 promise 2, 7 timeout
// (microtask queue order: promise-1, queueMicrotask, await continuation, then promise-2 queued after promise-1 runs)
```
```js
// Node
setTimeout(() => console.log('timeout'), 0);
setImmediate(() => console.log('immediate'));
process.nextTick(() => console.log('tick'));
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('promise'));
// CJS: tick, promise, then timeout/immediate (order not guaranteed)
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.2 (the stack must be empty before a queued job runs — that's the whole admission condition, and it's why host-entered callbacks start with an empty stack), 3.3.1 (whether the initial script runs as a job matters: in an ES module the top-level code is itself inside a promise job).

**Why interviewers ask:** It's near-universal and it has a small, exactly-correct core that's easy to fake — plenty of people say "promises run before timers" without being able to walk two queues. It's also the question that separates front-end from back-end candidates, because Node's phase model and `nextTick` have their own correct answer, and it's the frame under which real performance complaints (jank, INP, blocked servers) get diagnosed.
**Questions**
1. Explain the event loop. *(model below)*
2. Microtask vs macrotask? Give examples of each.
3. What's the output of the snippet above? Walk through it.
4. Why is `setTimeout(fn, 0)` not instant?
5. How can a promise chain freeze the UI? *(infinite microtask loop, or a heavy synchronous `.then` callback)*
6. How would you process 1 million items without freezing the browser? *(chunk via `setTimeout`/`scheduler.yield`, `requestIdleCallback`, a Web Worker, virtualization)*
7. `process.nextTick` vs `setImmediate` vs `setTimeout(0)` vs `Promise.then` in Node?
8. If JS is single-threaded, how does Node handle concurrency? *(non-blocking I/O delegated to the OS or libuv, with callbacks queued)*
9. What happens when an `await` is hit? *(The function pauses, control returns to the caller, and the continuation is queued as a microtask when the awaited promise settles.)*
10. Where does `requestAnimationFrame` fit in? 
11. How do you detect and debug event-loop blocking in Node? *(`perf_hooks.monitorEventLoopDelay`, `clinic.js`, `--cpu-prof`, and lag metrics in APM)*
12. What's the difference between concurrency and parallelism in JS? 

**Model answer, Q1:** "JavaScript runs on one thread with a call stack. Async operations like timers, network requests, and DOM events are handled by the host environment, and when they finish they place callbacks in queues. The loop takes one macrotask, runs it to completion, then drains every microtask, meaning promise callbacks and `queueMicrotask`, then in a browser may render, and repeats. That's why promise callbacks beat `setTimeout(0)`, and why long synchronous work blocks everything: nothing can run until the stack is empty. In Node the same idea applies with libuv phases, plus `nextTick` and promise queues between callbacks."


**Trade-offs:**
- **`queueMicrotask` vs `setTimeout(fn, 0)` for deferral:** `queueMicrotask` runs at the end of the current task, before any rendering or I/O, which is right for a short urgent continuation and catastrophic in a loop (total starvation, no paint, no input); `setTimeout` yields to the host, which is right for chunking long work and lets rendering and I/O proceed, at the cost of a clamped, non-deterministic delay and a minimum turn cost per chunk.
- **`setTimeout` chunking vs `scheduler.yield()`/`postTask`:** timers are universally supported but carry no priority and clamp; `scheduler.yield()` resumes at task priority so input and rendering stay responsive and ordering is more predictable; `postTask({ priority: 'background' | 'user-blocking' })` is explicit and future-facing but newer and less widely supported — use timers as the fallback. `requestIdleCallback` is the other option: it hands you the leftover time in a frame (`timeRemaining()`) and yields to input automatically, but availability and the deadline are inconsistent and a busy page may never call it.
- **`requestAnimationFrame` vs `setTimeout` for visual updates:** rAF is synchronised to the frame and runs before style/layout/paint, so a change lands in the same frame and reads consistent geometry; a timer drifts against the display, can tear or skip entirely on a slow frame, and gives you no guarantee of painting before the next repaint. For non-visual low-priority work use `requestIdleCallback`/`postTask` instead of rAF.
- **Microtasks vs `setImmediate` in Node:** a microtask runs before the loop leaves the current tick, which is too eager for I/O-bound code — a self-rescheduling microtask loop starves timers and I/O entirely; `setImmediate` defers to the check phase, so the loop reaches the poll phase and drains I/O first.
- **Main thread vs Web Worker vs `worker_threads`:** workers give true parallelism with no shared heap (structured clone, or transferables for zero-copy buffers) and are the correct answer for CPU-bound parsing, image work or heavy crypto; they cost message latency, a duplicated dependency graph, and no DOM access. In Node, offloading to workers or a separate process is how you stop one tenant's synchronous loop from stalling every request.

**Common wrong answers:** "JavaScript is asynchronous" (the language is synchronous with a synchronous call stack; the *host* provides asynchrony). "Promise callbacks run immediately if the promise is already resolved." "Microtasks always beat timers" stated without *when* the drain happens. "An infinite `setTimeout` loop freezes the page just like an infinite `.then` loop" (timers yield; microtasks don't). "Node uses a thread pool for network I/O."

**Probe:** "Why does `setTimeout(fn, 0)` sometimes land 4ms after you asked for zero, and inside a Node I/O callback why is `setImmediate` guaranteed to run before `setTimeout(0)` while from the main module the order is non-deterministic?" (Expect: the delay is a floor, not a promise — HTML clamps timers to 4ms once nesting level exceeds five and throttles background tabs, and the callback only runs on a future turn; and `setImmediate` lives in the check phase, which the loop reaches before timers next iteration, whereas from the main module you're racing the timers phase against an empty poll and it depends on how long startup took — so never depend on that order in either place.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **One task, then drain the microtask queue completely** — including microtasks enqueued by microtasks — then (browser only) consider rendering, then repeat. Not the vague slogan "microtasks have priority".
- **They apply the rule correctly, including the counter-intuitive cases:** a `.then` registered in the initial script runs before a `setTimeout(fn, 0)` registered in that same script; an `await null` continuation is a microtask and resumes before any timer; but a promise resolved *from inside* a timer callback only runs after that whole task finishes.
- **Correct classification:** promise reactions, `queueMicrotask`, `MutationObserver` and `await` continuations are microtasks; `setTimeout`/`setInterval`, I/O completions, DOM events, `MessageChannel`/`postMessage`, `MessagePort` and `scheduler.postTask` are tasks; `requestAnimationFrame` runs in the rendering steps, which is neither queue, and `requestIdleCallback` is an idle-callback task.
- **They know the failure modes:** an unbounded microtask chain blocks rendering and input entirely (a frozen tab with a responsive-looking network), whereas chained `setTimeout`s yield between iterations; tasks over 50ms register as Long Tasks and degrade INP.
- **Node specifics:** libuv's timers → pending callbacks → idle/prepare → poll → check → close phases, `process.nextTick` drained before promise microtasks between every callback since Node 11, `setImmediate` guaranteed to beat `setTimeout(0)` inside an I/O callback but non-deterministic from the main module, the ESM top-level inversion, and the 4-thread libuv pool for fs/DNS/crypto/zlib (`UV_THREADPOOL_SIZE`).
**Mistakes:** saying "JS is asynchronous" (the language is synchronous, the environment provides async); thinking `setTimeout(0)` runs "right after"; not knowing microtasks fully drain first; blocking Node's loop with sync APIs.

**Mini task:** write 10 ordering puzzles mixing `setTimeout`, promises, `async/await`, and `queueMicrotask`. Predict, then run. Also, block the Node loop with a CPU loop and measure `/health` latency with autocannon, then fix it with a worker.


**Self-check:** Predict the exact output order for a snippet mixing `setTimeout`, two chained `.then`s, `queueMicrotask`, and an `await`, and justify each position by naming the queue it waited in and when that queue was drained.

**Resources:** MDN "The event loop", MDN "Using promises" (the microtask queue section) and MDN "Microtask queue"; the HTML Living Standard's event-loop processing model, its rendering-opportunity steps, and the timers section (nesting-level clamping to 4ms); Node's "The Node.js event loop, timers, and `process.nextTick()`" plus `perf_hooks.monitorEventLoopDelay` and the libuv thread-pool docs; the W3C Long Tasks / Event Timing (INP) specs.
---

# 3.3.7 Promises
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** An object representing the eventual completion (fulfilled) or failure (rejected) of an async operation, and its resulting value.


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.6 (handlers are microtasks, which is the entire reason nothing runs synchronously), 3.3.4 (a bare callback passed to `.then` loses its receiver — the reason `promise.then(obj.handler)` is a bug).

**Why interviewers ask:** Promises are the substrate under every async API in the stack — `fetch`, Node streams, bundler plugins, React data fetching — and interviewers are looking for whether a candidate can reason about *when* a handler runs and *which* error it catches. It's also where the production experience shows: floating promises, unhandled rejections killing a Node process, sequential awaits in a loop.
**Key concepts**
- **States:** *pending → fulfilled* or *pending → rejected*. A settled promise **never changes** state or value (immutable once settled).
- **`.then(onFulfilled, onRejected)`** returns a **new promise**. The returned value resolves it, a returned *promise/thenable* is adopted (flattened), and a thrown error rejects it. **Callbacks always run asynchronously (as microtasks)**, even on already-resolved promises.
- **`.catch(fn)`** = `.then(undefined, fn)`. **`.finally(fn)`** runs on either outcome, gets no argument, and passes through the original value or error (unless `finally` throws).
- **Chaining and error propagation:** a rejection skips `.then` fulfillment handlers until a rejection handler is found. After `.catch` returns normally, the chain *recovers*.
- **`.then(a, b)` vs `.then(a).catch(b)`:** in the first, `b` won't catch errors thrown in `a`. In the second, it will.
- **Constructor:** `new Promise((resolve, reject) => {...})` runs the executor **synchronously**. Throwing inside it rejects. Only the first `resolve`/`reject` counts.
- **Static helpers**

| Method | Resolves when | Rejects when | Use for |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Promise.all([…])` | All fulfill (results array in input order) | **First** rejection (fail-fast) | Independent tasks that must all succeed |
| `Promise.allSettled` | All settle (`{status, value\|reason}`) | Never | Batch where partial failure is OK |
| `Promise.race` | **First to settle** | First to settle if it rejects | Timeouts |
| `Promise.any` | **First fulfillment** | All reject (`AggregateError`) | Fastest-mirror / fallback |
| `Promise.resolve(v)` / `reject(e)` | Wrap a value or error | | Normalize |
| `Promise.withResolvers()` (ES2024) | Returns `{promise, resolve, reject}` | | Externally controlled promises |

- **Unhandled rejections:** browsers fire `unhandledrejection`, and **Node (v15+) crashes the process by default**. Always handle or `catch`, and log with context.
- **Thenables:** any object with a `.then` method is adopted by `resolve`, which is how libraries interoperate.
- **Anti-patterns**
  - **Explicit construction:** wrapping an existing promise in `new Promise` (`new Promise(res => fetch().then(res))`). Just return the promise.
  - **Forgetting to `return` inside `.then`:** the chain doesn't wait.
  - **Nesting `.then` pyramids** instead of flat chains.
  - **`async` inside `forEach`:** `forEach` ignores returned promises (see 3.3.8).
  - **Swallowing:** `.catch(() => {})` hides bugs.
- **Promisify callbacks:**
```js
const promisify = fn => (...args) =>
  new Promise((resolve, reject) => fn(...args, (err, val) => (err ? reject(err) : resolve(val))));
// Node: util.promisify, fs/promises
```
- **Cancellation:** promises can't be cancelled. Use `AbortController` (supported by `fetch`, many Node APIs, and your own functions) to cancel the underlying work.
```js
const ac = new AbortController();
fetch(url, { signal: ac.signal }).catch(e => { if (e.name !== 'AbortError') throw e; });
ac.abort();
fetch(url, { signal: AbortSignal.timeout(5000) });   // built-in timeout signal
```

```js
Promise.resolve(1)
  .then(v => { throw new Error('boom'); })
  .then(v => console.log('skipped'))
  .catch(e => { console.log('caught', e.message); return 'recovered'; })
  .then(v => console.log(v))          // 'recovered'
  .finally(() => console.log('cleanup'));
```

**Questions**
1. What is a promise? What are its states?
2. How does promise chaining work? What does `.then` return?
3. `Promise.all` vs `allSettled` vs `race` vs `any`? *(model below)*
4. What happens if you don't handle a rejection?
5. `.then(a, b)` vs `.then(a).catch(b)`?
6. Does the executor run synchronously? Do `.then` callbacks?
7. How do you cancel a promise or request?
8. What's the difference between a callback and a promise? Callback hell? Inversion of control? *(promises give composition, central error handling, and guaranteed single, async settlement)*
9. How would you add a timeout to a promise? *(`Promise.race` with a timer, and clear the timer. Better: `AbortSignal.timeout`.)*
10. What does `finally` return?
11. Implement `Promise.all` / `Promise.race` / a minimal Promise. *(3.3.17)*

**Model answer, Q3:** "`all` waits for every promise and returns results in order, but rejects immediately on the first failure. `allSettled` always waits for everything and tells you each outcome, so it fits batch jobs where partial failure is acceptable. `race` settles with the first settled promise, whether success or failure, which is useful for timeouts. `any` fulfills with the first *success* and only rejects if all fail, with an `AggregateError`, which suits trying multiple mirrors."


**Mistakes:**
- **Forgetting `return` inside a `.then`:** the derived promise resolves with `undefined` and the next handler silently operates on nothing — the single most common promise bug; lint it with `promise/always-return`.
- **Floating promises:** calling an async function without `await` or `.catch` discards both the value and the error, and in Node an unhandled rejection terminates the process.
- **Sequential `await` in a loop** over independent work, turning `max(t1,t2)` into `t1+t2` — start the promises first, then await them.
- **`.catch(() => {})` as a habit:** it converts a crash into silence, which is worse than the bug; log with context and rethrow, or handle deliberately at the boundary.
- **Wrapping an existing promise in `new Promise((res) => fn().then(res))`:** adds a tick and a layer of confusion; return the promise. And remembering the executor runs *synchronously*, so doing heavy work inside it blocks before you get a promise.
- **Assuming `Promise.all` preserves completion order,** or that iterating its results means everything succeeded: results come back in input order regardless of which settled first, and the partial-failure case is invisible unless you reach for `allSettled`. A timeout `catch` has the mirror-image bug — the timer rejects while the underlying fetch keeps consuming a connection.
- **Forgetting `resolve` is first-wins and idempotent:** calling it again after settling is a silent no-op, so a second error path in the same executor disappears.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Promises vs callbacks:** promises return control of errors to the caller (you choose where rejection is handled, centrally), compose linearly, and can only settle once — an unhandled rejection is loud and reportable; the cost is that you can't cancel, and every legacy callback API still needs promisification (`util.promisify`, `events.once`, hand-rolled wrappers) with a different ergonomics for partial arguments and multiple callbacks.
- **`Promise.all` vs `allSettled`:** `all` is cheaper and fails fast but discards the successes of everything else *and* leaves those requests running; `allSettled` costs one wrapper object per item and forces a filter pass, but it's the only correct choice when partial success is meaningful — bulk uploads, migrations, notification fan-out where you report which rows failed.
- **`Promise.any` vs `Promise.all`:** `any` gives fastest-success semantics for mirrors, CDNs and fallbacks, but you only learn *why* everything failed by inspecting `AggregateError.errors`, so per-attempt diagnostics need `allSettled` with timestamps instead.
- **`AbortController` vs `Promise.race` with a timer:** `race` makes the *promise* look finished while the socket and the server work keep running, which is exactly the bug `AbortSignal.timeout` was added to fix — real cancellation needs the signal threaded through `fetch` and your own functions, at the cost of threading a parameter through every layer.
- **`async/await` vs explicit `.then`:** await reads linearly, gives a real `try/catch` and better stack traces; `.then` composes better at abstraction boundaries and lets you return a promise from a non-async context. The actual cost is mixing the two arbitrarily in one function.
- **Promises vs streams for many results:** a promise models one settled result, so an async generator or event-emitter-per-chunk pattern is how naive code loses backpressure and ordering guarantees; a `Readable` stream (or `for await`) handles unbounded data with buffering you control. Deferring with a resolved promise instead of `setTimeout` buys no real yield and starves rendering if it chains.

**Common wrong answers:** "`.then` waits for the callback's returned promise and runs synchronously if the promise is already resolved." "`Promise.all` cancels the others when one rejects." "`catch` catches every error in the chain." "`Promise.race` gives you the first *successful* result." Or chaining a `.then` midway and expecting the rest of the chain to wait for it.

**Probe:** "In `p.then(v => other()).catch(handle)` versus `p.then(v => other(), handle)` — which one catches a rejection coming out of `other()`, and why does that difference exist at all?" (Expect: the second cannot, because the two handlers are siblings reacting to the same settlement of `p`; the rejection produced by the first handler's *return value* belongs to the promise derived from `p`, which the second handler never sees. That's the whole reason `.then(a).catch(b)` exists, and it's why the two-argument form silently swallows errors from `a`.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **`.then` returns a new promise and applies the three resolution rules:** a returned plain value fulfils it, a returned thenable/promise is adopted (which flattens and costs one extra microtask tick), a throw rejects it. That's why `p.then(v => v + 1)` chains *values*, not promises.
- **Error propagation, precisely:** a rejection skips fulfilment handlers and travels forward along the chain until a rejection handler is found; `.then(a, b)` gives you two *siblings* over the same settlement so `b` cannot see an error thrown by `a`, while `.then(a).catch(b)` can; returning normally from a `catch` recovers the chain and the next `.then` sees a value; `finally` receives no argument and passes the original settlement through unless it throws or returns a rejected promise.
- **Combinator choice, not just names:** `all` (fail-fast on first rejection, results in *input* order, and the other promises keep running), `allSettled` (never rejects, one `{ status, value | reason }` per item), `race` (first to settle either way — timeouts), `any` (first *fulfilment*, `AggregateError` with `.errors` if all reject).
- **The asynchrony guarantees:** the executor runs synchronously and once; only the first `resolve`/`reject` counts; reactions always run as microtasks even on an already-settled promise; and a settled promise is immutable.
- **Operational discipline:** unhandled rejections (`unhandledrejection` in browsers, process exit in Node ≥15), that promises cannot be cancelled so `AbortController` is the real mechanism, and that a timeout must clear its timer and shouldn't leave the underlying work running.
**Mini task:** write a `fetchWithRetryAndTimeout` that uses `AbortController`, retries 3 times with exponential backoff and jitter, and only retries idempotent or network failures. Then build a promise-order quiz with 8 puzzles.


**Self-check:** Given a chain of `.then` that returns a promise, then throws, then a `.catch` that returns a value, then a `.finally`, write out the exact sequence of settlements and say which promise each handler was attached to.

**Resources:** MDN "Promise" (states, chaining, static methods) and MDN "Using promises"; the Promises/A+ spec's resolution procedure and thenable adoption (the authoritative source for the extra tick); MDN "Using AbortController" and `AbortSignal.timeout`; Node's `--unhandled-rejections` documentation and `events.once`; `Promise.withResolvers` (ES2024) in MDN for externally-controlled promises.
---

# 3.3.8 async/await and Concurrency Patterns
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Syntax over promises. An `async` function always returns a promise, and `await` pauses that function until a promise settles, without blocking the thread.


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.7 (an `async` function is a promise-returning function; `await` is sugar over `.then`), 3.3.6 (the continuation after `await` is a microtask, which is what lets you reason about interleaving order).

**Why interviewers ask:** This is where an interview stops being trivia and becomes engineering. Almost every real bug in this area is a concurrency bug — a slow response overwriting a newer one, a serialised loop, unbounded fan-out — and a candidate who can only recite `Promise.all` won't see them. It's also the section where the interviewer decides whether you can be trusted with data-fetching code unsupervised.
**Key concepts**
- **Mechanics:** `await x` ≈ `Promise.resolve(x).then(continueFunction)`. Code before the first `await` runs **synchronously**. The continuation is a microtask. A thrown error becomes a rejection, and `return v` resolves with `v`.
- **Error handling:** `try/catch/finally` around `await`. `await` on a rejected promise throws.
- **`return await` inside try/catch:** `return promise` (without await) bypasses the local `catch`, because the function returns before the promise rejects.
```js
async function a() { try { return failing(); }       catch { return 'handled'; } } // NOT handled
async function b() { try { return await failing(); } catch { return 'handled'; } } // handled
```
- **Sequential vs parallel**
```js
// Sequential: ~ t1 + t2 (use when B depends on A)
const user = await getUser(id);
const orders = await getOrders(user.id);

// Parallel: ~ max(t1, t2) (independent work)
const [profile, settings] = await Promise.all([getProfile(id), getSettings(id)]);

// Start early, await later
const pA = getA(); const pB = getB();
const a = await pA; const b = await pB;
```
- **Loops:** `for...of` with `await` is sequential. **`arr.forEach(async x => await …)` does NOT wait**, because `forEach` ignores returned promises, so errors become unhandled rejections and the code after continues early. Use `for...of`, or `await Promise.all(arr.map(async …))` for parallel.
- **Unbounded parallelism is dangerous:** `Promise.all(10000 requests)` can overwhelm APIs, DBs, and sockets. Use a **concurrency limiter** (3.3.17 `mapLimit`, or `p-limit`).
- **Top-level `await`:** allowed in ES modules. It blocks the module's dependents, so avoid it in widely imported modules.
- **Race conditions in async UI code:** a slow earlier request resolving after a later one overwrites newer state (typeahead). Fix by ignoring stale responses (a request id or flag), aborting with `AbortController`, or using TanStack Query (Phase 4).
- **Error aggregation:** `Promise.allSettled` plus filtering rejections.
- **Async iteration:** `for await (const chunk of stream)` for streams and paginated APIs (3.3.12).
- **Event emitters and callbacks in async code:** `events.once(emitter, 'x')` returns a promise.
- **Debugging:** async stack traces are supported in modern DevTools and Node. Name your async functions.

**Questions**
1. How does `async/await` work under the hood? What does an async function return?
2. How do you run async tasks in parallel with `await`? What's the pitfall of sequential awaits in a loop?
3. Why doesn't `forEach(async …)` work as expected? *(model below)*
4. `return promise` vs `return await promise` in a try/catch?
5. How do you limit concurrency to N? *(worker pool or `p-limit`)*
6. How would you prevent stale responses from overwriting newer ones in a search box?
7. How do you handle errors from multiple parallel calls and still get partial results? *(`allSettled`)*
8. Can you use `await` outside an async function? *(Top-level await in modules only.)*
9. What happens to the thread when you `await`? *(Not blocked, since control returns to the event loop.)*
10. Rewrite a callback pyramid using async/await.

**Model answer, Q3:** "`forEach` calls the callback and ignores whatever it returns. When the callback is `async`, each call returns a promise that `forEach` discards, so it doesn't wait for them. Code after the loop runs before the work finishes, and any rejection is unhandled. I'd use `for...of` with `await` for sequential work, or `await Promise.all(items.map(async …))` for parallel, with a concurrency limit if the list is large."


**Trade-offs:**
- **`Promise.all` vs a concurrency limit:** `Promise.all` is minimal and fastest for a handful of items, but 10k parallel calls exhaust sockets, trip rate limits, and hold 10k responses in memory; a limiter (`p-limit`, or 3–10 in flight) is the production default, at the cost of a helper and slightly worse wall-clock for the first batch.
- **`p-limit` vs a hand-rolled worker pool:** `p-limit` caps concurrency in one line and is well tested; a pool of N async workers pulling from a shared queue gives you backpressure plus a natural place to add retry, priority and progress reporting, at the cost of more code to get right. Take the pool when you also need throttling or progress.
- **Debounce vs throttle:** debounce waits for silence, which is right for typeahead and validation but delays the first request by the full interval and starves entirely if the user types continuously; throttle guarantees a call at most every N ms, which is right for scroll/resize/drag but fires on the leading edge and needs an explicit trailing call to catch the final state.
- **Sequential vs parallel pagination:** parallel pages are far faster but hammer the API and give you no natural resume point; sequential cursor pagination is polite and checkpointable, at the cost of latency. Sequential is the right default against an API you don't own. The trade-off you pay for hand-rolling these patterns instead of a combinator library (`p-limit`, `q`) is a dependency plus a mental model against 15 lines you own.
- **Abort on every request vs sequence-check only:** aborting frees the socket and server work immediately, which matters at scale and on metered connections, but `AbortError` needs filtering everywhere and aborting mid-flight can leave a resource half-released; sequence checks are free and always safe but pay full cost for every keystroke's request.

**Common wrong answers:** "`await` blocks the thread" (it yields — the thread runs other work). "`Promise.all` is always the answer." "`forEach` with `async` runs them in parallel." "`return promise` and `return await promise` are the same." Or that a floating promise is harmless because "it'll log somewhere". Or describing `Promise.all` as cancelling the remaining work.

**Probe:** "A typeahead debounces 200ms per keystroke and never aborts. A slow request for `cat` resolves after `category` has already rendered. What's the bug, and give two fixes with genuinely different trade-offs." (Expect: last-response-wins is not last-request-wins, so a stale query overwrites newer UI; fix one is `AbortController` per request plus ignoring `AbortError`, which actually cancels the work but requires threading cleanup through every layer; fix two is a monotonic request id or sequence check in the response handler, which needs no cancellation and is simpler, but the stale response still costs a request and still arrives.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **The lowering, stated precisely:** `async` wraps the body so it always returns a promise; `await v ≈ Promise.resolve(v).then(continuation)`; code before the first `await` runs synchronously on the current stack; a throw becomes a rejection; `return v` resolves with `v`. One model, and every question below follows from it.
- **`return await` vs `return promise` inside `try`/`catch`:** only the awaited form lets the local `catch` handle it, because without `await` the function returns before the rejection exists. Subtle, frequently wrong, and a good filter.
- **Sequential vs parallel as a deliberate choice, not a reflex:** dependent work *must* be sequential; independent work should be started together (`Promise.all`, or start the promises and await later); and they can explain the wall-clock difference rather than just the syntax.
- **Loop correctness:** `for...of` with `await` for sequential, `await Promise.all(items.map(...))` for parallel, and a limiter (`p-limit` or a hand-rolled worker pool) once the list is large or the endpoint is rate-limited. They should say *why* `forEach(async …)` fails — the returned promises are discarded, so the code after runs early and rejections become unhandled.
- **Real async hazards:** stale responses in a typeahead (fix by `AbortController` or a monotonically increasing request id), shared mutable state across concurrent tasks, unbounded `Promise.all` exhausting sockets or a rate limit, and `allSettled` for partial failure. A stray missing `await` makes the promise float, so the lint rules (`no-floating-promises`, `@typescript-eslint/await-thenable`) are part of the answer, as is naming async functions so stack traces stay readable.
**Mistakes:** accidentally serializing independent calls; unbounded `Promise.all`; missing `await`, which leaves a floating promise (lint with `@typescript-eslint/no-floating-promises`); `try/catch` around everything with no handling; mixing `.then` and `await` randomly.

**Mini task:** build a typeahead fetcher that debounces input, aborts the previous request, ignores stale responses, and handles errors, then compare its behavior to a naive version under a randomized-latency mock API.


**Self-check:** Rewrite a four-step chain (user → orders → items → totals) so only the genuinely dependent steps stay sequential, and state which awaits became parallel and why the other two couldn't.

**Resources:** MDN "Using promises" → "Sequencing and parallelizing promises"; MDN "`async` function" (including the explicit note on `return await` inside `try`/`catch`); MDN "Using AbortController" and `AbortSignal.timeout`; the `p-limit` README for the canonical limiter pattern; TanStack Query's docs on query cancellation and race conditions for the production framing.
---

# 3.3.9 Types, Coercion, and Equality
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.5 (`ToPrimitive` finds `valueOf`/`toString` by walking the prototype chain, which is why `[]` coerces to `''` and a custom class coerces to whatever its methods return), 3.3.1 (a `Symbol` value has no primitive counterpart, so arithmetic on one throws a `TypeError` rather than coercing).

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the cheapest high-signal screen there is, because every answer is fully determined and any hand-waving shows instantly. It also tests whether they can narrate a conversion *order* rather than recall a result, which is the same skill needed for `ToPrimitive` on objects, and it sets up the explicit-conversion discipline that Phase 5's TypeScript section then removes by hand.

**Definition:** JavaScript has seven primitive types plus objects, and its operators convert between them at runtime through the specification's abstract operations `ToPrimitive`, `ToNumber`, `ToString` and `ToBoolean` — that conversion process *is* what "coercion" means. Equality sits on top of it: `==` performs those conversions, `===` compares type and value with none.
**Key concepts**
- **Types:** 7 primitives (`string`, `number`, `bigint`, `boolean`, `undefined`, `null`, `symbol`) and objects. `typeof` returns `'string' | 'number' | 'bigint' | 'boolean' | 'undefined' | 'symbol' | 'function' | 'object'`. Note `typeof null === 'object'` (a historical bug) and arrays are `'object'` (use `Array.isArray`).
- **Primitive wrappers:** `'abc'.length` works because the engine temporarily boxes the primitive. `new String('a')` is an object, and you should avoid it.
- **Implicit coercion rules (the useful subset)**
  - **`+`:** if either operand (after `ToPrimitive`) is a string, concatenate. Otherwise add numerically.
  - **`-`, `*`, `/`, `%`, unary `+`:** convert to number.
  - **ToPrimitive on objects:** `Symbol.toPrimitive` → `valueOf` → `toString` (order flips for string hint). `[] → ''`, `{} → '[object Object]'`, and `Date` prefers string with `+`.
  - **ToNumber:** `''→0`, `' 12 '→12`, `'12px'→NaN`, `null→0`, `undefined→NaN`, `true→1`, `[]→0`, `[5]→5`, `[1,2]→NaN`.
  - **Truthiness:** falsy are `false, 0, -0, 0n, '', null, undefined, NaN`. Everything else is truthy, including `'0'`, `'false'`, `[]`, `{}`, and `new Boolean(false)`.
- **`==` (loose equality), simplified**
  1. Same type: behaves like `===`.
  2. `null == undefined` is true, and they equal *nothing else* (`null == 0` is false).
  3. number vs string: convert the string to a number.
  4. boolean: convert it to a number first (`[] == false` → `[] == 0` → `'' == 0` → `0 == 0` true).
  5. object vs primitive: `ToPrimitive(object)`, then compare.
- **`===`:** no coercion, but `NaN !== NaN`, and `+0 === -0`. **`Object.is`:** `NaN` equals `NaN`, and `+0` differs from `-0`. Use `Number.isNaN`, not global `isNaN` (which coerces).
- **Relational operators:** `null >= 0` is true (null → 0), but `null == 0` is false. Strings compare lexicographically (`'10' < '9'` is true).
- **Parsing:** `Number('12px')` → NaN, `parseInt('12px')` → 12, `parseInt('08')` → 8, `parseFloat('3.14abc')` → 3.14, and `['1','2','3'].map(parseInt)` → `[1, NaN, NaN]` (map passes the index as the radix).
- **Practical guidance:** use `===`, convert explicitly (`Number(x)`, `String(x)`, `Boolean(x)`), use `x == null` only for the "null or undefined" check, and in TypeScript lean on the type system.
- **Sorting:** `[10, 9, 1].sort()` sorts as strings → `[1, 10, 9]`. Always pass a comparator: `.sort((a, b) => a - b)`.
- **Numbers:** `0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3` (2.6.1), `Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER`, `BigInt` doesn't mix with numbers (`1n + 1` throws), `toFixed` returns a string, and `Math.round(-0.5)` is `-0`.

**Questions**
1. `==` vs `===`? When is `==` acceptable?
2. What are the falsy values?
3. What is `typeof null`? Why?
4. Predict: `[] + {}`, `[] == ![]`, `'5' - 2`, `'5' + 2`, `null + 1`, `undefined == null`, `NaN == NaN`.
5. How does `ToPrimitive` work? What are `valueOf` and `toString` for?
6. `isNaN` vs `Number.isNaN`?
7. Why does `['1','2','3'].map(parseInt)` return `[1, NaN, NaN]`?
8. How do you reliably check for an array, plain object, null, or NaN? *(`Array.isArray`, `Object.prototype.toString.call`, `=== null`, `Number.isNaN`)*
9. What's `Object.is` for?
10. Why does `[10, 1, 3].sort()` give `[1, 10, 3]`?


**Trade-offs:**
- **`===` vs `==`:** `===` makes every comparison a reviewable decision and keeps string/number duality from spreading through the layers; `==` is shorter but hides a conversion you didn't intend. The one defensible `==` is `x == null` for the combined null/undefined check — clearer than the `||` form and it can't accidentally match `0` or `''`.
- **Explicit conversion at the boundary vs implicit everywhere:** convert once where data enters (query params, form input, JSON) with `Number(x)` and a schema check, then keep one internal type. `Number` fails loudly on `'12px'` (good — you find out at the edge), whereas leaving `'12px'` to propagate lets arithmetic quietly produce NaN three layers away.
- **`Number.isNaN` vs global `isNaN`:** `Number.isNaN` is a type check with no coercion, so `isNaN('abc')` is true but `Number.isNaN('abc')` is false; the global coerces first, which makes it a "does this convert to a number" test. Use `Number.isNaN` for validation and never the global in application code.
- **`Object.is` vs `===`:** `===` conflates `NaN` (never equal to itself) and distinguishes nothing between `+0` and `-0`; `Object.is` is the SameValue algorithm, correct for both. Use it for `NaN`/`-0`-sensitive comparisons, cache keys, and `Map`/`Set` semantics — though note `-0` and `+0` still collide as `Map` keys because `Map` uses SameValueZero, which `Object.is` does not.
- **`parseInt` vs `Number`:** `parseInt` accepts a numeric *prefix* (`'12px'` → 12), takes a radix, and is right for formats where a prefix is meaningful; `Number` requires the entire string to be numeric, which is right for validating input. Always pass the radix to `parseInt` — omitting it is the historic octal trap. Wrapper objects are the other trap: `new String('a')` has methods and a prototype but `typeof` says `'object'`, `===` fails against the primitive, and `JSON.stringify` serialises it oddly.

**Common wrong answers:** "Loose equality converts both sides to strings" — it converts to a number (string comparison only happens when both operands are already strings). Or "`undefined == null` is false." Or "`[]` is falsy because it's empty." Or "global `isNaN` checks whether a value is a number." Or "NaN stands for not-a-number, so it should compare equal to itself." Or "`typeof` distinguishes arrays from objects."

**Probe:** "`[] == false` — is it true? Walk me through the conversions. Then tell me what you'd actually write if you wanted to check whether an array is empty." (Expect: true, via `false` → `0` → `[]` → `''` → `0` → `0 == 0`; and for emptiness, `arr.length === 0` or `!arr?.length`, because `if (arr)` is truthy for every array including an empty one — the coercion rules are correct, they're just answering a different question than the one you're asking.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Exact output, derived step by step.** They say "with `+`, if either operand's `ToPrimitive` yields a string, concatenate" and *then* apply it — a candidate who recites `'[object Object]'` as a memorised fact will get `[] + {}` vs `[] + []` wrong.
- **`ToPrimitive`'s order and hint:** ordinary hint tries `valueOf` then `toString`; the string hint (used by `+`, template literals and `String()`) prefers `toString`; `Symbol.toPrimitive` overrides everything; and `Date` throws a `TypeError` on the default/number hint and stringifies on the string hint.
- **The exact `ToNumber` table:** `''` → 0, `' 12 '` → 12, `'12px'` → NaN, `null` → 0, `undefined` → NaN, `true` → 1, `[]` → 0, `[5]` → 5, `[1,2]` → NaN — including the trap that `null` and `[]` are both `0`.
- **`==`'s five steps and its single exception,** and the discipline not to over-generalise: `null >= 0` is true while `null == 0` is false, and `'10' < '9'` is true because relational comparison of two strings is lexicographic.
- **Defaults:** `===` with one explicit conversion at the boundary, `x == null` as the only sanctioned `==`, `Number.isNaN` over global `isNaN`, `Object.is` where `-0`/`NaN` identity matters, and `Array.isArray` for arrays.

**Model answer, Q1:** "`===` compares type and value with no conversion at all, so it only behaves as expected when you already know both operands have the same type. `==` runs conversions: if the types match it's identical, `null` and `undefined` are equal to each other and to nothing else, a boolean is converted to a number first, and a number/string pair converts the string to a number. So `'0' == 0` is true while `'0' === 0` is false, and `null >= 0` is true even though `null == 0` is false, because relational operators always use numeric comparison. I only use `==` as `x == null`, a deliberate 'null or undefined' check that reads better than `x === null || x === undefined`; everywhere else it's `===` plus one explicit conversion at the boundary where the value enters the code."
**Mistakes:** relying on `==` coercion for cleverness; global `isNaN`; `if (value)` when `0` or `''` are valid values (use `value != null` or `??`); forgetting the sort comparator.

**Mini task:** write a table of 30 coercion expressions with your prediction, then check them in the console. Write a `typeOf(x)` utility that returns `'array' | 'null' | 'date' | 'regexp' | 'map' | …`.


**Self-check:** Name the eight falsy values, explain why `[]`, `{}`, `'0'` and `new Boolean(false)` are truthy, and say where each of those surprises you in real code.

**Resources:** MDN "Equality comparisons and sameness" (the `Abstract Equality Comparison` table and the `==`/`===`/`Object.is` comparison); ECMA-262 §7.1 "Type Conversion" (ToPrimitive with its hint rules, ToNumber with the string-conversion table, ToBoolean's eight falsy values) and §7.2.14 SameValue / §7.2.15 SameValueZero; MDN "Data types" → the coercion tables; MDN `parseInt` and `Array.prototype.sort` for the prefix-parsing and default-string-comparator behaviours.
---

# 3.3.10 Modern ES6+ Features
🟢 → 🟡 · **Must-know**

Check Baseline or Node-version support for anything newer than ES2022 before relying on it.

| Feature | Notes and gotchas |
|---|---|
| **Template literals** | Multi-line, interpolation, tagged templates (used by styled-components, `gql`, `sql` libraries that make injection-safe queries) |
| **Destructuring** | `const {a, b: renamed = 5, ...rest} = obj`, `const [x, , y] = arr`, nested, function params. Destructuring `null`/`undefined` throws. Defaults apply only for `undefined` |
| **Spread/rest** | `[...a, ...b]`, `{...a, ...b}` (shallow, later keys win, own enumerable only), `function f(...args)`. Spread of huge arrays into function args can overflow the stack |
| **Default params** | Evaluated at call time, can reference earlier params |
| **Shorthand / computed keys** | `{ a, [key]: 1, method() {} }` |
| **Optional chaining `?.`** | Short-circuits on `null`/`undefined` only: `a?.b`, `a?.[k]`, `fn?.()`. Don't overuse, since it can hide bugs |
| **Nullish `??`, `??=`, `\|\|=`, `&&=`** | `??` ignores only null and undefined (keeps `0`, `''`, `false`). Mixing `??` with `\|\|` requires parentheses |
| **Arrow functions** | Lexical `this`, no `arguments`, implicit return (wrap object literals in parentheses: `() => ({a: 1})`) |
| **`Map` / `Set` / `WeakMap` / `WeakSet`** | `Map`: any key type, insertion order, `size`, better than objects for dynamic keys and frequent add/delete. `Set`: unique values |
| **`Symbol`** | Unique keys, well-known symbols (`Symbol.iterator`, etc.) |
| **`BigInt`** | `123n`, arbitrary precision integers, can't mix with numbers, and `JSON.stringify` throws on it |
| **Array methods** | `map filter reduce find findIndex findLast some every flat flatMap includes at`. **Non-mutating (ES2023):** `toSorted toReversed toSpliced with`. `Array.from`, `Array.of`, `fill`, `entries`. **Mutating:** `push pop shift unshift splice sort reverse fill copyWithin` |
| **Object helpers** | `Object.entries/fromEntries/assign/hasOwn/freeze`, `structuredClone`, `Object.groupBy` / `Map.groupBy` (ES2024) |
| **Classes** | Fields, private `#x`, `static`, static blocks, getters/setters |
| **Modules** | `import`/`export`, dynamic `import()`, top-level `await` |
| **Numeric separators, exponent `**`** | `1_000_000`, `2 ** 10` |
| **`Intl`, `structuredClone`, `AbortController`, `queueMicrotask`, `Array.prototype.at(-1)`** | Modern built-ins |
| **`Promise.withResolvers`, Set methods (`union`, `intersection`, …), `Array.fromAsync`** | Very recent. Check support |
| **Error `cause`** | `new Error('msg', { cause: err })` |
| **`label:` / `finally` semantics** | `finally` runs even after `return`, and a `return` in `finally` *overrides* the earlier return or throw |

```js
const { data: { user = 'anon' } = {}, ...rest } = response;   // nested destructuring with defaults
const merged = { ...defaults, ...overrides };
const byId = new Map(users.map(u => [u.id, u]));
const grouped = Object.groupBy(items, i => i.category);       // ES2024
const last = arr.at(-1);
const sorted = [...arr].sort((a, b) => a - b);                // or arr.toSorted(...)
const fn = cb?.(value) ?? 'default';
```


**Key concepts**
- **Syntax versus library.** ES6+ gave you syntax (destructuring, spread, optional chaining, class fields) plus a handful of built-ins (`Map`, `Set`, `Symbol`, `BigInt`, `Intl`, `structuredClone`). Syntax you read from memory; built-ins you must check against a baseline.
- **Copying is one level deep unless you do the work.** Spread, `Object.assign`, `Array.from`, and `slice` copy own enumerable properties one level; nested objects stay shared by reference. `structuredClone` is the only built-in deep clone, and it throws on functions and DOM nodes and drops prototypes and accessors.
- **`??`, `||`, and `?.` are three different tools.** `||` treats `0`, `''`, `false`, and `NaN` as absent; `??` only `null` and `undefined`; `?.` short-circuits on `null`/`undefined` for exactly one hop. Mixing `??` with `||`/`&&` without parentheses is a SyntaxError.
- **Property order and key coercion are specified, not incidental.** `Object.keys` emits integer-like keys in ascending numeric order first, then string keys in insertion order, and `obj[o1]` runs `ToPropertyKey`, so every plain object used as a key collapses to `'[object Object]'`.
- **`Map`/`Set` versus plain object/array.** `Map` accepts any key type, keeps insertion order, exposes `size`, and is O(1) for add/delete; an object with dynamic keys needs `hasOwn` because it inherits from `Object.prototype`, and `delete` never shrinks the hidden-class slots.
- **`for...of` consumes `Symbol.iterator`, so any object can join the language.** Implement one method and you get spread, destructuring, `Array.from`, `Promise.all`, and `for...of` for free (see 3.3.12).
- **Newer built-ins need a baseline check.** `Object.hasOwn`, `at(-1)`, `structuredClone`, `toSorted`/`toSpliced`/`toReversed`/`with`, `Object.groupBy`, `Promise.any`, `Error` `cause`, `Array.fromAsync`, and `Set.prototype.union` each landed in a specific year and each needs a Browserslist/`@tsconfig/lib` check or a polyfill.
- **Private fields are enforced; Symbols are not.** `#x` is genuinely private and throws through a `Proxy` receiver; a `Symbol` key is skipped by `for...in`, `Object.keys`, and `JSON.stringify` but trivially reachable with `getOwnPropertySymbols`, so it is collision avoidance, not security.

**Prerequisites:** types and coercion (3.3.9), prototypes and classes (3.3.5), and hoisting and the TDZ (3.3.1).

**Why interviewers ask:** This is the daily vocabulary of every frontend codebase, and it is where "it works on my machine" quietly becomes a production bug. They ask it as a fluency filter: do you write `for` loops or `map`, do you know spread is shallow and `Object.freeze` is shallow, do you reach for `?.` reflexively or only where absence is genuinely expected. A candidate who recites feature names but cannot state the cost or the copying semantics of a feature fails regardless of years.

**Definition:** The post-ES5 language surface — destructuring, spread/rest, template literals, `Map`/`Set`, `Symbol`, `BigInt`, classes with `#private` fields, `?.` and `??`, the non-mutating array methods, and the built-ins added since — plus the runtime and syntax traps each one introduces.
**Questions**
1. `map` vs `forEach` vs `reduce` vs `flatMap`? Which mutate the array?
2. `Map` vs plain object? `Set` vs array for uniqueness?
3. Explain spread vs rest. Is spread a deep copy?
4. `??` vs `||`? What does `?.` do?
5. How does destructuring work with defaults? What if the value is `null`? *(Defaults apply only to `undefined`. `null` is kept.)*
6. Difference between `Object.freeze`, `seal`, `preventExtensions`? (3.3.14)
7. `for...of` vs `for...in` vs `forEach`?
8. What are tagged templates used for?
9. `WeakMap` vs `Map`? (3.3.13)
10. What's the difference between `slice`, `splice`, and `toSpliced`?


**Mistakes:**
- **Treating `?.` as error handling:** it converts a crash into a wrong value, and the crash was the correct signal.
- **Assuming `Object.freeze` is deep:** `Object.freeze(state)` leaves `state.user` fully mutable, so the guarantee is imaginary.
- **Mixing `??` with `||`/`&&` without parentheses:** `a || b ?? c` is a SyntaxError, added precisely because the intent is almost always ambiguous.
- **Mutating inside `map`/`forEach` because it "works":** the new array shares nested references with the old, so the "copy" mutates the original and the bug surfaces two renders later.
- **Destructuring without a length guarantee:** `const [a, b] = arr` on a short array yields `undefined`, not an error, and that `undefined` flows straight into arithmetic.
- **Handing an array's index to a callback with a second parameter:** `['1','2','3'].map(parseInt)` passes the index as the radix.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`Map` versus a plain object:** `Map` for dynamic or non-string keys, frequent add/delete, or when you want `size` and honest iteration order; a plain object when the shape is fixed, you need `JSON.stringify`, or you're building a DTO to send.
- **`?.` versus an explicit guard:** `?.` for a one-hop optional access in a chain you cannot guarantee; an explicit `if (!x) return` when absence is a bug you want surfaced, because `?.` converts it into a silent default.
- **`??` versus `||`:** `??` whenever `0`, `''`, or `false` is legitimate — counters, text inputs, feature flags; `||` only when every falsy value means "use the default".
- **`toSorted`/`toSpliced` versus `[...arr].sort()`:** the `to*` methods read better and skip the spread copy but need a newer baseline; the spread works everywhere and costs one allocation you can afford. On a hot path both are worse than sorting in place on data you own.
- **`BigInt` versus string or `Number`:** `BigInt` when you need exact 64-bit-plus arithmetic (`123n`); it cannot be mixed with `Number` and `JSON.stringify` throws on it, so serialise as a string.
- **Deep immutability tools versus hand-rolled copies:** `Object.freeze` and `as const` are cheap and shallow/compile-time respectively; real guarantees need a deep freeze or copy-on-write, which is a runtime cost you should choose deliberately.

**Common wrong answers:** "Spread is a deep copy" and "optional chaining replaced all the if-checks" are the two that lose people, and both are extremely common. Also "`Object.freeze` makes the object immutable" (it is shallow, and a write is a silent no-op in sloppy mode) and "`const` means the value can't change" (it means the binding can't be reassigned — `const o = {}; o.x = 1` is legal).

**Probe:** "`state.user.name` where `state = { user: null }` — where do you put `?.`, what breaks if you put it everywhere, and what does `state.user?.name ?? 'Unknown'` return when `name` is `''`?"
