(Expect: `?.` only where absence is legitimate — data not loaded yet — not on values the type system guarantees; sprinkling it everywhere converts a loud `TypeError` into a silently wrong render. `??` returns `''`, because `''` is not nullish, which is the whole reason to prefer it to `||` here.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Semantic precision, not feature enumeration.** "It returns a new array" is an answer; a list of ES2015 features is not.
- **Awareness of copying depth.** Anyone who says spread is a deep copy, or that `Object.freeze` is deep, is out.
- **Judgement about when the modern feature is the wrong choice.** `?.` on a value that must exist hides a bug; a `Map` for a fixed-schema object is ceremony.
- **Baseline awareness.** Naming `Object.groupBy` but not its target year, or shipping `at(-1)` without checking the browser floor, is unready.

**Model answer, Q1:** "`map`, `filter`, and `flatMap` return a new array, so they don't mutate. `forEach` doesn't mutate by itself either — it just gives you no return value, which is exactly why people write mutation inside it. `reduce` is the general fold: accumulator in, whatever you return out. None of the four mutate the receiver. What does mutate is `push`, `pop`, `splice`, `sort`, `reverse`, `fill`, and `copyWithin` — which is why I'd prefer `toSorted`, `toSpliced`, and `toReversed`, or `[...arr].sort()`, when the array might be someone else's state. And `flatMap` is `map` followed by a one-level `flat`, so a callback returning an array appends instead of nesting."
**Mini task:** rewrite a 40-line ES5 data-processing snippet using modern features and no mutation, then explain each choice.


**Self-check:** State whether `Object.freeze({a:{b:1}})` prevents `obj.a.b = 2`, and explain it in terms of what `freeze` actually walks.

**Resources:** MDN's "New features in JavaScript" guide and its ES2023/ES2024 pages; the TC39 finished-proposals list; `@babel/preset-env` and `core-js` docs on targets and polyfills; `caniuse.com` for a two-second baseline check; V8's "JavaScript added in…" blog series for the performance angle on `??`, `?.`, and destructuring.
---

# 3.3.11 Modules: ESM vs CommonJS
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

| | **ES Modules (ESM)** | **CommonJS (CJS)** |
|---|---|---|
| Syntax | `import x from './x.js'`, `export` | `require('./x')`, `module.exports` |
| Loading | **Static** (analyzable at parse time), asynchronous | **Dynamic**, synchronous |
| Bindings | **Live read-only bindings** | **Copies** of exported values (at require time) |
| Tree shaking | Yes (static structure) | Hard |
| Top-level `await` | Yes | No |
| Strict mode | Always | Optional |
| `this` at top level | `undefined` | `module.exports` |
| `__dirname` / `__filename` | Not available (`import.meta.url`, `import.meta.dirname` in newer Node) | Available |
| File extensions | **Required** in Node ESM (`./x.js`) | Optional |
| Conditional/lazy load | `await import('./x.js')` | `require` anywhere |
| Enable in Node | `"type": "module"` in `package.json`, or `.mjs` | Default, or `.cjs` |


**Prerequisites:** execution context and scope chain (3.3.2), prototypes and classes (3.3.5), and modern ES6+ features (3.3.10).

**Why interviewers ask:** Modules are where "works on my machine" stops working: the same file behaves differently under a bundler, under Node ESM, and in a browser, and the failure modes are notoriously confusing (`ERR_REQUIRE_ESM`, `__dirname is not defined`, two copies of React in one bundle, builds that won't tree-shake). They ask because a candidate who has only consumed a scaffolded app has no model of evaluation order, and that model is what separates "I can configure Vite" from "I understand why the library loaded twice".

**Definition:** The two module systems JavaScript ships with — CommonJS (`require`/`module.exports`, synchronous, Node-first) and ECMAScript modules (`import`/`export`, statically analysable, the web standard) — together with the evaluation-order semantics, the interop rules, and the `package.json` metadata that decide which one you actually get.
**Key concepts**
- **Exports:** named (`export const a`), default (`export default`), re-exports (`export * from`), and namespace imports (`import * as ns`). Prefer **named exports**: they're refactor-friendly, grep-friendly, and tree-shake better.
- **Module singleton:** a module is evaluated **once** and cached, and every importer sees the same instance. That's why a module-level object works as a singleton.
- **Live bindings:** an importer sees updates to an exported `let` (`export let count`). CJS exports copy the value.
- **Circular dependencies:** ESM hoists import bindings, so you may touch an uninitialized binding (TDZ error). CJS returns a *partial* `exports` object. The fix is to restructure (extract shared code), use lazy access inside functions, or use dependency injection.
- **Dynamic `import()`:** returns a promise, and is the basis for **code splitting** (`React.lazy`) and conditional loading.
- **Interop:** ESM can import CJS (default = `module.exports`). CJS cannot `require()` ESM synchronously (newer Node versions are relaxing this, so check yours), and must use `await import()`.
- **`package.json`:** `"exports"` field (the public entry points, conditional for `import`/`require`), `"main"`, `"module"`, `"type"`, and `"sideEffects": false` (helps bundlers tree-shake).
- **Barrel files** (`index.js` that re-exports everything) can hurt tree-shaking and dev startup in large codebases.
- **Browser:** `<script type="module">` is deferred, strict, CORS-fetched, and runs once per URL. Import maps map bare specifiers.

```js
// math.js
export const add = (a, b) => a + b;
export default function multiply(a, b) { return a * b; }
// app.js
import multiply, { add } from './math.js';
const { heavy } = await import('./heavy.js');   // lazy
```

**Questions**
1. ESM vs CommonJS? *(table)*
2. What does "live binding" mean?
3. What is tree shaking? Why does it need ESM? *(static import/export lets bundlers prove what's unused)*
4. How do you handle circular dependencies?
5. What's dynamic import used for?
6. Why are modules singletons? Use and risk? *(a shared state/cache is convenient, but it complicates testing and SSR, where per-request state would leak between users)*
7. Named vs default exports?
8. How do you use both ESM and CJS in one project?
9. What do `sideEffects` and `exports` in `package.json` do?


**Mistakes:**
- **Forgetting the file extension in Node ESM:** `import './x'` is a resolution error, because Node does not guess like a bundler.
- **Trusting the process-level module cache while debugging:** a module is evaluated once per process, so stale output usually means a watcher or a cached build, not a language bug.
- **Mixing `require` and `import` in one file** without knowing which interop path you're on — that is where `esModuleInterop` surprises come from.
- **Using `__dirname` in ESM** and getting `undefined`; use `import.meta.url`, or `import.meta.dirname` on newer Node.
- **Setting `"sideEffects": false` on a package with real side effects** — polyfills, global registration, or CSS imports then vanish in production builds.
- **Keeping per-request state in a module-level variable** because modules are singletons: on a server that state is shared across all users.

**Trade-offs:**
- **ESM versus CommonJS:** ESM for static analysis, tree shaking, top-level `await`, and web-standard tooling; CommonJS where you must support an old Node, an old bundler config, or a dependency that only ships CJS. Interop is cheapest in one direction, so make ESM your build output and treat CJS as the compatibility edge.
- **Named versus default exports:** named for refactor-ability, grep-ability, and tree shaking; default for a module that exports exactly one thing, or a component. Rule of thumb: named everywhere, default only at an entry point.
- **Dynamic `import()` versus static import:** dynamic for code splitting, optional or heavy dependencies, and platform-conditional code (Node-only `fs`); static for the critical path, because the bundler can only prefetch and tree-shake what it can see.
- **Dual publishing versus one build:** an `exports` map with `import`/`require` conditions keeps every consumer working but doubles CI and the surface you must test; ESM-only with a loud upgrade note is simpler if you control your consumers.
- **Barrel files versus deep imports:** a barrel is convenient but pulls a large graph in for a small import and can defeat tree shaking; deep imports are cheaper but must be listed in `exports` or they break on the next major.

**Common wrong answers:** "CommonJS is old, ESM is new, use ESM" gives no mechanism, so there is nothing to evaluate. Also "`import` and `require` are interchangeable", "ESM is always asynchronous" (evaluation is synchronous; only `import()` is async), and "`exports` is optional metadata" (it is the enforcement mechanism for your public API in modern Node).

**Probe:** "Two files import each other. In ESM the second one reads a `const` from the first and throws `Cannot access 'X' before initialization`; in CJS you silently get `undefined`. Why the difference, and what are the two real fixes?" (Expect: ESM hoists *bindings* but each module's body still runs in graph order, so inside a cycle the later module's body runs while the earlier one's bindings are in TDZ; CJS assigns `exports` properties as its body executes and hands back a partially filled object, so you read `undefined`. The two real fixes are extracting the shared code into a third module both import, or deferring the access into a function called after evaluation — dependency injection at the boundary is the third.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Static analysis is the reason ESM exists.** "Tree shaking needs provable structure" is the load-bearing sentence; a list of syntax differences without it misses the point.
- **Evaluation order and the TDZ.** Imports are hoisted, the module graph is evaluated depth-first, and touching a binding from a module that hasn't evaluated yet throws a `ReferenceError`.
- **Interop specifics.** `import cjs from './cjs'` yields `module.exports`; `require()` of ESM is the hard, version-dependent case.
- **Practical packaging.** The `exports` map, `"type"`, `sideEffects`, and why a barrel file hurts tree shaking.

**Model answer, Q2:** "A live binding means the importer doesn't get a snapshot of a value, it gets a reference to the *variable* in the exporting module. So with `export let count = 0` and a later `count++`, every importer sees the new value at read time. It's live but read-only — you cannot assign to an imported binding, which is the point. CommonJS has no such thing: `module.exports.count` copies the value at the moment `require` runs, so later mutation inside the exporting module is invisible to importers, and anyone who destructured it keeps the old primitive."
**Mini task:** create two modules that import each other and observe the failure in ESM and CJS, then break the cycle two ways. Then ship a small dual (ESM + CJS) package using the `exports` map.


**Self-check:** Say in one sentence each what `import` gives you that `require` does not, and name the ESM failure mode that sentence explains.

**Resources:** Node's "Modules" guide (`nodejs.org/api/esm.html`) and its packages/module-resolution docs for the `exports` algorithm; the TC39-39 spec's Modules chapter; MDN's "JavaScript modules" guide and its `<script type="module">` page; webpack's `sideEffects` and `output.module` docs; Vite's dependency pre-bundling and `resolve.dedupe` pages.
---

# 3.3.12 Iterators and Generators
🔴 Advanced · **Good-to-know** (asked at mid and senior levels, and it explains async/await and Redux-Saga)


**Prerequisites:** closures (3.3.3), prototypes and classes (3.3.5), and promises (3.3.7) — async generators only make sense once you know the microtask queue.

**Why interviewers ask:** Iterators explain four things at once: why `for...of` works on your custom object, how `async/await` is conceptually implemented, why lazy pagination is possible, and how Redux-Saga models side effects. It also separates people who know the spec vocabulary from people who have only used `for...of` — the iterator/iterable distinction, `return()` for cleanup, and `yield*` delegation are the tells.

**Definition:** The iterator protocol (an object with `next()` returning `{ value, done }`), the iterable protocol (an object with `[Symbol.iterator]()` returning an iterator), and the `function*` syntax that yields iterators whose execution can be suspended at `yield` and resumed by `next(value)` — plus the async variants, `Symbol.asyncIterator`, and `for await...of`.
**Key concepts**
- **Iterator protocol:** an object with `next()` returning `{ value, done }`. **Iterable protocol:** an object with `[Symbol.iterator]()` returning an iterator. `for...of`, spread, destructuring, `Array.from`, `Map`, `Set`, and `Promise.all` consume iterables.
- **Generators** (`function*`) are functions that can pause at `yield` and resume. Calling one returns an iterator. They're lazy (values are produced on demand) and can model infinite sequences.
- `yield` can also *receive* values: `const x = yield 1;` (the value passed to `next(v)`). `return()` and `throw()` allow cancellation and error injection. `yield*` delegates to another iterable.
- **Async iterators and generators** (`async function*`, `Symbol.asyncIterator`, `for await...of`): consume streams, paginated APIs, and event sequences.
- **Relation to `async/await`:** `async/await` is essentially a generator-driven coroutine runner over promises (the old `co` library).
- **Use cases:** lazy pipelines, pagination, ID generators, tree traversal, state machines, custom iteration over data structures, and Redux-Saga effects.

```js
class Range {
  constructor(start, end) { this.start = start; this.end = end; }
  *[Symbol.iterator]() { for (let i = this.start; i < this.end; i++) yield i; }
}
[...new Range(0, 5)];   // [0,1,2,3,4]

function* fibonacci() { let [a, b] = [0, 1]; for (;;) { yield a; [a, b] = [b, a + b]; } }
function* take(n, it) { let i = 0; for (const v of it) { if (i++ >= n) return; yield v; } }
[...take(8, fibonacci())];   // [0,1,1,2,3,5,8,13]

async function* paginate(url) {                  // async generator over pages
  let next = url;
  while (next) {
    const res = await fetch(next);
    const { items, nextUrl } = await res.json();
    yield* items;
    next = nextUrl;
  }
}
for await (const item of paginate('/api/items')) { /* process lazily */ }

function* inOrder(node) {                        // tree traversal
  if (!node) return;
  yield* inOrder(node.left); yield node.value; yield* inOrder(node.right);
}
```

**Questions**
1. What's the difference between an iterable and an iterator?
2. How does a generator work? What does `yield` do?
3. How would you make a custom object iterable?
4. How do generators relate to `async/await`?
5. When would you use an async generator? *(streaming and pagination)*
6. How do you stop iteration early and clean up? *(`break` calls `return()`, so use `try/finally` in the generator)*
7. Generate an infinite sequence without running out of memory.


**Mistakes:**
- **Assuming the body runs when the generator is called:** it runs on the first `next()`, so side effects before the first `yield` are deferred.
- **Swallowing `throw()` in a `catch` around `yield`:** an injected error runs your handler and may never reach the consumer.
- **Accidentally materialising a lazy source:** `[...take(1e9, range())]` defeats the entire point of the generator.
- **Using generators for CPU work without yielding regularly:** a generator that never yields is just an awkward function; the cooperative benefit comes from handing control back.
- **Assuming `Promise.all` cancels the losers:** on rejection the other operations keep running, so a producer's `finally` may never fire unless you cancel explicitly.
- **Adding `[Symbol.iterator]` to a prototype you don't own,** which makes `for...of` succeed where it should have thrown.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Generator pipeline versus array pipeline:** generators for unknown-length or expensive-to-materialise sources (streams, paginated APIs, infinite sequences) because they're O(1) memory and start producing immediately; arrays when you need random access, multiple passes, `.length`, or serialisation.
- **`for...of` versus `forEach`:** `for...of` allows `break`, `return`, and `await` inside the body; `forEach` allows none of those — but `forEach` skips holes in sparse arrays while `for...of` yields `undefined` for them.
- **Async generator versus a manual `await` loop:** async generators let the consumer `break` and have the producer clean up in `finally`; a manual `while` over `fetch` needs explicit `AbortController` wiring to achieve the same teardown.
- **Implementing `[Symbol.iterator]` versus extending a built-in:** the symbol keeps the type honest and works everywhere the protocol is consumed; subclassing `Array` or `Map` looks tidier but leaks constructor semantics and `instanceof` surprises.
- **Sync versus async iterators:** async generators add a microtask hop per value, which is a real cost in a hot loop — don't wrap an in-memory source in `async function*`.

**Common wrong answers:** "A generator returns an array" and "it's `async`/`await` with different syntax" — the second is the tempting one, because generators are synchronous unless declared `async function*`. Also confusing `return` inside a generator with an error: it is an ordinary completion whose value flows out of `for...of`, not a throw.

**Probe:** "You `for (const row of readFile(path))` over a custom async generator and `break` after the first row. What has to happen for the descriptor to be closed, and what if you forget?" (Expect: `break` triggers `iterator.return()`, which resumes the generator at the `yield` with a completion — the `finally` runs and closes the handle. Forget it and the generator is left suspended mid-`yield`, the handle leaks, and the surrounding promise never settles; with an async generator the consumer can even appear to hang on an un-awaited `finally`.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Clean separation of the two protocols,** with an example that isn't an array.
- **That `next(v)`'s argument arrives as the value of the `yield`** — the line that separates spec-readers from users.
- **Cleanup semantics:** `break`, `throw`, or `return` inside `for...of` invokes the iterator's `return()`, so `try/finally` in the generator is the only reliable teardown.
- **Laziness as the point,** with its practical consequence: an infinite sequence is a legitimate value.

**Model answer, Q1:** "An iterator *produces* values: it has `next()` returning `{ value, done }` and advancing internal state. An iterable *can be iterated*: it has a `[Symbol.iterator]()` method that returns a fresh iterator. So `for...of` asks the iterable for an iterator, then loops on that iterator's `next()`. One object can be both — an array's `[Symbol.iterator]()` returns a *new* iterator each call, which is why you can run two `for...of` loops over the same array. Generators return iterators; a generator that also defines `[Symbol.iterator]() { return this }` is both. And strings are iterable by code point, which is why `[...'a\u{1F600}']` has length 2 rather than 3 UTF-16 units."
**Mini task:** build lazy `map`, `filter`, `take`, and `chunk` helpers over iterables, and use them to process a 10-million-element range without allocating arrays. Implement a mini `co` that runs a generator of promises.


**Self-check:** Explain where the value passed to `next(x)` lands in the generator body, and what happens to the generator when a consumer `break`s out of `for...of`.

**Resources:** MDN's "Iteration protocols", "function*", and "Symbol.iterator" pages; the TC39-39 spec's iterator and generator-object sections; Jake Archibald's "Iterators and async generators" for the design intuition; the `Symbol.asyncIterator` proposal explainer; V8's blog on lazy sequences and generator performance.
---

# 3.3.13 Proxy, Reflect, Symbols, and Weak Collections
🔴 Advanced · **Good-to-know**

**Proxy and Reflect**
- `new Proxy(target, handler)` intercepts operations: `get`, `set`, `has`, `deleteProperty`, `ownKeys`, `apply`, `construct`, `defineProperty`, and `getPrototypeOf`. **Reflect** has matching methods that perform the default behavior and keep `this` and receiver semantics correct. Forward with `Reflect.get(...arguments)`.
- **Uses:** reactivity (**Vue 3** uses Proxies, whereas Vue 2 used `Object.defineProperty`), validation, default values, logging and tracing, negative array indexes, access control, API clients (`api.users.get()`), and mocking.
- **Limits:** a performance overhead, invariants that can't be violated (e.g., non-configurable properties), identity (`proxy !== target`), private fields don't work through proxies (`#x` throws on a proxy receiver), and internal slots on `Map`/`Date` need bound methods.
```js
const validated = (target) => new Proxy(target, {
  set(obj, prop, value, receiver) {
    if (prop === 'age' && !Number.isInteger(value)) throw new TypeError('age must be an integer');
    return Reflect.set(obj, prop, value, receiver);
  },
  get: (obj, prop, receiver) => (prop in obj ? Reflect.get(obj, prop, receiver) : `no_${String(prop)}`)
});

const negArray = arr => new Proxy(arr, {
  get: (t, p, r) => Reflect.get(t, typeof p === 'string' && /^-\d+$/.test(p) ? t.length + +p : p, r)
});
```

**Symbols**
- Unique, immutable primitives, used as collision-free property keys (`Symbol('id')`). They're skipped by `for...in`, `Object.keys`, and `JSON.stringify` (`Object.getOwnPropertySymbols` reveals them). `Symbol.for('key')` uses a global registry.
- **Well-known symbols** customize language behavior: `Symbol.iterator`, `Symbol.asyncIterator`, `Symbol.toPrimitive`, `Symbol.hasInstance`, `Symbol.toStringTag`.
- Not truly private (reflectable). Use `#private` fields for real privacy.

**WeakMap, WeakSet, WeakRef, FinalizationRegistry**
- **`WeakMap`:** keys must be objects, held **weakly**: if nothing else references the key, the entry can be garbage collected. Not iterable, with no `size`. **Uses:** private data per object, caching computed values keyed by objects (e.g., DOM nodes) without leaks, and metadata.
- **`WeakSet`:** membership tracking of objects (e.g., "already visited" in circular deep-clone).
- **`WeakRef`/`FinalizationRegistry`:** rare, non-deterministic, with no guaranteed timing. Avoid relying on them for logic.
```js
const meta = new WeakMap();
function track(el) { meta.set(el, { clicks: 0 }); }   // entry vanishes when `el` is GC'd
```


**Prerequisites:** prototypes and classes (3.3.5), closures (3.3.3), and types and coercion (3.3.9) — symbol keys go through `ToPropertyKey`.

**Why interviewers ask:** These are the primitives framework authors build on, so the question is really "do you understand how Vue 3 tracks reactivity, or can you only use Vue?" It also probes judgement: candidates who reach for `Proxy` everywhere without knowing the invariants, the identity problem, or that `#private` fields break through it. Weak collections are the quiet-leak section — knowing when *not* to use a `Map` is a maturity signal.

**Definition:** `Proxy`, an object that intercepts operations on a target through handler traps; `Reflect`, the namespace of functions that perform those operations correctly with receiver semantics intact; `Symbol`, collision-free primitive property keys including the well-known symbols that hook into language behaviour; and the weakly-referenced collections (`WeakMap`, `WeakSet`, `WeakRef`, `FinalizationRegistry`) whose entries disappear when their keys are collected.
**Questions**
1. What is a Proxy? Give a real-world use. How does Vue 3 reactivity use it?
2. Why use `Reflect` inside proxy traps?
3. What are Symbols used for? Are they private?
4. `Map` vs `WeakMap`? When would you choose `WeakMap`?
5. Why can't you iterate a `WeakMap`? *(Contents depend on GC timing, so enumerating would expose non-determinism.)*
6. What are the downsides of Proxies?
7. Name some well-known symbols and what they customize.


**Mistakes:**
- **Not forwarding the receiver:** `return obj[prop]` in a `get` trap silently rebinds inherited getters to the proxy.
- **Using `Object.keys` instead of `Reflect.ownKeys` in `ownKeys`:** symbols disappear, so `for...of`, spread, and `JSON.stringify` behave differently through the proxy than around it.
- **Proxying `Map`/`Date`/`Promise` without binding** and then debugging the wrong error for twenty minutes.
- **Trying to hide a non-configurable property through a trap:** it throws instead of returning your substitute value.
- **Keeping a `Map` keyed on DOM nodes or component instances** in a SPA — the classic "the app slows down the more I navigate" leak.
- **Assuming `#x` survives a proxy:** it throws `TypeError: Cannot read private member` because the proxy is a different object.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`Proxy` versus `Object.defineProperty`:** `Proxy` handles new properties, deletion, `in`, enumeration, and negative array indices with one object, so it's the right tool for deep reactivity; `defineProperty` is cheaper per access and works in older environments but must be applied per property, which is exactly why `Vue 2` couldn't see added properties and `Vue 3` can.
- **`Map` versus `WeakMap`:** `Map` when keys are primitives, when you need iteration or `size`, or when cleanup must be deterministic; `WeakMap` when the key is an object whose lifetime you don't control and the entry should die with it. For caching, a `WeakMap` is strictly safer — it can never be the thing keeping a detached DOM node alive.
- **`Symbol` key versus a namespaced string versus `#private`:** `Symbol` for library-internal keys that must not collide or serialise; a string prefix for anything you need to debug or send over the wire; `#private` for real encapsulation, since it's not reflectable.
- **Binding methods in the `get` trap versus passing through:** binding keeps `Map`/`Date`/`Promise` working but allocates a new function identity per read, which breaks `proxy.map === proxy.map` and some caching; not binding is faster but breaks those built-ins.
- **`WeakRef`/`FinalizationRegistry` versus not bothering:** the timing is non-deterministic, so they are acceptable for optional caches and diagnostics and unacceptable for anything whose correctness depends on when they run.

**Common wrong answers:** "`Proxy` makes the object private" and "`Symbol` keys are private" are both wrong — `Reflect.ownKeys` and `getOwnPropertySymbols` reveal them instantly. Also "`WeakMap` is a `Map` you can't iterate, so use it for memory safety everywhere": keys must be objects, it is useless for primitives, and you lose `size`, enumeration, and any ability to list what you cached.

**Probe:** "You wrap a `Map` in a proxy to log every `set`. What breaks when you call `proxy.set(k, v)`, and what's the one-line fix?" (Expect: `this` is the proxy, which has no `[[MapData]]` internal slot, so it throws `TypeError: Method Map.prototype.set called on incompatible receiver`. Fix in the `get` trap: if the value is a function, return it bound to the target — `get: (t, p, r) => { const v = Reflect.get(t, p, r); return typeof v === 'function' ? v.bind(t) : v; }`. Same trap for `Date`, `Set`, `Promise`, and any class with private fields.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **`Reflect` used with the receiver**, stated as a rule rather than trivia.
- **Awareness of invariant violations** and that they throw.
- **That `#private` and internal slots don't survive a proxy** — the detail that proves they actually tried it.
- **Honest use of weak collections:** a `WeakMap` is right for per-object metadata and object-keyed caches, the value stays strongly reachable *while the key is alive*, and the entry simply becomes unobservable afterwards.

**Model answer, Q2:** "Because the default behaviour is easy to get subtly wrong. If a trap does `obj[prop]` instead of `Reflect.get(obj, prop, receiver)` it loses the receiver: a getter inherited from the prototype then runs with the *proxy* as `this` rather than the original object, which in a reactive system means the getter's own reads get tracked and its writes get trapped — sometimes infinitely. `Reflect` also gives you the boolean the trap contract requires, `Reflect.ownKeys` returns keys *and* symbols in the specified order, and `Reflect.has` walks the full prototype chain. So the rule is: a trap that intercepts should end in a `Reflect` call with the same arguments, and any custom behaviour wraps around that. Forwarding `...arguments` also keeps you correct if you later add a trap you haven't thought about."
**Mini task:** build a tiny `reactive()` + `effect()` system with a Proxy and a dependency map (`WeakMap<target, Map<key, Set<effect>>>`) in about 40 lines.


**Self-check:** Say why `new Proxy(map, { get: (t, k) => t[k] })` fails on `proxy.set(...)`, and name the one-line fix.

**Resources:** MDN's `Proxy` and `Reflect` pages plus the "Proxy handler methods and traps" guide; the TC39-39 spec's proxy exotic object section; Vue 3's `reactive`/`effect` implementation in `packages/reactivity`; MDN's "Using Weak Maps" and the `WeakRef`/`FinalizationRegistry` proposal explainers.
---

# 3.3.14 Copying, Immutability, and Property Descriptors
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** (React and Redux depend on it)

**Copying**

| Technique | Depth | Preserves | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `b = a` | None | Same reference | Aliasing |
| `{...a}`, `[...a]`, `Object.assign({}, a)`, `arr.slice()` | **Shallow** | Own enumerable props | Nested objects still shared |
| `structuredClone(a)` | **Deep** | Dates, Maps, Sets, RegExp, typed arrays, circular refs | Throws on functions and DOM nodes, drops prototypes and class identity, ignores getters/setters |
| `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(a))` | Deep (lossy) | JSON types only | Drops `undefined`/functions/symbols, `NaN`→`null`, Date→string, throws on circular and BigInt |
| Hand-written `deepClone` / lodash `cloneDeep` | Deep | Customizable | See 3.3.17 |

**Immutability**
- **Why:** predictable state, cheap change detection by reference (`prev !== next`), time-travel debugging, and fewer bugs from shared mutation.
- **Immutable update patterns:**
```js
const next = { ...state, user: { ...state.user, name: 'Ada' } };           // nested object
const added = [...list, item];  const removed = list.filter(x => x.id !== id);
const updated = list.map(x => (x.id === id ? { ...x, done: true } : x));
const sorted = list.toSorted(cmp);                                          // non-mutating
```
- **Immer** lets you write "mutating" code on a draft and produces an immutable result with structural sharing (used inside Redux Toolkit).
- **Structural sharing:** unchanged branches are reused by reference, so updates stay cheap.
- **`Object.freeze`** is shallow, and in sloppy mode writes fail silently (in strict mode they throw). `Object.seal` blocks adding and removing, but allows editing. `Object.preventExtensions` blocks adding only. `Object.isFrozen` tests. `as const` in TypeScript is compile-time only.

**Property descriptors**
```js
Object.defineProperty(obj, 'id', { value: 1, writable: false, enumerable: false, configurable: false });
Object.getOwnPropertyDescriptor(obj, 'id');
```
- **Data property:** `value`, `writable`, `enumerable`, `configurable`. **Accessor property:** `get`, `set`, `enumerable`, `configurable`.
- Plain assignment creates properties as writable, enumerable, and configurable. `defineProperty` defaults them to `false`.
- **Getters/setters** enable computed and validated properties without changing the call syntax.


**Prerequisites:** modern ES6+ features (3.3.10), prototypes and classes (3.3.5), and types and coercion (3.3.9) for identity comparison and `NaN` handling.

**Why interviewers ask:** React and Redux made copying and identity a hiring screen, because `prev !== next` is the entire re-render mechanism and a candidate who mutates state has a bug they cannot see. Descriptors are the follow-up that filters the rest: they're what `freeze` is built on, why class methods are non-enumerable while object-literal methods are, why getters can be invisible, and why `Object.assign` triggers setters instead of creating own properties.

**Definition:** The semantics of obtaining a second reference to an object (shallow, deep, structured, and lossy-JSON), the discipline of changing state by producing a new value rather than mutating in place, and the property descriptor model — `value`/`writable`/`enumerable`/`configurable` for data properties, `get`/`set` for accessors — which is exactly what `Object.defineProperty` and `Object.freeze` manipulate.
**Questions**
1. Shallow vs deep copy? Which methods give which?
2. Why is `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify())` a poor deep clone? What would you use? *(`structuredClone`)*
3. What is immutability and why does React rely on it?
4. `Object.freeze` vs `seal` vs `preventExtensions`? Is freeze deep?
5. How do you update a nested object immutably? What does Immer do?
6. What are property descriptors?
7. What's structural sharing?
8. What are the downsides of immutability? *(copy cost for huge structures, verbosity, which Immer and persistent data structures mitigate)*


**Mistakes:**
- **Assuming spread and `Object.assign` create own data properties:** they perform a `Set`, so an inherited or own setter runs and no own property is created.
- **Spreading a `Map` or `Set`:** `{...myMap}` is `{}` because entries live in internal slots, not own properties — use `new Map(myMap)`.
- **Deep-freezing React state or an Immer draft,** making the next legitimate write throw in strict mode.
- **Using `JSON.stringify` for change detection:** key order matters, `undefined` disappears, cycles throw, and it is O(size) on every render.
- **Mutating with `sort`, `reverse`, or `splice` on an array that came from props or the store** — the copy you made was fine, the original is not.
- **Assuming `Object.freeze` protects against `arr.push`:** it throws only in strict mode, and only for own data properties; nested arrays stay wide open.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Immutable updates versus mutation:** immutable updates give O(1) change detection, safe sharing, and time-travel debugging, at the cost of allocation and verbosity; mutation is cheaper per operation but forces either in-place edits (breaking React/Redux) or a deep clone, which is usually worse than copying one path.
- **Hand-rolled `setIn` versus Immer:** hand-rolled is explicit, fast, dependency-free, and easy to reason about, but every path has to be written correctly; Immer gives draft-style mutation with structural sharing for free, at the cost of a Proxy layer, a `produce` boundary, and a dev-mode freeze that surprises people.
- **`Object.freeze` versus `as const` versus a runtime assert:** `as const` is compile-time and erased, `Object.freeze` is runtime and shallow, and a `deepFreeze` in development is the only thing that catches nested writes.
- **`structuredClone` versus a hand-written `deepClone`:** `structuredClone` for structured-data shapes (cycles, `Map`, `Set`, `Date`, `BigInt`, transferables, and often off the main thread); hand-written when you need prototype preservation, custom class semantics, or a deliberate decision about functions and accessors.
- **Accessors versus stored values:** an accessor lets you compute or validate without changing call syntax and can hide from enumeration, but a read runs the getter every time — reading twice can return two different values and can throw where a plain field couldn't.

**Common wrong answers:** "Spread clones the object", "`Object.freeze` prevents nested mutation", "`Object.freeze` throws when you assign" (silent in sloppy mode), and "Immer makes my code faster" (it makes mutation legal; performance is a separate claim).

**Probe:** "`Object.freeze(state)` in a reducer, then a nested update — what actually happens, and how would you make the freeze mean something?" (Expect: only `state`'s own properties become non-writable, so `state.user.name = 'x'` still succeeds and the "immutable" guarantee is a lie at depth. Options: deep-freeze once at module load for genuinely static fixtures, copy-then-mutate on paths you own, or rely on reducer discipline plus Immer's `produce`, which shallowly freezes its output and *throws* on a mutation of a frozen draft — the throw is the feature that surfaces bugs early.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Depth precision per method,** not the phrase "deep copy".
- **Why immutability is a correctness *and* performance tool:** reference equality for change detection, plus structural sharing so updates stay cheap.
- **Knowing `Object.freeze` is shallow** and that a write is a silent no-op in sloppy mode and a `TypeError` in strict mode/ESM.
- **Descriptors understood as the mechanism,** including the `defineProperty` defaults-to-`false` detail — that's the tell.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Only `b = a` gives you zero copies. Spread, `Object.assign`, `Array.from`, `Array.prototype.slice`, and a one-level `structuredClone` of a nested object are all shallow: they copy own enumerable properties, and the property *values* are the same references. So `{...a}` protects against `a.x = 1` but not against `a.nested.v = 2`. The real deep options are `structuredClone` — handles `Date`, `Map`, `Set`, `RegExp`, typed arrays, and cycles, but throws on functions and drops prototypes and accessors — and a hand-written `deepClone` with a `WeakMap` for cycles, which preserves prototypes but makes its own decisions about descriptors, symbols, and functions. `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify())` is deep but lossy: `undefined`, functions, and symbols vanish, `NaN` and `Infinity` become `null`, `Date` becomes a string, and a cycle throws."
**Mini task:** write `setIn(obj, path, value)` that returns a new object with structural sharing (unchanged branches keep identity), and tests proving `next.untouched === prev.untouched`.


**Self-check:** Name one operation that is deep for `{ a: { b: 1 } }` but shallow for `{ m: new Map([[1, { b: 1 }]]) }`, and say why.

**Resources:** MDN's `Object.freeze`, `Object.defineProperty`, `Object.assign`, and `structuredClone` pages; the HTML spec's structured clone algorithm; Immer's docs on `produce` and its "Pitfalls" page; Redux's "Fundamentals" chapter on immutability and time-travel debugging; the TC39-39 spec's `[[DefineOwnProperty]]` and `[[Get]]` sections.
---

# 3.3.15 Date, Intl, and Regex
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

**Date pitfalls**
- Months are **0-indexed** (`new Date(2025, 0, 31)` is January), and `getDay()` is the weekday while `getDate()` is the day of month.
- **Parsing:** `new Date('2025-01-31')` (date-only ISO) is parsed as **UTC**, but `new Date('2025-01-31T00:00:00')` (no offset) is parsed as **local time**. Non-ISO strings are implementation-dependent, so avoid them.
- A `Date` is a UTC timestamp (ms since epoch). **Time zones only matter on display and in calendar math.** `setMonth` overflow rolls over (`Jan 31` + 1 month → `Mar 3`).
- **Best practice:** store and transmit **UTC ISO 8601** (`toISOString()`) or epoch ms, convert at the edge for display, store the user's IANA time zone (`America/New_York`) for scheduling rather than offsets (DST), and never do date math by adding `86400000` across DST.
- **Libraries:** `date-fns`, `Luxon`, `Day.js` (avoid Moment, which is legacy). **`Temporal`** is the modern replacement API (check support and polyfills).
- **Intl** (built in, with no libraries needed):
```js
new Intl.DateTimeFormat('en-GB', { dateStyle: 'medium', timeZone: 'Asia/Kolkata' }).format(date);
new Intl.NumberFormat('de-DE', { style: 'currency', currency: 'EUR' }).format(1234.5);  // "1.234,50 €"
new Intl.RelativeTimeFormat('en', { numeric: 'auto' }).format(-1, 'day');               // "yesterday"
new Intl.PluralRules('en').select(1);                                                   // 'one'
new Intl.ListFormat('en').format(['a', 'b', 'c']);                                      // "a, b, and c"
```
Also `Intl.Collator` for locale-aware sorting (`['ä','z','a'].sort(new Intl.Collator('de').compare)`).

**Regex essentials**
- **Syntax:** character classes `\d \w \s [a-z]`, quantifiers `* + ? {n,m}` (**greedy** by default, lazy with `?`), anchors `^ $ \b`, groups `( )`, **named groups** `(?<year>\d{4})`, non-capturing `(?:…)`, alternation `|`, **lookahead** `(?=…)` `(?!…)`, **lookbehind** `(?<=…)` `(?<!…)`, and backreferences `\1`.
- **Flags:** `g` (global), `i`, `m`, `s` (dot matches newline), `u` (Unicode), `y` (sticky), `d` (indices), and `v` (set notation).
- **API:** `str.match(re)`, `str.matchAll(re)` (iterator with groups, needs `g`), `re.test(str)`, `re.exec(str)`, `str.replace(re, fn|string)`, `replaceAll`, and `split(re)`.
- **Gotcha: a regex with `g` or `y` is stateful** (`lastIndex`). Calling `re.test()` repeatedly on a shared `/x/g` alternates true/false. Avoid `g` with `test`, or reset `lastIndex`.
- **ReDoS:** nested quantifiers like `(a+)+$` or `(.*)*` backtrack exponentially on crafted input, so one request can block the event loop. Avoid ambiguous nested repetition, cap input length, use atomic-style patterns where possible, and use linear-time engines (RE2) for untrusted patterns.
- **Don't parse HTML or validate emails with regex.** Use a parser (or `type=email` plus verification).

```js
const m = '2025-01-31'.match(/(?<y>\d{4})-(?<m>\d{2})-(?<d>\d{2})/);
m.groups.y;                                        // '2025'
'a-b_c'.replace(/[-_](\w)/g, (_, c) => c.toUpperCase());   // 'aBC' (camelCase)
const re = /\d/g; re.test('1'); re.test('1');      // true, false!
```


**Prerequisites:** types and coercion (3.3.9), modern ES6+ features (3.3.10), and the event loop (3.3.6) — which is why one bad regex takes the whole page down.

**Why interviewers ask:** Time bugs and ReDoS are the two most common production incidents in a frontend codebase and neither shows up in a happy-path test. The question is a proxy for "will this person ship a bug that pages someone at 3am": will you store UTC and format at the edge, will you avoid month arithmetic, do you know a user-supplied pattern can hang the single thread. `Intl` separates people who know `toLocaleString` from people who know `Intl.DateTimeFormat` with an explicit `timeZone` and a cached formatter.

**Definition:** Three standard-library surfaces that are boring and brutal: `Date` (a wrapper around a UTC millisecond timestamp, with 0-indexed months and implementation-defined string parsing), `Intl` (the built-in, locale-aware formatting, pluralisation, and collation API), and `RegExp` (a backtracking matcher whose flags, `lastIndex`, and worst-case complexity are what actually bite).
**Questions**
1. Why can `new Date('2025-01-31')` show Jan 30 in some timezones? *(UTC parse, then local display in a negative-offset zone)*
2. How would you store and display times for users in different time zones?
3. Why does `new Date(2025, 0, 31).setMonth(1)` land in March?
4. What's ReDoS? Give an example pattern and a fix.
5. Greedy vs lazy quantifiers? Lookahead vs lookbehind?
6. Why does `re.test()` return alternating results with the `g` flag?
7. How do you format currency and relative time without a library?
8. Write a regex for a password policy, then explain why a regex alone isn't enough. *(length is better than complexity rules, check against breached lists, and rate-limit)*


**Mistakes:**
- **`new Date(2025, 0, 31).setMonth(1)`:** February has no 31st, so it rolls into March — build dates from components or clamp the day yourself.
- **Using `getDay()` as the day of the month:** it's the weekday (0–6 from Sunday); `getDate()` is the day.
- **Mixing `Date` with duration arithmetic** or comparing a `Date` to a "difference in minutes" without a timezone, then formatting as if it were local.
- **Reusing a `/g` regex across `test`/`exec` calls** without resetting `lastIndex`, which yields the alternating true/false that looks like a regex bug.
- **Expecting `match` without `g` to return all matches,** or calling `matchAll` on a non-global regex, which throws.
- **Interpolating untrusted input into a regex,** which either changes the pattern or hands an attacker a ReDoS primitive.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`Date` versus `Temporal` versus a library:** `Temporal` for any real date arithmetic (`until`, `since`, `add` with calendar units, zone conversion) once you can require a modern baseline or ship a polyfill; `date-fns` or `Day.js` for tree-shakeable helpers and ecosystem breadth; Moment only in legacy code. `Date` has no timezone-aware API at all, so scheduling is where you need one of the others.
- **`Intl` versus hand-rolled formatting versus a library:** `Intl` is built in, CLDR-complete, and correct for currency, dates, plurals, lists, and collation — use it, cache instances; hand-rolling is only worth it in a genuine hot loop and will be wrong for some locale; a library matters only when you don't want to ship locale data.
- **Fresh formatter per call versus a cached instance:** cached is dramatically faster and is what production code should do; constructing an `Intl` object inside a render or loop is a measurable perf bug.
- **Hoisted regex literal versus a constructed `new RegExp`:** a literal is compiled once but carries `lastIndex` state, which is the classic `g`-flag trap; a constructed regex is fresh each call, which is the fix when you must loop with `g`.
- **Regex versus a real parser:** regex for flat text (slugs, camelCase, ISO fields, simple log lines); a real parser, `URLSearchParams`, or `Intl.Segmenter` for anything nested — query strings, HTML, CSS selectors, grapheme boundaries.

**Common wrong answers:** Storing `new Date().toLocaleString()` "because it's easier" (that's formatting in the *server's* locale and zone); "months are 1-indexed"; "a month is always 30 days"; "`86400000` ms is a day"; "regex is fast so ReDoS isn't practical"; and validating an email address with one regex.

**Probe:** "A user in `America/New_York` books 09:00 every weekday. What do you store, and what is the UTC instant on the Monday after DST ends?" (Expect: store `{ hour: 9, zone: 'America/New_York' }` — not an offset and not a UTC time. Compute it by formatting candidate instants in that zone as an inverse lookup, or with `Temporal` by combining a plain date/time with the zone. The trap: 09:00 New York is 13:00 UTC while DST is in effect and 14:00 UTC when it isn't, so a hardcoded `-04:00` silently breaks every winter meeting.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **The UTC/local/ISO-parse distinction stated precisely:** date-only ISO forms parse as UTC, while a date-time without an offset parses as local.
- **Saying "store the IANA zone, not the offset,"** because a fixed offset is wrong for half of every year.
- **Awareness that month arithmetic overflows** and that `+86400000` is wrong across a DST boundary.
- **Concrete ReDoS mitigations,** not the phrase "it's exponential".

**Model answer, Q2:** "Store one canonical value: UTC ISO 8601 from `toISOString()`, or epoch milliseconds. Never store a formatted string or a fixed offset. For display, convert at the edge with `new Intl.DateTimeFormat(locale, { dateStyle: 'medium', timeZone, timeZoneName: 'short' })` and cache the formatter — constructing one is expensive and it's effectively a per-locale constant. If I need to schedule something I store the user's IANA zone name from `Intl.DateTimeFormat().resolvedOptions().timeZone`, not an offset, because offsets change with DST while the zone encodes the rule. I never add `86400000` to mean "tomorrow"; I build a new date from components in the target zone, or use `Temporal`. And I always render the zone alongside the time, otherwise users file the wrong-day bug."
**Mini task:** write `parseQuery(url)`, `camelToSnake`, and `slugify` using regex, then build a scheduler utility that converts "9am America/New_York every weekday" into UTC instants across a DST change.


**Self-check:** State from memory why `new Date('2025-01-31')` can render as Jan 30, and name the single change to your storage format that removes the entire class of bug.

**Resources:** MDN's `Date`, `Intl`, and `RegExp` reference pages plus "Date and time formats used in JavaScript"; the ECMA-402 specification for `Intl`; the `Temporal` proposal docs and MDN's Temporal guide; OWASP's "Regular expression Denial of Service" entry; `safe-regex` and `node-re2` for linear-time or statically validated matching.
---

# 3.3.16 Memory and Garbage Collection (JS view)
🔴 Advanced · **Good-to-know** (cross-ref 2.6.2)

- **Reachability:** a value is retained if it's reachable from a root (globals, the current stack, active closures, timers, event listeners). V8 uses generational, incremental, and concurrent mark-and-sweep GC.
- **Typical JS leaks and fixes**

| Leak | Fix |
|---|---|
| Event listeners never removed | `removeEventListener`, `AbortController` signal, cleanup in effects |
| `setInterval` never cleared | `clearInterval` on teardown |
| Closures capturing big objects | Narrow captured scope, null references |
| Unbounded caches and globals | LRU with a max size, TTL, `WeakMap` |
| Detached DOM nodes held in JS | Drop references on removal |
| Node: per-request data in module-level arrays, unbounded queues, uncleared listeners (`MaxListenersExceededWarning`) | Bounded structures, backpressure, cleanup |
- **Tooling:** DevTools Memory (heap snapshot diff, allocation timeline), Performance monitor, Node `--inspect` plus heap snapshots, `process.memoryUsage()`, `--heap-prof`, and `clinic heapprofiler`.
- **GC pauses:** allocate less in hot paths, reuse objects and buffers, and avoid creating closures per frame in animation loops.


**Prerequisites:** closures (3.3.3 — the most common retainer), prototypes and classes (3.3.5), and the event loop (3.3.6) — timers, listeners, and pending promises are all roots.

**Why interviewers ask:** Because "it gets slower the longer you use it" is a real bug class that tooling keeps invisible until you have the vocabulary. It separates candidates who can tell *high memory usage* (fine) from *unbounded growth* (a leak), and who reach for heap snapshots and dominators unprompted. It is also the one place where naming a leak as a *retention path* instead of "too many objects" shows real experience.

**Definition:** Why JavaScript holds onto memory — reachability from roots rather than reference counting — and what that implies in practice: V8's generational, incremental, concurrent mark-and-sweep collector, the pause cost of allocating in hot paths, and the retention patterns that keep dead data alive in a live-looking object graph.
**Questions:** What makes an object eligible for GC? Name 4 leak sources in a SPA and how to detect them. How would you find a leak in a Node service? *(Watch RSS and heap over time, take snapshots under load, compare retained sizes, and inspect dominators.)*


**Self-check:** Name the root that keeps a `setInterval` callback's captured closure alive, and explain why unregistering the interval is the only thing that works.

**Resources:** V8's "Trash talk: the Orinoco garbage collector" and the DevTools Memory panel docs on heap snapshots, dominators, and allocation instrumentation; MDN's `WeakMap` and `AbortController` pages; the `FinalizationRegistry` proposal explainer; Node's `--inspect`, `v8.getHeapStatistics()`, and `process.memoryUsage()` documentation.

**Mini task:** Build a leak on purpose: a counter component that subscribes to `window` and starts a `setInterval` on mount and never cleans up. In DevTools force GC, take a heap snapshot, locate the detached node and the growing closure context, and write down the retainer chain. Then fix it and take a second snapshot to prove the retained size is flat across ten mount/unmount cycles.

**Mistakes:**
- **Returning nothing from an effect that subscribes,** or a cleanup that forgets to remove the listener it added.
- **Storing per-request or per-user data in a module-level variable** on the server, sharing state across users.
- **Keeping a `Map` keyed on DOM nodes or class instances** that outlive the page — the retention path DevTools will happily show you.
- **Registering `window`/`scroll` listeners in a hot path** (per keystroke, per scroll frame) with no teardown.
- **Expecting `null`ing a variable to release a closure's reference** when the closure captured a `const` binding — you can null a `let`, you cannot null what the closure holds.
- **Chasing heap size instead of retained size:** 400 MB that is collected fine is not a leak; 20 MB that is never freed is.

**Trade-offs:**
- **WeakMap cache versus bounded LRU:** a `WeakMap` keyed on objects cannot be what keeps them alive, so it's leak-proof by construction and needs no eviction; an LRU with a max size or TTL gives deterministic memory, hit-rate stats, and TTL semantics, but will evict a key whose object is still in use and you must implement eviction (a `Map`'s insertion order makes it O(1)).
- **Reducing allocation versus reusing buffers:** short-lived allocations in a hot loop are cheap for a generational collector but still produce scavenge traffic and pauses; reusing arrays, `Buffer`s, or typed arrays removes that, at the price of stale references unless you null them out — so pools trade GC time for bug risk.
- **Explicit cleanup versus framework-managed cleanup:** manual `removeEventListener`/`clearInterval` is framework-independent and auditable; effect cleanups and `AbortController` signals mean less code but hide the lifecycle. Prefer explicit teardown in libraries, signal-based cleanup in components.
- **`WeakRef`/`FinalizationRegistry` versus leaking on purpose:** fine for optional caches and diagnostics; unacceptable for anything whose correctness depends on when they run.
- **GC tuning flags versus fixing the leak:** `--max-old-space-size` and `--max-semi-space-size` move the failure point (OOM later instead of a pause sooner); they're a mitigation for a known workload profile, never a fix.

**Common wrong answers:** "Call `delete obj.x` to free memory" (it releases a reference, not memory, and only helps if nothing else points at the object); "closures are a memory leak" (they're fine — retention is the problem); "memory grew, so we have a leak" (V8 grows the heap opportunistically and shrinks lazily under pressure — a monotonic climb across identical GC'd cycles is the signal); and "`global.gc()` fixed it" (that's a `--expose-gc` debug tool and a diagnostic, not a fix).

**Probe:** "A long-lived websocket handler appends parsed messages to a module-level array that a chart reads. Memory climbs linearly and never drops, and the heap snapshot shows a `Detached HTMLDivElement`. Where are the retained paths, and what's the smallest change?" (Expect: the module-level array is a root-reachable variable growing without bound — that's one leak; the detached div means the chart (or a listener closing over it) held a reference to a DOM node after unmount — that's the other. Smallest change: bound the buffer (ring buffer, or `splice` to the last N), clear the node reference and unsubscribe on close, then verify with two snapshot diffs around mount/unmount cycles until the detached count is flat.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Correct root list** — globals, stack, closures, timers, listeners, DOM — and that cycles alone don't prevent collection.
- **Eligible versus collected:** GC timing is non-deterministic, you cannot force it in production, so the only lever you have is ceasing to make things reachable.
- **Naming leaks as retention paths,** not as "we allocate too much".
- **An actual diagnostic procedure:** force GC, snapshot, diff or compare, read dominators, confirm growth with a repeated-action loop.

**Model answer, Q1:** "An object is eligible when it becomes unreachable from the GC roots: the global object, the current call stack and its locals, the scope chain of any live closure, and any host-held reference such as a timer, an event listener, a DOM node still in the document, or a `Map`/`Set` key you stored. It's purely reachability — there's no cycle collector, and `delete obj.x` frees a reference, not memory. In a SPA the four most common leaks are a listener never removed (fix with `removeEventListener`, or hand `AbortController.signal` to your API and call `abort()` on teardown), an uncleared `setInterval`/`setTimeout`, a closure over a large object that a long-lived callback keeps alive, and an unbounded `Map` or module-level array keyed on component instances or DOM nodes. I detect them by scripting a navigation loop, watching `performance.memory.usedJSHeapSize` or `process.memoryUsage().heapUsed` for a monotonic climb across identical cycles, then forcing GC, taking a heap snapshot, and reading the dominant retainer path to a detached node or a suspicious closure context."
---

# 3.3.17 MUST-IMPLEMENT-FROM-SCRATCH (22 Problems)


**Mistakes:**
- **Not stating assumptions**, then being unable to answer a probe about the code you just wrote.
- **Mutating the input inside a "pure" helper:** `sort`, `reverse`, `splice`, or pushing into the array handed to `chunk`.
- **Omitting the empty-array guard in `reduce`,** so a bare `reduce` throws a `TypeError` nobody mentioned.
- **Counting on `fn.length` for currying** when the signature has defaults or rest parameters — `fn.length` stops at the first of those.
- **Ignoring sparse arrays:** `myMap`, `myFilter`, and `myReduce` must check `i in this` or holes become visited slots.
- **Caching rejected promises** in `memoize`, turning a transient network failure into a permanent one.
- **Using `emit` while mutating the listener array** without copying it, so a listener that unsubscribes mid-emit corrupts the iteration.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Reference solution versus spec-first practice:** copying the section's code teaches nothing; writing cold from the spec and *then* diffing teaches everything. Practise the first pass unassisted, the diff second.
- **Small, complete, readable versus clever:** a 40-line readable Promise beats a shortcut that desugars through `async`, because the interviewer is scoring your model of the mechanism. Optimise only after the semantics are right.
- **Hand-rolled versus library:** every one of these exists in production (`lodash`, `p-limit`, `lru-cache`, `events`, `immer`). Knowing when *not* to hand-roll is itself a judgement signal — the exercise builds understanding, not a library.
- **`debounce` versus `throttle`:** "act when the user stops" versus "act at a fixed rate while the user is still going"; combining them (throttle with a trailing edge) is what a drag-and-drop preview actually needs.
- **WeakMap memo versus bounded `Map`:** the `WeakMap` cannot leak and needs no eviction but accepts only object keys and gives no size or iteration; a bounded `Map` is inspectable and evictable but is a thing you can forget to bound.
- **Spec-complete versus interview-scoped:** a spec-complete Promise handles subclassing and species; an interview-scoped one skips that. Say which one you're writing, out loud, before you start.

**Common wrong answers:** Implementing `bind` by returning an arrow (loses `new.target` and `prototype`, so `new bound()` breaks and `instanceof` fails); a Promise that calls handlers synchronously when already settled (breaks async ordering, produces Zalgo); a `debounce` that doesn't store the latest args (the trailing call fires with the *first* event's arguments); `sort()` with no comparator (lexicographic, so `[10, 9, 1].sort()` is `[1, 10, 9]`); `deepClone` using a `Set` of visited objects instead of a source-to-clone `WeakMap` (shared references silently become separate clones); `retry` with fixed backoff and no jitter (thundering herd); and `Promise.all` over unbounded input, which is connection exhaustion rather than concurrency.

**Probe:** "Your `deepClone` handles `Map`, `Set`, and `Date`. Here is an object with a `#secret` private field, and here are two objects that point at each other. What happens in each case?" (Expect: private fields are keyed to the *instance*, so a clone — even one made with `Object.create(Object.getPrototypeOf(value))` — cannot touch the original's `#secret`, and any code that does throws; that's why private state needs an explicit copy hook rather than reflection. The cycle is fine only if `seen` is threaded through *all* branches, including the `Map`/`Set` ones, because that's exactly where most implementations break: a plain-object-only memo recurses forever or duplicates shared references.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Assumptions stated before writing, and revised when probed** — which is what the section's own "How to practice" asks for.
- **Spec-level detail that only shows up if you understand the mechanism:** the three return paths of `new`, `new.target` inside a bound function, resolve-once plus thenable adoption, empty-iterable handling in `all` and `any`, `AggregateError`.
- **Edge-case ownership:** cycles, `NaN` (and `-0`), symbols, sparse arrays, `Map`/`Set` ordering, listeners mutating the list during `emit`.
- **Complexity claims that hold up:** LRU in O(1), `mapLimit` linear overall, `deepClone` linear in node count.
- **Honesty about omissions,** which is worth more than a silently incomplete implementation.

**Model answer, Q1:** "I'd insist on `new`/`instanceof` (2), the Promise core (3) plus one combinator (4), and one of the timing pair (5 or 6) — those four test spec recall, ordering, and edge-case instincts in one pass. If the role touches state or forms, I'd add `deepClone` (9). I'd skip the `map`/`filter`/`reduce` polyfills (11) because they're recall rather than reasoning, and I'd skip the `groupBy`/`chunk`/`zip` set (22) because it's typing speed. What I'd say up front: I'm implementing Promises/A+ semantics rather than the static methods, I'm not covering species or subclass behaviour, and I'll tell you when I assume rather than check. That framing is what buys me the time to think."

**Questions**
1. Which of these 22 would you insist on in a real interview, which two would you skip, and what assumptions would you state out loud before typing?
2. Walk me through your `new` implementation: what are the three possible return paths, and what happens if `Ctor` is an arrow function or a `class`?
3. In your Promise, why must callbacks be deferred to a microtask even when the promise was already fulfilled before `then` was called?
4. `debounce` versus `throttle` — which one for a type-ahead search box, and what do the `leading` and `trailing` flags actually change about the number of calls?
5. How do you get O(1) `get`/`set` in an LRU without a library, and what does the `memoize` in the same list have to get right that the LRU doesn't?
6. Which edge cases did you deliberately leave out, and how would a reviewer notice within the first two minutes?

**Key concepts**
- **Reconstruct the spec, not the snippet.** The exercise is to derive observable behaviour from the ECMA-262, Promises/A+, and MDN semantics, and only then write code. The solutions in this section are the *output* of that derivation — diffing yours against them is the feedback loop, and reading them first destroys the exercise.
- **`call`, `apply`, and `bind` are one mechanism.** All three reduce to "make `this` be the first argument". The real work is boxing primitives for sloppy mode, honouring `new.target` so a bound function is still constructible, and never leaving a temporary property on the receiver — hence a `Symbol` key plus a `delete` in a `finally`.
- **Promise correctness is a state machine plus a scheduling rule.** Three states, one irreversible transition, a resolve-once guard, thenable adoption behind a re-entrancy guard, and callbacks that go through `queueMicrotask` — *including* when the promise is already settled, or you get Zalgo.
- **Combinators differ in failure policy, not plumbing.** `all` fails fast and preserves input order; `allSettled` never fails; `race` settles once; `any` fails only after every input fails, with an `AggregateError`. All of them iterate eagerly, so the empty input needs explicit handling.
- **Timing helpers are about cancellation, not timers.** Each needs a stored `timer`, the latest args, the latest `this`, and a `cancel` — plus `flush` for `debounce` — and in React the wrapper must be stable (`useRef`/`useMemo`) or every render resets the timer.
- **Cycles need a `WeakMap`, not an array.** `deepClone` and `deepEqual` both thread a source-to-clone map so a cyclic structure terminates *and* shared references stay shared; a `WeakMap` also means no bookkeeping leak across long runs.
- **`Map` insertion order gives you O(1) LRU.** Delete-then-set marks recency and `.keys().next().value` is the LRU end, which is why the `Map` version and the classic doubly-linked-list version have identical complexity. The real discussion is eviction policy and the cache-key strategy that `memoize` needs and `LRUCache` doesn't.
- **Concurrency control is backpressure, not `Promise.all`.** `mapLimit` and `TaskQueue` exist because `await Promise.all(items.map(...))` opens N connections at once. The shared pattern is a shared cursor plus N workers, and a `finally` that always releases the slot.

**Prerequisites:** `this` binding (3.3.4), prototypes and classes (3.3.5), promises (3.3.7), async/await and concurrency patterns (3.3.8), copying and immutability (3.3.14), and memory and GC (3.3.16).

**Why interviewers ask:** Reimplementation is the only reliable test of whether you own a mechanism instead of having memorised its call signature. `call`/`apply`/`bind` and `new` probe call and construction semantics; the Promise exercises probe microtask ordering and thenable adoption; the timing and retry helpers probe whether you think about cancellation, leading versus trailing edges, jitter, and backpressure; `deepClone` and `deepEqual` probe the parts everyone skips — cycles, `Map`/`Set`, symbols, `NaN`. What is actually being scored is your narration: assumptions stated up front, edge cases named as you go, and an honest account of what you left out.

**Definition:** A curated set of 22 implementation exercises — `call`/`apply`/`bind`, `new` and `instanceof`, a Promise core, the Promise combinators, `debounce`/`throttle`, `curry`, `memoize`, a circular-safe `deepClone`, an event emitter, array polyfills, `flatten`, `pipe`/`compose`, `once`/`sleep`/`withTimeout`, `retry`, `mapLimit`, an O(1) LRU, lodash-style `get`, `deepEqual`, a Redux-like store, a task queue, and a set of one-liners — written from memory, with the goal being the specification you reconstruct rather than the code you paste.
**How to practice:** write each from memory, then test it against edge cases. In an interview, state assumptions, handle the main edge cases, and mention what you left out.

**Self-check:** Pick three of the 22, write them cold in twenty minutes, and for each one say out loud which spec sentence you are implementing and which edge case you are deliberately skipping.

**Resources:** MDN's `Function.prototype.bind`, `new`/constructor, and `Promise` pages; the Promises/A+ spec at promisesaplus.com for the resolution procedure; V8's "Promises and async/await" blog post for microtask ordering; "You Don't Know JS Yet" chapters 4–5 for coercion and `this` context; the `p-limit` and `lru-cache` READMEs to diff a production implementation against yours.

### 1. `call`, `apply`, `bind`
```js
Function.prototype.myCall = function (ctx, ...args) {
  ctx = ctx == null ? globalThis : Object(ctx);
  const key = Symbol('fn');
  ctx[key] = this;
  try { return ctx[key](...args); } finally { delete ctx[key]; }
};
Function.prototype.myApply = function (ctx, args = []) { return this.myCall(ctx, ...args); };

Function.prototype.myBind = function (ctx, ...preset) {
  const fn = this;
  if (typeof fn !== 'function') throw new TypeError('Bind must be called on a function');
  function bound(...args) {
    // when used with `new`, `this` is the new instance and ctx is ignored
    return fn.apply(new.target ? this : ctx, [...preset, ...args]);
  }
  if (fn.prototype) bound.prototype = Object.create(fn.prototype);
  return bound;
};
```
*Edge cases:* primitives as `this` (boxed), `new` on bound functions, preset args, a Symbol key to avoid collisions.

### 2. `new` and `instanceof`
```js
function myNew(Ctor, ...args) {
  const obj = Object.create(Ctor.prototype);
  const result = Ctor.apply(obj, args);
  return result !== null && (typeof result === 'object' || typeof result === 'function') ? result : obj;
}
function myInstanceOf(obj, Ctor) {
  if (obj === null || (typeof obj !== 'object' && typeof obj !== 'function')) return false;
  for (let p = Object.getPrototypeOf(obj); p; p = Object.getPrototypeOf(p)) {
    if (p === Ctor.prototype) return true;
  }
  return false;
}
```

### 3. Minimal Promise (A+-style core)
```js
class MyPromise {
  #state = 'pending'; #value; #callbacks = [];

  constructor(executor) {
    let called = false;
    const resolve = v => { if (called) return; called = true; this.#resolve(v); };
    const reject  = e => { if (called) return; called = true; this.#settle('rejected', e); };
    try { executor(resolve, reject); } catch (e) { reject(e); }
  }

  #resolve(x) {
    if (x === this) return this.#settle('rejected', new TypeError('Chaining cycle detected'));
    if (x !== null && (typeof x === 'object' || typeof x === 'function')) {
      let then;
      try { then = x.then; } catch (e) { return this.#settle('rejected', e); }
      if (typeof then === 'function') {            // adopt thenables
        let done = false;
        try {
          then.call(x,
            y => { if (!done) { done = true; this.#resolve(y); } },
            r => { if (!done) { done = true; this.#settle('rejected', r); } });
        } catch (e) { if (!done) { done = true; this.#settle('rejected', e); } }
        return;
      }
    }
    this.#settle('fulfilled', x);
  }

  #settle(state, value) {
    if (this.#state !== 'pending') return;
    this.#state = state; this.#value = value;
    for (const cb of this.#callbacks) queueMicrotask(cb);
    this.#callbacks = [];
  }

  then(onFulfilled, onRejected) {
    return new MyPromise((resolve, reject) => {
      const run = () => {
        const ok = this.#state === 'fulfilled';
        const handler = ok ? onFulfilled : onRejected;
        if (typeof handler !== 'function') return ok ? resolve(this.#value) : reject(this.#value);
        try { resolve(handler(this.#value)); } catch (e) { reject(e); }
      };
      if (this.#state === 'pending') this.#callbacks.push(run);
      else queueMicrotask(run);
    });
  }
  catch(onRejected) { return this.then(undefined, onRejected); }
  finally(fn) {
    return this.then(
      v => MyPromise.resolve(fn()).then(() => v),
      e => MyPromise.resolve(fn()).then(() => { throw e; }));
  }
  static resolve(v) { return v instanceof MyPromise ? v : new MyPromise(r => r(v)); }
  static reject(e) { return new MyPromise((_, rej) => rej(e)); }
}
```
*Talking points:* state immutability, async callbacks via microtasks, chaining returns a new promise, thenable adoption, and the "resolve once" guard.

### 4. `Promise.all`, `allSettled`, `race`, `any`
```js
const all = iterable => new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
  const items = [...iterable];
  if (!items.length) return resolve([]);
  const out = new Array(items.length); let left = items.length;
  items.forEach((p, i) => Promise.resolve(p).then(v => { out[i] = v; if (--left === 0) resolve(out); }, reject));
});

const allSettled = iterable => all([...iterable].map(p =>
  Promise.resolve(p).then(value => ({ status: 'fulfilled', value }),
                          reason => ({ status: 'rejected', reason }))));

const race = iterable => new Promise((resolve, reject) =>
  [...iterable].forEach(p => Promise.resolve(p).then(resolve, reject)));

const any = iterable => new Promise((resolve, reject) => {
  const items = [...iterable]; const errors = new Array(items.length); let left = items.length;
  if (!left) return reject(new AggregateError([], 'All promises were rejected'));
  items.forEach((p, i) => Promise.resolve(p).then(resolve, e => {
    errors[i] = e;
    if (--left === 0) reject(new AggregateError(errors, 'All promises were rejected'));
  }));
});
```
*Edge cases:* empty input, non-promise values, preserving result order (index, not completion order).

### 5. `debounce` (leading/trailing, cancel)
```js
function debounce(fn, wait = 0, { leading = false, trailing = true } = {}) {
  let timer, lastArgs, lastThis, result;
  const invoke = () => { const a = lastArgs, t = lastThis; lastArgs = lastThis = undefined; result = fn.apply(t, a); return result; };
  function debounced(...args) {
    lastArgs = args; lastThis = this;
    const callNow = leading && !timer;
    clearTimeout(timer);
    timer = setTimeout(() => { timer = undefined; if (trailing && lastArgs) invoke(); }, wait);
    if (callNow) invoke();
    return result;
  }
  debounced.cancel = () => { clearTimeout(timer); timer = undefined; lastArgs = lastThis = undefined; };
  debounced.flush = () => { if (timer) { clearTimeout(timer); timer = undefined; if (lastArgs) return invoke(); } return result; };
  return debounced;
}
```
*Use:* search-as-you-type, resize, autosave (wait for a pause). In React, keep the debounced function stable with `useRef`/`useMemo`, and cancel on unmount.

### 6. `throttle` (leading and trailing)
```js
function throttle(fn, wait) {
  let last = 0, timer = null, lastArgs, lastThis;
  const run = () => { last = Date.now(); timer = null; const a = lastArgs, t = lastThis; lastArgs = lastThis = undefined; fn.apply(t, a); };
  function throttled(...args) {
    lastArgs = args; lastThis = this;
    const remaining = wait - (Date.now() - last);
    if (remaining <= 0) { clearTimeout(timer); timer = null; run(); }
    else if (!timer) timer = setTimeout(run, remaining);
  }
  throttled.cancel = () => { clearTimeout(timer); timer = null; last = 0; lastArgs = lastThis = undefined; };
  return throttled;
}
```
*Debounce vs throttle:* debounce fires after activity *stops*, and throttle fires at most once per interval *during* activity (scroll position, mousemove, rate-limited API calls).

### 7. `curry` and partial application
```js
const curry = fn =>
  function curried(...args) {
    return args.length >= fn.length
      ? fn.apply(this, args)
      : (...more) => curried.apply(this, [...args, ...more]);
  };
const add3 = curry((a, b, c) => a + b + c);
add3(1)(2)(3); add3(1, 2)(3); add3(1)(2, 3);   // 6
const partial = (fn, ...preset) => (...rest) => fn(...preset, ...rest);
```
*Caveat:* `fn.length` ignores default and rest parameters.

### 8. `memoize`
```js
function memoize(fn, resolver = (...a) => JSON.stringify(a)) {
  const cache = new Map();
  return function (...args) {
    const key = resolver(...args);
    if (cache.has(key)) return cache.get(key);
    const val = fn.apply(this, args);
    cache.set(key, val);
    return val;
  };
}
```
*Discuss:* key serialization cost and collisions, unbounded growth (add an LRU or TTL), caching rejected promises (evict on rejection), and use only with pure functions.

### 9. `deepClone` (circular-safe)
```js
function deepClone(value, seen = new WeakMap()) {
  if (value === null || typeof value !== 'object') return value;      // primitives and functions by reference
  if (seen.has(value)) return seen.get(value);                        // circular references
  if (value instanceof Date) return new Date(value);
  if (value instanceof RegExp) return new RegExp(value.source, value.flags);
  if (value instanceof Map) {
    const m = new Map(); seen.set(value, m);
    value.forEach((v, k) => m.set(deepClone(k, seen), deepClone(v, seen))); return m;
  }
  if (value instanceof Set) {
    const s = new Set(); seen.set(value, s);
    value.forEach(v => s.add(deepClone(v, seen))); return s;
  }
  const out = Array.isArray(value) ? [] : Object.create(Object.getPrototypeOf(value));
  seen.set(value, out);
  for (const key of Reflect.ownKeys(value)) out[key] = deepClone(value[key], seen);   // includes symbols
  return out;
}
```
*Mention:* `structuredClone` covers most cases. This version ignores property descriptors and non-enumerable properties.

### 10. Event emitter
```js
class EventEmitter {
  #events = new Map();
  on(event, fn) { (this.#events.get(event) ?? this.#events.set(event, []).get(event)).push(fn); return this; }
  off(event, fn) {
    const list = this.#events.get(event); if (!list) return this;
    const i = list.findIndex(l => l === fn || l.original === fn);
    if (i > -1) list.splice(i, 1);
    return this;
  }
  once(event, fn) {
    const wrapper = (...a) => { this.off(event, wrapper); fn.apply(this, a); };
    wrapper.original = fn;
    return this.on(event, wrapper);
  }
  emit(event, ...args) {
    const list = this.#events.get(event);
    if (!list?.length) return false;
    for (const l of [...list]) l.apply(this, args);     // copy: listeners may unsubscribe during emit
    return true;
  }
}
```

### 11. Array polyfills: `map`, `filter`, `reduce`
```js
Array.prototype.myMap = function (cb, thisArg) {
  const out = new Array(this.length);
  for (let i = 0; i < this.length; i++) if (i in this) out[i] = cb.call(thisArg, this[i], i, this);
  return out;
};
Array.prototype.myFilter = function (cb, thisArg) {
  const out = [];
  for (let i = 0; i < this.length; i++) if (i in this && cb.call(thisArg, this[i], i, this)) out.push(this[i]);
  return out;
};
Array.prototype.myReduce = function (cb, ...init) {
  let i = 0, acc;
  if (init.length) acc = init[0];
  else {
    while (i < this.length && !(i in this)) i++;                 // find first present element (sparse arrays)
    if (i >= this.length) throw new TypeError('Reduce of empty array with no initial value');
    acc = this[i++];
  }
  for (; i < this.length; i++) if (i in this) acc = cb(acc, this[i], i, this);
  return acc;
};
```

### 12. `flatten` with depth
```js
function flatten(arr, depth = 1) {
  const out = [];
  for (const x of arr) {
    if (Array.isArray(x) && depth > 0) out.push(...flatten(x, depth - 1));
    else out.push(x);
  }
  return out;
}
// flatten([1,[2,[3,[4]]]], Infinity) → [1,2,3,4]
```

### 13. `pipe` and `compose`
```js
const pipe = (...fns) => x => fns.reduce((v, f) => f(v), x);
const compose = (...fns) => x => fns.reduceRight((v, f) => f(v), x);
const pipeAsync = (...fns) => x => fns.reduce((p, f) => p.then(f), Promise.resolve(x));
```

### 14. `once`, `sleep`, and `timeout`
```js
const once = fn => { let done = false, val; return function (...a) { if (!done) { done = true; val = fn.apply(this, a); } return val; }; };
const sleep = ms => new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, ms));
const withTimeout = (promise, ms) => {
  let t;
  const timeout = new Promise((_, rej) => { t = setTimeout(() => rej(new Error(`Timed out after ${ms}ms`)), ms); });
  return Promise.race([promise, timeout]).finally(() => clearTimeout(t));
};
```

### 15. `retry` with exponential backoff and full jitter
```js
async function retry(fn, { retries = 3, baseMs = 200, factor = 2, shouldRetry = () => true } = {}) {
  for (let attempt = 0; ; attempt++) {
    try { return await fn(attempt); }
    catch (err) {
      if (attempt >= retries || !shouldRetry(err)) throw err;
      await sleep(Math.random() * baseMs * factor ** attempt);       // full jitter avoids thundering herd
    }
  }
}
```
