*Discuss:* only retry transient or idempotent failures (5xx, network, 429 with `Retry-After`), cap the total time, and use idempotency keys for POST.

### 16. Concurrency limiter (`mapLimit`) and promise pool
```js
async function mapLimit(items, limit, fn) {
  const results = new Array(items.length);
  let next = 0;
  async function worker() {
    while (next < items.length) {
      const i = next++;                        // safe: single-threaded
      results[i] = await fn(items[i], i);
    }
  }
  await Promise.all(Array.from({ length: Math.min(limit, items.length) }, worker));
  return results;
}
```

### 17. LRU cache (O(1) with `Map` insertion order)
```js
class LRUCache {
  #cap; #map = new Map();
  constructor(cap) { this.#cap = cap; }
  get(k) {
    if (!this.#map.has(k)) return undefined;
    const v = this.#map.get(k); this.#map.delete(k); this.#map.set(k, v);   // mark most recent
    return v;
  }
  set(k, v) {
    if (this.#map.has(k)) this.#map.delete(k);
    else if (this.#map.size >= this.#cap) this.#map.delete(this.#map.keys().next().value);  // evict oldest
    this.#map.set(k, v);
  }
}
```
*Interview follow-up:* implement it with a doubly linked list plus a hash map (the classic version), and explain why `Map` gives the same complexity here.

### 18. `get(obj, path, default)` (lodash-style)
```js
function get(obj, path, def) {
  const keys = Array.isArray(path) ? path : path.replace(/\[(\w+)\]/g, '.$1').split('.').filter(Boolean);
  let cur = obj;
  for (const k of keys) { if (cur == null) return def; cur = cur[k]; }
  return cur === undefined ? def : cur;
}
// get({a:[{b:5}]}, 'a[0].b') → 5
```

### 19. `deepEqual`
```js
function deepEqual(a, b, seen = new WeakMap()) {
  if (a === b || (a !== a && b !== b)) return true;                  // equal, or both NaN
  if (typeof a !== 'object' || typeof b !== 'object' || !a || !b) return false;
  if (Object.getPrototypeOf(a) !== Object.getPrototypeOf(b)) return false;
  if (a instanceof Date) return a.getTime() === b.getTime();
  if (a instanceof RegExp) return String(a) === String(b);
  if (seen.get(a) === b) return true;                                 // circular
  seen.set(a, b);
  if (a instanceof Map || a instanceof Set) {                         // compare as arrays (order-sensitive simplification)
    return a.size === b.size && deepEqual([...a], [...b], seen);
  }
  const ka = Reflect.ownKeys(a), kb = Reflect.ownKeys(b);
  return ka.length === kb.length && ka.every(k => Object.hasOwn(b, k) && deepEqual(a[k], b[k], seen));
}
```

### 20. Observable / pub-sub store (Redux-like, 15 lines)
```js
function createStore(reducer, initial) {
  let state = initial; const listeners = new Set();
  return {
    getState: () => state,
    dispatch(action) { state = reducer(state, action); listeners.forEach(l => l()); return action; },
    subscribe(l) { listeners.add(l); return () => listeners.delete(l); },
  };
}
```

### 21. `Promise` queue / sequential runner and a `useDebounce`-style hook logic
```js
const runSequential = tasks => tasks.reduce((p, t) => p.then(acc => t().then(r => [...acc, r])), Promise.resolve([]));

class TaskQueue {
  #q = []; #running = 0;
  constructor(concurrency = 1) { this.c = concurrency; }
  add(task) {
    return new Promise((resolve, reject) => { this.#q.push({ task, resolve, reject }); this.#next(); });
  }
  #next() {
    while (this.#running < this.c && this.#q.length) {
      const { task, resolve, reject } = this.#q.shift();
      this.#running++;
      task().then(resolve, reject).finally(() => { this.#running--; this.#next(); });
    }
  }
}
```

### 22. `groupBy`, `chunk`, `unique`, `zip`, `sample` set (be able to write all in 2 minutes each)
```js
const groupBy = (arr, fn) => arr.reduce((acc, x) => { (acc[fn(x)] ??= []).push(x); return acc; }, {});
const chunk = (arr, n) => Array.from({ length: Math.ceil(arr.length / n) }, (_, i) => arr.slice(i * n, i * n + n));
const uniqueBy = (arr, fn) => [...new Map(arr.map(x => [fn(x), x])).values()];  // keeps last; reverse first to keep first
const zip = (...arrs) => Array.from({ length: Math.min(...arrs.map(a => a.length)) }, (_, i) => arrs.map(a => a[i]));
const range = (s, e, step = 1) => Array.from({ length: Math.max(0, Math.ceil((e - s) / step)) }, (_, i) => s + i * step);
```

**Bonus list to practice:** `Array.prototype.flat`, `Object.create` polyfill, `JSON.stringify` (simplified), `sleep` sort, `Function.prototype.toString`-free `compose`, an async `waterfall`, a `Promise.prototype.finally` polyfill, a simple `fetch` wrapper with interceptors, a mini jQuery-style `$` wrapper, an `EventTarget`-style `addEventListener` with delegation, a `setInterval` built on `setTimeout`, and `clearAllTimeouts`.

# 3.3.18 CODE-OUTPUT DRILLS (predict first, then check)

**1. Hoisting**
```js
console.log(a); var a = 1;                     // undefined
console.log(typeof b); let b = 2;              // ReferenceError (TDZ)
foo(); function foo() { console.log('hi'); }   // 'hi'
bar(); var bar = () => 1;                      // TypeError: bar is not a function
```
**2. Loop closures:** `var` → `3 3 3`; `let` → `0 1 2`.

**3. Name collision**
```js
var x = 1; function x() {}
typeof x;   // 'number' (function hoisted first, then the assignment overwrites it)
```
**4. `this`**
```js
const o = { n: 1, f() { return this?.n; }, g: () => this?.n };
const f = o.f;
o.f(); f(); o.g();      // 1, undefined, undefined (module scope `this`)
```
**5. Event loop**
```js
console.log('A');
setTimeout(() => console.log('B'), 0);
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('C'));
console.log('D');
// A D C B
```
**6. Promise chains**
```js
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('a')).then(() => console.log('b'));
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('c')).then(() => console.log('d'));
// a c b d (interleaved by microtask rounds)
```
**7. async ordering**
```js
async function a1() { console.log('a1 start'); await a2(); console.log('a1 end'); }
async function a2() { console.log('a2'); }
console.log('script start');
setTimeout(() => console.log('timeout'), 0);
a1();
new Promise(r => { console.log('p1'); r(); }).then(() => console.log('p2'));
console.log('script end');
// script start, a1 start, a2, p1, script end, a1 end, p2, timeout
```
**8. Coercion**
```js
[] + []            // ''
[] + {}            // '[object Object]'
1 + '2'            // '12'
'3' * '4'          // 12
true + 1           // 2
null + 1           // 1
undefined + 1      // NaN
[] == false        // true
null == 0          // false
null >= 0          // true
NaN === NaN        // false
```
**9. `parseInt` and `sort`**
```js
['1', '2', '3'].map(parseInt)   // [1, NaN, NaN]
[10, 9, 1].sort()               // [1, 10, 9]
```
**10. Prototype**
```js
function Dog() {}; Dog.prototype.speak = () => 'woof';
const d = new Dog(); d.speak = () => 'meow'; delete d.speak;
d.speak();   // 'woof' (own property deleted, so the chain lookup finds the prototype's)
```
**11. Object keys order and key coercion**
```js
Object.keys({ b: 1, 2: 1, a: 1, 1: 1 });   // ['1', '2', 'b', 'a']
const k = {}, o1 = { x: 1 }; k[o1] = 'v'; Object.keys(k);  // ['[object Object]']
```
**12. `finally`**
```js
function f() { try { return 'try'; } finally { console.log('finally'); } }
f();   // logs 'finally', returns 'try'
function g() { try { return 'try'; } finally { return 'finally'; } }
g();   // 'finally' (overrides)
```
**13. Closures and shared state**
```js
function make() { const fns = []; for (var i = 0; i < 3; i++) fns.push(() => i); return fns; }
make().map(f => f());   // [3, 3, 3]
```
**14. Default params and TDZ**
```js
function f(a = b, b = 1) { return [a, b]; }
f();       // ReferenceError (b is in TDZ when a is evaluated)
f(5);      // [5, 1]
```
**15. Spread vs reference**
```js
const a = { n: { v: 1 } }; const b = { ...a }; b.n.v = 2; a.n.v;   // 2 (shallow copy)
```
**16. `Promise.all` rejection**
```js
Promise.all([Promise.resolve(1), Promise.reject('x'), new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 100))])
  .then(console.log, e => console.log('err', e));      // 'err x' immediately
```
**17. `await` in loops**
```js
[1, 2, 3].forEach(async n => { await sleep(10); console.log(n); });
console.log('done');   // 'done' prints first, then 1 2 3
```
**18. `typeof` and arrays**
```js
typeof []; typeof null; typeof function () {}; typeof NaN;   // 'object' 'object' 'function' 'number'
```
**19. Getter and setter**
```js
const o = { _v: 1, get v() { return this._v; }, set v(x) { this._v = x * 2; } };
o.v = 5; o.v;   // 10
```
**20. Node ordering (CJS)**
```js
process.nextTick(() => console.log('tick'));
Promise.resolve().then(() => console.log('promise'));
queueMicrotask(() => console.log('micro'));
// tick, promise, micro
```


**Self-check:** Without running anything, state the output order of drill 5 and drill 20, and name the queues — plus the Node-only one — that decide it.

**Resources:** MDN's "JavaScript event loop" guide and its microtask/macrotask reference; the HTML spec's "Perform a microtask checkpoint" and event loop processing model; V8's "Understanding microtasks and macrotasks" post; Node's event loop documentation section on `process.nextTick` versus `setImmediate` versus `setTimeout`; the TC39-39 spec's job and execution-context sections for the ordering rules behind these drills.

**Mini task:** Do the 20 drills cold with a 10-minute cap, writing the predicted output and a one-line reason for each *before* running any of them. Score per drill, and for every miss write the governing rule as a single sentence — that set of sentences is your real revision list. Re-run the missed ones three days later.

**Mistakes:**
- **Skipping the prediction** and reading the comment — the only way to lose the benefit.
- **Answering the value without the mechanism,** which is the part actually being scored.
- **Guessing silently when unsure,** instead of naming your rule so the interviewer can locate the gap.
- **Cramming the whole set in one session** the night before; the rules decay faster than the list appears to warrant.
- **Confusing an error with an output:** half these snippets throw (`ReferenceError`, `TypeError`), and predicting the error type and roughly where it comes from is part of the test.
- **Running the whole set as one script:** the first throw hides every later drill, so run them independently.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Predict-then-run versus run-first:** predicting first is the entire value; running first turns a memory test into a reading test. The run is only for the explanation, and re-predicting afterwards is what turns the rule into recall.
- **Twenty drills once versus five drills repeatedly:** spaced repetition beats one bulk session, because you're building automatic recall of a rule, not familiarity with a list.
- **Node versus browser for the timing drills:** drill 20 is Node-only, so run those in Node; if you care about the browser, add `MessageChannel`/`postMessage` as a macrotask source and note that module-scope `this` differs between the two environments.
- **Mental execution versus running them:** mental execution is the exam skill; running the real runtime catches environment surprises that pure reasoning hides, and it keeps the answers honest.
- **Breadth versus depth:** breadth is right for a screen; if you miss three ordering drills, stop drilling `sort` and rebuild the event-loop model, because more of the same won't help.

**Common wrong answers:** Putting `a1 end` last in drill 7 and promise callbacks after timers in drills 5 and 20; treating `typeof null === 'object'` as a bug rather than a spec quirk; expecting `[10, 9, 1].sort()` to give `[1, 9, 10]`; describing spread as a deep copy (drill 15); answering drill 10's `delete` as "the object has no `speak` at all" instead of "the own property is gone, so lookup continues up the chain"; and expecting `null >= 0` to be `false` because `null == 0` is `false` — the relational operator coerces to number first.

**Probe:** "Drill 14: `function f(a = b, b = 1) { return [a, b]; }` — what does `f()` do, and which rule produces it?" (Expect: a `ReferenceError`, because parameters are initialised left to right and `b`'s binding exists but is uninitialised — the TDZ applies to parameters exactly as it does to `let`. `f(5)` returns `[5, 1]` because `a`'s default only runs for `undefined`, so `b` is never touched. The natural follow-on: name the other TDZ sites — `let`, `const`, `class`, default parameters — and confirm `var` never has one.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Whether they predicted before looking,** and said out loud whether each snippet returns a value or throws.
- **Mechanism, not just the value:** "because `var` is function-scoped" beats a bare `3 3 3`.
- **Precision on ordering:** microtasks before macrotasks, `nextTick` before promises in Node, a `return` inside `finally` overriding an earlier `return`.
- **Speed and honesty under pressure:** "I'm not sure — here's the rule I'd apply and what I'd check" beats a confident wrong answer.

**Model answer, Q1:** "I cover the comment lines and predict every output *and* whether it's a value or a thrown error, then run it. I score per drill, not overall: a drill passes only if I named the value *and* the mechanism — 'undefined, because `var` is hoisted and initialised to `undefined`' passes, a bare 'undefined' doesn't, and neither does the right value with a wrong reason. The target is all twenty, and the ones I miss are the revision list. The value is entirely in the misses: each one points at a specific rule — hoisting, `this`, prototype lookup, or queue order — not at a gap in my typing."

**Questions**
1. How do you actually run this set, and what counts as a pass on a drill?
2. Which drill do most candidates get wrong, and what is the underlying rule?
3. Explain drill 5's ordering in terms of the two queues, not from memory of the answer.
4. Drill 2 prints `3 3 3` with `var` and `0 1 2` with `let` — say why, in one sentence each.
5. Why does `['1', '2', '3'].map(parseInt)` give `[1, NaN, NaN]`?
6. Drill 20 prints `tick, promise, micro` — why that order, and why is that drill Node-only?
7. What is the fastest way to get honest signal out of this set rather than a false pass?

**Prerequisites:** hoisting and the TDZ (3.3.1), execution context and scope chain (3.3.2), closures (3.3.3), `this` binding (3.3.4), prototypes (3.3.5), the event loop and promises (3.3.6, 3.3.7), and types and coercion (3.3.9).

**Why interviewers ask:** Output prediction is the cheapest and least forgiving screen there is: it removes the option of writing code carefully and leaves you with the model you actually have. Interviewers use it in the first fifteen minutes to sort, then to re-check the same concepts under time pressure, and because it exposes the classic mid-level failure — someone who can *use* the language fluently but has never internalised the ordering rules, so their answers are plausible and wrong in a consistent, diagnosable way.

**Definition:** A self-graded prediction set of twenty short snippets — hoisting and TDZ, `this`, loop closures, microtask versus timer ordering, coercion, prototype lookup, property order, `finally`, spread shallowness, and Node's `nextTick` queue — each with its output written beneath it, to be predicted before it is read.
---

# PHASE 3.3 RAPID-FIRE (under 30 seconds each)

| # | Question | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | `var`/`let`/`const`? | Function vs block scope, TDZ, reassignment |
| 2 | Are `let`/`const` hoisted? | Yes, but uninitialized (TDZ) |
| 3 | What is a closure? | Function + its lexical environment, kept alive |
| 4 | Fix `var` loop with `setTimeout`? | `let`, IIFE, or pass the value |
| 5 | Scope vs context? | Variable visibility (lexical) vs `this` (call-time) |
| 6 | How is `this` determined? | `new` > `bind/call/apply` > `obj.fn()` > default; arrows are lexical |
| 7 | Arrow function `this`? | From the enclosing scope, and can't be re-bound |
| 8 | `call` vs `apply` vs `bind`? | Invoke with args / invoke with array / return a new function |
| 9 | `prototype` vs `__proto__`? | Function's template for instances vs an object's actual link |
| 10 | Are classes real? | Sugar over prototypes, with private fields as the exception |
| 11 | `new` does what? | Create, link prototype, bind `this`, return the object |
| 12 | Microtask vs macrotask? | Promises/queueMicrotask vs timers/I/O/events |
| 13 | Order after a task? | All microtasks, then render, then the next macrotask |
| 14 | Why `setTimeout(0)` isn't instant? | Minimum delay, and the stack must be empty |
| 15 | Node `nextTick` vs `setImmediate`? | Before microtasks vs the check phase |
| 16 | Promise states? | Pending → fulfilled/rejected, then immutable |
| 17 | `all`/`allSettled`/`race`/`any`? | Fail-fast / wait all / first settled / first success |
| 18 | `.then(a,b)` vs `.then(a).catch(b)`? | Whether errors in `a` are caught |
| 19 | `forEach(async)` problem? | It doesn't await, so use `for...of` or `Promise.all` |
| 20 | `return await` in try? | Needed to catch rejections locally |
| 21 | Cancel a fetch? | `AbortController` |
| 22 | `==` vs `===`? | Coercion vs strict |
| 23 | Falsy values? | `false 0 -0 0n '' null undefined NaN` |
| 24 | `typeof null`? | `'object'` (historic bug) |
| 25 | `??` vs `\|\|`? | Nullish only vs any falsy |
| 26 | Shallow vs deep copy? | Spread copies one level, `structuredClone` is deep |
| 27 | `Object.freeze` deep? | No, it's shallow |
| 28 | ESM vs CJS? | Static, live bindings, async vs dynamic, copies, sync |
| 29 | Tree shaking needs? | Static ESM |
| 30 | Generator? | Pausable function returning an iterator |
| 31 | `Map` vs object? | Any key type, ordered, size, no prototype keys |
| 32 | `WeakMap` use? | Metadata/private data without preventing GC |
| 33 | Proxy use? | Reactivity, validation, with `Reflect` for defaults |
| 34 | Debounce vs throttle? | After activity stops vs at most once per interval |
| 35 | ReDoS? | Catastrophic backtracking from nested quantifiers |
| 36 | `Date` month index? | 0-based |
| 37 | Date-only ISO string parses as? | UTC |
| 38 | Common JS leak sources? | Listeners, timers, closures, unbounded caches, detached DOM |
| 39 | `Object.create(null)`? | A prototype-less dictionary |
| 40 | `parseInt` in `map` bug? | The index is passed as the radix |

# PHASE 3.3 FINAL READINESS CHECKLIST

**Language core**
- [ ] I can explain hoisting, the TDZ, and var/let/const with predicted outputs
- [ ] I can describe execution contexts, the scope chain, and lexical scoping
- [ ] I can explain closures with 3 practical uses and fix the loop bug three ways
- [ ] I can state all the `this` rules and precedence, and fix a lost `this`
- [ ] I can explain prototype chains, `new`, and how classes desugar
- [ ] I know coercion rules well enough to predict tricky expressions

**Async**
- [ ] I can explain the event loop, microtasks vs macrotasks, and the Node phases
- [ ] I can predict output ordering for mixed timers, promises, and async/await
- [ ] I know all promise combinators and their failure semantics
- [ ] I can write sequential, parallel, retried, timed-out, and concurrency-limited async code
- [ ] I know how to cancel work and avoid stale-response races

**Modern JS**
- [ ] I'm fluent with destructuring, spread, optional chaining, nullish ops, Map/Set, and the non-mutating array methods
- [ ] I can compare ESM and CJS, handle circular imports, and use dynamic imports
- [ ] I can write generators and async iterators, and explain how they relate to async/await
- [ ] I understand Proxy/Reflect, Symbols, and weak collections and their use cases
- [ ] I know copying options and can update nested state immutably

**Implementation round**
- [ ] I can write `bind`, `new`, a minimal Promise, `Promise.all`, `debounce`, `throttle`, `curry`, `memoize`, `deepClone`, and an event emitter from memory
- [ ] I can write `retry`, `mapLimit`, and an LRU cache
- [ ] I solved all 20 code-output drills and can explain each answer

**Hygiene**
- [ ] I can find and fix a memory leak and an event-loop blocker
- [ ] I handle dates in UTC, use `Intl`, and avoid ReDoS patterns

**Contents**
- **3.4 TypeScript:** type system model, tsconfig, narrowing, generics, utility, mapped and conditional types, typing React and Express, Zod, 15 type challenges

**Resources for this part:** TypeScript Handbook and *Effective TypeScript* (Dan Vanderkam), Total TypeScript and type-challenges (GitHub), web.dev (Learn Performance, Learn Accessibility), MDN, the W3C ARIA Authoring Practices Guide, Chrome DevTools docs, *High Performance Browser Networking* (Ilya Grigorik, free), and the Vite and Webpack docs.

---

# 3.4 TYPESCRIPT

## 3.4.1 The Mental Model: What TypeScript Is
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · Prerequisites: JS Core (3.3)

**Definition:** A statically typed superset of JavaScript. A type checker analyzes your code at build time, then the types are **erased**, and plain JavaScript is what runs.

**Why interviewers ask:** Most full stack teams are TypeScript-first. Questions separate people who *annotate* from people who *design with types*.


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.9, 3.3.11
**Key concepts**
- **Types are erased.** There's no runtime type information. Data from the network, `localStorage`, `JSON.parse`, or env vars is untyped at runtime, so validate it (Zod, Valibot) at boundaries.
- **Structural typing ("duck typing"):** compatibility depends on *shape*, not declared name. Two unrelated types with the same members are interchangeable. Contrast with Java's nominal typing. Use **branded types** when you need nominal behavior.
- **Type checking vs transpiling:** `tsc` does both. esbuild, SWC, Babel, and Vite only *strip* types and never check. So run `tsc --noEmit` separately in CI. Check with `isolatedModules`/`verbatimModuleSyntax` so every file can be transpiled alone.
- **Type inference:** let the compiler infer locals, and annotate **function boundaries** (parameters, public return types, exported APIs). Over-annotating adds noise, and under-annotating exports leaks accidental types.
- **Soundness:** TS is intentionally *not fully sound* (casts, `any`, array covariance, index access without `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`). It trades perfect safety for usability.
- **Erasable syntax:** recent Node versions (22.18+ / 23.6+) can run `.ts` files by stripping types. Features that emit code (`enum`, `namespace`, constructor parameter properties) aren't supported in strip-only mode. TS 5.8's `erasableSyntaxOnly` flags them.
- **Where the compiler helps most:** refactoring, autocompletion, catching `undefined` access, exhaustive `switch`, and API contracts between frontend and backend (shared types, tRPC, OpenAPI codegen).

**Questions**
1. TypeScript vs JavaScript: what do you gain and lose?
2. Do types exist at runtime? How do you validate API responses? *(No. Use runtime schemas such as Zod and infer types from them.)*
3. What is structural typing? When is it a problem?
4. Why doesn't Vite or esbuild report type errors? 
5. When should you annotate and when should you infer?

**Model answer, Q2:** "No, TypeScript types are erased at compile time, so a `User` type can't protect me from a malformed API response. At trust boundaries (HTTP responses, request bodies, env vars, `JSON.parse`, storage) I validate with a runtime schema like Zod and derive the static type from it with `z.infer`. That gives one source of truth, and inside the boundary I can trust the types."


**Self-check:** In one sentence each, explain what a type buys you at a function boundary, and what it can never buy you at a network boundary.

**Resources:** TypeScript Handbook — "Is TypeScript a Type-Checked Version of JavaScript?", "Type Compatibility", and "Type Erasure"; the compiler docs for `erasableSyntaxOnly` and `verbatimModuleSyntax`; *Effective TypeScript* (Dan Vanderkam), items on structural typing and on inference; `typescript-eslint`'s `no-explicit-any` and type-checked lint configurations.

**Mini task:** Compile one module twice, once with `tsc` and once with esbuild, and diff the output to see precisely what survives and what doesn't. Then take a JS file, turn on `--strict`, and fix every error without `any` or `as`. Done-condition: `tsc --noEmit` is clean and a script runs it before `build`.

**Mistakes:**
- **`JSON.parse(x) as User`:** a cast is a claim, not a check, so it launders bad data through the whole application as if it were typed.
- **A type-only import written as a value import:** without `import type` or `verbatimModuleSyntax` the import survives erasure and can become a run-time require of something that no longer exists.
- **Letting an `any` cross a public signature:** it disables checking for every downstream caller, so the error surfaces far from the cause.
- **Trusting `paths` to rewrite imports:** it only affects module *resolution* for the compiler and editor; the emitted specifier still needs a bundler alias or `tsc-alias`.
- **Annotating inferred locals "to be safe":** it duplicates what the compiler already knows and rots the moment the initialiser changes.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`tsc --noEmit` as a CI gate vs bundler-only builds:** the extra step costs CI minutes and catches everything the bundler throws away; skip it and your editor is your only defence.
- **Annotating every local vs annotating boundaries:** annotations everywhere are noise and a maintenance tax; annotations nowhere let accidental inferred types leak out of your public API.
- **Brands vs distinct wrapper classes:** a brand (`string & { readonly __brand: 'UserId' }`) is zero-cost and erases, but gives you no runtime check; a class or smart constructor validates at construction and has to be carried around.
- **Literal unions over `enum`:** literal unions erase, narrow in `switch`, and compose with `Exclude`/`Extract`; `enum` emits run-time code, which breaks strip-only mode and `isolatedModules`.
- **Checking every layer vs checking only trust boundaries:** checking everything is noise; checking nothing is a lie. The boundary is where `unknown` becomes a known type.

**Common wrong answers:** "TypeScript catches bugs before run time" (only the ones the checker can prove); "Vite or esbuild will report my type errors" (they don't check); "`strict` makes it null-safe" (`any` and `as` are still available and they opt out); "`as const` narrows everything at run time" (nothing is narrowed at run time, it just changes the static type).

**Probe:** "Your client types the response as `User`, and production shows `role: 'adminstrator'`. Where exactly did the type system fail you?" (Expect: nowhere — the annotation is erased and nothing validated at run time. Fix is a schema at the boundary plus `z.infer`, and defensiveness where a bad payload is expensive.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Whether they treat TypeScript as a compile-time-only tool** — no runtime representation survives, so a wrong `User` from an API is a production bug, not a type error.
- **Structural vs nominal thinking:** they know compatibility is by shape, and that a brand or a class (real private fields) is how you stop two same-shaped types being interchangeable.
- **Checker vs transpiler:** esbuild, SWC, Babel and Vite strip types without checking, so `tsc --noEmit` has to exist somewhere in CI.
- **Where they draw the annotation line:** infer inside a function, annotate at the boundary (parameters, exported return types, public contracts).
---

## 3.4.2 `tsconfig` and Strictness
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

```jsonc
{
  "compilerOptions": {
    "target": "ES2022",                // output JS level; match your runtime
    "lib": ["ES2023", "DOM", "DOM.Iterable"],
    "module": "ESNext",                // or NodeNext for Node ESM
    "moduleResolution": "Bundler",     // "NodeNext" for Node libs/servers
    "strict": true,                    // enables the strict family (see below)
    "noUncheckedIndexedAccess": true,  // arr[i] and obj[key] become T | undefined
    "exactOptionalPropertyTypes": true,
    "noImplicitOverride": true,
    "noFallthroughCasesInSwitch": true,
    "verbatimModuleSyntax": true,      // forces `import type` for type-only imports
    "isolatedModules": true,
    "skipLibCheck": true,              // skip checking .d.ts in node_modules (speed)
    "noEmit": true,                    // bundler emits; tsc only checks
    "jsx": "react-jsx",
    "paths": { "@/*": ["./src/*"] }    // type-level only. Bundler needs the same alias!
  },
  "include": ["src"]
}
```
- **`strict`** turns on: `noImplicitAny`, `strictNullChecks` (the most valuable: `null`/`undefined` aren't assignable to everything), `strictFunctionTypes`, `strictBindCallApply`, `strictPropertyInitialization`, `noImplicitThis`, `alwaysStrict`, `useUnknownInCatchVariables`.
- **`paths`** doesn't rewrite imports in emitted code. Configure the bundler alias too (or use `tsc-alias`).
- **`moduleResolution`:** `Bundler` for apps built by Vite/Webpack/Next. `NodeNext` for code run directly by Node (requires `.js` extensions in relative imports and honors `package.json` `exports`).
- **Project references / `composite`:** split a monorepo into incrementally built projects.
- **Migrating a JS codebase:** `allowJs` + `checkJs`, convert file by file, start with leaf modules, turn on `strict` flags one at a time, and use `// @ts-expect-error` (fails when the error disappears, unlike `@ts-ignore`) with a ticket.


**Prerequisites:** 3.4.1, 3.8.2

**Why interviewers ask:** Every TypeScript codebase has one and it is the first thing you change, so it is also the cheapest way to see whether a candidate knows what the checker actually guarantees. Plenty of people turned `strict` on once in 2018 and never looked at the file again, so the follow-ups are where the signal is.

**Definition:** `tsconfig.json` is the JSON contract the compiler reads: it picks the language level, target, module system and resolution mode, switches individual checks on and off, and decides whether `tsc` emits JS and declarations or only type-checks. `strict` is an umbrella flag that switches on the family of strictest individual checks.
**Questions:** What does `strict` enable? Why is `strictNullChecks` the most important? `@ts-ignore` vs `@ts-expect-error`? Why can `paths` work in the editor but fail at runtime? How would you migrate a 200k-line JS app?


**Self-check:** Name the eight flags `strict` turns on, then name three commonly assumed flags that it does not.

**Resources:** TypeScript docs — "Compiler Options in `tsconfig`" (including the `strict` family entry) and the `tsc` CLI reference; the `moduleResolution` section of the tsconfig reference; TS 5.5 release notes on `isolatedDeclarations`; `typescript-eslint`'s recommended type-checked config and its `strictest` preset.

**Mini task:** Run `npx tsc --init`, then produce a strict config for a small Vite app and a separate one for a Node ESM library, and add `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` and `exactOptionalPropertyTypes` to both. Done-condition: `tsc --noEmit` is clean in CI for both projects, and you can say which flags came from `strict` and which you added deliberately.

**Mistakes:**
- **Leaving `"module": "commonjs"` with a bundler:** you emit CJS into an ESM graph, which breaks `import.meta`, top-level await, and tree-shaking.
- **Writing `.js` extensions on relative imports under `moduleResolution: Bundler`:** it resolves, but you now fight the bundler's own resolution and lose `.ts` ergonomics.
- **Setting `declaration: true` without `isolatedDeclarations` (TS 5.5+):** build times collapse and you're still expected to annotate every exported signature by hand.
- **Assuming `paths` is fixed by `tsc`:** with `noEmit` it's fine; without it, emitted specifiers are unresolved at run time.
- **Flipping `strictNullChecks` off globally to unblock a merge:** one switch removes the most valuable check for the entire repository.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`moduleResolution: Bundler` vs `NodeNext`:** Bundler matches what Vite, webpack and Next actually do and lets you write extensionless relative imports; NodeNext matches what Node runs, honours `package.json` `exports`, and demands `.js` extensions in emitted relative specifiers.
- **All strict flags at once vs one at a time:** all-at-once is the correct end state and the only one CI can enforce; flag-by-flag is the only survivable migration for a large JS codebase.
- **`skipLibCheck: true` vs `false`:** true is near-universal (faster installs, immunity to upstream `.d.ts` breakage); false surfaces real type conflicts between dependencies at real CI cost and with no way to fix the upstream code.
- **`verbatimModuleSyntax` vs `isolatedModules` alone:** `isolatedModules` merely permits correct `import type` usage; `verbatimModuleSyntax` enforces it and stops `import x = require()` surprises, at the cost of breaking older configs that use default imports of types.
- **One root config vs project references:** a single config is simple and easy to reason about; `composite` + references give per-package `tsc -b` caching, real package boundaries, and much faster CI on a monorepo.

**Common wrong answers:** "`strict: true` includes `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`" (it doesn't — it's a separate, more expensive opt-in); "you need `tsc-alias` whenever you use `paths`" (only when `tsc` emits); "just turn `strictNullChecks` off for that one file" (the flag is per-program, so you'd disable it for everyone); "`skipLibCheck: false` is the secure choice" (it mainly catches conflicts between dependency types, which you don't control, at a large CI cost).

**Probe:** "You switch on `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` and get 400 errors, mostly `arr[i]` possibly undefined, in files you didn't touch. What's your plan?" (Expect: fix hot paths properly with `for…of`, `.at()`, or an explicit bounds check; use `!` only where the invariant is provable and localise it in a helper; scope the flag to new code with a second tsconfig if adoption has to be gradual; never blanket-`!` the codebase.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **The exact contents of the `strict` family, and which flags are *not* in it:** `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, `exactOptionalPropertyTypes`, `noImplicitOverride`, `noPropertyAccessFromIndexSignature`, and `verbatimModuleSyntax` are separate opt-ins.
- **Knowing `strictNullChecks` is all-or-nothing per program,** so a staged migration needs either separate tsconfigs per project or a staged fix list, not per-file toggles.
- **That `paths` is resolution-only** and that `moduleResolution: Bundler` requires `module: ESNext`/`Preserve`, while `NodeNext` requires explicit `.js` extensions in relative specifiers.
- **Judgement on `skipLibCheck`,** `isolatedDeclarations`, and project references for a monorepo — i.e. which knobs are speed and which are safety.

**Model answer, Q1:** "`strict` is just an umbrella for `noImplicitAny`, `strictNullChecks`, `strictFunctionTypes`, `strictBindCallApply`, `strictPropertyInitialization`, `noImplicitThis`, `useUnknownInCatchVariables`, and `alwaysStrict`, and you can set them individually. `strictNullChecks` matters most because `null` and `undefined` stop being assignable to everything, so the compiler forces you to prove a value is there — it turns `user.name.length` from a run-time `TypeError` into a compile error. On `@ts-ignore` vs `@ts-expect-error`: expect-error is better because it fails once the error is gone, so the debt can't hide. `paths` only rewrites module resolution for the compiler and editor, never the emitted specifier, so the bundler needs the same alias or you need `tsc-alias`. For a 200k-line app I'd enable `allowJs` with `checkJs`, add `noImplicitAny` first, fix file by file from the leaves, then treat `strictNullChecks` as its own focused pass because it's all-or-nothing per program, keep `noEmit` so the bundler still emits, and land each `@ts-expect-error` with a ticket."
---

## 3.4.3 Core Types and Everyday Features
🟢 → 🟡 · **Must-know**

- **`any` vs `unknown` vs `never` vs `void`**
  - `any` switches off checking (and infects whatever it touches). **`unknown`** is the type-safe top type: you must narrow before use. **`never`** is the bottom type: no value (functions that always throw, impossible branches, exhaustiveness checks). **`void`** means "return value is ignored".
  - Use `unknown` for `catch (e)`, `JSON.parse` results, and generic inputs.
- **Literals and `as const`:** `'GET' | 'POST'`; `as const` makes an object deeply `readonly` with literal types, so you derive types from values: `const ROLES = ['admin','user'] as const; type Role = typeof ROLES[number];`
- **`satisfies` (4.9):** validates a value against a type *without widening* it. It keeps precise inference and still catches typos in keys.
```ts
const routes = { home: '/', user: '/u/:id' } satisfies Record<string, string>;
routes.home;      // type is the literal-ish string, and routes.nope is an error
```
- **Tuples:** `[string, number]`, optional and rest elements, named: `[id: number, label?: string]`.
- **Enums vs union of literals:** numeric enums are loosely typed (any number is assignable in older versions), emit runtime code, and don't work in strip-only mode. Prefer **string literal unions** or `as const` objects. `const enum` has bundler and `isolatedModules` pitfalls.
- **`type` vs `interface`**

| | `interface` | `type` |
|---|---|---|
| Object shapes | Yes | Yes |
| Unions, tuples, primitives, mapped/conditional | No | **Yes** |
| Extending | `extends` (clearer errors, cached, slightly faster) | intersection `&` |
| Declaration merging | **Yes** (useful for augmenting libraries) | No |
| Guideline | Public object contracts, class shapes, augmentation | Everything else. **Be consistent per codebase** |
- **Function types:** overloads (several call signatures, implementation signature hidden), optional and default params, `this` parameters, rest args, generic functions.
- **Classes:** `public/private/protected/readonly`, `#private` (true runtime privacy) vs TS `private` (compile-time only), `abstract`, `implements`, parameter properties (`constructor(private db: Db) {}`).
- **Object-type gotchas:** excess property checks apply only to fresh object literals. `{}` means "any non-nullish value" (avoid it). `object` means non-primitive. `Function` is too loose (use a call signature). `Object.keys(o)` returns `string[]` because objects can have extra keys, so cast deliberately or use a typed helper. Index signatures (`Record<string, T>`) make every key "exist" (hence `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`).
- **Non-null assertion `!` and casts `as T`:** assertions *override* the compiler and don't check anything. Prefer narrowing, type guards, or schemas. Treat each `as`/`!` as a code smell that needs justification.
- **Declaration files:** `.d.ts` describe untyped JS. `@types/*` packages (DefinitelyTyped), `declare module 'x'`, `declare global { interface Window { analytics: … } }`, and module augmentation for third-party types.
- **`import type`** is erased entirely and avoids accidental runtime imports and cycles.


**Prerequisites:** 3.4.1, 3.4.2

**Why interviewers ask:** This is the layer you touch in literally every file, and the failure modes here — `any` leaking outward, `Object.keys` returning `string[]`, enums in a strip-only build — are exactly what shows up in real review comments. It also separates people who know the vocabulary from people who know where the holes are.

**Definition:** The built-in type vocabulary and the everyday features that decide whether a codebase stays honest: the top and bottom types (`any`, `unknown`, `never`), literal types and `as const`, `satisfies`, tuples, `interface` vs `type`, enums, function and class features, and the places the system lets you lie (`as`, `!`, `{}`, index signatures).
**Questions**
1. `any` vs `unknown`? When is each acceptable?
2. `type` vs `interface`?
3. Why prefer union literals or `as const` over `enum`?
4. What does `satisfies` do that `: Type` doesn't?
5. What is `never` used for? *(exhaustiveness)*
6. Why is `Object.keys(obj)` typed `string[]`?
7. TS `private` vs `#private`? 
8. How do you add a property to Express's `Request` or `window`? *(declaration merging and `declare global`)*


**Trade-offs:**
- **`type` vs `interface`:** `interface` gives clearer error messages, checks faster, and merges declarations — which is exactly how library augmentation works; `type` is the only option for unions, tuples, and mapped or conditional types. Pick one per codebase and use `interface` for public object contracts.
- **`enum` vs string literal union with `as const`:** literals erase, narrow inside `switch`, and work with `Exclude`/`Extract`; `enum` gives you a run-time object and a nominal-ish identity some library APIs expect, at the cost of emitting code and confusing bundlers.
- **`as const` vs hand-written literal unions:** `as const` makes the value the source of truth, so adding a variant is one edit; a hand-written union has to be kept in sync with the value by hand, and drifts.
- **`unknown` plus guards vs a runtime schema:** guards are free, dependency-free and instant, but only as complete as the checks you write; a schema validates at run time, gives field-level errors, and hands you a validated value — worth it exactly at trust boundaries, noise everywhere else.
- **Shallow `readonly` vs a recursive `DeepReadonly`:** deep is genuinely safer but costs inference time, fights any library that mutates, and makes error messages unreadable past two levels.

**Common wrong answers:** "`any` is `unknown` without the safety" (it's the opposite: `unknown` is the safe version); "`satisfies` is sugar for `as`" (it checks, `as` doesn't); "`private` makes it private at run time" (only `#private` does); "`Object.keys(o) as (keyof typeof o)[]` is safe because those keys exist" (an index signature makes every string key exist, and `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` still adds `undefined`); "enums are just objects" (numeric enums accept any number from older code and are not nominal in the way people assume).

**Probe:** "You've got a 40-key `Record<string, Config>` and want to iterate it with full key typing. What breaks and what's the fix?" (Expect: `Object.keys` returns `string[]`, so `cfg[key]` is `Config | undefined` under `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`. Fix with a typed helper — `function entriesOf<T extends object>(o: T): [keyof T, T[keyof T]][]` — or keep a separate `as const` key tuple, rather than casting at each call site.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **That `unknown` is a top type and `never` a bottom type,** and that the difference from `any` is usability, not assignability.
- **Precision about the escape hatches:** `Array.isArray` narrows `unknown` to `any[]`, `Object.keys` gives `string[]`, excess property checks only fire on fresh object literals, and `{}` means "any non-nullish value".
- **Deriving unions from values** with `as const` plus `typeof X[number]` instead of hand-maintaining a parallel type.
- **Declaration merging and module augmentation** as the mechanism for `Express.Request` or `Window`, and `declare module` for untyped JS.
- **Rejection of enums in erasable or `isolatedModules` builds,** with the reason (they emit run-time code).

**Model answer, Q1:** "`any` opts out of checking: it's assignable to everything and everything is assignable to it, so one `any` disables checking for every expression it touches and for the callers of whatever you return it from. `unknown` is the top type — you can store anything in it, but you can't use it until you narrow, which makes it the right type for `JSON.parse`, `catch (e)` and generic input. Rule of thumb: `unknown` at the boundary, narrow inward; `any` only where the value is immediately erased or handed back out, and with an eslint-disable and a comment saying why. `never` is the bottom type — no value is assignable to it, which is what makes exhaustiveness checks work."
**Mistakes:** `any` as an escape hatch that spreads; `as` to silence errors; `Function`/`{}`/`Object` types; relying on enums; unvalidated `JSON.parse(...) as T`; forgetting that `catch (e)` is `unknown`.


**Self-check:** `as const`, `: Type`, and `satisfies Type` each do something different to the inferred type — what, and which one would you reach for on a route table you want fully key-checked?

**Resources:** TypeScript Handbook — "Everyday Types", "Literal Types" (including `as const`), "The `satisfies` Operator", "Declaration Merging" and "Module Augmentation"; `typescript-eslint`'s `no-unsafe-*` rules; *Effective TypeScript* items on preferring `unknown`, on type widening, and on avoiding `as`.

**Mini task:** Rewrite a module that uses `enum`, `any`, `JSON.parse(...) as T`, and `Object.keys` over a config so it contains zero `any` and zero `as`, deriving every union from `as const` values, and enable `@typescript-eslint/no-explicit-any` and `no-unsafe-assignment` as errors. Done-condition: lint passes, and adding a new enum member turns into a compile error at every non-exhaustive `switch`.
---

## 3.4.4 Narrowing, Discriminated Unions, Type Guards
🟡 → 🔴 · **Must-know**

**Narrowing mechanisms:** `typeof`, `instanceof`, `in`, equality, truthiness, `Array.isArray`, optional-chaining checks, assignment, control flow analysis (early return), and user-defined guards.

```ts
// Discriminated union: a shared literal "tag" lets the compiler narrow
type Result<T> = { ok: true; value: T } | { ok: false; error: Error };

function unwrap<T>(r: Result<T>): T {
  if (r.ok) return r.value;     // narrowed to { ok: true }
  throw r.error;                // narrowed to { ok: false }
}

// Exhaustiveness: adding a new variant makes this a compile error
type Action = { type: 'add'; item: string } | { type: 'remove'; index: number } | { type: 'clear' };
const assertNever = (x: never): never => { throw new Error(`Unhandled: ${JSON.stringify(x)}`); };

function reducer(state: string[], a: Action): string[] {
  switch (a.type) {
    case 'add':    return [...state, a.item];
    case 'remove': return state.filter((_, i) => i !== a.index);
    case 'clear':  return [];
    default:       return assertNever(a);
  }
}

// User-defined type guard (the predicate is *trusted*, so a wrong guard is a lie)
const isString = (x: unknown): x is string => typeof x === 'string';

// Assertion function
function assertDefined<T>(x: T | null | undefined, msg = 'Expected value'): asserts x is T {
  if (x == null) throw new Error(msg);
}

// `in` narrowing
type Cat = { meow(): void }; type Dog = { bark(): void };
const speak = (p: Cat | Dog) => ('meow' in p ? p.meow() : p.bark());
```
- **Why model state as discriminated unions:** it makes illegal states unrepresentable.
```ts
// ❌ allows { loading: true, error: Error, data: User }
type BadState = { loading: boolean; data?: User; error?: Error };
// ✅
type State = { status: 'idle' } | { status: 'loading' } | { status: 'success'; data: User } | { status: 'error'; error: Error };
```
- **Narrowing caveats:** narrowing is lost across callbacks and after mutation. `filter(Boolean)` doesn't narrow (use `.filter((x): x is T => x != null)`, and TS 5.5 infers some predicates automatically). Narrowing by property works on `const`/readonly references more reliably.


**Prerequisites:** 3.4.3, 3.3.9

**Why interviewers ask:** This is where TypeScript stops being annotation and becomes design. Modelling async state as a discriminated union and getting exhaustiveness for free is the single most valuable thing a mid-level candidate can demonstrate on a whiteboard, and the lost-narrowing and `filter(Boolean)` traps separate the senior answers from the rest.

**Definition:** TypeScript's control-flow analysis, which narrows a wide static type to a precise one inside each branch using `typeof`, `instanceof`, `in`, equality, truthiness, `Array.isArray`, assignment, and user-written `x is T` predicates. Discriminated unions make that narrowing exhaustive and provable.
**Questions**
1. What's a discriminated union? Why model async state that way?
2. How do you do exhaustive checking?
3. How do type guards work, and what's the risk of writing one? 
4. Why doesn't `.filter(Boolean)` change the array's type? How do you fix it?
5. How do you narrow `unknown` safely? *(typeof checks, guards, or a schema)*


**Self-check:** Why does `assertNever` need the enclosing function's return type to be annotated, and what silently rots if you replace it with `default: throw new Error()`?

**Resources:** TypeScript Handbook — "Narrowing", "Discriminated Unions", "Control Flow Analysis", "Type Predicates" and "Assertion Functions"; TS 5.5 release notes on inferred type predicates; `typescript-eslint`'s `no-unnecessary-condition` and `no-unnecessary-type-assertion`; `type-challenges`' `Is Union` and `Extract` exercises.

**Mini task:** Model a fetch lifecycle as `idle | loading | success<T> | error`, write a `match`/`map` pair over it, and drive a UI from it. Done-condition: adding a `refreshing` variant produces compile errors in every `switch` and map that hasn't handled it, and the file contains no `as`.

**Mistakes:**
- **Using `as` where a guard belongs:** the assertion is unchecked, so the narrowing you wrote down never happens at run time.
- **Narrowing a value inside a callback that runs later:** `items.forEach(i => i.name)` errors because narrowing doesn't survive into the closure for a mutable binding; use `for…of`, or re-check inside.
- **`filter(Boolean)` then treating the result as `T[]`:** TS keeps `(T | undefined)[]`, and without `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` you get an `undefined` dereference much later with no stack trace pointing at the cause.
- **Using `in` to narrow a union whose members share the property:** if both variants declare it, `in` excludes neither and you silently get the whole union back.
- **Mutating a discriminated object after narrowing** (`state.status = 'done'`): the narrowed type is stale, and the compiler can't tell you — build new objects instead.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Discriminated union vs a class hierarchy:** unions are plain data — serialisable, and they work with Redux, Immer and RHF — and give exhaustiveness; subclasses put behaviour next to the state but lose `never`-based exhaustiveness and need `instanceof`.
- **`switch` with `assertNever` vs a handler map:** a map is less code and types its handlers off an event map, but `switch` gives you per-variant destructuring and the compile error when a variant has no branch.
- **Hand-written guards vs schema validation:** guards are zero-cost and dependency-free; schemas validate at run time and produce errors worth showing a user. Use both — guards for internal shapes, schemas at the boundary.
- **Boolean flags vs union states:** flags are quicker to write and impossible to trust (`loading && error` is representable); unions are more verbose to construct and force every consumer to handle every state, which is the point.

**Common wrong answers:** "TypeScript knows your `switch` is exhaustive" (only via a `never` default with an explicit return type); "narrowing carries into callbacks and `setTimeout`" (it doesn't, except for `const` bindings, and then only because they can't be reassigned); "`as` is safer than a guard because it's explicit" (`as` skips the check entirely, so it's the least safe option); "`instanceof` narrows to the exact class" (it narrows to the constructor's instance type, which fails across realms and after a bundle duplicates the class).

**Probe:** "Your `switch` covers `idle`, `loading`, `success`, and `error`, and someone adds a `refreshing` variant. What actually stops that shipping?" (Expect: only if every `switch` ends in `default: return assertNever(state)` *and* the enclosing function's return type is annotated — otherwise the new variant silently falls through. `noFallthroughCasesInSwitch` catches a fallthrough between cases, never a missing case.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Narrowing by discriminant and the `never` exhaustiveness pattern,** with the return type annotated so `assertNever` can return `never`.
- **Understanding when narrowing is invalidated:** mutation, reassignment, and crossing into a callback — plus the fact that narrowing survives into a closure only because the binding is `const`.
- **That a type predicate is trusted rather than verified,** and where that makes guards the wrong tool.
- **Knowing `.filter(Boolean)` doesn't narrow,** that TS 5.5 infers predicates for inline arrow functions but not for `Boolean`, and the fix is an explicit predicate.
- **`in` narrowing requiring disjoint members,** and the difference between narrowing a union and narrowing an intersection.

**Model answer, Q1:** "A discriminated union is a union of object types that share one literal-typed field — a `status` or `type` tag — so testing `state.status === 'loading'` eliminates every other member without any casting. I model async state that way because the flags version can represent `loading: true, error: new Error(), data: undefined`, an illegal state a reviewer will eventually ship, whereas the union makes it unrepresentable: `data` exists only on the success variant and `error` only on the failure variant, so the compiler forces every consumer to handle each state. It also gives exhaustive `switch` checking, so adding a variant turns every non-exhaustive `switch` into a compile error rather than a silent fallthrough."
---

## 3.4.5 Generics
🟡 → 🔴 · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Type-level parameters that let one function, class, or type work over many types while preserving the relationship between inputs and outputs.

```ts
function first<T>(xs: readonly T[]): T | undefined { return xs[0]; }

// Constraint: K must be a key of T, so the return type is tied to the exact key
function pluck<T, K extends keyof T>(items: T[], key: K): T[K][] { return items.map(i => i[key]); }
pluck([{ id: 1, name: 'a' }], 'name');     // string[]

// Generic with default, and a constraint on shape
interface ApiResponse<T = unknown> { data: T; status: number }

// Generic React-like list component, inference from props
function groupBy<T, K extends PropertyKey>(arr: T[], fn: (x: T) => K): Record<K, T[]> {
  return arr.reduce((acc, x) => { (acc[fn(x)] ??= []).push(x); return acc; }, {} as Record<K, T[]>);
}

// Generic class
class Repository<T extends { id: string }> {
  #items = new Map<string, T>();
  save(item: T) { this.#items.set(item.id, item); }
  find(id: string): T | undefined { return this.#items.get(id); }
}
```
- **Inference:** the compiler infers `T` from arguments, and explicit type args are rarely needed. If a type parameter appears only once in a signature, it's usually pointless (`function f<T>(x: T): void`).
- **`keyof`, `typeof`, indexed access `T[K]`, `T[number]`** are the vocabulary for relating types.
- **Constraints (`extends`)** limit what's acceptable and unlock member access.
- **`const` type parameters (5.0)** infer literals: `function f<const T>(x: T)`.
- **`NoInfer<T>` (5.4)** blocks inference from a position.
- **Variance:** function parameters are checked contravariantly under `strictFunctionTypes` (for function-typed properties, not method syntax, which stays bivariant).
- **Overloads vs generics vs unions:** prefer a generic or union parameter over many overloads when you can.


**Prerequisites:** 3.4.3, 3.4.4

**Why interviewers ask:** Generics are the dividing line between someone who can use TypeScript and someone can design an API in it, and the question is cheap to ask and hard to fake: the wrong answer is almost always `T extends any` sprinkled around, or a union of overloads written out longhand.
**Questions**
1. What problem do generics solve over `any` or unions?
2. What does `K extends keyof T` give you? Write `pluck`.
3. How does inference work, and when would you specify type arguments manually?
4. Write a typed `groupBy`, `debounce`, or `memoize`.
5. What's `T[number]`? What's the difference between `typeof x` in a type position and in an expression?

**Typed `debounce` example**
```ts
function debounce<A extends unknown[]>(fn: (...args: A) => void, ms: number) {
  let t: ReturnType<typeof setTimeout> | undefined;
  return (...args: A) => { clearTimeout(t); t = setTimeout(() => fn(...args), ms); };
}
```


**Self-check:** What's the difference between `T[K]` and `T[keyof T]`, and what does that difference do to the type of `pluck(users, 'name')`?

**Resources:** TypeScript Handbook — "Generics", "Keyof Type Operator", "Indexed Access Types", "Type Inference", "Type Annotations vs Inference" and "Variance"; TS 5.0 release notes (`const` type parameters) and TS 5.4 release notes (`NoInfer`); `type-challenges`' `Parameters`, `ReturnType`, `Currying` and `Memoize` exercises; *Effective TypeScript* on when a generic constraint is too clever.

**Mini task:** Write a typed `memoize` that keeps per-argument-key results — `memoize<A extends unknown[], R>(fn: (...args: A) => R)` returning `(...args: A) => R`, with a `Map` keyed by a serialised argument list and an optional custom `cacheKey`. Done-condition: `memoize((s: string, n: number) => s.repeat(n))` returns `string`, no `any` appears, and you can explain why `A extends unknown[]` is what keeps the argument tuple intact.

**Mistakes:**
- **`T extends object` on a parameter that can be an array or function:** `object` excludes primitives but says nothing about the members you then access.
- **Relaxes a key constraint with `K extends keyof T | (string & {})` to make an error go away:** it compiles and silently reopens the hole that `noUncheckedIndexedAccess` was closing.
- **A mutable generic field in a class:** it makes the class invariant, so `Box<string>` isn't assignable to `Box<unknown>`; a `readonly` field or a variance annotation changes that.
- **Spreading an input and annotating the result as the input type:** `return { ...input, extra: 1 }` typed as `T` silently drops `extra` from the type, and the caller can't see it.
- **A recursive generic with no depth limit:** `DeepReadonly<T>` or `Flatten<T>` over a self-referential type can hang the checker; measure with `tsc --generateTrace` and cap the depth.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Generic vs overloads vs union parameter:** a generic gives one implementation and a precise relationship; a union parameter is simplest and loses per-member precision; overloads are the escape hatch when the relationship isn't expressible, at the cost of duplicate signatures and worse inference.
- **Constraining with `extends T` vs putting `keyof` in the parameter:** the constraint checks the argument and lets you use `T[K]` inside the body; `keyof T` in the parameter only checks the argument and gives you nothing inside.
- **`readonly T[]` vs `T[]` in a parameter:** `readonly` accepts both mutable and immutable arrays, so the signature doesn't demand a reference you could mutate, and you correctly can't push into it.
- **Explicit type arguments vs inference:** explicit args earn their place when inference is wrong (a union argument where you want the element type, disambiguating overloads, or a contextually inferred return); otherwise they're duplication that rots.
- **Static-only generics vs runtime-validated ones:** a bare `parse<T>()` is a lie — the type is a promise the function doesn't keep. Pair the generic with a schema or a brand so there's something enforcing it.

**Common wrong answers:** "`K extends keyof T` is just documentation" (it checks the argument *and* unlocks `T[K]`); "`T[]` is always fine to accept" (arrays are covariant, so `string[]` is assignable to `(string | number)[]` and the mutation hole is real); "explicit type arguments are best practice" (they're noise you must update on every signature change); "generics are for reusable code" (that's what a generic is for, but the value is preserving the relationship, and that's what they actually test).

**Probe:** "`pluck(users, 'name')` is returning `(string | number)[]` instead of `string[]`. Why, and what's the one-word fix?" (Expect: the return type is `T[keyof T]` rather than `T[K]`, or `K` was declared as `string` instead of `extends keyof T`. Indexed access by the generic parameter preserves the specific key; indexed access by `keyof T` widens to the union of all value types.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Reaching for a generic over overloads or a union parameter,** and being able to say why in one sentence.
- **Understanding where inference comes from:** arguments, contextual return types, and the levers for steering it — default type parameters, `const` type parameters (TS 5.0), and `NoInfer<T>` (TS 5.4).
- **Fluent use of the type-level operators:** `keyof`, `typeof x` in a type position, `T[K]`, `T[number]`, and that `extends` is the only way to get member access on `T`.
- **Recognising a useless type parameter** (`function f<T>(x: T): void`) and an unsound constraint (`T extends object` where you actually touch members).
- **Variance:** `strictFunctionTypes` checks function-typed *parameters* contravariantly but leaves method syntax bivariant, which is why a handler `(x: Animal) => void` rejects a `Dog` argument while `{ handle(x: Animal): void }` accepts it.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Generics keep the *relationship* between inputs and outputs while the caller picks the concrete type. `any` throws the relationship away — `first(any[])` returns `any`, so every call site learns nothing and the caller has to cast. A union of overloads preserves it but repeats the same body once per type. With `<T>`, one implementation covers every type and the compiler checks each call site: `first<T>(xs: readonly T[]): T | undefined` gives `string | undefined` for strings and `User | undefined` for users, and a bad argument fails at the call. The cost is inference, and a type parameter that appears only once in the signature buys you nothing — `function log(x: T): void` is `any` with extra steps, and `T extends any` is `any` spelled the same way."
---

## 3.4.6 Utility, Mapped, Conditional, and Template Literal Types
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know (utilities)** / **Good-to-know (building your own)**

**Built-in utilities**

| Utility | Result |
|---|---|
| `Partial<T>` / `Required<T>` | All optional / all required |
| `Readonly<T>` | All `readonly` (shallow) |
| `Pick<T, K>` / `Omit<T, K>` | Keep / drop keys |
| `Record<K, V>` | Object with keys K and values V |
| `Exclude<U, X>` / `Extract<U, X>` | Remove from / keep in a union |
| `NonNullable<T>` | Remove `null`/`undefined` |
| `ReturnType<F>` / `Parameters<F>` | Function's return / param tuple |
| `Awaited<T>` | Unwrap promises (recursive) |
| `InstanceType<C>`, `ConstructorParameters<C>` | Class helpers |

**Mapped types:** `{ [K in keyof T]: … }` with modifiers (`readonly`, `?`, and `-readonly`/`-?` to remove) and key remapping with `as`.

**Conditional types:** `T extends U ? X : Y`, with `infer` to capture parts. They're **distributive** over naked union type parameters (`T extends U` applies to each member). Wrap in `[T]` to prevent that.

**Template literal types:** `` `get${Capitalize<K>}` ``, which build string unions.

```ts
type MyPick<T, K extends keyof T> = { [P in K]: T[P] };
type MyOmit<T, K extends PropertyKey> = { [P in keyof T as P extends K ? never : P]: T[P] };
type MyReadonly<T> = { readonly [P in keyof T]: T[P] };
type DeepReadonly<T> =
  T extends (...a: any[]) => any ? T :
  T extends object ? { readonly [K in keyof T]: DeepReadonly<T[K]> } : T;
type MyReturnType<F> = F extends (...a: any[]) => infer R ? R : never;
type MyAwaited<T> = T extends PromiseLike<infer U> ? MyAwaited<U> : T;
type TupleToUnion<T extends readonly unknown[]> = T[number];
type Getters<T> = { [K in keyof T as `get${Capitalize<string & K>}`]: () => T[K] };
type UnionToIntersection<U> =
  (U extends unknown ? (x: U) => void : never) extends (x: infer I) => void ? I : never;
type PathsOf<T> = T extends object
  ? { [K in keyof T & string]: T[K] extends object ? K | `${K}.${PathsOf<T[K]>}` : K }[keyof T & string]
  : never;
// Make some keys optional
type PartialBy<T, K extends keyof T> = Omit<T, K> & Partial<Pick<T, K>>;
// Distributive vs non-distributive
type ToArray<T> = T extends unknown ? T[] : never;        // string | number → string[] | number[]
type ToArrayND<T> = [T] extends [unknown] ? T[] : never;  // (string | number)[]
```

**Practical patterns:** deriving types from schemas and constants (`z.infer`, `typeof CONFIG`, `keyof typeof`), typed event maps (`on<K extends keyof Events>(e: K, cb: (p: Events[K]) => void)`), typed route params, API client types from OpenAPI, and `Pick` for DTOs and view models.


**Prerequisites:** 3.4.5, 3.4.3

**Why interviewers ask:** These decide whether a codebase shares one contract or hand-writes three copies of it. A senior candidate reads them fluently even when they rarely write them, and it's easy to tell the two groups apart by asking for `DeepReadonly` live on a whiteboard.

**Definition:** The higher-order vocabulary for deriving types from other types: utilities like `Partial`, `Pick`, `Omit`, `Record` and `Awaited` for reshaping, mapped types for transforming every property of a type, conditional types with `infer` for branching on types, and template literal types for building string unions — with the distributive-conditional rule quietly changing results.
**Questions**
1. `Pick` vs `Omit` vs `Partial`? Implement `Pick` and `Omit`.
2. What is a mapped type? How do you make every property optional or remove `readonly`?
3. What is `infer`? Write `ReturnType` and `Awaited`.
4. What does "distributive conditional type" mean and how do you disable it?
5. When is advanced type-level code a bad idea? *(Slow compile times, unreadable errors, and the maintenance burden. Types should clarify the API, not become a second program.)*
6. `Omit` on a union type has a gotcha. What? *(`Omit` isn't distributive, so it collapses unions. Use a distributive helper.)*


**Self-check:** Why is `Awaited<Promise<Promise<number>>>` still `number`, and what would a non-recursive version hand you instead?

**Resources:** TypeScript Handbook — "Utility Types", "Mapped Types", "Conditional Types", "Template Literal Types", "Key Remapping via `as`" and "Distributive Conditional Types"; the `type-challenges` repo (Easy: `Pick`, `Omit`, `Exclude`, `Readonly`; Hard: `DeepReadonly`, `GetType`, the TS 5.1 template-literal pattern-matching exercises); Total TypeScript's type-level articles; TS 4.1 release notes (key remapping) and TS 4.5 release notes (tail-call elimination in conditional types).

**Mini task:** Implement without looking at `lib.d.ts`: `Pick`, `Omit`, `Awaited`, and `DeepReadonly`. Then build an `EventMap` — a mapped type with key remapping that turns `{ login: { userId: string } }` into `{ onLogin: (cb: (p: { userId: string }) => void) => void }`. Done-condition: a type-level `Equal<A, B>` assertion proves each one matches the built-in's behaviour, including the union case that stock `Omit` gets wrong.

**Mistakes:**
- **Reaching for `Omit<T, K>` on a union:** `Omit<Dog | Cat, 'name'>` does not give you a union — `keyof` of a union is the *intersection* of its keys and the mapped type is not distributive, so you silently get one object with only `name` and whatever both members share, losing `breed` and `lifespan` with no error. Use `T extends unknown ? Omit<T, K> : never`.
- **Letting a naked conditional distribute when you meant a whole-union check:** `T extends any ? A : B` fires per member, and for `any` it returns the *union of both branches* while for `never` it returns `never` — so a generic wrapper silently widens. Reach for `[T] extends [U]` when you are comparing types, tuples or arrays.
- **Trusting helper types for precision they do not have:** `ReturnType<T>` has been unconstrained since TS 4.7, so `ReturnType<NotAFunction>` is `any` rather than a type error, and `ReturnType<any>` — or a value that came out of untyped `JSON.parse` — is `any`, which is a type assertion in utility clothing. Constrain it with `T extends (...args: any[]) => any`, and remember `Partial<T>` does not make reads safe: every access is `T | undefined` even after you assign.
- **Writing recursive `Deep*` types with no depth guard:** `DeepReadonly<T>` over a self-referencing type (`TreeNode`, a JSON value, Express's `Request`) expands until the checker gives up with TS2589 "Type instantiation is excessively deep", and even without that error every `tsc` run and every keystroke pays for it — cap the depth with a tuple counter or bail out on functions and index signatures, and profile with `tsc --generateTrace`.
- **Losing the literal union before the type-level code sees it:** without `as const`, `const STATUSES = ['a', 'b']` is `string[]`, so `(typeof STATUSES)[number]` is `string`, `Exclude<Status, 'draft'>` collapses, and `` `get${Capitalize<K>}` `` becomes a template type that accepts any string — every mapped type built on top then type-checks your typos.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Built-in utilities vs hand-rolled equivalents:** using `Pick`/`Omit`/`Awaited` is free and readable; reimplementing them is a learning exercise, not production code — the interesting cases are the ones the lib doesn't cover.
- **`as const` plus `typeof` vs hand-written literal unions:** the value becomes the source of truth, but a const assertion can't express "the *keys* form a union" without indexing with `[number]`, which is a step people miss.
- **Distributive vs non-distributive conditionals:** distributive gives you union filtering for free; non-distributive is what you need for tuple comparisons and for `any`, where a distributive conditional over `any` produces a union of *both* branches.
- **Recursive `Deep*` types vs explicit ones:** recursion is DRY but drags the checker through every level and can hit instantiation depth; explicit types are boring and cheap, and boring is fine in a shared library.
- **Template literal types vs string constants:** `` `id-${string}` `` expresses patterns no constant can, at the cost of noisier autocomplete and worse error messages; for a closed set of keys, `as const` is better.

**Common wrong answers:** "conditional types are run-time logic" (they're pure compile-time and erase completely); "`Omit` distributes over unions because it's built on `Pick`" (it doesn't, and that's the gotcha); "`infer` is destructuring sugar" (it's a capture that works anywhere a type is checked — tuples, arrays, objects, not just functions); "a distributive conditional is always a bug" (the tuple wrapper is the deliberate fix, but it changes semantics, so you have to know which one you want).

**Probe:** "`Omit<Dog | Cat, 'name'>` gives you a single object with only the keys common to both. Why, and what's the fix?" (Expect: `Omit` is `Pick<T, Exclude<keyof T, K>>`, `keyof` of a union is the intersection of its keys, and the mapped type is not distributive, so the union collapses. Fix: a distributive helper, `type DistributiveOmit<T, K extends PropertyKey> = T extends unknown ? Omit<T, K> : never`, which applies per member.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Whether they can write `Pick` and `Omit` from scratch,** and why the standard library defines `Omit` as `Pick<T, Exclude<keyof T, K>>` rather than a mapped type with key filtering.
- **Modifier handling in mapped types:** `[K in keyof T]` copies `readonly` and `?`, `-readonly` and `-?` remove them, and `as` remaps keys.
- **Distributivity and how to suppress it,** plus the practical consequence that `Omit` over a union collapses it because `keyof` a union is the *intersection* of its keys.
- **Awareness of cost:** these are compile-time only, but a deep recursive type shows up in editor latency and CI, measurable with `tsc --generateTrace`.
- **Judgement about stopping:** a `PathsOf<T>` nobody can read is a maintenance liability, and the error messages it produces are worse than the explicit type it replaced.

**Model answer, Q3:** "`infer` is how a conditional type captures part of what it matched. `ReturnType<F>` is `F extends (...args: any[]) => infer R ? R : never` — `infer R` binds the return position and `R` becomes the result. `Awaited<T>` is `T extends PromiseLike<infer U> ? Awaited<U> : T`, and it's recursive because a promise is allowed to resolve to another promise, which is why `Awaited<Promise<Promise<number>>>` is `number` rather than `Promise<number>`. Note the built-ins use `(...args: any[])`, which is why `ReturnType<any>` is `any` instead of an error."
---

## 3.4.7 TypeScript with React, Express, and Zod
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know (full stack)**

**React (see Phase 4 for patterns)**
```tsx
import type { ComponentPropsWithoutRef, ReactNode, ChangeEvent } from 'react';
import { useState, useRef } from 'react';

// Extend native element props: pass-through attributes stay type-safe
type ButtonProps = ComponentPropsWithoutRef<'button'> & { variant?: 'primary' | 'ghost' };
export function Button({ variant = 'primary', ...rest }: ButtonProps) {
  return <button data-variant={variant} {...rest} />;
}

// Discriminated props: "href" and "onClick" can't both be required together
type LinkOrButton =
  | { as: 'a'; href: string; onClick?: never }
  | { as: 'button'; onClick: () => void; href?: never };

// Generic component, with T inferred from `items`
export function List<T>({ items, render }: { items: readonly T[]; render: (item: T) => ReactNode }) {
  return <ul>{items.map((it, i) => <li key={i}>{render(it)}</li>)}</ul>;
}

function Search() {
  const [user, setUser] = useState<User | null>(null);        // explicit when initial value is null/[]
  const ref = useRef<HTMLInputElement>(null);                  // DOM ref, starts null
  const onChange = (e: ChangeEvent<HTMLInputElement>) => setQuery(e.target.value);
  /* … */
}
```
- Props are typed with plain function signatures. `React.FC` is optional (it no longer adds implicit `children`, as of React 18 types). In React 19, `ref` is a normal prop for function components, so `forwardRef` is being phased out.
- Type `children` explicitly (`ReactNode`), type events with `React.ChangeEvent<HTMLInputElement>`/`MouseEvent`/`FormEvent`, and type context with a non-null default or a custom hook that throws when missing.
- Reducer state with discriminated unions, and typed hooks (`useAppDispatch`) for Redux Toolkit.

**Express**
```ts
import type { Request, Response, NextFunction, RequestHandler } from 'express';

// Request<Params, ResBody, ReqBody, Query>
type Params = { id: string };
type Body = { name: string };
const updateUser: RequestHandler<Params, UserDto | { error: string }, Body> = async (req, res) => {
  const user = await svc.rename(req.params.id, req.body.name);
  if (!user) return res.status(404).json({ error: 'Not found' });
  res.json(user);
};

// Augment Request (declaration merging)
declare global { namespace Express { interface Request { user?: AuthUser } } }

// Async wrapper so rejected promises reach the error middleware (Express 4)
const wrap = (fn: RequestHandler): RequestHandler => (req, res, next) => Promise.resolve(fn(req, res, next)).catch(next);
```
**Important:** `req.body` types are *claims*, not guarantees. Validate with a schema first.

**Zod: runtime validation + inferred types**
```ts
import { z } from 'zod';

export const CreateUser = z.object({
  email: z.string().email(),
  age: z.coerce.number().int().min(13).optional(),
  role: z.enum(['admin', 'user']).default('user'),
});
export type CreateUserInput = z.infer<typeof CreateUser>;      // one source of truth

// Express middleware
const validate = <S extends z.ZodTypeAny>(schema: S): RequestHandler => (req, res, next) => {
  const parsed = schema.safeParse(req.body);
  if (!parsed.success) return res.status(422).json({ errors: parsed.error.flatten().fieldErrors });
  req.body = parsed.data;                                      // validated and typed
  next();
};

// Typed fetch wrapper that validates responses
class HttpError extends Error { constructor(public status: number, message: string) { super(message); } }
async function fetchJson<S extends z.ZodTypeAny>(url: string, schema: S, init?: RequestInit): Promise<z.infer<S>> {
  const res = await fetch(url, init);
  if (!res.ok) throw new HttpError(res.status, await res.text());   // fetch doesn't reject on 4xx/5xx
  return schema.parse(await res.json());
}
```
- **Sharing types across the stack:** a shared package in a monorepo (schemas and DTOs), OpenAPI codegen (`openapi-typescript`), **tRPC** (end-to-end inference with no codegen, ideal when one team owns both sides in TS), and GraphQL Code Generator.
- **Env vars:** validate `process.env` once at startup with a schema (`t3-env`/Zod) so missing config fails fast.
- **Branded types** for IDs and units:
```ts
type Brand<T, B extends string> = T & { readonly __brand: B };
type UserId = Brand<string, 'UserId'>;  type OrderId = Brand<string, 'OrderId'>;
const loadUser = (id: UserId) => { /* … */ };   // can't pass an OrderId by mistake
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.4.4, 3.4.6, 3.4.3

**Why interviewers ask:** This is the day job, so it's where real experience shows. The question that decides the answer is whether they know `req.body: CreateUserDto` is a *claim* and that the fix is a schema at the boundary — that distinction is essentially the whole field of run-time validation, and it's where weak candidates hand-wave.

**Definition:** Applying the type system to a specific stack: typing React props so components stay extensible without losing inference, typing Express handlers whose `RequestHandler<Params, ResBody, ReqBody, Query>` generics line up, and using Zod as the single source of truth from which both the run-time check and the static type are derived.
**Questions**
1. How would you type a reusable `Button` that accepts all native button props?
2. How do you type `useState` with a nullable value? `useRef` for a DOM node vs a mutable value?
3. Why isn't `req.body: CreateUserDto` type-safe by itself? What do you do? 
4. How do you share types between a React frontend and a Node backend? Trade-offs of tRPC vs OpenAPI codegen?
5. How do you model a component whose props are mutually exclusive?
6. What are branded types and when would you use them?

**Model answer, Q3:** "The annotation is only a compile-time claim. Express will happily hand me whatever JSON the client sent. I validate at the edge with a Zod schema in a middleware, return a `422` with field errors on failure, and then the handler gets the parsed value, whose type comes from `z.infer`. That way the runtime check and the static type can't drift apart."


**Self-check:** Why isn't `const body: CreateUser = req.body` a validation, and where exactly does the check have to live for it to count as one?

**Resources:** Zod's documentation on schemas, `z.infer`, `safeParse` and error formatting; Express's TypeScript docs plus `@types/express`'s `RequestHandler<Params, ResBody, ReqBody, Query>` signature; React's TypeScript guide (`ComponentPropsWithoutRef`, `useRef` typing); tRPC's docs on inference versus OpenAPI codegen with `openapi-typescript`; `t3-env` for validating `process.env` at startup.

**Mini task:** Add Zod to one Express route end to end: schema, a `validate` middleware that answers 422 with `error.issues`, a handler typed from `z.infer`, and the same schema imported by the React caller through a `fetchJson` helper that checks `res.ok` first. Done-condition: make the server return a wrong shape, watch the client throw with the offending field path, and confirm the component never renders — with no `as` anywhere in the client path.

**Mistakes:**
