- **Putting the `declare global` Express augmentation in a file with no imports or exports:** it stops being a module, so the augmentation is scoped wrongly and never applies. And declaring `user?: AuthUser` as optional means every handler must handle "no user", which quietly hides missing auth.
- **Writing `useState<User>(null)`:** under `strictNullChecks` that's an error, and the correct fix — `useState<User | null>(null)` — forces every consumer to handle the empty state.
- **Importing a schema with `import type`:** the type erases, the validator disappears, and the module resolves to nothing at run time.
- **Parsing a response into a narrowed shape and then re-sending that object:** validation strips unknown keys, which is fine for reads and wrong when the object goes back to the API.
- **Assuming a `RequestHandler` return-type annotation changes the response:** the generic types the body only; headers, status codes and cookies are set at run time and nothing checks them.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Schema-first vs type-first:** a Zod schema is one source of truth with a parse cost and a run-time dependency; a plain interface is free at run time but you hand-maintain it and re-implement validation anyway — `satisfies` for config objects, a separate validator for anything from outside.
- **Shared schema package vs OpenAPI codegen vs tRPC:** a shared package gives instant no-build inference and is the simplest option for one TS monorepo; codegen keeps the contract language-agnostic and enforceable by other consumers but introduces regeneration and drift; tRPC has the best developer experience when both sides are TypeScript and one team owns both, and is awkward across languages or org boundaries.
- **Validating in the browser vs on the server:** client-side validation is user experience, not security — the server must re-validate, and the cheapest way to guarantee the two agree is to import the same schema in both.
- **Branded IDs vs nominal wrapper classes:** a brand is zero-cost and erases but validates nothing; a class or smart constructor validates at construction and adds a value you must thread through every call.
- **`React.FC` vs a plain function with typed props:** props typed as an intersection with `ComponentPropsWithoutRef` keep native attributes flowing through, and a generic component needs `<T,>` in `.tsx` to disambiguate from JSX syntax — a small tax for inference clarity.

**Common wrong answers:** "`req.body: CreateUserDto` is enough because Express parses JSON" (a compile-time claim only; the client sends whatever it likes); "`any` on the handler parameters keeps things simple" (it disables checking for the whole handler body); "tRPC is always better than OpenAPI codegen" (it's TS-only and same-repo — a non-TS consumer or a third-party API needs a spec-first contract); "branded types are just `as` with extra steps" (a brand is checked everywhere; `as` is unchecked at the point of use and doesn't survive a refactor).

**Probe:** "A shared Zod schema package is consumed by a Vite React app (`moduleResolution: Bundler`) and a Node service (`NodeNext`). What breaks first, and what's the fix?" (Expect: the package's own build output and `exports` map — it has to ship ESM for the browser and CJS (or ESM-only with `type: module` plus `.js` extensions in emitted relative imports) for Node; and the schema must be a *value* import, so `import type` on the consumer side is fine but `verbatimModuleSyntax` will force it, which is exactly what you want.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Extending native props** with `ComponentPropsWithoutRef<'button'>` so pass-through attributes stay typed, instead of re-declaring `onClick` or wrapping in `any`.
- **Discriminated props** — `as: 'a'` requires `href` and forbids `onClick` — instead of six optional props and an `if` in the body.
- **Knowing `useState<T>` matters when the initial value is `null` or `[]`,** that the annotation must be `User | null` or every consumer lies, and that `useRef<T>(null)` gives a mutable box whose `.current` needs a null check.
- **Treating `req.body` as untrusted:** schema in middleware, `safeParse`, 422 with field-level errors, and `z.infer` as the only place the type is written down.
- **Sharing contracts deliberately:** shared schema package vs OpenAPI codegen vs tRPC, with the language-boundary trade-off stated rather than "tRPC is better".
- **Branded IDs,** so a `UserId` can't be passed where an `OrderId` belongs.
---

## 3.4 TypeScript: Challenges and Pitfalls

**Type challenges to solve (from `type-challenges`):** `Pick`, `Readonly`, `TupleToObject`, `First`, `Length`, `Exclude`, `Awaited`, `If`, `Concat`, `Includes`, `Push`, `Parameters`, `ReturnType`, `Omit`, `DeepReadonly`, `Flatten`, `Trim`, `Capitalize`, `UnionToIntersection`.

**Pitfalls checklist**
- `any` leaking out of `JSON.parse`, `Object.assign`, untyped libraries. Enable `noImplicitAny` and ESLint `no-explicit-any`.
- Passing objects through functions with `as` casts ("type laundering").
- Optional vs `undefined` (`exactOptionalPropertyTypes`).
- Date, `Map`, and class instances don't survive `JSON.stringify`, so the type says `Date` but the runtime value is a `string`.
- Enums in strip-only or `isolatedModules` setups.
- Deeply recursive types slowing the editor (profile with `tsc --generateTrace`).

**Mini tasks**
1. Take a loosely typed Express + React app and turn on `strict` and `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`. Fix everything without `any` or `as`.
2. Build a type-safe event emitter: `emitter.on('login', (user) => …)` with an `Events` map where payloads are checked.
3. Build a typed API client (`api.get('/users/:id', { params: { id: '1' } })`) that infers the response from a route map.
4. Implement 10 type challenges without looking up the answers.

**Self-check:** Can you explain why a `User` type doesn't protect against bad API data, and show the Zod pattern that fixes it, in 90 seconds?

---

# 3.5 BROWSER INTERNALS

## 3.5.1 Browser Architecture and the Rendering Pipeline
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** (strongly tested for frontend-leaning roles)

**Architecture:** modern browsers are multi-process: a **browser** process (UI, navigation), **renderer** processes (one per site or tab, via **site isolation**, running Blink/WebKit + the JS engine), a **GPU** process, and a **network** service. In a renderer, the **main thread** runs JS, style, layout, and paint recording. The **compositor thread** and raster workers handle scrolling and `transform`/`opacity` animations independently of the main thread, which is why those stay smooth while JS is busy.

**The pipeline (every frame that changes visuals)**
```
JS ─▶ Style (match CSS, compute styles) ─▶ Layout/Reflow (geometry)
   ─▶ Paint (record draw ops per layer) ─▶ Raster ─▶ Composite (GPU assembles layers)
```
1. **Parse HTML → DOM** (incremental, streaming). The **preload scanner** looks ahead for resources (scripts, CSS, images) while the main parser is blocked.
2. **Parse CSS → CSSOM.** CSS is render-blocking, and scripts wait for pending CSS (they may query styles).
3. **Style calculation:** match selectors, compute final values. DOM + CSSOM yield the **render tree** (excludes `display:none`, includes pseudo-elements).
4. **Layout:** compute size and position (the cascade of geometry, expensive because changes can ripple).
5. **Paint:** produce draw instructions (text, colors, borders, shadows) per layer.
6. **Composite:** layers (for `transform`/`opacity`/`will-change`/video/canvas, fixed elements) are rastered and combined on the GPU.

**What triggers what**
- **Reflow (layout):** changing geometry (`width`, `height`, `top/left`, `margin`, `padding`, `font-size`, adding/removing DOM nodes, window resize, content change). Always followed by paint.
- **Repaint:** changing appearance without geometry (`color`, `background`, `visibility`, `box-shadow`, `outline`).
- **Composite only:** `transform`, `opacity`.
- **Forced synchronous layout ("layout thrashing")** happens when JS *reads* a layout-dependent value (`offsetHeight`, `getBoundingClientRect()`, `scrollTop`, `getComputedStyle().height`) after *writing* styles, so the browser must layout immediately. In a loop it repeats per iteration.
```js
// ❌ thrashing: read/write interleaved → N forced layouts
for (const el of items) el.style.width = (container.offsetWidth / 2) + 'px';
// ✅ batch reads, then writes
const w = container.offsetWidth / 2;
for (const el of items) el.style.width = w + 'px';
```
- **Fixes:** batch reads and writes, use `requestAnimationFrame` for writes, prefer `transform`, use `DocumentFragment` or build off-DOM, toggle classes instead of many inline styles, use `contain`/`content-visibility`, and virtualize long lists.
- **Parser-blocking and render-blocking:** `<script>` without `defer`/`async`, CSS `<link>`, and `@import`. **Critical rendering path** optimization means minimizing the bytes, round trips, and blocking resources before first paint.
- **Frame budget:** about 16.7ms at 60Hz. Long tasks (over 50ms) block input.
- **`DOMContentLoaded` vs `load`** (3.1.1), and the **`readyState`** progression (`loading` → `interactive` → `complete`).


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.1, 3.2.2, 3.2.9, 3.3.6

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the mechanism behind every performance question later in the loop — why `transform` is cheaper than `left`, what a long task does to INP, why `content-visibility` helps. Candidates who can say *which stage* a change lands in are the ones who can fix a page instead of guessing at it, and it's the most-tested "advanced fundamentals" topic.

**Definition:** The route from bytes to pixels and the process model that runs it: a browser process, one renderer process per site (site isolation), a GPU process and a network service. Inside a renderer, the main thread parses HTML and CSS, runs JavaScript, computes styles, lays out and records paint ops, while raster workers and the compositor thread produce and combine layers off the main thread.
**Questions**
1. Walk through the rendering pipeline. Where does JS fit?
2. Reflow vs repaint vs composite? Give triggers for each.
3. What's layout thrashing? Show a fix.
4. Why are `transform` and `opacity` animations smoother than `left`? *(compositor thread, no layout/paint)*
5. What's the preload scanner?
6. Why does CSS block rendering and scripts?
7. What's the render tree? What isn't in it? *(`display:none` elements, `<head>`)*
8. How does site isolation affect architecture? *(One renderer per site, so cross-site iframes are out-of-process, and Spectre mitigations apply.)*


**Mistakes:**
- **Reading layout right after writing it:** `offsetHeight`, `getBoundingClientRect()`, `scrollTop` and `getComputedStyle(el).height` after a style write force a synchronous layout, and inside a loop that is one layout per iteration. `getComputedStyle` returns a live object, so every property read re-resolves it — snapshot the value into a constant, then do the writes.
- **Animating a layout property:** `height`, `width`, `top`, `left` and `margin` put style, layout and paint on the main thread on every frame of the animation, so a dropdown or modal janks under any background load. Animate `transform: translateY()` on a pre-laid-out panel, or `scaleY()` with `transform-origin: top` over a fixed-height element.
- **Rebuilding markup with `innerHTML +=`, or appending while measuring:** `innerHTML +=` re-parses the whole subtree each time, destroying listeners, focus and scroll position, and it is quadratic in the number of appends; appending one node at a time while interleaving a layout read triggers a layout per append. Build a `DocumentFragment` or a detached node, then insert once.
- **Leaving `will-change: transform` (or `translateZ(0)`) on permanently:** the hint forces layer promotion whether or not an animation happens, and every composited layer costs GPU memory and raster time, so a page of promoted cards ends up slower than the one you were optimising. Add it just before the animation and remove it afterwards.
- **Assuming `requestAnimationFrame` or `setTimeout` chunking fixes jank:** rAF callbacks run *before* style, layout and paint in the same frame, so reading geometry inside one still forces a synchronous layout; and `setTimeout(fn, 0)` yields to rendering but not to input, so the long task still eats clicks and INP stays high. Yield to real input with `scheduler.yield()` or `isInputPending()`.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Compositor-thread animation vs main-thread animation:** `transform` and `opacity` keep animating while JS is busy, but each promoted layer costs memory and raster time, and a layer that needs re-rastering (animating a `filter` that pulls in new content) drops back to the main thread.
- **`will-change` or `translateZ(0)` vs no hint:** a hint avoids the promotion hitch at the start of an animation; left on permanently it wastes memory and can pin stale content.
- **`contain: content` or `content-visibility: auto` vs a flat DOM:** containment scopes invalidation, and `content-visibility: auto` skips layout and paint for off-screen content, but it costs accessibility — skipped content isn't focusable until revealed and its size isn't known until measured.
- **Batch building off-DOM (`DocumentFragment`, one `innerHTML`) vs many incremental `appendChild`s:** batching gives one layout and paint per batch; incremental appends are the right choice when you're streaming results in over time.
- **Reading layout synchronously vs observers:** direct reads are simple but force layout; `ResizeObserver` and `IntersectionObserver` are async and batched, which is the right tool for "react when this changes".

**Common wrong answers:** "the browser repaints every frame" (only when something invalidated); "layers are free" (each composited layer costs GPU memory and raster time — hundreds of them make a page slower); "`translateZ(0)` always helps" (it forces a promotion; on a page with thousands of elements it's a memory regression); "CSS blocks scripts because the parser can't read it" (CSS blocks *rendering*, and it blocks scripts because they may query styles and would otherwise render unstyled); "reflow is always expensive so avoid layout" (layout is only expensive when it invalidates a large subtree — small local layouts are cheap).

**Probe:** "You animate a dropdown's height from 0 to 200px on open and the trace shows layout on every frame. Make it smooth without changing what it looks like." (Expect: height is a layout property, so animate `transform: scaleY()` on a wrapper with `transform-origin: top` over a fixed-height element, or animate `translateY`/`opacity` on a pre-laid-out panel, or `clip-path` (composited in Chrome). Verify in the Frames track that Layout bars disappear and the work stays off the main thread.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Correct stages in order, and the right thread for each:** script, style and layout and paint-record on the main thread; raster and composite elsewhere.
- **Triggers mapped to stages:** geometry → reflow, which always implies paint; appearance only → repaint; `transform` and `opacity` → composite only.
- **Forced synchronous layout explained as read-after-write,** with a batching fix rather than "avoid layout".
- **Knowing that `transform` still costs a layer,** and that `will-change` left on permanently is a memory regression, not a free speedup.
- **Why CSS blocks rendering and scripts,** plus `DOMContentLoaded` vs first paint vs `load`.
- **Site isolation:** a renderer per site means a cross-origin iframe is out-of-process, so a compromised renderer can't read the rest of the page.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Bytes arrive, the parser builds the DOM incrementally while the preload scanner runs ahead of it looking for subresources. CSS goes into the CSSOM; stylesheets are render-blocking, and a script waits for pending CSS because it may query styles. JavaScript runs on the renderer's main thread — the same thread that does style, layout and paint recording — which is why a long task freezes the page. When a task finishes, the browser may take a rendering opportunity: recompute style for invalidated subtrees, lay out if geometry changed, record paint ops, then hand the layer tree to raster workers and the compositor, which combine layers on the GPU. The main thread never rasterises anything; it only produces the instructions."
**Mini task:** record a Performance trace of a page that thrashes layout (use the snippet above on 2,000 nodes), find the purple "Layout" bars with the warning "Forced reflow", fix it, and compare.


**Self-check:** Name the pipeline stages in order, say which thread does raster, and explain why reading `offsetHeight` immediately after writing `style.width` is expensive.

**Resources:** web.dev's "Rendering performance", "Avoid layout thrashing" and "Optimize long tasks" guides; the Chrome DevTools Performance panel documentation on the Frames track, forced-reflow warnings and layer visualisation; MDN's "Rendering performance" page; the HTML standard's rendering steps and the CSS Containment spec (`contain`, `content-visibility`); the Rendering tab docs for paint flashing and layer tree inspection.
---

## 3.5.2 JavaScript Engine (V8) in Practice
🔴 Advanced · **Good-to-know**

- **Pipeline:** parse → AST → **Ignition** (bytecode interpreter) → hot functions optimized by **TurboFan** (JIT, machine code) with **speculative optimization** based on observed types. If an assumption breaks (a function that always got ints suddenly gets a string), it **deoptimizes**. A cheaper baseline tier (Sparkplug, Maglev) sits between them.
- **Hidden classes (shapes) and inline caches:** objects with the same property insertion order share a shape, so property access becomes a fast offset lookup. **Writing practices that keep code fast:** initialize all properties in the constructor in the same order, don't `delete` properties (set to `undefined`/`null`), keep arrays homogeneous and dense (no holes), and don't mix types in hot functions (monomorphic > polymorphic > megamorphic).
- **GC:** generational (young "scavenger", old "mark-compact"), incremental and concurrent (Orinoco), so GC pauses are short, but allocation pressure still costs.
- **Don't micro-optimize prematurely.** Profile first, because most slowness is network, bundle size, DOM work, or algorithms.
- **Startup cost:** parse and compile time scales with JS bytes, which is why **shipping less JS** beats clever code. Code caching helps repeat visits.


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.9, 3.3.16, 3.6.5

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the differentiator for a senior or performance-leaning role, and it's the only topic where "my code is slow" can be answered with mechanism rather than folklore. The tell is whether they understand that V8 optimises for the types it has actually seen, not for the types you declared.

**Definition:** How a JIT actually executes JavaScript: source is parsed to an AST and compiled to bytecode by Ignition, hot functions are re-compiled by Sparkplug and Maglev, and finally optimised by TurboFan into machine code using speculative assumptions drawn from the shapes and types it has observed, with garbage collection that is generational, incremental and concurrent.
**Questions:** What does TurboFan do? What's deoptimization and why might your function trigger it? What's a hidden class? Why can `delete obj.x` slow code? Where do you start when JS is slow? *(Performance panel profile and flame chart, find long tasks, fix the algorithm or reduce work before micro-tuning.)*


**Self-check:** Explain, in terms of feedback and inline caches, why adding properties in a consistent order keeps code fast and `delete` doesn't.

**Resources:** V8 blog posts — "Ignition and TurboFan", "Sparkplug", "Maglev" and "Fast properties"; MDN's "Memory management" and "Garbage collection" guide; the Chrome DevTools Performance panel docs and `performance.measureUserAgentSpecificMemory()`; Node's `--max-old-space-size` and `v8.getHeapStatistics()` references; web.dev's INP and long-task guidance for where profiling should start.

**Mini task:** In DevTools, profile three loops that each build 100k objects: (a) adding properties in a consistent order, (b) `delete`ing a property inside the loop, (c) alternating number and string values in the same field. Use `--allow-natives-syntax` with `%GetOptimizationStatus` to see which functions get optimised and which deopt, and compare the three flame charts. Done-condition: you can point at a line in the flame chart and name the mechanism responsible for the difference.

**Mistakes:**
- **Adding properties in a different order per code path:** `if (x) o.a = 1; o.b = 2` produces two different hidden classes, so every call site reading `a` or `b` goes polymorphic and then megamorphic and the access stops being an offset load. Declare all fields in the constructor, in one order, with values of the intended type.
- **`delete`ing a property to make it optional:** `delete obj.field` moves the object into dictionary mode, discarding its shape and inline caches, and V8 never goes back — do it in a loop and the cliff is measurable. Assign `undefined`/`null` instead, knowing the key is then still visible to `in`, `Object.keys` and `'field' in obj`.
- **Storing two different types in one field of a hot function:** pushing a number into a field that held a string changes its representation, so TurboFan's speculation breaks, the function deopts, and if the values keep alternating it never gets promoted again. Normalise once at the boundary — IDs as numbers, dates as epoch numbers or ISO strings, never both.
- **Monkey-patching built-in prototypes:** overriding `Array.prototype.filter` or adding enumerable properties to `Object.prototype` invalidates shapes and array fast paths for the whole page, leaks the extra keys into every `for…in`, and deoptimises code you never touched. Put the helpers in module scope instead.
- **Creating sparse arrays:** `new Array(n)`, assigning far past the end (`arr[1000] = x`), or `delete arr[0]` moves the elements into dictionary mode, so `.map`, `.filter` and iteration deoptimise. Use `Array.from({ length: n }, …)`, `push`, or `.fill()`/`.splice()` to keep elements packed.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Monomorphic code vs ergonomic generic code:** one shape per hot function means normalising or casting at the edges; one generic function that accepts anything is megamorphic in the middle.
- **`obj.x = undefined` vs `delete obj.x`:** keeping the key preserves the shape and V8's feedback, but the property still exists, which costs a little memory and confuses `in` and `Object.keys` checks.
- **Fresh immutable objects vs in-place mutation:** fresh objects keep shapes stable and enable structural sharing for React, Immer and memoisation; mutation avoids garbage but causes layout thrashing and defeats memoisation.
- **Ship less JS vs hand-optimise:** byte count beats clever code, because parse, compile and code-cache behaviour all scale with bytes — that's a build-time problem, not a micro-optimisation.
- **Profiling vs guessing:** the Performance panel names the hot line in about a minute; guessing costs hours and tends to add the wrong optimisation.

**Common wrong answers:** "V8 compiles everything to machine code up front" (it parses to bytecode and only optimises hot functions — the rest stay in Ignition or Sparkplug); "deoptimisation means the code is permanently broken" (it re-profiles and re-optimises); "GC pauses are the main cause of jank" (usually main-thread work and layout; GC is concurrent in modern V8); "use `Map` instead of objects because it's faster" (both are hash structures — `Map` is better for dynamic keys and avoids shape churn, not intrinsically faster); "adding `type` comments makes it faster" (they were removed and never affected run-time behaviour).

**Probe:** "A hot function alternates between `number` and `string` values for the same field and gets slower over a long session. What's happening?" (Expect: the field's representation is polymorphic, so the inline cache goes polymorphic and then megamorphic while TurboFan repeatedly deopts, leaving the function in a lower tier. Fix by normalising the shape once at the boundary, splitting into monomorphic functions, or keeping the values one type — numeric IDs as numbers, dates as epoch numbers or ISO strings, never both.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Speculation and deoptimisation as a feedback loop,** not as "the JIT got confused" — including that deopt is normal and re-optimisation is cheap.
- **Hidden classes as the reason property-adding order matters,** and `delete` moving an object to dictionary mode.
- **Monomorphic vs polymorphic vs megamorphic** and that mixing value types in a hot function is a measurable regression.
- **Honest framing of GC:** generational with incremental, concurrent marking and sweeping, so full pauses are short — the cost is allocation rate and retained memory, not long stop-the-world collections.
- **The right first move on "JS is slow":** profile, then reduce bytes and main-thread work before micro-tuning.

**Model answer, Q1:** "TurboFan is the optimising JIT: it takes bytecode plus V8's feedback — inline cache records saying which object shapes and field representations a function has seen — and emits machine code that assumes those observations hold, so a specific receiver map, a field that's a small integer rather than a double, a callee that never gets overridden. When an assumption breaks, V8 deoptimises: it discards the optimised code, runs the function in Ignition again, records the new shape, and re-optimises, usually cheaply. A deopt loop is when it keeps being deoptimised, and then V8 leaves it in a lower tier. A hidden class is V8's internal shape — objects whose properties are added in the same order share a shape, so a property read becomes an offset load instead of a hash lookup, and inline caches go monomorphic, then polymorphic for a handful of shapes, then megamorphic, which is a real lookup. `delete obj.x` forces the object into dictionary mode, which is why the same code gets slower when you delete in a loop. Where do I start when JS is slow? Profile first — flame chart, long tasks, allocation — because most slow JS is network, bytes parsed, DOM work, or a bad algorithm, not engine trivia."
---

## 3.5.3 Events: Propagation, Delegation, and Listener Behavior
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Three phases:** **capture** (window → target) → **target** → **bubble** (target → window). `addEventListener(type, fn, { capture, once, passive, signal })`. Handlers default to the bubble phase.

- **`target` vs `currentTarget`:** the element that fired the event vs the element the listener is attached to. `this` in a regular-function listener is `currentTarget`.
- **`stopPropagation()`** stops further propagation. **`stopImmediatePropagation()`** also stops other listeners on the same element. **`preventDefault()`** cancels the default action (link navigation, form submit) but not propagation.
- **Delegation:** attach **one** listener to a common ancestor and inspect `event.target` (`closest('[data-action]')`). Benefits: fewer listeners, dynamic children work automatically, and less memory.
```js
list.addEventListener('click', (e) => {
  const btn = e.target.closest('button[data-id]');
  if (!btn || !list.contains(btn)) return;
  remove(btn.dataset.id);
});
```
- **Events that don't bubble:** `focus`/`blur` (use `focusin`/`focusout`), `mouseenter`/`mouseleave` (use `mouseover`/`out`), `load`, `scroll` on elements. Capture works for them.
- **Passive listeners:** `{ passive: true }` promises you won't call `preventDefault()`, so the browser can scroll without waiting for your handler (touch and wheel listeners on `window`/`document`/`body` are passive by default in Chrome).
- **Cleanup:** remove listeners (`removeEventListener` needs the same function reference and capture flag), or pass an `AbortSignal` to remove many at once.
```js
const ac = new AbortController();
window.addEventListener('resize', onResize, { signal: ac.signal });
window.addEventListener('keydown', onKey,   { signal: ac.signal });
ac.abort();   // removes both
```
- **Custom events:** `new CustomEvent('cart:add', { detail, bubbles: true, composed: true })` and `dispatchEvent`.
- **Pointer Events** (`pointerdown/move/up`) unify mouse, touch, and pen. Use `setPointerCapture` for drags, `touch-action` CSS to control gestures.
- **Keyboard:** `event.key` (not deprecated `keyCode`), `event.code` for physical keys, `isComposing` for IME input, and `keydown` vs `keyup` vs `input`.
- **`click` fires on Enter/Space for native buttons**, which is one more reason to avoid `div` buttons.
- **React's synthetic events** are delegated at the root container (not `document`) since React 17. `e.stopPropagation()` in React stops React propagation, and native listeners attached to `document` can still fire.
- **Unload:** don't use `unload`/`beforeunload` for analytics (they break bfcache and are unreliable on mobile). Use `visibilitychange`/`pagehide` with `navigator.sendBeacon()` or `fetch(..., { keepalive: true })`.


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.6, 3.5.1, 3.1.8

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the most-tested fundamentals topic in frontend loops, and it's where you find out whether someone debugs with DevTools or by guessing. Real bugs — a modal that closes when you click inside it, an analytics handler that never fires, a listener that fires twice — are propagation mistakes, and the delegation caveats show whether they've shipped anything.

**Definition:** The DOM event model: an event dispatched at a node propagates through three phases — capture from the root down to the target, the target itself, then bubbling back up — with `target` fixed as the originating node while `currentTarget` changes with each step, and handlers able to cancel propagation or the default action independently.
**Questions**
1. Explain capture vs bubble. How do you listen in the capture phase?
2. `target` vs `currentTarget`? `stopPropagation` vs `preventDefault`?
3. What is event delegation? Benefits and caveats? *(Non-bubbling events, and `target` might be a child element, so use `closest`.)*
4. What does `passive: true` do?
5. How do you remove an anonymous listener? *(You can't. Use a named reference or `AbortController`.)*
6. Why shouldn't you use `unload` to send analytics?
7. How would you implement drag-and-drop with pointer events?


**Self-check:** Why doesn't `stopPropagation()` in a delegated handler stop another handler on the same element, and what do you call instead?

**Resources:** MDN's "Event bubbling" and `EventTarget.addEventListener()` pages, including the options table; the DOM standard's "Dispatching events" algorithm; the HTML spec's event-processing chapter; web.dev's "Third-party JavaScript and the event loop" for passive listeners; React 17 release notes on root-level event delegation; MDN's `pagehide` and `navigator.sendBeacon()` pages.

**Mini task:** Build a delegated table: one `click` listener on the container, rows added and removed dynamically, `data-action` on buttons, and nested icon spans. Add a capture-phase `focus` listener for keyboard navigation and an `AbortController` that tears both down. Done-condition: clicking an icon fires the row action exactly once, Tab navigation works, and after `abort()` neither handler runs — including on rows added afterwards.

**Mistakes:**
- **Reading `e.target` in a delegated handler:** a click on a nested `<span>` or an SVG icon makes `target` the icon, so `target.dataset.id` is `undefined` and the row action silently does nothing — use `currentTarget` for the node you are bound to and `closest('[data-action]')` to find the actionable ancestor, then check it is still inside your container. `closest()` does not cross a shadow boundary, so use `composedPath()` there.
- **Delegating an event that never arrives:** `focus`/`blur`, `mouseenter`/`mouseleave`, `load` and element `scroll` do not bubble, so a listener on the container never fires. Use `focusin`/`focusout` and `mouseover`/`mouseout` with `relatedTarget` checks, or listen in the capture phase. Same trap for `new CustomEvent('cart:add', { detail })` — `bubbles` defaults to `false`, and `composed` defaults to `false` so it will not leave a shadow root.
- **Confusing the three cancellation tools:** `stopPropagation()` only halts the walk to ancestors — other listeners on the same node still run, which is what `stopImmediatePropagation()` is for — and `preventDefault()` cancels only the default action (navigation, submit, checkbox toggle) and does nothing unless `event.cancelable` is true, which it is not for most `keydown`s or for `scroll`. In React, `e.stopPropagation()` stops only React's synthetic tree, so a native listener on `document` still fires.
- **Registering listeners with a fresh reference each render:** `addEventListener('click', () => …)` in a render or effect means `removeEventListener(fn)` never matches — removal needs the same function reference *and* the same capture flag — so handlers accumulate and the action fires two, three, ten times. Hoist the function or register once with `{ signal: controller.signal }` and `abort()`.
- **Promising passive and then calling `preventDefault`:** with `{ passive: true }` the browser has already started scrolling by the time your handler runs, so `preventDefault()` is ignored with a console warning; conversely, omitting it on a `wheel`/`touchmove`/`scroll` listener on `window`/`document`/`body` blocks scrolling until your handler returns. Declare passive for observe-only handlers, never for one you need to cancel.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Delegation vs per-element listeners:** one ancestor listener is cheap and survives DOM churn, but you pay a tree walk per event and can't easily give individual handlers `once` or `passive` semantics; direct listeners are precise but must be cleaned up and leak if you forget.
- **Capture vs bubble for the same hook:** capture sees the event before any handler can stop it, which is what analytics, debug tooling and delegated `focus` need; bubble sees the fully-resolved state, which is what most UI code wants.
- **`stopPropagation` vs `stopImmediatePropagation`:** the first leaves other listeners on the same node running, which is what composable component libraries need; the second takes the event over completely.
- **Passive vs non-passive listeners:** passive is right for `scroll`, `touchmove` and `wheel` handlers that only observe; the cost is that you cannot call `preventDefault()`.
- **`AbortController` teardown vs manual `removeEventListener`:** a signal removes a whole group atomically and is what frameworks use; manual removal is fine for a one-off but depends on the function reference surviving, which closures over props easily break.

**Common wrong answers:** "`stopPropagation()` stops other listeners on the same element" (that's `stopImmediatePropagation`); "`preventDefault()` stops the click reaching ancestors" (it cancels the default action only — navigation, submit, checkbox toggle — and never stops propagation); "anonymous listeners can be removed by type" (removal needs the same function reference, which is why frameworks use `AbortController`); "`passive: true` makes your handler faster" (it lets the browser scroll without waiting for your handler — the win is compositor latency, not your function); "delegation always beats per-element listeners" (it can't work for non-bubbling events, and a delegated handler that walks the tree on every event can cost more than a few direct listeners).

**Probe:** "One `click` listener on a table, rows rendered dynamically, and buttons containing nested `<span>`s and `<svg>` icons. Some rows' remove buttons fire twice, and only rows added after load are affected. What are the two bugs?" (Expect: (1) double registration — a per-row listener added on top of the delegated one, which only affects rows added later; (2) reading `e.target` instead of `closest()`, so a click on the icon has no `dataset.id`. Also note `closest()` doesn't cross a shadow boundary, so use `composedPath()` there.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **The three phases in order, and that only bubbling events reach the bubble phase,** plus capture as the workaround for `focus`/`blur`.
- **`target` vs `currentTarget`,** and why a delegated handler must use `closest()` — and `composedPath()` inside shadow DOM, where `target` is retargeted to the host.
- **`stopPropagation` vs `stopImmediatePropagation` vs `preventDefault`,** and that `preventDefault` only does anything when `cancelable` is true.
- **Passive listeners as a performance contract,** and that calling `preventDefault()` in one is ignored with a console warning.
- **Correct teardown:** the same function reference and capture flag for `removeEventListener`, or an `AbortSignal` to remove a group at once.

**Model answer, Q1:** "An event dispatched on a node propagates along its ancestor chain. The capture phase runs from `window` down to the target's parent, the target phase fires on the target itself, and the bubble phase runs back up to `window` — but only for events with `bubbles: true`. `addEventListener(type, fn)` registers in the bubble phase; you get capture with `{ capture: true }` or the third positional argument. Capture is the only phase available to non-bubbling events, which is how you delegate focus: listen for `focus` in the capture phase on the container and it behaves like `focusin`. One subtlety: at the target, both capture-registered and bubble-registered listeners run, in registration order, which is why `stopPropagation` doesn't stop the other listeners on that same node."
---

## 3.5.4 Same-Origin Policy, CORS, and Cross-Origin Controls
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** (full stack classic)

**Origin** = scheme + host + port. `https://a.com` ≠ `http://a.com` ≠ `https://a.com:8443` ≠ `https://api.a.com`. **Same-site** (eTLD+1, i.e. `a.com`) is a looser concept used for cookies.

**Same-Origin Policy (SOP):** scripts from one origin can't *read* responses from another (embedding is mostly allowed: images, scripts, CSS, iframes, and form posts can be *sent* cross-origin but not read). SOP protects users' data on other sites. It is enforced **by browsers**, not servers.

**CORS** is a controlled *relaxation* of SOP via response headers.
- **Simple requests** (GET/HEAD/POST with safelisted headers and content types `text/plain`, `application/x-www-form-urlencoded`, `multipart/form-data`) go straight out, and the browser checks `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` on the response.
- **Preflighted requests:** anything else (methods like PUT/PATCH/DELETE, `Content-Type: application/json`, `Authorization` or custom headers) triggers an `OPTIONS` request first:
```http
OPTIONS /api/items HTTP/1.1
Origin: https://app.example.com
Access-Control-Request-Method: PUT
Access-Control-Request-Headers: content-type, authorization

HTTP/1.1 204 No Content
Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://app.example.com
Access-Control-Allow-Methods: GET, PUT, DELETE
Access-Control-Allow-Headers: content-type, authorization
Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true
Access-Control-Max-Age: 600        ← cache the preflight
Vary: Origin
```
- **Credentials:** the request needs `credentials: 'include'` (fetch) and the server must send `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true` with a **specific** origin, since `*` is not allowed with credentials. Cookies must also satisfy their `SameSite` rules.
- **Reflecting `Origin` blindly** (echo whatever is sent) while allowing credentials is a vulnerability. Use an allowlist, and add `Vary: Origin` so caches don't mix responses.
- **`Access-Control-Expose-Headers`** lets JS read non-safelisted response headers.
- **What CORS is NOT:** it's not server-side access control. `curl` and attackers' servers ignore it. It doesn't stop CSRF for simple requests (the request is still *sent*). It doesn't hide an unauthenticated API.
- **Common errors:** "No 'Access-Control-Allow-Origin' header" (the server didn't send it, or an error response such as a 500/redirect lacks CORS headers), "wildcard with credentials", "preflight doesn't pass" (the `OPTIONS` route is missing or behind auth), "header not allowed".
- **Solutions:** configure CORS middleware with an allowlist on the API. In dev, use a **dev-server proxy** (`/api` → backend) so the browser sees one origin. In prod, serve the frontend and API under one origin through a reverse proxy, or use the API gateway/CDN to add headers.
```js
import cors from 'cors';
const allowed = new Set(['https://app.example.com']);
app.use(cors({
  origin: (origin, cb) => cb(null, !origin || allowed.has(origin)),   // no Origin = non-browser client
  credentials: true,
  maxAge: 600,
}));
```
- **Related controls:** **CORP** (`Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy`), **COOP/COEP** (**cross-origin isolation**, required for `SharedArrayBuffer` and high-res timers), `postMessage` (always check `event.origin` and target origin), `<iframe sandbox>`, `Referrer-Policy`, `SameSite` cookies, and CSP `frame-ancestors`/`connect-src` (Phase 7).


**Prerequisites:** 3.9.2, 3.5.6

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the most common full-stack "it works locally and breaks in production" bug, and it's a security topic interviewers probe from both directions: the candidate who thinks CORS protects their API, and the candidate who thinks it's a client-side check they can configure away. Getting the enforcement point right is the whole question.

**Definition:** Two related but distinct controls: the same-origin policy, which by default stops scripts from *reading* data loaded from a different origin, and CORS, a set of response headers by which a server explicitly opts in to those reads — a protocol the browser enforces on the response before handing it to JavaScript.
**Questions**
1. What is the same-origin policy and why does it exist?
2. What is CORS? Who enforces it? Does it protect your API? *(model below)*
3. Simple vs preflighted requests? Why does `application/json` trigger a preflight?
4. Why can't you use `*` with credentials?
5. You get a CORS error but the server logs show the request succeeded. Why? *(The browser blocked *reading* the response. The request was still sent.)*
6. How do you fix CORS in development? In production?
7. What does `Vary: Origin` do?
8. Origin vs site? *(Same-site is eTLD+1, so `a.example.com` and `b.example.com` are cross-origin but same-site.)*
9. How does `postMessage` stay safe? 

**Model answer, Q2:** "CORS is a browser mechanism that lets a server opt in to cross-origin reads that the same-origin policy would otherwise block. The server sends headers like `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, and for non-simple requests the browser first sends an OPTIONS preflight. The browser enforces it, so it protects users' browsers from malicious sites reading authenticated responses, but it doesn't protect my API from curl or other servers. I still need authentication, authorization, and CSRF defenses."


**Mistakes:**
- **Treating CORS as access control on the API:** the headers are enforced by the browser on the *response*, so the preflight and the real request are still sent and still executed — `curl`, Postman and any server-side attacker ignore CORS entirely, and a simple `POST` with `Content-Type: text/plain` is a working CSRF vector. You still need authentication, authorisation and CSRF defences, and a CORS error in the console means the read was blocked, not that the request failed.
- **Reflecting `Origin` without an allowlist:** `cors({ origin: true })`, or a manual `res.setHeader('Access-Control-Allow-Origin', req.headers.origin)`, echoes whatever arrives, so with `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true` any site can read your users' authenticated responses. Check a static allowlist — and note that `*` becomes illegal the moment `credentials: 'include'` is set, so the browser rejects the wildcard outright.
- **Forgetting `Vary: Origin`:** with a reflected or per-tenant `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` and no `Vary: Origin`, a CDN or shared cache stores one origin's response and hands its header to another origin, breaking the app for some users and, worse, serving an allowlisted header to a site you never approved. It reproduces only in production, behind the cache.
- **Mounting the CORS middleware too late, or only on the main router:** auth middleware, a rejecting body parser or a rate limiter answers the `OPTIONS` preflight before CORS runs, and the preflight is sent *without* credentials, so it gets a 401 or a 30x to a login page and the browser reports a CORS failure while your server log shows the real request succeeding. Same when an error handler or redirect produces a response without the headers, or when `Origin` is `null` (a sandboxed iframe or a `file://` page) and your allowlist only holds real origins.
- **Assuming a successful CORS response is fully readable:** non-safelisted response headers (`X-Total-Count`, `Location`, `Content-Disposition`) stay invisible without `Access-Control-Expose-Headers`, and a cross-site cookie needs `SameSite=None; Secure` *and* `credentials: 'include'` — fetch defaults to `same-origin` — otherwise the request goes out with no session at all and every response looks unauthenticated.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Dev-server proxy vs explicit CORS headers in dev:** a proxy makes the browser see one origin, so there are no preflights and no cookie juggling, but it hides the real deployment topology and adds a hop; explicit headers exercise exactly the production behaviour during development.
- **Single origin behind a proxy or CDN vs a separate API origin:** one origin removes preflights, simplifies cookies and CSP, and saves a connection; separate origins scale and fail independently but cost preflights, cookie partitioning, and `SameSite=None; Secure` cookies.
- **`SameSite=Lax` cookies vs `SameSite=None` cross-site sessions:** `Lax` (the default where unspecified) blocks the cookie on cross-site subrequests and is the safe choice; `None` supports a genuinely cross-site frontend and requires `Secure` plus a real CSRF defence.
- **Reflecting `Origin` after an allowlist check vs a static list:** static lists are auditable and cache-friendly; reflection is easier across environments but only with a strict allowlist and `Vary: Origin`, and never with credentials enabled.
- **CORS vs CORP/COEP:** `Cross-Origin-Resource-Policy` blocks `no-cors` loads of subresources such as images and scripts; `Cross-Origin-Opener-Policy` and `Cross-Origin-Embedder-Policy` buy you `crossOriginIsolated` for `SharedArrayBuffer` and high-resolution timers, at the cost of breaking every third-party resource you load.
- **`postMessage` with `targetOrigin: '*'` vs an explicit origin:** `'*'` delivers to whatever document happens to be in the frame at delivery time; the explicit target origin is the security control, and you must still check `event.origin`.

**Common wrong answers:** "CORS is enforced by my server, so adding the middleware is what makes it secure" (it's the browser that blocks; `curl` and other servers ignore it entirely); "a CORS error means the request failed" (it usually succeeded server-side — the browser refused to expose the response); "`Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` is fine for a public API" (the browser rejects the wildcard whenever the request is credentialed, and with a public API it lets any site read it); "CORS prevents CSRF" (a simple cross-site form POST is sent regardless — you need `SameSite`, a token, or a custom-header requirement plus an `Origin` check); "`mode: 'no-cors'` fixes it" (you get an opaque response with status 0 and unreadable headers, which is not data).

**Probe:** "Your preflight returns 204 with `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: https://app.example.com` and `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true`, yet the browser still reports a CORS failure. Your `curl` works. What differs?" (Expect: look at the Network tab, not curl — a redirect or a 500 response often drops the CORS headers, `Origin: null` from a sandboxed iframe or `file://` isn't covered by a normal allowlist entry, or the failing request is the credentialed one where a wildcard is illegal. The status of the `OPTIONS` request and the exact response headers settle it.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Origin = scheme + host + port, exact match,** and the separate, looser concept of same-site (eTLD+1) that `SameSite` cookies use.
- **Who enforces CORS:** the browser, on the response, not the server — and the request is sent either way, which is the entire CSRF story.
- **Simple vs preflighted requests** and the exact triggers: a non-simple method, a non-safelisted `Content-Type`, or a non-safelisted header such as `Authorization`. Including that the preflight itself is sent without cookies and is cacheable via `Access-Control-Max-Age`.
- **Credential rules:** `credentials: 'include'`, `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true`, and a specific origin rather than `*`; plus the nuance that a manually-set `Authorization` header doesn't make a request credentialed, while a stored HTTP-auth entry does.
- **The real production fixes:** one origin behind a reverse proxy or CDN, an explicit allowlist, and `Vary: Origin` so a shared cache can't serve one origin's `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` to another.
**Mini task:** build a tiny API on port 4000 and a page on port 3000. Reproduce a simple-request error, a preflight failure, and a credentials failure, then fix each with a precise header change, then replace everything with a dev proxy.


**Self-check:** You're told to add `Access-Control-Allow-Origin: *` to a public, unauthenticated API. Why might that be the wrong answer anyway, and what would you add instead?

**Resources:** MDN's "Same-origin policy", "Cross-Origin Resource Sharing" and CORS request-header reference pages; the Fetch standard's "CORS protocol" section; MDN's `SameSite` cookie-attribute page; PortSwigger's CORS vulnerabilities cheat sheet; Chrome's Private Network Access documentation (the preflights that fire for public → private requests).
---

## 3.5.5 Browser Storage
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

| | Cookies | `localStorage` | `sessionStorage` | IndexedDB | Cache API / OPFS |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Capacity | ~4KB per cookie | ~5MB per origin | ~5MB per origin | Large (a share of disk, quota-managed) | Large |
| Sent to server | **Every matching request** | No | No | No | No |
| Lifetime | Session or `Expires`/`Max-Age` | Until cleared | Per tab (cleared on tab close) | Until cleared or evicted | Until cleared or evicted |
| API | `document.cookie` / `Set-Cookie` | Sync, strings only | Sync, strings only | **Async**, structured data, indexes, transactions | Async, request/response pairs, files |
| Accessible by JS | Unless `HttpOnly` | Yes | Yes | Yes | Yes (workers too) |
| Typical use | Sessions, auth, preferences needing the server | Small UI prefs (theme) | Wizard state per tab | Offline data, large caches | Offline assets, large files |

**Cookie attributes (security-critical)**
- `HttpOnly`: JS can't read it (mitigates token theft via XSS). `Secure`: HTTPS only. `SameSite=Lax|Strict|None` controls cross-site sending (`None` requires `Secure`). `Lax` is the modern browser default. `Domain`/`Path` scope. `Max-Age`/`Expires`. Prefixes `__Host-` (Secure, no Domain, Path=/) and `__Secure-`.
- **Third-party cookies** are being restricted or blocked in browsers, so don't build core flows on them.
```http
Set-Cookie: __Host-sid=abc123; Path=/; Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Lax; Max-Age=86400
```

**`localStorage` details**
- **Synchronous and blocking** on the main thread. Don't store large or frequently written data. Strings only (`JSON.stringify`). Shared across tabs of the same origin (the `storage` event notifies *other* tabs). It can throw `QuotaExceededError` or be unavailable in private modes, so wrap it in `try/catch`. It's not available in workers or SSR.
- **Never store secrets or auth tokens in `localStorage`** if XSS is a concern, since any injected script can read them. Prefer `HttpOnly` cookies (Phase 7 discusses the trade-offs, including CSRF).
- **Storage partitioning** and Safari's Intelligent Tracking Prevention can cap script-writable storage lifetime (about 7 days of inactivity in some conditions), so don't treat browser storage as durable. Request persistence with `navigator.storage.persist()` for important offline data.

**IndexedDB:** transactional object-store database. The raw API is verbose, so use `idb`, Dexie, or localForage. **Use for:** offline-first apps, drafts, caching API results.

**Choosing:** server-relevant and sensitive → `HttpOnly` cookie; small non-sensitive prefs → `localStorage`; per-tab ephemeral → `sessionStorage`; large/structured/offline → IndexedDB; assets → Cache API.


**Prerequisites:** 3.5.4, 3.5.1, 3.5.3.

**Why interviewers ask:** Every app has to keep something across a reload, and the default choice is usually wrong. It exposes a security position (does the session token live in JS-readable storage?), a performance position (`localStorage` is a synchronous main-thread call), and a data-modelling position (is this durable, and what happens when it isn't?).

**Definition:** The five origin-scoped persistence mechanisms available to a web app — cookies, `localStorage`, `sessionStorage`, IndexedDB, and the Cache API / OPFS — which differ in capacity, whether reads are synchronous, whether the browser attaches them to outgoing requests, whether JS can read them, and whether the data survives eviction.
**Questions**
1. Cookies vs localStorage vs sessionStorage vs IndexedDB?
2. Where should you store a JWT or session ID? Why? 
3. Explain `HttpOnly`, `Secure`, and `SameSite`.
4. Why is `localStorage` a performance concern?
5. How do you sync state between tabs? *(the `storage` event, `BroadcastChannel`, or a SharedWorker)*
6. Why is storage not a reliable database? *(Eviction, user clearing, private mode, ITP, quotas.)*
7. What happens to cookies sent with every request regarding performance? *(Header bloat on every asset request. Serve static assets from a cookieless domain, and keep cookies small.)*


**Mistakes:**
- **Writing on every keystroke or every render tick,** generating a synchronous write per event and eventually a `QuotaExceededError` that throws inside a React effect.
- **Assuming `localStorage` always parses.** Stored data can be truncated, hand-edited, or written by an older version, so `JSON.parse` needs a `try/catch`, a version field, and a schema check with a Zod fallback to defaults.
- **Assuming SSR-safe:** `localStorage` and `window` don't exist during server rendering or in a Node test environment; guard with `typeof window`, or the hydration mismatch is subtle.
- **Ignoring the storage event's scope,** so two open tabs silently diverge: it fires only in *other* same-origin documents, never in the tab that wrote the value.
- **Trusting durability,** shipping offline data with no sync story and no `persist()` request, then discovering Safari ITP has already aged the site data out after seven days.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`HttpOnly` cookie vs `localStorage` token:** cookie is unreadable by JS and scoped for the server, but reintroduces CSRF and adds header bytes to every request; `localStorage` avoids CSRF entirely but a single XSS exfiltrates the token for its whole lifetime.
- **`localStorage` vs IndexedDB:** `localStorage` for a few string keys you read synchronously at boot; IndexedDB when you need structure, indexes, transactions, blobs, or more than a few hundred KB — accepting async and a much more verbose raw API.
- **`sessionStorage` vs `localStorage`:** `sessionStorage` is per-tab and dies with it, so a multi-tab user doesn't fight themselves; `localStorage` shares state, which is what you want for preferences and dangerous for transient wizard state.
- **Storage event vs `BroadcastChannel` vs `SharedWorker`:** `storage` is free and already there but only reports changes after the fact and carries no protocol; `BroadcastChannel` gives you a real message channel per origin; a `SharedWorker` is a shared address space but needs one instance per origin and has poor Safari support for the lifetime you want.
- **IndexedDB vs Cache API:** IndexedDB is a queryable store for app data with transactions; Cache Storage is a `Request`→`Response` store for assets (available in windows and service workers, not in dedicated workers) and is what backs offline.
- **Treat storage as a cache vs as a database:** caching idempotent GET responses with a TTL is cheap and self-heals; anything a user typed is unsynchronised state that must be persisted in IndexedDB *and* on the server, with a versioned migration.

**Common wrong answers:** "`localStorage` is encrypted"; "IndexedDB has a fixed 5 MB limit like `localStorage`"; "sessionStorage is shared between tabs of the same origin"; "`HttpOnly` protects against CSRF".

**Probe:** "Your app reads a 2 MB JSON blob out of `localStorage` on every render and the page janks for 300 ms on a mid-range Android phone." (Expect: the read is synchronous and the `JSON.parse` doubles it; move it to IndexedDB with an async wrapper, memoise the parse, read once at boot, and migrate the existing key with a versioned schema.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Correct placement of the session token,** with the XSS-readability argument made explicitly rather than "cookies are more secure".
- **Knowledge that `localStorage` is synchronous and string-only,** and that it blocks the main thread on both read and write.
- **Awareness that all of this is best-effort:** quotas, user clearing, private mode, eviction, storage partitioning, and Safari's seven-day cap on script-written site data.
- **Use of `navigator.storage.estimate()` and `navigator.storage.persist()`** as the answer to "how do I know I have room / how do I ask to keep it".

**Model answer, Q2:** "It depends on whether a server needs to see it. If the session is server-verifiable I'll put it in an `HttpOnly`, `Secure`, `SameSite=Lax` cookie with a `__Host-` prefix, so an injected script can't read it and it can't be set by a subdomain; the CSRF risk that creates I handle with `SameSite` plus CSRF tokens. A JWT in `localStorage` is readable by any XSS payload, so I'd only do that if the API is on another origin and I can't use cookies — and then it's a short-lived, narrowly-scoped access token, not a refresh token, and CSP is the real mitigation."
**Mini task:** build a `useLocalStorage` hook that is SSR-safe, syncs across tabs via the `storage` event, tolerates quota errors, and validates the parsed value with a schema.


**Self-check:** For a given feature — "stays logged in across reloads", "remembers the theme", "works offline with 50 000 offline messages", "survives a tab crash" — can you name the mechanism, its exact failure modes, and one security consequence, without hesitating?

**Resources:** MDN's Web Storage API and Storage quotas and eviction criteria guides; the HTML Living Standard's storage section; OWASP's Session Management Cheat Sheet; MDN's `navigator.storage` (`StorageManager`) docs; Jake Archibald's "Storage for the Web" talk; RFC 6265bis for cookie semantics.
---

## 3.5.6 HTTP Caching (Browser and CDN)
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**The model:** a cached response is **fresh** (use without asking) or **stale** (revalidate with the server using validators).

**Headers**
- **`Cache-Control` (response):**
  - `max-age=N` fresh for N seconds (browser). `s-maxage=N` for shared caches (CDN).
  - **`no-cache`**: may store, but **must revalidate** before every use. (Not "don't cache".)
  - **`no-store`**: don't store at all (sensitive data).
  - `public` / `private` (private: browser only, not shared caches). `must-revalidate`. **`immutable`**: it'll never change while fresh, so skip revalidation on reload.
  - `stale-while-revalidate=N`: serve stale for N seconds while refreshing in the background. `stale-if-error=N`.
- **Validators:** `ETag` (content fingerprint) → client sends `If-None-Match`; `Last-Modified` → `If-Modified-Since`. The server answers **`304 Not Modified`** (no body) if unchanged.
- **`Vary`:** which request headers the cached response depends on (`Accept-Encoding`, `Origin`, `Accept-Language`). Mis-set `Vary` causes cache poisoning or fragmentation.
- Without explicit freshness, browsers apply **heuristic freshness** (often a fraction of the time since `Last-Modified`), so set headers deliberately.
- **Reload behavior:** normal navigation uses the cache, a regular reload revalidates, and a hard reload bypasses it.

**The standard strategy**

| Resource | Header | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Fingerprinted assets (`app.3f9a1c.js`, hashed CSS/images/fonts) | `public, max-age=31536000, immutable` | Content hash in filename = safe to cache forever. A new deploy = new filename |
| HTML documents | `no-cache` (or short `max-age` + `stale-while-revalidate`) | Must point to the latest hashed assets |
| Public API data that can be slightly stale | `public, max-age=60, stale-while-revalidate=300` | Fast and cheap |
| Per-user API responses | `private, no-cache` or `no-store` | Never in shared caches |
| Sensitive data | `no-store` | Don't persist |

- **Cache busting:** content hashes (best), versioned URLs (`?v=2` is weaker, since some proxies ignore the query string), and CDN purge for non-hashed assets.
- **Layers:** memory cache → disk cache → service worker cache → CDN edge → origin. Each can serve independently.
- **bfcache (back/forward cache):** whole-page snapshots that make back/forward navigation instant. Pages are blocked by `unload` handlers, open connections, and sometimes `Cache-Control: no-store`, so use `pagehide`/`visibilitychange` instead.
- **Common bug:** deploying a new `index.html` that references assets not yet uploaded, or caching `index.html` for a year so users are stuck on old code. Upload assets first, then HTML, and keep old assets available for a while for open tabs.


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.3, 3.5.1.

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the highest-leverage performance lever that costs no engineering time, and it's the number one source of production incidents in this area — stale code after a deploy, and per-user data served to the wrong person by a misconfigured CDN. A strong answer knows the difference between freshness, storage, and revalidation, because every caching bug is a confusion between those three.

**Definition:** The header contract that lets the browser, any intermediary, and the CDN edge reuse a response they already hold — `Cache-Control` decides whether a response is stored at all (`no-store`), stored but forced to revalidate (`no-cache`), or fresh enough to reuse without a request (`max-age` / `s-maxage` / `Expires`), while `ETag` and `Last-Modified` make revalidation cheap by turning it into a `304` with no body.
**Questions**
1. `no-cache` vs `no-store`?
2. How do ETags and `304` work?
3. How do you cache a SPA's assets and HTML? *(table)*
4. Why do hashed filenames allow `immutable`?
5. What's `stale-while-revalidate`? 
6. What does `Vary: Accept-Encoding` do? What can go wrong with a wrong `Vary`?
7. Users report seeing an old version after deploy. Diagnose.  *(HTML cached too long, a service worker serving stale assets, a CDN not purged, or an incomplete deploy ordering)*
8. `private` vs `public`? `max-age` vs `s-maxage`?


**Mistakes:**
- **Caching HTML for a year,** pinning every visitor to the build that happened to be deployed when the TTL expired.
- **Deploy ordering:** publishing the new `index.html` before its hashed assets exist, so the first visitor gets `404`s and a white screen; upload assets first, and keep the previous release's assets for at least the bfcache and open-tab lifetime.
- **`Vary: Accept-Encoding` at both layers,** which fragments the cache per variant and, with a CDN that also compresses, produces double-encoded or mismatched bodies.
- **Setting `s-maxage` and forgetting `max-age`,** so the edge holds the response for a day while every browser still revalidates it — you paid for a CDN and got nothing.
- **Trusting a DevTools trace with "Disable cache" checked,** which shows the network truth and hides the cache behaviour you're trying to debug.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Fingerprinted immutable assets vs stable URLs:** a content hash in the filename plus `public, max-age=31536000, immutable` is the only way to get a year-long TTL with zero revalidation; stable URLs force either revalidation forever or versioned query strings some proxies ignore.
- **Long `max-age` vs `stale-while-revalidate`:** a long TTL is the fastest but every user sees a stale edge for its whole life; SWR keeps the response instant while a fresh copy is fetched in the background, trading a bounded window of staleness for a much better repeat experience.
- **`private, no-cache` vs `no-store` for per-user data:** `no-cache` still allows revalidation from cache (fast, and the body is refetched); `no-store` is the correct choice for genuinely sensitive responses, at the cost of no offline availability at all.
- **ETag vs `Last-Modified`:** an ETag is a content fingerprint and correct for any generation, but costs a hash per request; `Last-Modified` is free but only has one-second resolution and lies when content changes twice within a second — so use `Last-Modified` as the fallback and an ETag as the real validator.
- **Service worker cache vs CDN cache:** the SW gives per-device offline and stale-while-revalidate logic you fully control but requires a deploy discipline and a version bump on every asset change; the CDN is transparent and global but needs origin-level headers and purge API access.
- **`max-age=0` on HTML vs true `no-store`:** `max-age=0` is the pragmatic choice — it keeps the document revalidateable and lets the browser still show something from cache while offline, at the cost of a request on every navigation.

**Common wrong answers:** "`no-cache` means don't cache it"; "`no-store` still allows the CDN to keep it while you clear it client-side"; "hashed filenames mean you don't need cache headers"; "an ETag always saves bandwidth" (it saves the body, not the connection or the latency).

**Probe:** "A user hard-reloads the page and still gets last week's build." (Expect: DevTools → Application → Service Workers and unregister/update, check whether a service worker is serving precached assets, then check whether the edge is serving cached HTML with a long `s-maxage`, then check whether the old hashed assets are still on the origin, then check the `Age` header to see which layer is lying.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Clean separation of the three axes** — stored or not (`no-store`), fresh or not (`max-age`/`Expires`/`s-maxage`), and revalidation policy (`no-cache`, `must-revalidate`) — rather than treating `Cache-Control` as one on/off switch.
- **Precision on the layer split:** browser cache honours `max-age`; a shared cache prefers `s-maxage` over `max-age`.
- **That a `304` carries no body** but must still update the stored metadata (`Cache-Control`, `ETag`, `Date`), and that `If-None-Match` takes precedence over `If-Modified-Since`.
- **Awareness of heuristic freshness** when no explicit lifetime is set, and of `Vary` as part of the cache key.

**Model answer, Q1:** "`no-store` means nobody may keep a copy — the response is not written to disk or memory by any cache. `no-cache` means 'you may store this, but it is never implicitly fresh, so before every reuse you must revalidate with the origin'. People read it as 'don't cache' because of the name. What I actually want for HTML is usually `no-cache`: I want it in the cache so I can revalidate in one cheap round trip, and I get the new document the moment the deploy lands."
**Mini task:** serve a small app with Express and set the headers in the table. Verify with DevTools (Size column shows "disk cache", the Status 304) and `curl -I`. Break it deliberately by caching `index.html` for a year, then fix it.


**Self-check:** Can you say, from memory, which layer uses `s-maxage` instead of `max-age`, what `Age` is measuring, and the one directive people consistently misread — without pausing?

**Resources:** RFC 9111 (*HTTP Caching*); RFC 5861 (stale-while-revalidate / stale-if-error); RFC 8246 (the `immutable` directive); MDN's HTTP caching headers reference; web.dev's "HTTP caching" and "Caching best practices" guides; Chrome's DevTools Network reference panel documentation.
---

## 3.5.7 Web Workers and Off-Main-Thread Work
🔴 Advanced · **Good-to-know**

- **Dedicated Worker:** `new Worker(new URL('./w.js', import.meta.url), { type: 'module' })` has its own thread and event loop, with no DOM access. Communicate with `postMessage` (data copied via the **structured clone algorithm**, which can't clone functions or DOM nodes).
- **Transferables** (`ArrayBuffer`, `MessagePort`, `OffscreenCanvas`, `ImageBitmap`) are moved without copying: `postMessage(buf, [buf])`. The sender loses access.
- **`SharedArrayBuffer` + `Atomics`:** true shared memory, requires cross-origin isolation (COOP + COEP).
- **SharedWorker:** one worker shared by tabs of the same origin. **Service Worker:** network proxy (3.9). **Worklets:** audio/paint.
- **Use for:** parsing big JSON/CSV, image/video processing, crypto, search indexing, diffing, compression, WASM, heavy charting, and anything over about 50ms that blocks input.
- **Costs:** startup time, serialization overhead for large messages, and no DOM. Comlink gives an RPC-style API over `postMessage`.
```js
// main.js
const worker = new Worker(new URL('./parse.worker.js', import.meta.url), { type: 'module' });
worker.onmessage = ({ data }) => render(data);
worker.onerror = console.error;
worker.postMessage({ csv: bigString });

// parse.worker.js
self.onmessage = ({ data }) => {
  const rows = data.csv.split('\n').map(l => l.split(','));   // heavy work off the main thread
  self.postMessage(rows);
};
```
- **Node's equivalent:** `worker_threads` (Phase 5).


**Prerequisites:** 3.5.1, 3.5.2.

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the sharpest test of whether someone understands that "the main thread" is the scarce resource and knows how to move work off it without introducing a bottleneck somewhere else. It also separates people who have profiled a slow interaction from people who pattern-match on "make it async".

**Definition:** A `Worker` runs JavaScript on its own OS thread with its own event loop and no DOM access, exchanging data with the page through `postMessage` and the structured clone algorithm, with zero-copy hand-off for transferable objects and `SharedArrayBuffer` + `Atomics` for genuinely shared mutable memory.
**Questions:** When would you use a Web Worker? What can't you do in one? How is data passed, and what's a transferable? Workers vs `requestIdleCallback` vs chunking? *(Workers for CPU-heavy; chunking for DOM-bound work, which can't move to a worker.)* How would you keep a 10MB CSV import from freezing the UI?


**Self-check:** Can you state the threshold for moving work off the main thread, the three ways to hand data to a worker and what each costs, and the one thing a worker can never do?

**Resources:** MDN's Web Workers API guide and the `WorkerGlobalScope` reference; the HTML spec's "Structured cloning" and "Transferable objects" sections; MDN's `SharedArrayBuffer` and cross-origin isolation (`COOP`/`COEP`) docs; Comlink; Jake Archibald's "When should I use a Web Worker?" talk.

**Mini task:** Build the 40 MB CSV import as a real worker: read the `File`, transfer the bytes (not a string), parse in chunks, post progress, and return only aggregates. Add a cancel button that calls `terminate()`, and verify the main thread stays responsive by painting a counter in a `requestAnimationFrame` loop that never drops a frame by more than a few milliseconds.

**Mistakes:**
- **Serialising large payloads both ways,** so the "off-thread" refactor moves work rather than removing it, and the structured clone of a big object graph is itself a long task.
- **Transferring a buffer you still need,** which detaches it and makes every later access throw.
- **Never calling `terminate()`,** leaking a thread per component mount and its entire heap.
- **Letting the worker bundle into the main chunk** instead of using `new Worker(new URL('./x.worker.js', import.meta.url), { type: 'module' })`, which bundlers statically recognise — otherwise you've added a thread and still parsed everything on the main thread.
- **Assuming errors surface:** `error` events on a dedicated worker don't reliably reach the page's `window.onerror`, so failures need explicit `postMessage` error envelopes plus an `onmessageerror` handler for un-cloneable messages.
- **Unbounded message queues,** letting the main thread flood the worker while it's busy, so you need an in-flight flag or a `MessageChannel`-based ack protocol.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Dedicated Worker vs chunking with `scheduler.yield()`:** chunking keeps the data on the main thread with zero serialisation and is the right answer for DOM-bound or incremental work; a worker wins only when the work is genuinely off-DOM and large enough to pay for the clone and the startup.
- **`scheduler.yield()` vs `requestIdleCallback`:** `yield()` resumes at higher priority and is guaranteed a slot, which is what responsiveness needs; `requestIdleCallback` only fires when the browser is idle, has no timeout guarantee, and isn't in Safari without a polyfill — so it fits preloading and analytics, not user-visible work.
- **Structured clone vs transferables vs `SharedArrayBuffer`:** clone is safe and simple but copies both directions; transferables (`ArrayBuffer`, `MessagePort`, `ImageBitmap`, `OffscreenCanvas`, `VideoFrame`) hand off ownership in O(1) but the sender's handle is detached; `SharedArrayBuffer` is true shared memory but needs cross-origin isolation and explicit `Atomics` synchronisation, plus it's a whole-app security tradeoff because it enables side-channel attacks.
- **Classic vs module worker:** `{ type: 'module' }` gives you `import`, top-level await, and no `importScripts`, and is statically analysable by bundlers; classic workers are more widely legacy-compatible and allow `importScripts`.
- **Dedicated vs SharedWorker vs ServiceWorker:** dedicated is per-owner and simplest; SharedWorker shares one instance across same-origin tabs (useful for one WebSocket, poor support and awkward lifecycle); a ServiceWorker is a network proxy with an install/activate lifecycle and no page DOM at all.
- **Worker pool vs one worker per job:** a pool amortises startup and keeps memory flat; per-job workers are simpler to reason about but pay startup plus termination on every item.

**Common wrong answers:** "Offloading makes everything non-blocking"; "you can reach the DOM from a worker"; "`SharedArrayBuffer` just works"; "workers are free, since the thread already exists"; "`postMessage` passes by reference".

**Probe:** "You move a 40 MB CSV parse into a worker and the page still stutters, and the transfer itself takes 400 ms." (Expect: they're reading the CSV from the DOM or from `localStorage` into a JS string, so the string is serialised once before it even leaves — `postMessage` takes a transferable `ArrayBuffer` or a `Blob`/`File`, or a `ReadableStream` from `file.stream()`; and the result coming back must be small, e.g. aggregates, not the whole row set.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **A criterion, not a list:** the work must be CPU-bound and DOM-free, and long enough to cross the ~50 ms long-task threshold.
- **Awareness of the hidden costs:** structured clone serialises both ways, so a worker that returns a large object can cost more than the work it saved.
- **Correct fallback reasoning:** chunking and yielding for DOM-bound work, because a worker has no `document` and no layout engine.
- **Practical awareness** of worker startup time and of terminating workers when the view unmounts.

**Model answer, Q1:** "When the main thread is doing pure CPU work with no DOM in sight, and that work is long enough to matter — I'd say over about 50 ms, since that's the long-task threshold and roughly three frames. Concretely: parsing a multi-megabyte JSON or CSV payload, image and video processing with WebCodecs or `OffscreenCanvas`, hashing and crypto, building a search index, diffing or merging large structures, compression, WASM, and chart layout maths. The counter-test matters as much: if the work has to create DOM nodes, move it to a worker and you've lost — you either ship the result back as data and pay a second main-thread render, or you bail out. In that case I keep it on the main thread and chunk it with `scheduler.yield()` or `isInputPending()`, because yielding between chunks keeps input latency low without any serialisation cost."
---

## 3.5.8 Observers and DOM APIs
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

| Observer | Watches | Typical use |
|---|---|---|
| **IntersectionObserver** | Visibility relative to a root/viewport | Lazy loading, infinite scroll, scroll-spy, viewability analytics, pause off-screen video |
| **ResizeObserver** | Element size changes | Responsive components, charts, auto-grow textareas |
| **MutationObserver** | DOM tree/attribute changes (fires as a microtask batch) | Reacting to third-party DOM changes, extensions |
| **PerformanceObserver** | Performance entries (LCP, long tasks, layout shift, resources) | RUM and web-vitals |

```js
// Infinite scroll without scroll listeners
const io = new IntersectionObserver(async ([entry]) => {
  if (!entry.isIntersecting || loading) return;
  loading = true;
  try { await loadNextPage(); } finally { loading = false; }
}, { rootMargin: '400px' });          // start loading before it's visible
io.observe(document.querySelector('#sentinel'));

// Lazy image swap
const imgIO = new IntersectionObserver((entries, obs) => {
  for (const e of entries) if (e.isIntersecting) { e.target.src = e.target.dataset.src; obs.unobserve(e.target); }
});
document.querySelectorAll('img[data-src]').forEach(img => imgIO.observe(img));

// Long tasks (RUM)
new PerformanceObserver(list => list.getEntries().forEach(e => report('long-task', e.duration)))
  .observe({ type: 'longtask', buffered: true });
```
- **Prefer observers over `scroll`/`resize` handlers:** they're asynchronous, batched, and off the hot path.
- **ResizeObserver loop errors** ("ResizeObserver loop completed with undelivered notifications") happen if a callback changes sizes that trigger more observations. It's usually benign, but avoid layout feedback loops.
- **Always `disconnect()`/`unobserve()`** on cleanup (React effect return).

**DOM API essentials**
- **Selection:** `querySelector(All)` returns a **static** NodeList. `getElementsByClassName` returns a **live** HTMLCollection. `closest()`, `matches()`, `contains()`.
- **Manipulation:** `textContent` (safe, no parsing) vs **`innerHTML`** (parses HTML, an XSS sink, so sanitize with DOMPurify or avoid). `createElement`, `append`, `replaceChildren`, `insertAdjacentHTML`, `DocumentFragment` and `<template>` for batching, `classList`, `dataset`, `toggleAttribute`.
- **Timing APIs:** `requestAnimationFrame` (visual updates before paint), `requestIdleCallback` (low-priority work, not in Safari, so feature-detect), `queueMicrotask`, `scheduler.postTask`/`yield` where supported.
- **Trusted Types** (a CSP feature) forces dangerous sinks to receive sanitized typed values.


**Prerequisites:** 3.5.1, 3.5.3.

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the practical core of "do you understand how the browser schedules work".
