Almost every real UI problem — infinite scroll, lazy images, virtualised lists, charts, RUM — is solved with an observer, and the follow-up questions expose whether they know about the batching model, the cleanup requirement, and the classic feedback-loop bug.

**Definition:** A family of callback-based observation APIs — `IntersectionObserver`, `ResizeObserver`, `MutationObserver`, `PerformanceObserver` — that batch and defer notifications so the browser can coalesce them at the right point in the frame, replacing high-frequency `scroll`/`resize` polling with work scheduled off the hot path.
**Questions**
1. How would you implement infinite scroll or lazy loading efficiently? 
2. IntersectionObserver vs scroll listener? 
3. `innerHTML` vs `textContent`? 
4. Live vs static NodeList?
5. How do you detect when an element's size changes?
6. How would you measure real-user long tasks and LCP?


**Self-check:** For each of the four observers — when does its callback run relative to layout, style and paint, what does a single callback receive, and what must you call to stop it?

**Resources:** MDN's guides to `IntersectionObserver`, `ResizeObserver`, `MutationObserver`, and `PerformanceObserver`; the `layout-shift` and `long-animation-frame` performance entry specs; the `web-vitals` library's attribution documentation; web.dev's "Optimize CLS" and "Optimize INP" guides; Chrome's "Rendering performance" and Performance panel documentation.

**Mini task:** Build one page with all four observers: infinite scroll with a sentinel and a re-entrancy lock, below-the-fold image lazy-loading with `width`/`height` reserved, a `ResizeObserver`-driven auto-growing textarea and chart, and a RUM script reporting LCP, long tasks and CLS to a local endpoint. Then unmount everything and take a heap snapshot to prove no observer or listener survived.

**Mistakes:**
- **Forgetting `buffered: true`** on a `PerformanceObserver` and concluding an entry type "never fires".
- **Not unobserving**, which is a memory leak of the target element and its captured closure, and produces phantom callbacks after unmount.
- **Observing `document.body` or `documentElement` with `ResizeObserver`,** guaranteeing a feedback loop when the callback adjusts size; scope it, or use `contain: size` / `content-visibility: auto`.
- **Writing to the DOM inside a `ResizeObserver` callback,** which triggers `ResizeObserver loop completed with undelivered notifications` — defer the write to `requestAnimationFrame` or fix the sizing model instead of silencing it.
- **Setting an image's `src` from an observer without reserving dimensions,** turning a lazy-loading win straight into a CLS regression.
- **Reading layout inside a `MutationObserver` callback,** which fires as a microtask before rendering and so forces a synchronous reflow on every batch.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`IntersectionObserver` vs native `loading="lazy"`:** the native attribute uses the browser's own distance-from-viewport heuristic, gets priority hints and layout-shift avoidance for free, and works in `<img>` and `<iframe>` without JS — so use it first and reach for an observer only when you need custom thresholds, dynamic `srcset`, or per-element analytics.
- **Observer-based lazy loading vs eager `srcset`:** observers let you defer the request until you're near the viewport and report viewability, at the cost of JS and a possible flicker; eager `srcset` with `loading="lazy"` on the browser's terms is simpler and usually faster.
- **`ResizeObserver` vs media queries and `window.resize`:** `ResizeObserver` sees element-driven size changes (a sidebar collapsing, a font loading, a container query) that no viewport event reports, but it costs a callback per observed box and one wrong implementation away from a loop error; media queries are free and declarative, so use observers for things CSS can't observe.
- **`MutationObserver` vs framework lifecycle hooks:** observers work on DOM a framework doesn't own — third-party widgets, extensions, injected ads — at the cost of a microtask per batch and no knowledge of intent; in your own component tree, lifecycle hooks are cheaper and clearer.
- **`requestAnimationFrame` vs `setTimeout(0)` vs `requestIdleCallback`:** rAF is the only one aligned to paint, so all visual writes belong there; `setTimeout` is clamped and unpainted; `requestIdleCallback` is for work with no deadline and is absent from Safari, so feature-detect and never build user-visible work on it.
- **Static `querySelectorAll` NodeList vs live `getElementsBy*` HTMLCollection:** static is predictable and safe to hold across a render; live is zero-cost to refresh but silently changes length while you iterate, which is a bug in a loop.

**Common wrong answers:** "`IntersectionObserver` fires synchronously during scroll"; "`MutationObserver` fires once per mutation"; "`ResizeObserver` replaces `window.resize`" (it only watches element boxes, and needs an explicit viewport listener for that); "ResizeObserver loop errors mean your app is broken"; "`rootMargin` percentages are relative to the viewport width".

**Probe:** "Your infinite scroll fires `loadNextPage()` three times for a single scroll gesture." (Expect: the callback is async so the in-flight flag isn't visible until the awaits resolve; add a lock before the `await`, `unobserve` the sentinel while loading, and re-observe after appending.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Knowing the delivery model:** callbacks are asynchronous, batched, and delivered at a specific point in the frame — IntersectionObserver and ResizeObserver after layout and before paint, MutationObserver as a microtask — so they cannot be used to synchronously read a value.
- **Threshold semantics,** including that a zero threshold with `intersectionRatio > 0` is the normal trigger and that `rootMargin` grows the root box.
- **Cleanup discipline:** `unobserve`, `disconnect`, and React effect returns, with the reason (leaked observers keep elements and their closures alive).
- **Practical experience** with `ResizeObserver` loop errors and with fixing them rather than suppressing them.

**Model answer, Q1:** "With `IntersectionObserver` and a sentinel element, not a scroll handler. I put one empty `div` at the end of the list and observe it with a `rootMargin` of a few hundred pixels so the callback fires before the sentinel is on screen; `isIntersecting` with a zero threshold is the trigger, `threshold: 1` on a tall sentinel is a better signal when you want the whole footer visible. Then an in-flight guard stops the re-entrancy — the callback is asynchronous, so `isIntersecting` can already be stale by the time the async load resolves and several entries can queue while the fetch is in flight — and I `unobserve()` the sentinel, append the new page, then re-`observe()` it. For images I either use native `loading="lazy"` plus explicit `width`/`height`, or observe and assign `dataset.src` then `unobserve` immediately, and always disconnect on unmount."
---

## 3.5 Other Browser Internals Q&A

| Question | Strong answer outline |
|---|---|
| `fetch` vs XHR? | Promise-based and streaming. `fetch` **doesn't reject on HTTP errors** (check `res.ok`), no built-in upload progress (XHR has it), cancel with `AbortController` |
| What does `fetch` do with cookies? | `credentials: 'same-origin'` by default. Use `'include'` for cross-origin cookies |
| What are `defer`'d scripts' timing vs `DOMContentLoaded`? | They run before `DOMContentLoaded` fires |
| `history.pushState` vs hash routing? | `pushState` changes the URL without reload (server must serve the SPA on those paths). Hash routes don't hit the server |
| What happens on `window.onerror` and `unhandledrejection`? | Global error hooks for monitoring (Sentry). Send via `sendBeacon` |
| How do iframes affect security/perf? | Separate browsing context, `sandbox`, `allow`, `loading="lazy"`, `postMessage` with origin checks, clickjacking via `frame-ancestors` |
| How does the browser decide script/CSS priority? | Heuristics (CSS and sync scripts are high, images low, `fetchpriority` hints, preload) |
| `Page Visibility` API? | Pause timers, polling, and video when hidden: `document.visibilityState` |
| What is a secure context? | HTTPS (or localhost), required for service workers, Clipboard, Geolocation, Web Crypto subtle, and many other APIs |

**Self-check:** Can you narrate render pipeline → events → CORS → storage → caching in 5 minutes, each with one bug you've seen?

---

# 3.6 WEB PERFORMANCE

## 3.6.1 Core Web Vitals and Measurement
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Google's user-centric metrics for loading, interactivity, and visual stability. They're ranking signals and the shared vocabulary for performance work.

| Metric | Measures | Good | Poor |
|---|---|---|---|
| **LCP** (Largest Contentful Paint) | When the main content (largest image/text block) renders | ≤ 2.5 s | > 4 s |
| **INP** (Interaction to Next Paint) | Responsiveness: the (near-)worst interaction latency across the visit. **Replaced FID in March 2024** | ≤ 200 ms | > 500 ms |
| **CLS** (Cumulative Layout Shift) | Unexpected layout movement | ≤ 0.1 | > 0.25 |

Evaluated at the **75th percentile** of real users, separately for mobile and desktop. Supporting metrics: **TTFB** (≤ ~800 ms good), **FCP**, **TBT** (lab proxy for INP), **Speed Index**.

**Lab vs field data**
- **Lab** (Lighthouse, WebPageTest, DevTools): controlled, reproducible, good for debugging, but it's a single simulated device and network, and can't measure INP without scripted interactions (TBT stands in).
- **Field/RUM** (CrUX, PageSpeed Insights field section, your own beacon using the `web-vitals` library): what real users experience, and what Google ranks on. **Use lab to diagnose and field to prioritize and verify.**
```js
import { onLCP, onINP, onCLS } from 'web-vitals/attribution';
const send = ({ name, value, id, attribution }) =>
  navigator.sendBeacon('/rum', JSON.stringify({ name, value, id, page: location.pathname, attribution }));
onLCP(send); onINP(send); onCLS(send);
```
- **Performance budgets:** set limits (JS ≤ N KB, LCP ≤ 2.5s) and enforce them in CI (Lighthouse CI, bundlesize/size-limit) so regressions fail PRs.
- **Business link:** conversion and retention correlate with speed, so tie metrics to outcomes when making the case to stakeholders.


**Prerequisites:** 3.5.1, 3.5.2, 3.5.8.

**Why interviewers ask:** Because it's the shared vocabulary of the whole discipline and the first place a candidate has to prove they can tell a lab number from a real-user number. A strong answer knows the exact thresholds, explains *why* p75 over 28 days is the unit, and says out loud that you prioritise with field data and diagnose with lab data — the distinction that separates people who optimise a Lighthouse score from people who improve INP.
**Questions**
1. What are the Core Web Vitals and their thresholds? What replaced FID and why? *(INP, because it measures all interactions' responsiveness, not just the first input's delay)*
2. Lab vs field data? Which would you trust for prioritizing and why?
3. Why is the 75th percentile used?
4. How would you set up performance monitoring for a production app? *(RUM with `web-vitals`, segmented by route, device, and connection, with dashboards, alerts, and CI budgets)*


**Self-check:** Can you state all six threshold numbers (good and poor for LCP, INP, CLS), say what replaced FID and why, and explain why p75 exists — in under ninety seconds?

**Resources:** web.dev's "Web Vitals" article and the LCP/INP/CLS metric pages; Chrome's "Web Vitals" documentation on `web.dev/vitals`; the `web-vitals` library README (including the attribution build); CrUX and the PageSpeed Insights CrUX panel; Lighthouse's performance scoring documentation; W3C's *Web Vitals* community group for the metric definitions.

**Mini task:** Instrument a real route with `web-vitals/attribution`, sending every value to an endpoint with route, connection type and device class; then add a Lighthouse CI budget job that fails the PR when JS exceeds a size limit or the lab LCP exceeds 2.5 s. Done when you can produce a p75 LCP per route per form factor from your own data and reproduce it in the panel.

**Mistakes:**
- **Registering the observer after first paint,** or without `buffered: true`, so the metric never reports — the most common RUM bug in production code.
- **Filtering out "good" values before sending,** which means you can compute a p75 from your own data but not a mean or a distribution, and you lose the ability to see how many visits had no metric at all.
- **Sampling by a cookie or a percentage without weighting,** which silently drops the users on the worst connections and makes the report look better every quarter.
- **Treating a Lighthouse score as a Core Web Vital,** or optimising TTFB and ignoring INP because Lighthouse's simulated interaction didn't catch it.
- **Comparing field numbers across mobile and desktop,** which will always look like a regression on one of them.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Lab vs field:** lab is reproducible and gives you a call tree but describes a synthetic device; field is real but aggregate, slow-moving, and gives you no cause. Neither is a substitute, and the mistake is choosing one.
- **Own RUM vs CrUX:** your own beacon gives per-route, per-build and per-device granularity with attribution, but you have to instrument it, sample sensibly, and host the dashboards; CrUX is free and unbiased across the whole web, but it's only available for origins with enough traffic and is aggregated to 28-day buckets.
- **Single beacon with `sendBeacon` vs navigation-timing pingback:** the beacon gives you full attribution and works on subdomains; a `Timing-Allow-Origin`-enabled image ping gets you LCP and CLS for free with no script and no data policy to worry about, at the cost of no attribution and no custom dimensions.
- **p75 as a target vs p75 as a gate:** gating CI on absolute thresholds is blunt and flaky for lab runs, so combine a fixed budget for regressions with a segmented field trend for the real signal.
- **In-page attribution vs sampling:** full attribution for every view is the most useful data and the most expensive in engineering and privacy review; sampling keeps costs down but biases exactly the low-end tail you're trying to understand.

**Common wrong answers:** Quoting a p50 target; saying "INP measures how fast the page loads"; treating Lighthouse's INP proxy as real INP; reporting only metric values that exceed the threshold (which destroys the distribution and the percentile).

**Probe:** "Your CrUX data shows LCP 'good' overall, but one of your three top landing pages is at 3.4 s on mobile. What do you do first?" (Expect: the aggregate was hiding it — get page-level, device-level and connection-level RUM rather than a global number, and check whether the specific LCP element on that route differs from the others.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **The thresholds quoted exactly:** LCP good ≤ 2.5 s, needs improvement 2.5–4.0 s, poor > 4.0 s; INP good ≤ 200 ms, needs improvement 200–500 ms, poor > 500 ms; CLS good ≤ 0.1, needs improvement 0.1–0.25, poor > 0.25.
- **That FID was retired** in March 2024 because it only measured the input delay of the *first* interaction, while INP measures the full latency — event timestamp to the next paint, after all handlers and the following `requestAnimationFrame` callbacks — of the worst interaction on the page, at roughly the 98th percentile of interactions.
- **Correct scoping:** p75, 28-day window, segmented by form factor, from CrUX or your own RUM.
- **The lab/field split** used for the right purpose in each direction.

**Model answer, Q2:** "I trust the field data for prioritising and the lab for diagnosing. Field data is real users on real devices and real networks, aggregated over 28 days at the 75th percentile and split by form factor, so it tells me what actually happens — and it's what Google ranks on and what a conversion target should be tied to. Its weakness is attribution: a number tells me a route is slow but not which phase. That's what the lab is for, because a controlled run under 4× CPU throttling and Slow 4G is reproducible, so I can change one thing and see the effect. The failure mode is optimising the lab: Lighthouse runs one simulated Moto G on one profile, and it can't measure INP at all without scripted interactions, which is why TBT stands in as its proxy. So: field to decide what to fix and to verify it worked, lab to find out how."
---

## 3.6.2 Optimizing LCP
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**LCP = four phases:** **TTFB** → **resource load delay** (discovering the LCP resource late) → **resource load duration** → **element render delay** (blocked by CSS/JS or client rendering).

**Fixes by phase**
- **TTFB:** CDN and edge caching, server/DB speed, avoid redirect chains, HTTP/2 or 3, streaming SSR, cache HTML where possible, `103 Early Hints`.
- **Load delay:** make the LCP element **discoverable in the initial HTML** (don't inject via JS, don't `loading="lazy"` it, avoid CSS `background-image` for hero content, and CSR-only apps delay everything). Use `<link rel="preload" as="image" fetchpriority="high">` and `fetchpriority="high"` on the `<img>`.
- **Load duration:** right-size and compress images (AVIF/WebP, `srcset`), serve from a CDN, preconnect to the image origin, and avoid giant hero videos.
- **Render delay:** inline critical CSS, remove render-blocking CSS and JS, don't block on web fonts for LCP text (`font-display: swap`/`optional`), and prefer SSR/SSG so content exists before JS hydrates.


**Prerequisites:** 3.6.1, 3.5.1, 3.1.3.

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the metric stakeholders actually see, it is almost always dominated by one fixable mistake, and the interviewer's real question is whether you'll break the number into phases instead of blindly running a checklist. Candidates who say "compress the image" when 3 s of a 4 s LCP is resource *load delay* fail here.

**Definition:** Largest Contentful Paint is the time from navigation start until the largest image or text block *visible in the viewport* is painted; optimising it means attributing the number to one of four phases — TTFB, resource load delay, resource load duration, and element render delay — and attacking whichever is largest.
**Questions:** What element counts as LCP? Your LCP is 4.5s. What do you check first? *(Break it into the four phases, using the Performance panel's insights or `web-vitals` attribution, then fix the largest phase.)* Why is lazy-loading the hero image harmful? How does CSR affect LCP?


**Self-check:** Given any LCP regression, can you name the four phases in order, say which one the panel shows as largest, and give the specific fix for that phase?

**Resources:** web.dev's "Optimize LCP" and "Largest Contentful Paint (LCP)" articles; the `largest-contentful-paint` performance entry specification; the `web-vitals` attribution build's LCP fields; MDN's `fetchpriority` and `rel="preload"` references; Jake Archibald's "The fastest image format is AVIF" and Core Web Vitals talks.

**Mini task:** Take a page with a JS-injected hero image at 4.8 s LCP on throttled mobile, and land it under 2.5 s by making four measured changes — SSR the hero markup, add `fetchpriority="high"`, serve AVIF at the right `srcset` size, and inline the critical CSS — recording the panel's phase breakdown after each change so you can attribute the win.

**Mistakes:**
- **`loading="lazy"` on the hero image,** which in Chromium delays the request until the lazy-loading distance threshold is met and can add seconds to the metric.
- **Discovering the LCP resource late** — client-side injected, behind a route-level `Suspense` boundary, or behind a font or stylesheet, so it starts downloading after the JavaScript has run.
- **CSS `background-image` for content**, because the preload scanner can't see it and the browser only learns about it after the CSSOM exists, adding the whole stylesheet round trip to the metric.
- **Ignoring TTFB,** so you spend a week on images while 2.5 s of the 4 s was spent in the server or an un-warmed lambda.
- **Deleting the old asset right after a deploy,** so users on an open tab get a 404 hero and an LCP that never arrives.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Critical-CSS inlining vs a blocking stylesheet:** inlining removes a round trip and kills render-blocking entirely, at the cost of duplicating styles, breaking long-term HTTP caching of that CSS, and needing tooling to avoid drift.
- **Streaming SSR vs static generation:** streaming gets real content into the first flush fast and improves TTFB-to-LCP on dynamic pages; SSG/ISR gives the fastest and most cacheable possible response but requires the page to be knowable at build time.
- **`<link rel="preload">` vs nothing vs `fetchpriority="high"`:** `fetchpriority` is a priority hint with no extra request and no wasted bandwidth if unused, so prefer it for a known LCP image; `preload` also helps discovery but risks downloading a resource you never use, and warns in the console when you do.
- **SSR/SSG vs CSR:** anything the client must render is a render delay by construction; a CSR-only app pays parse-and-execute time before the LCP element exists at all, so even a perfect network won't get under 2.5 s on a slow phone.
- **Hero video vs hero image:** a video background can be beautiful and cannot compete on LCP, because LCP stops at the poster or first frame and the file is orders of magnitude larger; a poster with `preload="none"` and a deferred video is the honest compromise.
- **Fixing render delay by shrinking CSS vs by sequencing it:** delete unused CSS and CSS-in-JS runtime if you can; otherwise inline critical and defer the rest, because render-blocking is the cost, not the size.

**Common wrong answers:** "LCP is when `load` fires"; "lazy-loading the hero image would improve LCP"; "only images count"; "just run Lighthouse and do what it says".

**Probe:** "Your LCP element is a text heading and it's still 3.8 s — there is no image on the page." (Expect: the text can't paint until render-blocking CSS resolves and, if the font is render-blocking, until the font arrives; also check whether client-side rendering is what produces the heading at all, since text that appears via hydration arrives very late.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Correct enumeration of eligible elements,** including text blocks and CSS background images, and the fact that an image inside a CSS background is a common cause of a slow, "invisible" LCP.
- **Breaking the number into the four phases and acting on the largest,** rather than listing generic advice.
- **Understanding of the first-input cutoff** and why interactive hero carousels and video backgrounds poison LCP structurally.
- **Naming the mechanism, not just the intent:** `fetchpriority="high"`, `rel="preload"`, inline critical CSS, `font-display: swap` or `optional`, streaming SSR.

**Model answer, Q1:** "The LCP element is whichever eligible element paints largest: an `<img>`, an `<image>` inside an SVG, a `<video>`'s poster, a block element with a CSS `background-image` loaded through `url()`, or the largest single *text block* — which is one element and all of its text nodes, not each paragraph separately. It's measured from navigation start, and the final value is locked in at the first `keydown` or `pointerdown`, which is why a click-to-load facade caps how good your LCP can ever be. At 4.5 s I read the breakdown first: the Performance panel's LCP track, or `web-vitals/attribution`, gives me TTFB, load delay, load duration and render delay as numbers. If load delay is seconds, the LCP resource wasn't discovered in the initial HTML — it's injected by JS, or behind a lazy boundary, or blocked behind a bundle; I preload it and mark it `fetchpriority='high'`. If render delay dominates, something is render-blocking, and I inline critical CSS, stream the HTML, and stop making the browser wait for a font before it can paint."
---

## 3.6.3 Optimizing INP and Main-Thread Work
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**INP phases:** **input delay** (main thread busy when the user taps) → **processing time** (your handlers) → **presentation delay** (rendering the result).

**Fixes**
- **Break up long tasks** (over 50ms) and yield to the main thread so input can be handled in between:
```js
const yieldToMain = () =>
  globalThis.scheduler?.yield ? scheduler.yield() : new Promise(r => setTimeout(r, 0));

async function processAll(items) {
  let deadline = performance.now() + 40;
  for (const item of items) {
    work(item);
    if (performance.now() > deadline) { await yieldToMain(); deadline = performance.now() + 40; }
  }
}
```
- **Do less in event handlers:** show visual feedback immediately, then defer non-urgent work (analytics, logging, state fan-out).
- **React specifics:** `useTransition`/`useDeferredValue` to mark non-urgent updates, `memo`/`useMemo` where profiling justifies them, virtualize long lists, split state so fewer components re-render, avoid expensive work in render, and reduce **hydration cost** (RSC, islands, selective hydration).
- **Reduce main-thread work overall:** ship less JS, defer third-party scripts, avoid layout thrashing, use `content-visibility: auto`, move computation to Web Workers, and reduce DOM size (large DOMs of thousands of nodes make every style/layout expensive).
- **Debounce/throttle** high-frequency handlers (scroll, input), and use passive listeners.


**Prerequisites:** 3.6.1, 3.5.1, 3.5.7.

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the metric that separates "fast page" from "usable app", and it's the one that can't be improved by shrinking images. Interviewers are listening for whether the candidate knows that most of a bad INP is *input delay* — the main thread was already busy when the user tapped — and whether they've actually shipped the fix rather than just named `useMemo`.

**Definition:** Interaction to Next Paint is the latency of the worst interaction on a page view — measured from the input event's timestamp to the next paint after all of its handlers and the following `requestAnimationFrame` callbacks complete — and it decomposes into three phases you attack separately: input delay, processing duration, and presentation delay.
**Questions:** What is INP? How is it different from FID? A button click takes 600ms to update the UI. How do you investigate? *(Performance panel, find the long task and its call tree, check handler work, forced layouts, re-render scope, then fix by deferring, splitting, memoizing, or workers.)* What is a long task? How does hydration hurt INP? How does `useTransition` help?


**Self-check:** Can you name the three INP phases, say which one you would bet on first for a bad INP, and describe the tool that tells you which one it actually was?

**Resources:** web.dev's "Optimize INP" and "Interaction to Next Paint (INP)" articles, including the "INP debugging steps" flow; the `event` timing and `long-animation-frame` entry specifications; React's `useTransition` and `useDeferredValue` documentation; MDN's `scheduler.yield` and `isInputPending` references; the `web-vitals` attribution build.

**Mini task:** Take a page where clicking "Apply filters" on a 5 000-row list costs 600 ms, and get it under 200 ms: virtualise the list, move the filter computation to a worker with a transferable result, render the filtered count in a transition, and show the INP phase breakdown from `onINP` before and after.

**Mistakes:**
- **Optimising the handler while input delay dominates,** because the handler was never the problem — the 400 ms that came before it was.
- **Assuming INP improves when CLS improves,** or fixing layout shift and declaring interactivity solved; they measure different phases of a frame.
- **Attributing to a whole page** instead of a specific interaction, which hides that one route or one widget is the problem.
- **Over-memoising,** trading every re-render for a large permanent memory cost and complexity, without a profile showing the re-render was the cost.
- **Sync work in an event handler** — reading `localStorage`, parsing a large payload, or forcing layout by reading geometry after a write — all of which land inside the interaction's processing time.
- **Splitting long tasks by adding `await`s without actually yielding,** which serialises the work but still keeps the microtask queue full, so the browser never gets a chance to process input.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`scheduler.yield()` vs `setTimeout(0)` vs `requestIdleCallback`:** `yield()` is priority-aware and yields to pending input, which is exactly what responsiveness needs; `setTimeout(0)` is clamped and can still land after several queued tasks; `requestIdleCallback` doesn't exist in Safari and gives no deadline guarantee, so it's only for work with no user-facing deadline.
- **React `useTransition`/`useDeferredValue` vs splitting state:** transitions let React interrupt and keep the urgent update responsive, which is a scheduling fix; splitting state and reducing the rendered surface is a structural fix and is usually the one that actually reduces work.
- **Virtualisation vs pagination:** virtualisation keeps all data in memory and cuts DOM size dramatically, at the cost of anchor/scroll maths, `Ctrl+F` not finding off-screen text, and print and accessibility edge cases; pagination is simpler and cacheable but adds navigation cost and breaks the "one continuous list" feel.
- **Web Worker vs main-thread chunking:** a worker removes the work from the interaction path entirely but costs serialisation and startup; chunking keeps it on the main thread where it's cheap and interruptible, and is usually enough for work under a few hundred milliseconds.
- **Debouncing vs deferring:** debouncing collapses rapid events into one handler run, which is correct for input-as-you-type, but it adds latency to the result and can starve a metric you want live; a trailing edge plus `isInputPending` where available is often better than a fixed debounce.
- **Third-party scripts vs features:** removing or facade-loading an analytics or chat tag buys back more INP than most app-level optimisations, but the tag is usually owned by another team, which makes it a negotiation problem rather than a technical one.

**Common wrong answers:** "INP is FID with a different name"; "measure the click handler with `performance.now()`"; "`useMemo` everywhere fixes INP"; "long tasks don't matter for INP".

**Probe:** "INP is 600 ms on a product listing page but 120 ms everywhere else, and it only happens for users with a mouse on desktop." (Expect: desktop-only and mouse-only means it isn't a rendering path — suspect a hover, `mouseenter`, or `pointermove` handler doing work, or a debounce that fires late on mouse-driven filters, and check the Interactions track for which interaction type is reported.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **The three-phase decomposition used as a diagnostic,** with the attribution numbers actually named.
- **Understanding that input delay is usually the culprit** and that it is caused by work *before* the interaction, which no amount of handler optimisation fixes.
- **Precision about what INP measures** — one interaction per interaction group (a tap, drag and release counts once), pointer and keyboard only, up to the next paint.
- **Concrete, shipped fixes:** chunking with `scheduler.yield()`, deferring analytics behind `requestIdleCallback`, moving parsing and crypto to a worker, virtualising long lists, `content-visibility: auto`.
- **Honesty about the limits:** reducing bundle size, deferring third parties, and reducing DOM size help INP, but a cheap-looking app can still have bad INP if the main thread is busy during the interaction.

**Model answer, Q1:** "INP is the full latency of the worst qualifying interaction — the ~98th percentile of interactions on the page — not the first one and not just the handler. FID, which it replaced in March 2024, only measured the input delay of the first interaction, so a page whose fifth click took 900 ms could score an FID of 20 ms. A 600 ms click is roughly 100 ms input delay, 400 ms processing, and 100 ms presentation, and I'd confirm that split rather than guess: the Performance panel's Interactions track names the interaction and shows its three phases, and `onINP`'s attribution build hands me `interactionTarget` plus `inputDelay`, `processingDuration` and `presentationDelay`. Input delay that big means something else was running — I look at the long task immediately preceding the interaction and the call tree above it. Processing time that big means my handler is doing work: sync XHR-style reads, a big `JSON.parse`, sorting thousands of rows, or a giant state fan-out, and I fix it by moving the computation to a worker or yielding with `scheduler.yield()`. Presentation delay that big means layout or paint is the cost — forced synchronous layout from reading a geometry property after writing, or a huge re-render scope — and I batch reads and writes and shrink the re-render."
---

## 3.6.4 Optimizing CLS
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Causes → fixes**
- Images/video/iframes without dimensions → set `width`/`height` or `aspect-ratio`.
- Ads, embeds, banners injected late → reserve space with `min-height`/placeholder, and avoid inserting content *above* existing content.
- Web fonts swapping and changing metrics → `font-display`, preload key fonts, and fallback fonts with matched metrics (`size-adjust`, `ascent-override`, or Next's `next/font`).
- Late-loading CSS → inline critical CSS.
- Animations that move layout → use `transform` instead of `top/margin/height`.
- Client-rendered content replacing a skeleton of a different size → size skeletons to match.
- Layout shifts within 500ms of user input are excluded (expected shifts). Debug with the DevTools "Layout Shifts" track and the Rendering panel's shift regions.


**Prerequisites:** 3.6.1, 3.5.1.

**Why interviewers ask:** CLS is the metric people feel but never report, it's entirely about foresight in how you author markup, and it's the one where an interviewer can walk you through a real page and ask "which of these four things shifts?". It also separates people who know CSS from people who know when the browser will decide the size of something.

**Definition:** Cumulative Layout Shift is the 75th-percentile of the largest *burst* of unexpected layout shifts in a page view — a burst being a session window of at most 5 seconds with no more than 1 second between consecutive shifts — where each shift scores as its impact fraction (how much of the viewport the moved elements occupy) multiplied by its distance fraction (how far they travelled).
**Questions:** What counts as a layout shift? Name five causes and fixes. Why do fonts cause CLS and how do you minimize it?


**Self-check:** Can you define a layout shift score mathematically, name the four session-window rules, and list three shifts that are excluded from the metric?

**Resources:** web.dev's "Optimize CLS" and "Cumulative Layout Shift (CLS)" articles; the `layout-shift` performance entry specification; CSS Fonts Level 4 on `font-display` and metric overrides; the CLS DevTools workflow in Chrome's "Optimize CLS" guide; Core Web Vitals' `web.dev/articles/defining-core-web-vitals-thresholds`.

**Mini task:** Take an article page and drive its CLS from above 0.25 to under 0.1: add `width`/`height` to every image and embed, reserve the ad slot with `min-height`, build a metric-matched fallback `@font-face` with `size-adjust` and `ascent-override`, convert one animation to `transform`, and confirm with the `layout-shift` entries' `sources` that nothing unexpected remains.

**Mistakes:**
- **Setting `width: 100%` on an image without `height` or `aspect-ratio`,** which gives the browser no ratio to reserve — the single most common CLS bug.
- **Inserting content above the fold on load,** which shifts everything below it; the layout shift score is proportional to the moved area, so a small banner at the top is expensive.
- **Animating `height`, `top`, `margin` or `width`,** each of which is a layout property and therefore a shift on every frame, where `transform: translateY()` would not be counted.
- **Conditionally rendering an error or empty state** into space that was sized for loaded content, or swapping a filled container for a skeleton of another size.
- **Fixing CLS with `visibility: hidden` or removing content,** which technically changes the layout and either shifts things or hides the problem at the user's expense.
- **Relying on `useLayoutEffect` timing** as a guarantee, when the real fix is reserving the space in CSS so nothing has to move after load.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`font-display: swap` vs `optional`:** `swap` gives a ~100 ms block period then swaps in the web font whenever it arrives, which is better for brand consistency and repeat views but is the single largest font-related CLS source; `optional` gives the font ~100 ms and otherwise keeps the fallback for that entire page load, so CLS is near zero at the cost of first-visit visitors sometimes seeing the fallback.
- **Metric-overridden fallback vs preloading the font:** overriding `ascent-override`/`size-adjust` on a `@font-face` that sources a local font is free and never shifts, but needs per-font tuning and can look subtly wrong; preloading gets the real font before first paint when it wins the race and silently does nothing when it doesn't.
- **Reserving space with skeletons vs `min-height`:** a skeleton that matches the real content's dimensions needs design discipline but is accurate; `min-height` is a blunt reserve that can leave a gap or still be wrong when the content is taller than the guess.
- **Skeleton of a different size vs client-rendered content:** a mismatched skeleton is a guaranteed shift on every load; getting the dimensions to match, or reserving via CSS `aspect-ratio`, removes it.
- **`content-visibility: auto` vs rendering everything:** it skips rendering off-screen subtrees and is a large CLS-adjacent win for long documents, but you must set `contain-intrinsic-size` or the scrollbar and scroll position jump as the estimate is replaced by the real size.
- **Fixed sticky headers vs `position: sticky`:** fixed headers overlay content and are a top CLS source if their height isn't reserved; sticky headers stay in flow, at the cost of needing `z-index` and background handling.

**Common wrong answers:** "Animating with CSS is fine because GPU-accelerated" (`transform` is; `top` is not); "CLS is per page load" (it's a session-window percentile); "add `overflow: hidden` to fix shifts"; "lazy loading causes CLS".

**Probe:** "CLS is 0.28 on an article page but 0 on the home page, and both use the same web font." (Expect: the article page has a pull-quote, ad slot or image injected after load, or the fallback font has very different metrics and the text below the fold reflows — check the `layout-shift` entries' `sources` for the offending node.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **The mechanics, not just the causes:** impact fraction × distance fraction, session windows, the 500 ms input exclusion, and that only *existing* elements shifting counts.
- **That width/height or `aspect-ratio` is the fix** for unsized media — and the reason it works (the browser derives an aspect ratio from the attributes before the bytes arrive).
- **Understanding font CLS as a metrics problem,** not a loading problem, and knowing `font-display: optional` as the zero-CLS option with a repeat-visit cost.
- **Knowing how to measure it:** the `layout-shift` entry with `hadRecentInput`, the DevTools Layout Shifts track, and the shift sources.

**Model answer, Q1:** "A layout shift is a change in the start position of a visible element between two frames, where it wasn't caused by a transform or by a recent input — shifts within 500 ms of a user interaction get `hadRecentInput` and are excluded, and newly inserted elements don't count unless they push something that already existed. Five causes and fixes: images, video and iframes without reserved dimensions, fixed by `width`/`height` attributes or `aspect-ratio`; late-injected banners, ads and embeds, fixed by reserving the space with `min-height` or a skeleton and never inserting above existing content; web fonts swapping to a fallback with different metrics, fixed by matching the fallback with `size-adjust`, `ascent-override`, `descent-override` and `line-gap-override`, or by using `font-display: optional` which has a zero-length swap period; late-loading CSS reflowing the page, fixed by inlining critical CSS; and animating layout properties, fixed by animating `transform` instead of `top`, `height` or `margin`, which never counts toward CLS."
---

## 3.6.5 JavaScript, Bundles, and Loading Strategy
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Why JS is the costliest byte:** it must be downloaded, **parsed, compiled, and executed** on the main thread, and slow phones pay far more than your laptop. 200KB of JS costs more than 200KB of images.

**Tactics**
- **Measure first:** bundle analyzers (`vite-bundle-visualizer`, `webpack-bundle-analyzer`, `source-map-explorer`, `next build` output), Chrome DevTools **Coverage** tab (unused bytes), and Lighthouse "Reduce unused JavaScript".
- **Code splitting:** route-level (`React.lazy` + dynamic `import()`, Next.js automatic per-route), component-level for heavy, rarely used widgets (editors, charts, maps, modals), and vendor chunking for cache stability.
- **Tree shaking:** ESM imports, `sideEffects: false`, avoid barrel-file re-exports of huge libraries, import specifically (`import debounce from 'lodash-es/debounce'`).
- **Replace heavy dependencies:** `moment` → `date-fns`/`dayjs`/Temporal, full `lodash` → native or `lodash-es`, icon packs → per-icon imports, and check **bundlephobia**-style size before adding a dependency.
- **Modern output:** target modern browsers with `browserslist` to avoid shipping legacy transforms and polyfills to everyone.
- **Third-party scripts:** a top cause of poor INP and LCP. Load with `async`/`defer`, after interaction or idle, use facades (click-to-load YouTube embeds), self-host critical ones, move to a worker (Partytown), and audit tag-manager sprawl.
- **Preload/prefetch strategy:** preload critical, prefetch likely next routes (`<Link prefetch>` on hover or viewport), and don't over-preload.
- **Rendering strategy choice:** SSG/ISR for mostly static pages, SSR with streaming for dynamic personalised ones, RSC to keep heavy libraries on the server, islands (Astro) for mostly static pages with small interactive pieces, and CSR only where SEO and first paint matter less.
- **Avoid request waterfalls:** fetch in parallel, hoist data fetching to the route level (loaders, RSC), `preconnect` to API/CDN origins, and avoid fetch-on-render chains (component A renders, then fetches, then renders child B, which fetches).
- **Compression and protocol:** Brotli for text (gzip fallback), HTTP/2 or 3 multiplexing (so don't over-bundle, but also don't create hundreds of tiny files), and long-lived caching with hashed filenames (3.5.6).
- **Lists and DOM size:** virtualization (TanStack Virtual, react-window) for thousands of rows, pagination or infinite scroll with windowing, and `content-visibility: auto` for long documents.
- **Service worker / client caching, SWR/TanStack Query** for data reuse.


**Prerequisites:** 3.5.2, 3.1.3, 3.5.6.

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the highest-return performance lever in frontend work and the one that scales: a media optimisation helps one page, a bundle audit helps the whole app. Interviewers want to see that you measure before cutting, that you can name the specific failure mode you're removing, and that you can reason about the trade-offs rather than "code split everything".

**Definition:** The strategy for minimising what JavaScript costs the main thread — how many bytes you ship, whether they ship on first load, whether they're parser-blocking, and how much of your code actually runs — because every byte of JS must be downloaded, parsed, compiled and executed on the same thread that handles input, on devices far slower than your laptop.
**Questions**
1. Your bundle is 2MB. How do you shrink it? *(analyze → remove/replace heavy deps → split by route/component → tree shake → modern targets → compress → lazy-load third parties)*
2. Why is JS more expensive than images of the same size?
3. How does code splitting work with `import()` and React `lazy`/`Suspense`? Pitfalls? *(chunk-load failures after deploys need an error boundary and retry. Too many tiny chunks create waterfalls.)*
4. What causes request waterfalls and how do you avoid them?
5. How do you handle third-party scripts?
6. SSR vs SSG vs CSR for performance? 
7. How do you decide what to preload vs prefetch?


**Self-check:** Can you explain why 200 KB of JS is more expensive than 200 KB of images, then name the three things you'd check in that order on a 2 MB bundle?

**Resources:** Chrome's "Reduce JavaScript payloads with code splitting" and "Coverage panel" documentation; web.dev's *Core Web Vitals* and "Performance budgets" guides; `bundlephobia` and `source-map-explorer` for bundle cost analysis; React's `lazy`/`Suspense` and Server Components documentation; `browserslist` and the Vite/webpack build-target options; Jake Archibald's "The Cost of JavaScript".

**Mini task:** Take a 1.5 MB main chunk down under 400 KB of critical-path JavaScript: run the analyzer, find the three largest dependencies, replace or defer at least one, split by route, add `sideEffects: false`, and set a `size-limit` budget in CI so the next PR fails if it grows back.

**Mistakes:**
- **Code splitting before auditing,** so the bundle shrinks on paper and the critical path doesn't, or gets worse.
- **Not setting `sideEffects: false`** (or importing through barrel files that re-export an entire library), which silently disables tree shaking.
- **A chunk-load 404 after a deploy,** because hashed chunks are deleted while a user still has the old HTML — keep previous releases around, add a retry and an error boundary around `lazy`, and serve a static fallback shell.
- **Deferring a script the page actually needs,** which just moves the cost past first paint and can leave a broken UI; `defer` scripts still run before `DOMContentLoaded` and still block it.
- **Third-party tags loaded in `<head>` without `async`,** turning a 40 KB tag manager into a render-blocking resource you don't control.
- **Waterfall by construction:** fetching in a component's `useEffect` so each render triggers a request, then a child fetches after the parent renders — hoist to loaders, or run the fetches in parallel.
- **Assuming `browserslist` is set correctly,** shipping modern syntax with a legacy target and letting the bundler down-compile — and polyfill — for everyone.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Route-level splitting vs one bundle:** splitting gives a smaller critical path per route but a navigation-time fetch for the next route, so it only pays off if the route transition is prefetched or streamed from a router that already has it.
- **`preload` vs `prefetch`:** `preload` is for a resource this page definitely needs, and it competes for bandwidth with the LCP resource if you overuse it; `prefetch` is for a likely *next* navigation, low priority, and wasted on a guess — prefetching everything is just as bad as preloading everything.
- **Vendor chunk vs many small chunks:** a separate vendor chunk makes deploys cheap because third-party code rarely changes, at the cost of a second large request and duplicated dependencies across entries; a hundred tiny chunks is worse on HTTP/2 because you lose coalescing and add scheduling overhead.
- **Bundle splitting vs RSC/islands:** splitting still ships code that only runs on the server's behalf into the browser bundle; moving that work off the client entirely is a bigger win, at the cost of a build and architecture change.
- **Lazy-loading a component vs deleting it:** lazy loading a rarely used widget helps the initial bundle but adds a waterfall when the user opens it, so a facaded trigger or an inline lightweight version is often better than a suspense boundary.
- **Modern targets vs legacy support:** targeting modern browsers with `browserslist` avoids shipping legacy transforms and polyfills to everyone, but raises the minimum browser version you support — a product decision, not purely a technical one.
- **Self-hosting third parties vs a tag manager:** self-hosting removes an extra DNS and TLS handshake and gives you control of the code path, but you take on the updates and compliance burden; a tag manager gives control of the *timing* without the maintenance, and is where sprawl hides.

**Common wrong answers:** "Use `defer` and it'll be fine" (it doesn't reduce parse-and-compile or execution cost); "minify and gzip solves it"; "code splitting is free"; "tree shaking works because ESM has no side effects" (it needs `sideEffects: false` and no barrel re-exports).

**Probe:** "You cut the bundle by 60% and p75 INP didn't move. What now?" (Expect: bundle size is a proxy — the remaining cost is likely hydration, third parties, or a long task; go to the field attribution for `interactionTarget`, then check for long-animation-frames and hydration time before concluding JS volume isn't the bottleneck.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Measurement before removal:** bundle analyzer plus the Coverage panel plus Lighthouse's unused-JS figure, and the willingness to delete code rather than just move it.
- **Naming the actual cost model** — download plus parse plus compile plus execute — and that it lands on the main thread.
- **A priority order:** remove or replace, then split by route, then lazy-load, then defer third parties; splitting first without auditing just makes more requests.
- **Understanding of splitting pitfalls,** especially chunk-load failures after a deploy (old HTML referencing deleted chunks) and the request waterfalls too many tiny chunks create.
- **Trade-off fluency** on preload versus prefetch and on rendering strategy per route.

**Model answer, Q2:** "Because an image is a payload problem and JavaScript is a compute problem. Bytes go over the network, get written to the decoder, and the GPU composites them — the main thread does almost nothing. JS has to be downloaded, then parsed into an AST, compiled to bytecode, and then either interpreted or JIT-compiled to machine code, and only then does it do its work, all of it on the main thread that also handles input and layout. So the cost is superlinear in bytes and heavily dependent on device: on a 4×-throttled mid-range phone, a couple of hundred KB of JS costs roughly a second of main-thread time, which is pure added INP and it shows up whether or not the user interacts immediately. On top of that, JS is paid for twice — once in download and again in parse-and-compile — and only a fraction of what you ship is used on a given route."
---

## 3.6.6 Images, Fonts, and CSS Performance
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

- **Images (usually the heaviest bytes):** AVIF/WebP with fallbacks, `srcset`/`sizes`, correct dimensions (don't ship 4000px for a 400px slot), quality 60-80, lazy-load below the fold, eager + `fetchpriority="high"` for LCP, an image CDN or framework component (`next/image`) with automatic resize and format negotiation, and `decoding="async"`. Replace GIFs with `<video muted loop playsinline autoplay>`. SVG for icons (inline sprite or per-icon).
- **Fonts:** WOFF2 only, **subset** to needed glyphs, **variable fonts** to replace many weights, `font-display: swap` (text visible immediately, reflow later) or `optional` (no swap on slow loads, best for CLS), preload only the one or two critical fonts (with `crossorigin`), self-host (avoids an extra connection), limit weights and families, and match fallback metrics.
- **CSS:** inline critical CSS and defer the rest, remove unused CSS, avoid `@import`, keep selectors reasonable, avoid huge `box-shadow`/`blur`/`backdrop-filter` areas, use `contain` and `content-visibility`, and prefer Tailwind or CSS Modules' dead-code elimination.
- **Video/embeds:** `preload="metadata"`, poster images, lazy iframes, and facades.


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.7, 3.2.13, 3.6.2.

**Why interviewers ask:** Media is usually the largest share of page weight, so it's where the biggest wins are, and the fixes are concrete and verifiable. It's also the section where interviewers check whether you understand that most image "optimisation" is really about *when* the browser learns the dimensions, and that font loading is a layout problem disguised as a performance one.

**Definition:** The bytes and the layout side effects of the non-JavaScript resources — image format, dimensions, and loading priority; font format, subsetting, preloading and swap behaviour; and how much CSS is render-blocking — all of which show up directly and immediately in LCP and CLS.
**Questions:** How would you cut page weight from 5MB to 1MB? Walk through your font loading strategy. `font-display: swap` vs `optional`?


**Self-check:** Can you give the image format ladder with quality targets, explain why an image without dimensions is both an LCP and a CLS problem, and state exactly what `optional` buys you and what it costs?

**Resources:** web.dev's "Use AVIF format", "Serve images with correct MIME types", "Efficiently load third-party fonts", and "Preload a font" articles; MDN's `font-display`, `<picture>`, `srcset`/`sizes` and `aspect-ratio` references; Squoosh and `sharp` for conversion; the CSS Fonts Level 4 metric override descriptors; Chrome's Network panel reference for transferred vs decoded size.

**Mini task:** Take a 5 MB page down to under 1 MB: convert the images with `sharp` or `cwebp` into an AVIF/WebP/JPEG `<picture>` set with correct `srcset`/`sizes`, add `width`/`height` everywhere, swap the GIF for a muted video, subset the fonts to Latin and preload only the one critical WOFF2 with `crossorigin`, then verify with `curl -I` on every URL and a before/after Lighthouse trace that LCP fell and CLS stayed at 0.

**Mistakes:**
- **Forgetting `crossorigin` on a font preload,** which makes the preload fetch twice: once in CORS mode and once in no-CORS mode, so it warns in the console and wastes the benefit.
- **`loading="lazy"` on the hero image** and on anything inside the initial viewport — the metric then depends on the browser's lazy-loading distance guess.
- **Shipping a 4000 px hero into a 400 px slot,** which is a download problem and an LCP problem, and is usually invisible because the browser's CSS resizes it for free.
- **No `alt`, no `width`/`height`, and no `decoding="async"`,** which cost accessibility, CLS, and main-thread decode time respectively.
- **Preloading four or five fonts,** so the font requests compete with the LCP image and make the page slower than not preloading at all.
- **Using `@import` in CSS,** which makes the stylesheet itself a render-blocking resource that discovers its dependencies only after it's parsed — that's a serialised round trip per file.
- **Deleting a font subset when a user's locale needs it,** which produces tofu; use `unicode-range` to ship subsets and keep a non-subset fallback.

**Trade-offs:**
- **AVIF vs WebP vs JPEG:** AVIF wins on bytes but costs more to encode, which matters for a build pipeline with thousands of originals, and needs a fallback; WebP is a safe middle ground with universal support; JPEG is the floor you can't drop.
- **`srcset` vs one big image:** `srcset` with `sizes` makes the page adaptive to viewport and DPR but can serve a wrong guess if `sizes` is imprecise, which shows up as a blurry or oversized download — the whole point is that the browser picks, so `sizes` has to describe your actual layout.
- **Lazy loading vs eager loading below the fold:** lazy loading saves bandwidth and main-thread work but delays the request so far that a fast scroll shows placeholders, and it can hurt LCP if the browser guesses the element is above the fold; eager loading everything costs bandwidth on every visit.
- **`font-display: swap` vs `optional`:** swap risks a reflow (CLS) but always eventually shows the brand font; optional keeps the page stable and metric-safe by using the fallback for the whole first load and only using the cached font on return visits.
- **Preloading vs leaving it to the stylesheet discovery:** preload buys the request's priority, at the cost of wasted bandwidth and a console warning when unused; relying on the CSS `@font-face` discovery means the font competes with everything else discovered at the same moment.
- **Inline critical CSS vs inlining all CSS:** inlining everything removes render-blocking entirely but loses caching and inflates every HTML response; inlining only the above-the-fold rules needs tooling to stay in sync and is the usual answer.
- **Self-hosted fonts vs a font CDN:** self-hosting avoids a third-party DNS/TLS handshake and a cross-origin request, but you own subsetting, caching and updates; a font CDN gives worldwide edge delivery and fewer round trips, at the cost of another origin in the critical path.

**Common wrong answers:** "Use `font-display: block` to hide the flash" (it hides text for up to three seconds — invisible text is a worse failure than a reflow); "preload all fonts"; "AVIF replaces JPEG in the `src` attribute" (no fallback without `<picture>`); "lazy-load every image".

**Probe:** "Your team preloads four font files and users still see a flash of fallback text on 3G." (Expect: preloading is a hint, not a guarantee, and four preloads compete with the LCP resource for bandwidth; preload one or two critical WOFF2 subsets, match the fallback metrics so the swap doesn't reflow, and consider `font-display: optional` or `size-adjust`.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **That the format ladder is AVIF → WebP → JPEG,** with `<picture>` and `type` so old browsers still get something.
- **Dimensions are a CLS fix and an LCP fix,** and the mechanism (`width`/`height` or `aspect-ratio` giving the browser a ratio before the bytes arrive).
- **Font knowledge beyond `font-display: swap`:** subsetting, `unicode-range`, WOFF2, one preload, `crossorigin` being required even same-origin because fonts are fetched in anonymous CORS mode, and `size-adjust`/`ascent-override` on the fallback.
- **The swap vs optional trade-off** stated explicitly, including what `optional` costs on first visit.
- **Awareness that CSS is render-blocking and `@import` adds a round trip** on top of that.

**Model answer, Q1:** "I audit before I touch anything — the Network panel sorted by transferred size, plus `curl` on the real URLs to see what the CDN actually returns. Then, in order of payoff: convert every image to AVIF with a WebP and JPEG fallback, since AVIF is roughly 50% smaller than either at matched quality, at around quality 60 (WebP ~75, JPEG ~70); check that no image is being served larger than its rendered box and add `srcset`/`sizes` for viewport- and DPR-dependent widths; add explicit `width` and `height` to every image, video and iframe so the browser can reserve the box, which kills CLS; load below-the-fold images with `loading="lazy"` and `decoding="async"` while the LCP image stays eager with `fetchpriority="high"`; replace a 4 MB animated GIF with a muted, inline `<video>`; and cut CSS by inlining critical rules and deferring the rest. For fonts: WOFF2 only, subset to the glyphs actually used, one variable font instead of four weights, one or two critical fonts preloaded with `crossorigin`, and metric-matched fallbacks. That gets a typical content page from 5 MB to under 1 MB."
---

## 3.6.7 The "Page Is Slow" Playbook
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** (a classic scenario)

1. **Define and scope:** which page/route, which users (mobile? region?), which metric (LCP, INP, CLS, or "feels slow"), since when (a deploy?), using field data first.
2. **Reproduce:** Lighthouse/DevTools with mobile CPU throttling (4× or 6×) and "Slow 4G", plus WebPageTest from a representative region.
3. **Network waterfall:** TTFB high? Redirects? Render-blocking CSS/JS? Large payloads? Late-discovered LCP resource? Request waterfall? Missing caching or compression?
4. **Performance panel:** long tasks, scripting vs rendering vs painting, forced layouts, hydration cost, third-party scripts.
5. **Bundle and coverage:** analyze size and unused code.
6. **Backend:** API latency (p95/p99), N+1 queries, missing indexes, cache hit rates, cold starts (Phases 5-6).
7. **Fix the biggest bottleneck first**, measure again, and **prevent regression** (budgets in CI, RUM alerts).
8. **Communicate** impact in user and business terms.

**Mini tasks**
1. Take a deliberately slow page (unoptimized hero image, blocking script, no dimensions, a big bundle). Take it from LCP 5s+ to under 2.5s on throttled mobile, recording each change's impact.
2. Add `web-vitals` RUM reporting to an app and chart INP by route.
3. Split a 1.5MB bundle into route chunks and measure the real effect with the analyzer.


**Resources:** web.dev's "Learn Performance" course and the Core Web Vitals optimisation guides; Chrome DevTools' Performance and Lighthouse panel documentation; WebPageTest; CrUX and the PageSpeed Insights field-data section; Lighthouse CI and `size-limit` for budgets; Jake Archibald's "How to make a fast site" and the HTTP caching guidance.

**Mistakes:**
- **Starting with tooling instead of definition,** spending a day in Lighthouse on the wrong page or the wrong metric.
- **Optimising the lab number,** celebrating an 80-point Lighthouse improvement with zero field movement and no regression prevention.
- **Fixing the largest thing rather than the largest *contribution*,** rearchitecting a 200 ms component while 2 s of TTFB sits untouched.
- **No baseline,** so the team can't tell whether the last three changes helped, hurt, or did nothing.
- **Attacking third-party code instead of third-party loading,** rewriting a tag's internals when the win was in *when* it runs.
- **Shipping without a guard,** so the same regression returns in six weeks because nobody was watching.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Lab reproduction vs field measurement:** lab gives a call tree and reproducibility but describes a synthetic device and can't measure INP without scripted interaction; field is real and aggregate but gives no cause. Fix in the lab, verify in the field.
- **Fixing TTFB vs fixing the client:** TTFB fixes are usually infrastructure work with dependencies and a long lead time; client fixes are immediate and owned by you, but capped by the TTFB. Compute the split before choosing, because on a 4 s LCP that is 2.5 s of TTFB, no amount of client work gets under 2.5 s.
- **Third-party removal vs mitigation:** removing a tag you don't control is best and needs a negotiation; self-hosting or deferring it to idle or first interaction gets most of the benefit without anyone else's permission; a facade (click-to-load) is the strongest option when the embed isn't needed above the fold.
- **CI budget on absolute values vs relative diffs:** an absolute budget is simple and blunt and will annoy everyone when a legitimately important dependency lands; a diff budget plus a ratcheting absolute ceiling catches regressions without blocking planned work.
- **Prioritising p75 vs p95:** p75 is the Google-graded metric and matches how most organisations report; p95 shows you the worst experience, which is often what support tickets describe, and is where the biggest wins are hiding — worth tracking both.
- **One big fix vs many small ones:** a single architectural change (SSR, RSC, islands) costs a quarter and removes the whole class of problem; a dozen small fixes land this week and each buys a fraction of a second. The right answer is usually one big fix plus a budget to stop regressing.

**Common wrong answers:** "Run Lighthouse and fix what it flags"; "add a CDN"; "the fix is code splitting"; "just add caching headers"; shipping a fix with no before/after field measurement.

**Probe:** "The fix improved your local Lighthouse score from 4.1 to 2.8 but CrUX p75 hasn't moved in three weeks." (Expect: lab and field disagree, so the lab change was real but immaterial — either it fixed a case that isn't in the tail, or the field is dominated by a segment they never tested, so they need segmented RUM before claiming success.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Field data before lab data,** and understanding that a lab run is for attribution, not for prioritisation.
- **Scoping before optimising:** which route, which users, which metric, which date.
- **Decomposition into the phases** — TTFB, transfer, main-thread work, layout — rather than a list of tactics.
- **Prevention:** CI budgets, RUM alerting on a threshold breach, and a budget that fails the PR.
- **Communication** in user and business terms, with a before/after measured on field data, not a Lighthouse badge.
- **Cost/benefit:** does the fix move p75 for real users, or does it just move a lab number?

**Model answer, Q1:** "First I ask what 'slow' means and which route, because 'slow' splits cleanly into three different problems with three different owners. Second I get field data rather than a lab number — CrUX or my own RUM — segmented by route, form factor and connection, so I'm looking at real users rather than a simulation, and so I know whether this is one page or every page. Third I check whether it correlates with a deploy or a date, which turns a vague complaint into a bisect target. Fourth I quantify it with the three Core Web Vitals plus TTFB, so 'slow' becomes 'LCP 4.1 s on mobile' and has a threshold to fail. Fifth only then do I reproduce it under lab conditions — DevTools with 4× CPU throttling and Slow 4G, plus WebPageTest from a representative region — because reproduction is for diagnosis, not for deciding what to fix."

**Questions**
1. A stakeholder says "the site feels slow" with no metric and no page. What are the first five things you do, and in what order?
2. Field p75 LCP is 1.9 s on desktop and 4.1 s on mobile; lab LCP is 1.8 s. Where do you look first, and why do you trust the field number over the lab number?
3. A route regressed from 1.5 s to 5 s LCP after a deploy that "only added a dependency". How do you find it, and how do you stop it happening again?
4. INP is 600 ms on the search results page and fine everywhere else. Walk me through the diagnosis.
5. You've fixed the bottleneck and the lab number improved. What stops it regressing on someone else's machine next month?
6. The team that owns the slow third-party tag won't change anything. What can you do without them?

**Prerequisites:** 3.6.1, 3.5.1, 3.5.6.

**Why interviewers ask:** It's the classic senior-level scenario question: there is no single right answer, only whether you can run a disciplined sequence without guessing, and whether you can tell the difference between "slow to load", "slow to respond", and "janky". Candidates who jump straight to Lighthouse, or straight to code splitting, fail; candidates who quantify first pass.

**Definition:** A repeatable diagnostic procedure for turning an unmeasured complaint — "the site is slow" — into a ranked list of bottlenecks with evidence attached: scope it with field data, reproduce it under controlled lab conditions, attribute it to a phase of the pipeline, fix the largest contributor, and lock the result in with budgets and alerts.
**Self-check:** Can you take any "the site is slow" report and run this playbook, naming the tool for each step?

---

# 3.7 ACCESSIBILITY IN DEPTH

*(Builds on 3.1.5.)*

## 3.7.1 The Accessibility Tree, Names, Roles, and Values
🟡 → 🔴 · **Must-know**

- The browser derives an **accessibility tree** from the DOM (plus ARIA and CSS effects). Assistive tech reads *that*. Each node exposes **role** (what it is), **name** (what it's called), **state/properties** (checked, expanded, disabled, required, invalid), and **value**.
- **Accessible name computation (simplified order):** `aria-labelledby` → `aria-label` → native source (`<label>`, `alt`, `<legend>`, `<caption>`, button/link text content) → `title` (last resort) → placeholder (weak fallback, don't rely on it).
- **Test names and roles with Testing Library:** `getByRole('button', { name: /save/i })` simultaneously encourages accessible markup (Phase 9).
- **What removes content from the tree:** `display: none`, `visibility: hidden`, the `hidden` attribute, `aria-hidden="true"` (not for focusable elements, which creates "ghost" focus), and `inert`.
- **Never put `aria-hidden` on focusable content**, and never use `role="presentation"` on elements that need semantics.
- **Common naming bugs:** icon-only buttons with no label, links that say "click here"/"read more" (give context), repeated identical link names that go to different places, images with filename alt text, inputs labelled only by placeholder, and `aria-label` that doesn't include the visible text (breaks voice control, as WCAG 2.5.3 "Label in Name" requires the visible text be in the accessible name).


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.2, 3.1.5.

**Why interviewers ask:** It is the one abstraction that explains *why* a screen reader says what it says. Candidates who know it can debug a bad announcement instead of cargo-culting `aria-label`, and can argue the first rule of ARIA (native first) from a mechanism rather than from a slogan. It also separates people who have shipped a real product from people who have run axe once.

**Definition:** The accessibility tree is the parallel, semantically-resolved DOM that the browser hands to the platform accessibility API (UIA on Windows, AX on macOS/iOS, AT-SPI on Linux). Each node exposes four things to assistive tech: **role** (computed from the explicit `role` attribute, then the implicit mapping from the element), **accessible name**, **value**, and a set of **states and properties** (`checked`, `expanded`, `pressed`, `selected`, `disabled`, `required`, `invalid`, `describedby`, `errormessage`).
**Questions:** What's the accessibility tree? How is an element's accessible name computed? What's wrong with `<a>click here</a>`? When should you use `aria-labelledby` over `aria-label`? *(When visible text already exists, so it stays in sync and translated.)*


**Self-check:** Given `<button aria-labelledby="lbl"><svg aria-hidden="true"/></button>` and `<input aria-label="Search" placeholder="Search">`, what is the accessible name of each, which one fails WCAG 2.5.3, and what changes if you swap `aria-label` for `aria-labelledby`?

**Resources:** MDN "Accessibility tree" and "ARIA" guides; the W3C **Accessible Name and Description Computation** spec (accname 1.2) for the real precedence rules; the WAI-ARIA 1.2 specification's role/state definitions; `aria-query` on npm for the `role → element` mapping; Testing Library's `getByRole` docs; Chrome DevTools' accessibility reference pane.

**Mini task:** Open Chrome DevTools → Elements → Accessibility (or run `document.querySelector('button').getAttributeNames()` next to the computed Accessibility tree in the panel) on three deliberately-bad buttons: an icon-only button with no label, a `<div role="button">` with a click handler, and a form input labelled only by `placeholder`. Record what the tree reports for role, name and states in each case, then fix all three with native markup and re-check. Done when the tree reports a real `button` role with a meaningful name for each, and axe reports zero violations on the page.

**Mistakes:**
- **`aria-hidden="true"` on focusable content:** it stays in the tab order and can be focused while being invisible to AT, producing "ghost focus" where focus is silently in nowhere. Use `inert` to remove both, and remember `inert` also blocks pointer events.
- **`role="presentation"`/`none` on something that still needs semantics:** it strips the native role, so a `<table role="presentation">` or `<ul role="presentation">` loses its table/list structure that screen reader users navigate by. `role="presentation"` is only right when the descendants already carry their own semantics and you want the wrapper ignored.
- **Duplicating a role that contradicts the element:** `role="checkbox"` on a `<button>` gives you the announced state but not the space-bar behaviour users expect from a checkbox.
- **Naming by placeholder or `title` only:** the accessible name must be stable and programmatic; a placeholder vanishes on input and a `title` is a last-resort fallback that some AT skips.
- **Adding ARIA attributes that reference IDs which do not exist yet** — a client-rendered `aria-describedby` pointing at a not-yet-mounted error node fails 3.3.1 without any console error.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`aria-label` vs `aria-labelledby`:** `aria-label` is a self-contained string, quick and translation-friendly through your string table, but it overrides visible text (breaking 2.5.3) and cannot be reused. `aria-labelledby` composes a name from DOM that stays in sync with the visible label and is translated with it, at the cost of ID wiring and `useId()` discipline in React.
- **Native element vs `role` + `aria-*`:** `<button>` gives you role, name-from-content, Enter/Space activation, disabled state and focusability for free. A `<div role="button">` gives you the announcement and none of the behaviour, so it only wins when native styling or markup is genuinely impossible.
- **Descriptive name vs terse name:** longer names cost screen reader time and clutter the rotor/list of buttons. Use a short unique name ("Delete invoice") and put detail in `aria-describedby` when the surrounding context does not carry it.
- **`.sr-only` text vs `aria-label`:** visually-hidden text is in the accessibility tree, is translatable, is copied by users, and works when CSS fails. `aria-label` keeps the DOM clean but is invisible to search, to `Ctrl+F`, and to anyone reading the source.

**Common wrong answers:** "The accessibility tree is the DOM with ARIA attributes on it" (it is computed and pruned — CSS and ARIA both remove nodes, and it is a different structure the platform layer sees). Confusing the accessible *name* with the accessible *description*. Believing `aria-label` fixes behaviour. Claiming `aria-labelledby` reads hidden text in all cases — hidden referenced nodes are only used if directly referenced, and hidden *subtrees inside* the referenced node are skipped.

**Probe:** "You add `aria-label="Close"` to a button that already reads `Close dialog` — what does VoiceOver announce, and why would that fail an audit?" (Expect: the ARIA label replaces the visible text entirely, breaking voice-control users who say "click Close dialog" (WCAG 2.5.3 Label in Name), and failing anyone using speech input; the visible text must be contained in the accessible name, so `aria-label` must include it or be dropped in favour of `aria-labelledby` pointing at the visible text.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Role/name/state resolution order, not just a list.** They want the precedence between `aria-labelledby`, `aria-label`, native labelling, name-from-content, `title`, `placeholder`, and the fact that role comes from explicit `role` first, then implicit mapping.
- **Awareness that the tree is computed, not hand-authored.** CSS that removes content from it (`display: none`, `visibility: hidden`, `hidden`, `inert`), and `aria-hidden="true"`, take nodes out; `.sr-only` and visually-hidden text stay in.
- **Understanding the consequence of a wrong role.** A `<div role="button">` reports `button` to AT and then does nothing on Enter/Space, which is worse than a plain `<div>` because the promise is broken.
- **Awareness of `aria-labelledby` vs `aria-label` vs `aria-describedby`:** labelledby for a name computed from other elements (it follows the reference even if the referenced element is otherwise hidden), `aria-label` for a static string override, `describedby` for supplementary hint or error text. `aria-labelledby` wins over `aria-label`, and `aria-describedby` concatenates onto whatever name was computed.

**Model answer, Q1:** "It's the tree the browser builds from the DOM — plus ARIA and CSS — that the screen reader actually consumes. Every node has a role, an accessible name, a value and states. For the name, `aria-labelledby` wins, then `aria-label`, then the native mechanism like `<label for>` or `alt`, then the element's own content for roles that name from content, then `title`, and `placeholder` last. Crucially it only describes — it changes what is *announced*, not behaviour, which is why you still need keyboard handlers and states."
---

## 3.7.2 ARIA Patterns and Keyboard Interaction
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

- **ARIA Authoring Practices Guide (APG)** defines expected roles, states, and **keyboard behavior** for widgets (tabs, menu, combobox, listbox, dialog, accordion, tree, disclosure, tooltip). Users expect the standard keys, so follow APG precisely or use a headless library that already does (Radix, React Aria, Headless UI, Ark).
- **Focus-management techniques**
  - **Roving tabindex:** only the active item has `tabindex="0"`, the rest `-1`. Arrow keys move focus and update the tabindex (tabs, toolbars, radio-like groups). One Tab stop for the whole widget.
  - **`aria-activedescendant`:** focus stays on the container/input (e.g., a combobox input) and the attribute points to the "virtually focused" option.
- **Tabs (APG)**
```html
<div role="tablist" aria-label="Settings">
  <button role="tab" id="t1" aria-selected="true"  aria-controls="p1" tabindex="0">General</button>
  <button role="tab" id="t2" aria-selected="false" aria-controls="p2" tabindex="-1">Billing</button>
</div>
<div role="tabpanel" id="p1" aria-labelledby="t1" tabindex="0">…</div>
<div role="tabpanel" id="p2" aria-labelledby="t2" hidden>…</div>
```
```js
tablist.addEventListener('keydown', (e) => {
  const tabs = [...tablist.querySelectorAll('[role=tab]')];
  const i = tabs.indexOf(document.activeElement);
  const next = { ArrowRight: (i + 1) % tabs.length, ArrowLeft: (i - 1 + tabs.length) % tabs.length,
                 Home: 0, End: tabs.length - 1 }[e.key];
  if (next === undefined) return;
  e.preventDefault();
  tabs.forEach((t, j) => { t.tabIndex = j === next ? 0 : -1; t.setAttribute('aria-selected', String(j === next)); });
  tabs[next].focus();    // update panels' `hidden` too
});
```
- **Dialog/modal:** `role="dialog"` + `aria-modal="true"` + a name (`aria-labelledby`), move focus inside on open (first focusable or the dialog itself), trap Tab within, close on `Esc`, return focus to the trigger on close, make the background inert, and lock body scroll. Native `<dialog>.showModal()` handles most of this.
- **Disclosure/accordion:** a `<button aria-expanded aria-controls>` toggling a region. Use native `<details>` when possible.
- **Menu button:** `aria-haspopup`, `aria-expanded`, arrow-key navigation, `Esc`, typeahead. **Don't use `role="menu"` for site navigation** (a list of links in a `<nav>` with a disclosure button is correct). `role="menu"` is for application-style menus.
- **Combobox/autocomplete:** `role="combobox"`, `aria-expanded`, `aria-controls` the listbox, `aria-activedescendant`, `aria-autocomplete`, and announcing result counts via a live region. It's the hardest widget, so use a library.
- **Tooltip:** `role="tooltip"` + `aria-describedby`, shown on both hover **and** focus, dismissible with `Esc`, and hoverable (WCAG 1.4.13 Content on Hover or Focus).
