- **Keyboard essentials:** all interactive elements reachable and operable, visible focus (WCAG 2.4.7; and 2.4.11 *Focus Not Obscured*, so sticky headers must not cover the focused element), logical order, no keyboard traps, and shortcuts that can be turned off or remapped (2.1.4).


**Prerequisites:** 3.7.1, 3.1.8.

**Why interviewers ask:** Custom widgets are where accessibility projects actually fail, and this is the highest-leverage place to show judgement: knowing when a native element or a headless library is the correct answer beats writing another combobox. Interviewers are looking for whether you treat keyboard support as part of the component's contract, not a later QA pass.

**Definition:** The set of role, state and keyboard conventions that a given widget must follow for assistive tech to drive it — formalised in the ARIA Authoring Practices Guide (APG). ARIA changes the *semantics announced*; the APG defines the *behaviour* users then expect from those semantics, and any mismatch between the two is worse than plain HTML.
**Questions**
1. Build an accessible tab list. What's roving tabindex?
2. What must an accessible modal do? How does `<dialog>` help?
3. `aria-activedescendant` vs roving tabindex?
4. Is `role="menu"` appropriate for a site's navigation dropdown? *(No)*
5. How would you make a custom select accessible? Or why would you not? *(Use native `<select>`, or a proven library. Rolling your own is a large cost.)*
6. What does `aria-expanded` communicate? What other states do toggles use? *(`aria-pressed`, `aria-checked`, `aria-selected`)*


**Self-check:** Name the five obligations of an accessible modal, and explain in one sentence why you would choose `aria-activedescendant` for a combobox but roving tabindex for a tablist.

**Resources:** the WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices Guide (apg.patterns) for the per-widget keyboard tables; WAI-ARIA 1.2 spec for role/state definitions; MDN's ARIA widget roles and `aria-activedescendant` pages; `<dialog>`: MDN and the HTML spec's "blocking modal" section; the "ARIA First Rule" discussion on web.dev.

**Mini task:** Build a tablist from scratch in vanilla JS with roving tabindex, wrapping arrows, Home/End, manual *and* automatic activation behind a flag, and panels wired with `aria-labelledby` plus `hidden`. Done when Tab enters the tablist once and leaves after the panels, arrows move focus with or without selection per the flag, VoiceOver announces the tab name with "selected" and "tab N of M", and axe reports no violations.

**Mistakes:**
- **Adding roles without behaviour:** `role="button"` on a `<div>` with no `keydown` handler for Enter and Space, so it is announced as a button but does nothing.
- **Positive `tabindex`:** every positive value jumps ahead of all naturally focusable elements, so Tab order diverges from DOM and visual order and fails WCAG 2.4.3. `tabindex="0"` and `"-1"` only.
- **Removing the focus outline without a replacement:** `outline: none` with no alternative indicator fails 2.4.7; use `:focus-visible` and a real ring, and remember 2.4.11 requires the focused element not to be hidden under a sticky header.
- **Un-synchronised state:** visually showing the active tab without moving `aria-selected`, or toggling the panel without updating `hidden`, so the announcement and the reality diverge.
- **`aria-controls` / `aria-owns` pointing at IDs that are unmounted or duplicated:** both are broken silently, and duplicate IDs (common when a list renders two instances of a component) break the reference for every instance.
- **Building a combobox from scratch:** it needs `aria-expanded`, `aria-controls`, `aria-activedescendant`, `aria-autocomplete`, scroll-into-view, IME support, typeahead and a result-count announcement. The bug count is high and the users are few.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Roving tabindex vs `aria-activedescendant`:** roving tabindex keeps DOM focus on the item, so browsers handle scrolling and text selection normally, but you must manage `tabindex` and focus() on every arrow key. `aria-activedescendant` leaves DOM focus on the container, which is what you want inside a text input (the combobox case), but you must implement scrolling into view yourself and it needs extra IE/older-AT caveats. Never both — they fight over who is active.
- **Native `<dialog>.showModal()` vs a hand-built modal:** native gives top-layer rendering, trap, Esc and backdrop for free and behaves correctly with nested dialogs; hand-built gives full control over animation and portal structure but you re-implement the trap and hit shadow DOM and iframe edge cases. Use native unless design forces otherwise.
- **Native `<select>`/`<details>` vs APG components:** native is nearly always correct — free keyboard, AT and mobile picker behaviour. Custom wins only when the design needs multi-select chips, tagging or async search inside the control.
- **`role="menu"` vs a disclosure nav button:** `role="menu"` promises application-style arrow-key navigation and typeahead, and browsers/screen readers give it menu-specific shortcuts. For site navigation the correct pattern is a `<nav>` of links behind a `<button aria-expanded>` — declaring `role="menu"` makes users expect behaviour you did not build.
- **Home-grown component vs headless library (Radix, React Aria, Headless UI, Ark):** a library buys you the APG keyboard map, focus management and thousands of hours of AT bug reports, at the cost of an API to learn, unstyled markup you have to restyle, and a version you do not control. Roll your own only when the interaction is genuinely novel or you need full styling control of internals.

**Common wrong answers:** Treating ARIA as behaviour ("I added `role="dialog"` so it's accessible"). Using `role="menu"` for site navigation. Putting `tabindex="0"` on every tab so each is a tab stop. Reaching for `aria-activedescendant` on a tabs implementation because they saw it in a combobox tutorial. Reimplementing `<select>` or a datepicker from scratch without flagging the maintenance cost.

**Probe:** "Your tabs load their panel from an API and it takes 800ms — do you keep automatic activation?" (Expect: switch to manual activation so arrows only move focus and Enter/Space commits, otherwise every arrow press fires four requests and the screen reader announces four loading states out of order; also mark the panel `aria-busy="true"` while fetching.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **They can state the five or six obligations of a pattern**, not just the attributes: role wiring, keyboard contract, focus management, state synchronisation, and returning focus.
- **Genuine command of the two focus models:** roving tabindex for composites whose items are all real DOM focus targets (tabs, radiogroup, toolbar, menu) versus `aria-activedescendant` when DOM focus must stay on one element (combobox input, listbox).
- **Knowing APG's optional behaviour choices:** automatic versus manual tab activation, and choosing automatic only when activation is cheap and synchronous.
- **Judgement about not building it.** The highest-scoring answer to "custom select" is "I wouldn't, unless native is unusable".

**Model answer, Q1:** "A tablist is a single tab stop, so I use roving tabindex: the selected tab has `tabindex="0"` and every other tab `tabindex="-1"`, and arrow keys move both focus and the tabindex. Each tab is `role="tab"` with `aria-selected` and `aria-controls`, the container is `role="tablist"` with a label, and each panel is `role="tabpanel"` with `aria-labelledby` pointing back at its tab, `tabindex="0"` when it has no focusable content. Arrows wrap, Home/End jump to first/last, and I pick automatic activation — arrow moves focus and selection together — unless the panel hits the network, in which case it's manual activation and you need Enter or Space to select."
---

## 3.7.3 Focus Management in SPAs and Dynamic UIs
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

- **The SPA problem:** client-side route changes don't reload the page, so screen readers hear nothing and focus stays on the clicked link. **Fix:** on navigation, update `document.title`, move focus to the new page's `<h1>` (give it `tabindex="-1"`) or the main container, and/or announce the new page title in a live region. Frameworks (Next.js has a route announcer, React Router has options) help, but verify.
- **Modals/drawers:** move focus in, trap, restore on close.
- **Deleting an item:** move focus to a logical neighbor (next item, list heading, or "undo" message), never lose it to `<body>`.
- **Loading states:** `aria-busy="true"` on the updating region, announce completion via `role="status"`.
- **Inline validation errors:** on submit, focus the first invalid field or an error summary at the top (with links to fields).
- **Skip links** (`<a href="#main">Skip to content</a>`), and landmark regions for fast navigation.
- **`:focus-visible`** for keyboard-only focus rings, and never remove outlines without a clear replacement.
- **Don't autofocus aggressively**, since it disorients screen reader users and scrolls pages unexpectedly (except in modals and search dialogs, where it's expected).
- **Focus trapping edge cases:** shadow DOM, iframes, and portal-rendered content. Prefer the native `inert` attribute on the background to avoid fragile manual traps.

```js
// After client-side navigation
function onRouteChange(title) {
  document.title = title;
  const h1 = document.querySelector('h1');
  h1.setAttribute('tabindex', '-1');
  h1.focus({ preventScroll: false });
}
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.7.2, 3.1.8.

**Why interviewers ask:** Every SPA accessibility complaint traces back to focus, and focus is the one thing no automated tool can verify for you. This question is also a proxy for whether the candidate has ever shipped and maintained a single-page app, because unmanaged focus is the default outcome of `innerHTML` swaps and client-side routing.

**Definition:** Deliberately deciding where keyboard focus goes, when it moves, and where it returns, at every point in the application where the DOM changes underneath the user — route changes, modals, deletions, form submissions and async updates. `tabindex="-1"` makes an element programmatically focusable but removes it from the tab sequence; `tabindex="0"` puts it back into the natural tab order.
**Questions:** How do you make an SPA navigation accessible? Where should focus go after closing a modal / deleting a row / submitting an invalid form? What does `tabindex="-1"` do vs `0`? Why avoid positive tabindex?


**Self-check:** After an SPA navigation and after deleting the last row in a list, say out loud exactly where focus goes, which attribute made the target focusable, and what the user hears.

**Resources:** MDN on `HTMLElement.focus()` and `tabindex`; the WAI-ARIA APG "Managing Focus" guidance; Next.js's `usePathname`/route-announcer implementation and React Router's focus-management guidance; WCAG 2.4.3 Focus Order, 2.4.7 Focus Visible and 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured; the `inert` attribute polyfill notes on MDN.

**Mini task:** In a React Router or Next.js app, build a route-change effect that sets `document.title`, focuses the new `<h1 tabindex="-1">`, and announces the page name in a persistent polite live region; then add row deletion from a list that correctly picks the next target, the previous target when it was the last row, and the list container when the list is empty. Done when a screen reader announces the new page title on every navigation and Tab from the list never escapes to the top of the document.

**Mistakes:**
- **Focusing an element that isn't focusable:** `h1.focus()` silently does nothing without `tabindex="-1"`, and you ship a fix that looks right in code and does nothing.
- **Losing focus to `<body>` on deletion or unmount:** the single most common SPA screen reader bug — after that, the user's place in the page is gone and Tab restarts from the top of the document.
- **Autofocusing on route change *and* scrolling to the top twice:** double-scroll, and on real page loads `autofocus` can strand keyboard users mid-page.
- **Wrapping the whole app in a manual trap** (or relying on `overflow: hidden` to trap): keyboard users cannot escape at all, which is WCAG 2.1.2 failure and can make a page unusable.
- **Focus fighting React:** calling `focus()` in the render body or without a dependency array, or re-focusing on every keystroke because the effect depends on the value being typed.
- **Ignoring 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured:** the focused item scrolls under a sticky header, so keyboard users cannot see what they have selected. Use `scroll-margin-top` to reserve the sticky header's height.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Focus to the new `<h1>` vs the `<main>` container vs announce only:** the `<h1>` gives a screen reader the page title as its first utterance, which is the clearest signal; focusing `<main>` is more forgiving with complex templates; announcing only and leaving focus on the clicked link is the most forgiving for keyboard users who dislike focus jumps but leaves the virtual cursor ambiguous. Pick one and be consistent.
- **Route announcer built in-house vs framework vs library:** Next.js ships one for free; React Router has no built-in equivalent, so you write ~15 lines or take a community package. Owning it means you control the wording and timing; borrowing it means fewer bugs and one more dependency.
- **`focus()` with default scroll vs `focus({ preventScroll: true })` + manual scroll:** default keeps the element in view but fights scroll restoration and can jump under a sticky header; `preventScroll` plus explicit scrolling is precise but two pieces of code to get right.
- **`inert` on the background vs a manual focus trap:** `inert` is declarative, survives portal-rendered content and shadow DOM, and is handled by the browser's sequential focus navigation; a manual trap is what you write when you need animation and nested overlays, and it is where traps break.
- **Delete immediately with an undo toast vs delete behind a confirmation:** a reversible optimistic delete with an "Undo" announcement needs no focus gymnastics beyond moving to a neighbour; a confirm dialog needs a whole modal focus routine but prevents destructive mistakes. Undo is the better pattern where recovery is cheap.

**Common wrong answers:** "Focusing the body is fine, the screen reader will just start from the top." Aggressive `autofocus` on page load. Calling `focus()` without `tabindex="-1"` first (a no-op on non-interactive elements). Blindly returning focus to the trigger when the trigger has been deleted, which throws or strands focus. Moving focus to every field as the user tabs (fights the browser and the user's expectation).

**Probe:** "The user deletes the last row in a table and focus lands on `<body>` — where exactly do you send it, and what if the list becomes empty?" (Expect: previous row's next logical target, then the list heading or the table's `caption`/container which you make `tabindex="-1"`; if the list is now empty, focus the empty-state heading or a "nothing here yet" status region rather than body.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **A complete, ordered routine for route change:** `document.title`, programmatic focus to a `tabindex="-1"` heading or `<main>`, a live-region announcement, and scroll reset — and knowing that announcement and focus are not redundant.
- **Correct `tabindex` semantics:** `-1` is focusable-by-script only, `0` is in the natural tab order, positive values break 2.4.3 by hoisting elements ahead of everything else.
- **A sensible focus target for each destructive or invalid action:** next sibling then previous sibling then list container for a deletion; the error summary or first invalid field on failed submit; never `<body>`.
- **Awareness of return focus:** remembering the element that opened a dialog and restoring it on close, with a fallback when the trigger has been unmounted.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Client-side routing doesn't fire a page load, so nothing tells the user anything changed. I do four things in a route-change effect: update `document.title`, render an `<h1 tabindex="-1">` for the new page and call `focus()` on it, give the `<main>` an `id` and skip link, and announce the new page name in a polite live region. `tabindex="-1"` is the key bit — it makes the heading programmatically focusable so a screen reader lands on the new page and reads the title, without adding a stop to the normal Tab sequence. I also scroll to top and reset any scroll restoration only when the user hasn't already navigated back. Frameworks help — Next.js ships a route announcer — but I always verify with a screen reader, because the announcer can fire before the heading has mounted."
---

## 3.7.4 Live Regions and Dynamic Announcements
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

- **Live regions** announce DOM changes without moving focus. `aria-live="polite"` (waits for a pause), `"assertive"` (interrupts, use sparingly), `role="status"` (implicit polite), `role="alert"` (implicit assertive), `aria-atomic` (read the whole region vs the change), `aria-relevant`.
- **Gotcha:** the live region container must **exist in the DOM before** its content changes. Injecting a new `role="status"` element that already has text often isn't announced. Render an empty container on load and update its text.
- **Use cases:** form errors and success messages, toasts ("Saved"), search result counts ("12 results"), cart updates, chat messages, and loading/complete states.
- **Anti-patterns:** announcing every keystroke or progress tick, using `assertive` for non-critical info, toasts that vanish before they can be read (WCAG 2.2.1 timing), and live regions inside `display:none`.
```html
<div id="live" role="status" aria-live="polite" class="sr-only"></div>
<script>
  function announce(msg) { live.textContent = ''; requestAnimationFrame(() => (live.textContent = msg)); }
</script>
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.7.1, 3.1.5.

**Why interviewers ask:** Announcements are the difference between an app that feels responsive and one that feels broken to a screen reader user, and the failure modes are non-obvious — a region inserted at the same moment its content appears usually stays silent, so many candidates have shipped live regions that never fired and never knew. It also tests whether they understand async UI at all.

**Definition:** A live region is a DOM subtree the platform accessibility layer watches for content changes, so assistive tech can announce an update without moving focus or interrupting the user's current position. Politeness is set with `aria-live` (`polite` waits for a pause, `assertive` interrupts), and `role="status"`/`role="alert"` are shorthand for the implicit polite/assertive cases.
**Questions:** How do you announce an async result to screen readers? `polite` vs `assertive`? Why might a live region not announce?


**Self-check:** You have a toast that fires once and then never again — list the three most likely causes in the order you would debug them.

**Resources:** MDN `aria-live` and `role="status"` pages; the WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices Guide's live-region guidance; WCAG 4.1.3 Status Messages (AA); Deque's "ARIA live regions" article for the pre-exist rule in depth; Apple and Microsoft accessibility-platform notes on announcement ordering.

**Mini task:** Build an async search-results count and a save confirmation in a real app. Render one persistent polite `role="status"` region on mount, update its text on resolve, deliberately submit the same query twice to force the identical-string problem, and fix it with the clear-then-`requestAnimationFrame` pattern. Done when the count is announced on both identical and different queries with VoiceOver or NVDA, and a second live region marked `aria-busy="true"` correctly suppresses announcements while a slow panel is still loading.

**Mistakes:**
- **Injecting the live region at the same time as the content:** the most common silent failure. Render the container empty on mount.
- **Re-using the identical string:** setting `textContent` to the same value is a no mutation, so nothing is announced. Clear, then set in the next frame — or swap between two identical elements.
- **Announcing inside `display: none` or a hidden tab panel:** hidden content is not in the accessibility tree, so there is nothing to announce; move focus or un-hide instead.
- **Live regions inside a shadow root or a portal that replaces the node:** some AT pairings miss changes in shadow DOM; verify with a real screen reader rather than assuming.
- **Assertive for routine updates, and multiple live regions firing at once:** the user gets a queue of noise and learns to ignore the mechanism.
- **No way to dismiss or re-read the message:** if it is information rather than feedback, it must also be available as static text somewhere.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Live region vs moving focus:** live regions preserve the user's place and never disorient, but they are easy to miss if the user's virtual cursor is elsewhere and the announcement is long; focus moves are unmissable but destroy context — a blind user who was reading paragraph four is now at the top of the page. Reserve focus moves for genuine navigation and destructive changes.
- **Single region vs one region per message type:** a single global announcer keeps the DOM tiny and the queue ordered but requires you to clear and re-set for repeated identical strings; per-component regions are easier to reason about but can collide and double-announce when nested (a `role="alert"` inside a `role="status"`).
- **`aria-atomic="true"` vs `false`:** atomic re-reads the whole region so a changed counter like "3 of 10" is not read as just "3", but it re-announces unchanged text too; non-atomic reads only the delta and sounds clipped for list or status updates.
- **Toast that auto-dismisses vs persistent inline status:** toasts are cheap and familiar but must respect WCAG 2.2.1 Timing Adjustable if they carry information the user needs, and must survive mouse-over if hoverable (1.4.13); an inline `role="status"` region next to the thing that changed is more reliable and always readable.
- **`role="alert"` vs `role="status"`:** alert is assertive and reserved for errors and time-sensitive blockers; using it for a routine "Saved" trains users to interrupt their own speech with noise, which is how live regions get turned off.

**Common wrong answers:** "Use `aria-live` and it just works" (region must exist first). `aria-live="assertive"` for every toast. Animating a live region (some AT won't fire on a node whose content changes together with a transform/opacity). Moving focus to every toast, which is what most people do and what breaks keyboard users. Adding `aria-live` to a container that is `display: none` until the message arrives. Announcing every keystroke or every progress tick.

**Probe:** "Your `role="status"` toast has fired reliably for months, then a refactor wraps it in a conditional and now nothing is announced. Why?" (Expect: the region is being mounted at the same time as its text, so it is not in the accessibility tree when the change happens — the region must exist in the DOM before the content changes, or you need the clear-then-set-on-`requestAnimationFrame` trick for repeated identical strings.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **They know the region must pre-exist.** This is the single thing that separates someone who has debugged announcements from someone who has read about them.
- **Correct politeness choice and reasoning about the queue:** `polite` announcements queue behind speech and are order-dependent, so two rapid updates may be collapsed or reordered; `assertive` interrupts and, overused, makes an app unusable.
- **Knowing when a live region is the wrong tool:** moving focus to a new page or to the error summary is better than announcing it, and a toast with an action needs focus, not just speech.
- **Awareness of related properties:** `role="status"` (implicit `aria-live="polite"`, implicit `aria-atomic="true"`), `role="alert"` (implicit assertive), `aria-atomic`, `aria-relevant="additions text"` to ignore removals, and `aria-busy` to hold announcements until an update settles.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Async results are announced through a live region that is already in the DOM before the request resolves — render an empty `role="status"` container on mount and set its `textContent` when the promise settles, clearing it first and re-setting on the next frame if the same message repeats. I use `polite` by default so it waits for a pause, `assertive` only for things that block the user's next action like a failed payment. I also move real focus for the things a live region is a poor substitute for: toasts that contain a link get focus moved to the link, and page-level content changes get a programmatic focus change. If it still doesn't announce, the usual causes are the region being added and populated in the same tick, the region being inside `display: none` or `visibility: hidden`, or the update happening in a portalled container that replaced the region node."
---

## 3.7.5 Visual Accessibility: Contrast, Zoom, Motion, and Preferences
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

- **Contrast:** text 4.5:1 (3:1 for large text), **non-text UI** (input borders, icons, focus rings, chart elements) 3:1 (WCAG 1.4.11), and states (hover, disabled are exempt for disabled). Tools: DevTools color picker, axe, Stark, WebAIM contrast checker. Remember dark mode, gradients, and text over images.
- **Don't rely on color alone** (links need underline or another cue, errors need an icon and text, chart series need patterns or labels).
- **Reflow and zoom:** content must work at 400% zoom / 320 CSS px wide without two-dimensional scrolling (1.4.10), text resizes to 200% (1.4.4), and users can set text spacing (1.4.12), so don't use fixed heights that clip text. Use `rem`, flexible layouts, and avoid `user-scalable=no`.
- **Orientation:** don't lock to portrait or landscape unless essential (1.3.4).
- **Motion:** respect `prefers-reduced-motion`, avoid parallax and large auto-moving content, provide pause/stop for anything moving over 5s (2.2.2), and never flash more than 3 times per second (2.3.1).
- **Touch and pointer (2.2 additions):** targets at least 24×24 CSS px (2.5.8), alternatives to drag-only interactions (2.5.7, e.g. buttons to reorder), and pointer cancellation (don't fire on `down`).
- **User preferences:** `prefers-color-scheme`, `prefers-contrast`, `prefers-reduced-transparency`, and `forced-colors` (Windows High Contrast, where custom backgrounds and `box-shadow` focus rings may vanish, so use `outline`/`border`, and system colors like `CanvasText`).
- **Readable content:** plain language, `lang` attributes for foreign phrases (`<span lang="fr">`), consistent navigation and help (3.2.3, 3.2.6), no content that only works on hover.


**Self-check:** You have a primary button, a disabled button, a form field in its error state, and a focus ring on a sticky header. State the ratio each one needs, the criterion number, and which of the four are exempt.

**Resources:** WCAG 1.4.3 Contrast (Minimum), 1.4.11 Non-text Contrast, 1.4.10 Reflow, 1.4.4 Resize Text, 1.4.12 Text Spacing, 1.4.1 Use of Color, 2.3.1 Three Flashes; MDN's `prefers-reduced-motion`, `prefers-contrast`, `forced-colors` and `forced-color-adjust` pages; web.dev's "Rendering themes" and "Windows High Contrast" articles; the WebAIM Contrast Checker and the axe-core colour-contrast rule.

**Mini task:** Audit your design system's colour tokens in both themes, in every state, and report every pair below 4.5:1 for text and 3:1 for non-text; then add a `@media (prefers-reduced-motion: reduce)` gate on the duration token and verify with DevTools' rendering emulation that parallax and slide transitions stop while a fade remains. Done when the palette report is either fixed or documented with accepted-risk decisions, and a single CSS change disables non-essential motion everywhere in the app.

**Mistakes:**
- **Testing contrast on hover/focus/disabled states:** the default state passes and the state users actually operate in fails 1.4.11.
- **`user-scalable=no` or `maximum-scale=1`:** fails 1.4.4 and does not even stop pinch-zoom on modern iOS; it makes the page worse for low-vision users.
- **Fixed `px` heights on anything containing text:** clips at 200% text resize or with the 1.4.12 spacing overrides, and drops content entirely.
- **`transition: all` combined with `prefers-reduced-motion` gating on a subset of properties:** something large still animates; gate the duration token instead of chasing selectors.
- **`outline: none` plus a `box-shadow` ring:** the shadow disappears in forced-colors mode and on some Windows backgrounds, leaving no focus indicator at all.
- **Conveying state by colour only:** a red border on an invalid field, a green bar in a chart, a link distinguished only by hue — all fail 1.4.1 without a second cue such as an icon, underline, or `::before` text.
- **Blinking or auto-playing content with no pause control,** or anything flashing above three times per second, which is a hard Level A failure (2.3.1).

**Trade-offs:**
- **Repalette the design system vs ship an accessible default theme:** a repalette fixes every surface permanently but is slow and may need a design review; a separate high-contrast theme is faster to ship but doubles the themes you test and lets a bad palette linger. Do the token pairing first and treat the theme as a stopgap.
- **`forced-color-adjust: auto` vs `none`:** the default (`auto`) lets the OS replace your colours with the user's, which preserves usability under High Contrast but can lose your brand and layout hints; `none` keeps your colours and is a WCAG 1.4.11 failure in forced-colors mode. Use `auto` by default and only opt out for a component you have explicitly re-styled.
- **CSS-only `prefers-reduced-motion` vs a JS motion context vs a user-facing toggle:** CSS is the lowest-effort and covers most cases but cannot stop video autoplay, canvas or scroll-jacking; JS covers everything and can be driven by an app preference; a visible toggle is the only way to honour intent when the OS setting is inconvenient. Ideally CSS plus a toggle that also writes the setting.
- **Fixed-height toolbars vs fluid layouts for reflow:** fixed heights keep a tight layout but clip text when the user increases line-height or spacing (1.4.12) and at 400% zoom; fluid layouts and `min-height` cost a little design control and never clip.
- **In-viewport `ArrowUp`/`ArrowDown` rearrangements vs explicit "Move up" buttons:** drag or key reordering is compact and fast for pointer users; buttons cost vertical space but are required by 2.5.7 Dragging Movements and are the only thing many motor-impaired users can hit at a 24px target.

**Common wrong answers:** "Contrast is 4.5:1, done." Believing disabled elements need to pass (they are exempt) or that logos and decorative backgrounds need to pass (exempt, but text inside a logo does). Assuming zoom support means they support pinch-zoom and that `user-scalable=no` is a legitimate shortcut (WCAG 1.4.4). Implementing `prefers-reduced-motion` by wrapping each animation in its own media query. Treating `forced-colors` as a bug to be fixed with `!important` overrides rather than adopted.

**Probe:** "Your secondary text colour passes at 4.5:1 on white but the same token is reused on a dark surface in the theme file — what happened and how do you catch it systematically?" (Expect: contrast is a relationship between two colours, not a property of one token, so you need a token *pair* validated against each theme's surface, in CI, plus a check of every state; and non-text tokens need the same treatment against their own surface.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Correct numbers, applied to the right thing.** 4.5:1 and 3:1 for text with the correct large-text threshold, and 3:1 from 1.4.11 for non-text UI and focus indicators — a very common mix-up is applying 4.5:1 to icons or 3:1 to body text.
- **They treat it as a token/lint problem,** not a per-screen fix: contrast pairs in the design system, a CI check on the palette, `prefers-reduced-motion` behind one duration variable.
- **They know the non-CSS parts:** target size needs padding, not just a bigger icon; reflow needs layout that survives 320px; forced-colors needs system colour keywords and `currentColor`, not CSS-only styling.
- **Honest trade-off awareness:** forcing a palette change can be resisted by design, so they can talk about pairing a token change with an accessible-default theme rather than silently shipping a failing palette.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Text is 4.5:1 at AA, dropping to 3:1 for large text, which is 18pt regular — 24 CSS px — or 14pt bold, about 18.7px. Non-text contrast is a separate criterion, 1.4.11, at 3:1, and it covers the visual boundary of controls, their states, focus indicators and meaningful graphics — an input border, a checkbox tick, a chart series, a toggle track. It does not cover disabled controls or purely decorative backgrounds. I check with the DevTools picker or axe, and I check the real states, because a border that only appears on hover or focus usually fails the state it is actually used in."

**Questions**
1. What contrast ratios does WCAG AA require for text and for non-text UI, and what counts as "large text"?
2. Which of your design system's colours fail today, and how would you fix them without a visual redesign?
3. What breaks at 400% zoom / 320px wide, and how do you build so it does not?
4. How do you implement `prefers-reduced-motion` across an existing animation system?
5. Why does a `box-shadow` focus ring vanish in Windows High Contrast, and what do you use instead?
6. A form shows errors in red only. What is wrong and what is the minimum fix?
7. `prefers-contrast: more` and `forced-colors: active` — what do you do with each?
8. What does WCAG 2.2 require for target size, and what are the exemptions?

**Prerequisites:** 3.2.7, 3.2.9, 3.2.12, 3.1.5.

**Why interviewers ask:** It is the accessibility area product teams most often skip and most often get caught out on, and it is the area a designer or design-systems interviewer will care about. It also proves you can reason about a requirement as a system property (a token, a media query, a build step) instead of a per-component fix.

**Definition:** The parts of WCAG that are not about markup or keyboard behaviour: contrast and colour use, reflow at zoom and small widths, text resize and spacing overrides, orientation, motion and flashing, target size, and honouring user preferences such as `prefers-reduced-motion`, `prefers-color-scheme`, `prefers-contrast` and `forced-colors`. Almost all of it is enforceable in CSS, which means it is enforceable by design tokens and a CI check.
---

## 3.7.6 WCAG 2.2 Highlights and Compliance Context
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

- **WCAG 2.2 AA additions:** 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured (Minimum), 2.5.7 Dragging Movements, 2.5.8 Target Size (Minimum), 3.2.6 Consistent Help, 3.3.7 Redundant Entry (don't make users retype info), 3.3.8 Accessible Authentication (no cognitive-test-only login, so allow paste, password managers, and passkeys). 2.2 also removed the obsolete 4.1.1 Parsing criterion.
- **Conformance levels:** A, AA (the typical legal target), AAA.
- **Legal and organizational context:** ADA (US), Section 508, **European Accessibility Act** (applies to many consumer products and services in the EU from June 2025), EN 301 549, AODA. Deliverables include an **ACR/VPAT** and an accessibility statement.
- **Process:** shift left (design annotations, accessible component library, linting, CI checks, manual audits before launch, user testing with people who use assistive tech), and track issues like any other bug class.


**Self-check:** Name the four WCAG 2.2 AA additions, the criterion that was deleted, the level you would target, and the document you would hand to a procurement team.

**Resources:** the W3C WCAG 2.2 Recommendation and its "What's New in WCAG 2.2" comparison page; the W3C "Understanding Conformance" guidance; EN 301 549 and the European Accessibility Act (Directive (EU) 2019/882, applicable from 28 June 2025, with a microenterprise exemption for service-providing microenterprises); the US DOJ 2024 web rule (WCAG 2.1 AA) and Section 508; ITI's VPAT 2.5 for the ACR format.

**Mini task:** Pick your product's main journey, list every WCAG 2.2 AA success criterion that applies (aim for at least fifteen), record pass/fail/not-applicable with evidence for each, and write a one-page accessibility statement covering known issues, contact routes and a remediation date. Done when a colleague can reproduce each pass claim and the known-issues list has owners and dates rather than vague commitments.

**Mistakes:**
- **Quoting the wrong level for a criterion,** e.g. calling 2.5.8 Target Size AAA when it is AA, or 3.3.8 AAA when it is AA — graders notice this immediately because it is checkable.
- **Using the "essential" exception as a blanket excuse.** Exceptions are for content that is genuinely incidental or an individual choice of technique; a core flow cannot be exempted.
- **Claiming conformance for a template while the CMS lets authors ship broken pages** — conformance covers the delivered content, so the authoring experience and the guardrails are part of the claim.
- **Running the audit once and declaring victory,** with no component-level guardrails, so every new feature re-introduces the same violations.
- **Producing a VPAT that omits failures,** which converts a useful document into a misrepresentation and creates liability.

**Trade-offs:**
- **WCAG 2.1 AA vs 2.2 AA as the internal target:** 2.2 is the current recommendation and its additions (focus not obscured, target size, accessible authentication) are what auditors increasingly check; 2.1 is what most legislation and EN 301 549 currently reference, so shipping 2.2 AA satisfies 2.1 AA plus extras. Target 2.2 AA and document it.
- **Full conformance vs a documented partial scope:** full conformance is the defensible position and forces the work; a scoped claim on key flows with a published accessibility statement and a dated remediation plan is realistic for a large legacy estate but must be explicit about what is not covered, or it is a misrepresentation.
- **ACR/VPAT generated per release vs on demand:** per release costs engineering time every cycle; on demand keeps it cheap but goes stale and undermines its value when procurement asks. Automate it from the CI scan and refresh on a schedule.
- **Automated gating in CI vs warnings:** a hard gate on a curated, high-severity rule set (blocking violations only) stops regressions without blocking delivery on the long tail; a full strict gate on every rule blocks work that does not affect users and gets pressure applied to disable the gate. Ship the strict version, then carve out documented exceptions with owners.
- **In-house expertise vs a third-party audit:** an audit gives you a legally-usable report and finds what automation misses, but it is a snapshot, not a process, and it is expensive; in-house audits are continuous but only credible if someone on the team genuinely knows screen readers.

**Common wrong answers:** "WCAG 2.2 is law." (It is a standard; the law points at it, and the referenced version differs by jurisdiction.) Naming 2.4.11 as AAA or forgetting that 4.1.1 Parsing is gone. Claiming a Lighthouse or axe score makes a site conformant. Believing an exemption applies because a feature is new, hard, or would take a redesign. Treating the VPAT as a marketing artefact rather than a scoped claim about specific pages and criteria.

**Probe:** "Your marketing site has to ship Friday and you have found three AA failures — one missing `alt`, one focus indicator under a sticky header, one 20px tap target. Which is in scope?" (Expect: all three are AA failures and none is a legitimate exception — `alt` is 1.1.1 A, the obscured focus indicator is 2.4.11 AA, the target size is 2.5.8 AA — so the answer is either fix the cheap ones (alt, target size are minutes) and fix or mitigate 2.4.11 with `scroll-margin-top`, or get a documented risk acceptance and fix date rather than shipping silently.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Precise recall of the 2.2 additions,** especially the four AA criteria, and that 4.1.1 Parsing was removed — a detail that shows they read the changelog rather than paraphrasing a blog post.
- **They can separate WCAG from the law.** They know WCAG is a technical standard that legislation and procurement rules reference, and that the required version and level vary (EAA points at EN 301 549, which references WCAG 2.1 AA; US federal Section 508 still references 2.0 AA; the DOJ web rule references 2.1 AA).
- **They understand what a conformance claim means:** full pages, complete processes, all functionality covered, no reliance on an exception for content that is central to the experience.
- **Process maturity:** they talk about components, CI, ownership and defect tracking rather than a pre-launch audit and a VPAT PDF nobody reads.

**Model answer, Q1:** "WCAG 2.2 added nine criteria and removed one. The four AA additions are 2.4.11 Focus Not Obscured, so a sticky header can't cover the element you're tabbing to; 2.5.7 Dragging Movements, so anything draggable needs a single-pointer alternative like Move up/down buttons; 2.5.8 Target Size Minimum at 24 by 24 CSS pixels; and 3.3.8 Accessible Authentication, so no cognitive-function-only login, and you must allow paste and support password managers and passkeys. At A it added 3.2.6 Consistent Help, so a help link is in the same relative place on every page, and 3.3.7 Redundant Entry, so you never make someone retype information they already gave you. And it deleted 4.1.1 Parsing, because the parsing failures it described are now validator bugs rather than a content problem."

**Questions**
1. What changed in WCAG 2.2, and which of the new criteria are at AA?
2. Which conformance level do we normally have to meet, and who says so?
3. What is the European Accessibility Act, when does it apply, and what is the small-business exemption?
4. What is an ACR/VPAT, and what does it commit you to?
5. How do you make accessibility part of delivery rather than a pre-launch scramble?
6. Can you claim WCAG conformance for part of a site? What are the rules?

**Prerequisites:** 3.1.5.

**Why interviewers ask:** Because it turns "we care about accessibility" into a defensible, scoped engineering decision. A candidate who can name the 2.2 deltas, say which level is legally required in their jurisdiction, and describe what an ACR and a conformance claim actually mean is talking about shipping products, not about lint rules.

**Definition:** WCAG 2.2 is the current W3C Recommendation (published October 2023) for making web content accessible; it is structured as four principles — Perceivable, Operable, Understandable, Robust — across three conformance levels A, AA and AAA. WCAG 2.2 added nine success criteria (four at AA) and removed the obsolete 4.1.1 Parsing, and it sits inside a layer of procurement and statute that determines who is actually legally required to comply.
---

## 3.7.7 Testing Accessibility
🟡 → 🔴 · **Must-know**

| Layer | Tools | Catches |
|---|---|---|
| **Lint (dev time)** | `eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y`, `eslint-plugin-vuejs-accessibility` | Missing alt, invalid ARIA, click handlers on non-interactive elements |
| **Unit/component** | `jest-axe`/`vitest-axe`, Testing Library role queries | Naming, roles, basic violations |
| **E2E/CI** | `@axe-core/playwright`, Cypress axe, Lighthouse CI, Pa11y | Page-level violations on real flows |
| **Design system** | Storybook a11y addon | Per-component regressions |
| **Manual (required)** | Keyboard-only pass, screen reader (**VoiceOver + Safari** on macOS/iOS, **NVDA + Firefox/Chrome** on Windows, TalkBack on Android, JAWS in enterprise), zoom to 200-400%, high contrast/forced colors, reduced motion, voice control | The 60-70% automation misses: focus order, meaningful alt text, announcement quality, usability |

- **Automated tools find about 30-40% of issues.** A green Lighthouse score is not accessibility.
- **Minimum screen-reader smoke test:** navigate by landmarks and headings, tab through all controls (are names and states announced?), complete the main flow (forms, dialogs), and check dynamic updates are announced.
```js
// Playwright + axe in CI
import AxeBuilder from '@axe-core/playwright';
test('checkout has no a11y violations', async ({ page }) => {
  await page.goto('/checkout');
  const { violations } = await new AxeBuilder({ page }).withTags(['wcag2a', 'wcag2aa', 'wcag22aa']).analyze();
  expect(violations).toEqual([]);
});
```

**React specifics:** use `htmlFor` for labels and `useId()` to generate stable unique IDs for label/description wiring, avoid `div` click handlers, manage focus with refs and effects, use fragments to avoid invalid wrapper markup (like `div` inside `ul`), announce route changes, prefer accessible headless libraries for complex widgets, and run `jsx-a11y` in lint.


**Prerequisites:** 3.7.1, 3.7.2, 3.7.6.

**Why interviewers ask:** Testing is where accessibility work is won or lost in practice, so this question separates people who have shipped an accessible product from people who have only run a scanner. It also reveals process maturity: the strong answers talk about components and CI guardrails, not about an audit before launch.

**Definition:** The layered practice of verifying accessibility automatically at several points in the pipeline — lint, component tests, E2E/CI, design-system Storybook — and then, necessarily, by hand with a keyboard, a screen reader, zoom, forced colours and voice control, because automated tooling detects only a minority of real issues.
**Questions**
1. How do you test accessibility? What do automated tools miss?
2. Walk through a manual screen-reader test of a checkout form.
3. How would you make accessibility part of the team's process, not a pre-launch scramble?
4. How do you choose between building a custom component and using an accessible headless library?
5. What changed in WCAG 2.2? 
6. How would you audit an existing app with limited time? *(Automated scan to catch low-hanging fruit, then keyboard-only and screen-reader passes on the top 3 user flows, then prioritize by severity and user impact.)*

**Mini tasks**
1. Build accessible tabs, a modal, and a combobox (or use Radix/React Aria for the combobox) and verify each with VoiceOver or NVDA.
2. Add jest-axe and Playwright axe checks to a project, then introduce three deliberate violations and confirm CI catches the ones automation can, and write down the ones it can't.
3. Fix SPA route-change focus and announcements in a React Router or Next.js app.


**Resources:** Deque's axe and `@axe-core/playwright` documentation and the axe-core rule list with its WCAG tags; Testing Library's `getByRole` and `ByRole` matcher docs; the WebAIM "Automated Accessibility Testing" coverage article; the WAI-ARIA Authoring Practices Guide for the keyboard contract to test against; Accessibility Insights for Windows; WCAG 2.2 for the criteria tags to configure.

**Mistakes:**
- **Adding axe and then disabling every failing rule,** which converts the check into a rubber stamp and puts the failures in a comment for someone to fix in a year.
- **Awaiting the wrong thing in E2E:** running `analyze()` on a client-rendered route before its content mounts produces a false pass.
- **Using `container.querySelector` or `getByTestId` for accessibility assertions,** which tests that the DOM exists rather than that the user can perceive it.
- **Testing only Chromium.** VoiceOver on macOS, NVDA on Windows and TalkBack on Android expose different platform bugs — especially with `aria-activedescendant`, virtual cursor behaviour and `role="alert"` timing.
- **Never running an automated test as a real screen reader user,** i.e. trusting the reported tree instead of listening to announcements, and missing duplicated, verbose or meaningless names.
- **Treating axe as a gate but not fixing the source,** e.g. suppressing at the call site instead of fixing the component, so every consumer inherits the violation.

**Trade-offs:**
- **eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y vs type-level or test-level enforcement:** lint is instant and covers every file but a fixed subset of rules and false positives on legitimate patterns; tests using role queries plus axe catch real regressions in behaviour but only on what you wrote a test for. Run lint for the cheap 80% and role-based tests for the semantics.
- **Gate CI strictly vs warn:** a hard gate on blocking-level rules stops regressions and is the only version that survives, but the long tail of pre-existing violations will block every PR until baselined; a warn-only check gets ignored. Baselined hard gate on new violations, plus a burn-down issue for the existing ones.
- **E2E axe vs Lighthouse CI:** axe on named flows is faster, more targeted and gives actionable selectors; Lighthouse also audits performance and SEO and is easier to justify to non-engineers, but it runs the page in isolation and is less controllable. Use axe in the build, Lighthouse as a reporting artefact.
- **Dedicated component library vs general-purpose tooling:** a maintained accessible component library (Radix, React Aria, Headless UI) eliminates whole classes of defect and is usually the right investment; bespoke tooling you write matches your exact stack and workflow but costs ongoing maintenance. Buy the widgets, build the lint/test/CI glue.
- **Third-party audit vs continuous internal practice:** an audit produces the legally-usable report and finds real problems fast; continuous internal practice prevents regression between audits. An audit without guardrails is a one-off cost centre, so do both and sequence the audit as the forcing function.

**Common wrong answers:** Treating a green Lighthouse accessibility score as evidence of conformance. Only running automation and calling the audit done. Testing with a mouse and a screen reader but never by keyboard alone. Writing tests against implementation details (test ids, class names) instead of roles and names. Scanning an SPA before the route has rendered, so axe reads an empty shell. Adding `@axe-core/playwright` to CI and immediately suppressing the whole suite or adding blanket disables when it goes red.

**Probe:** "Your E2E axe check passes but a screen reader user still cannot complete checkout — what are the four most likely causes axe structurally cannot see?" (Expect: focus order or focus management wrong, no announcement for dynamic updates like the basket count or validation errors, names/labels present but meaningless to a human ("button", "edit", "Read more"), and keyboard traps or focus indicators that exist but are obscured or invisible.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Layered strategy with distinct purposes per layer,** not "we run axe", and an accurate sense that automation misses focus order, meaning of alt text, announcement quality and usability.
- **Knowledge that the manual pass is a specific, scripted procedure** — keyboard-only, screen-reader smoke test, zoom, forced colours, voice control — not "click around and see".
- **Testing perception, not markup:** role/name/state queries and listening to announcements, rather than asserting on DOM structure or on the presence of `aria-*` attributes.
- **Process thinking:** guardrails in the design system, lint in the repo, axe in CI, accessibility tracked in the normal defect tracker with severity.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Layered, and the layers catch different things. Lint with `eslint-plugin-jsx-a11y` catches missing `alt`, invalid ARIA and click handlers on non-interactive elements at commit time. Component tests use jest-axe plus Testing Library role queries, so I assert the role, name and state a user would perceive rather than implementation detail. E2E runs `@axe-core/playwright` with `withTags(['wcag2a','wcag2aa','wcag22aa'])` on the critical flows so page-level violations fail CI, and Storybook catches per-component regressions. Then manual, which automation cannot replace: keyboard-only pass for focus order, traps, shortcuts and visible focus; a real screen reader — VoiceOver on Safari or NVDA on Firefox — for names, states, announcements and landmark navigation; 200–400% zoom; forced-colors; reduced motion; and voice control. Automation flags roughly a third to a half of issues depending on the study, and misses focus order, alt-text quality, whether an announcement makes sense, whether a label makes sense to a human, and usability."
**Self-check:** Can you name the accessible name computation order, the five things an accessible modal must do, and three things axe cannot detect?

---

# 3.8 BUILD TOOLS AND MONOREPOS

## 3.8.1 What Build Tools Do and the Landscape
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Responsibilities:** transpile (TS/JSX/new syntax → target JS), **bundle** (resolve the module graph into few files), minify, tree shake, code split, handle assets (CSS, images, fonts, SVG), inject env vars, produce source maps and content-hashed filenames, run a **dev server with HMR**, and generate the HTML shell/manifest.

| Tool | Notes |
|---|---|
| **Vite** | Dev server serves **native ES modules** on demand (no bundling in dev) with **esbuild** pre-bundling third-party deps, so cold start and HMR are fast. Production builds use Rollup (with Rolldown, a Rust port, rolling out). The de facto choice for SPAs and most frameworks |
| **Webpack** | The long-time standard: highly configurable (loaders, plugins, Module Federation), but slower and config-heavy. Huge legacy footprint (CRA is deprecated) |
| **Rspack** | Rust, mostly Webpack-compatible (a migration path from Webpack) |
| **esbuild** | Go, extremely fast bundler/transpiler/minifier. No type checking, limited plugin hooks, so it's used under other tools |
| **SWC** | Rust compiler (Babel replacement) used by Next.js, Vitest-adjacent tooling |
| **Babel** | Plugin-based JS transformer, still used for custom transforms (e.g., React Compiler plugin, some libraries). Slower |
| **Turbopack** | Rust bundler integrated into Next.js |
| **Rollup / tsup / tsdown / unbuild** | Library bundling (clean ESM/CJS output, `.d.ts` generation) |
| **Parcel, Bun** | Zero-config bundler / runtime with a built-in bundler |
| **tsc** | Type checker and (optionally) emitter. Use `--noEmit` with bundlers |

**Why Vite dev is fast:** it doesn't bundle your source. The browser requests modules over native ESM, and Vite transforms each file lazily on request. Dependencies are pre-bundled once (they're CommonJS-heavy and have many files). HMR invalidates only the changed module chain. Large apps can still suffer from many unbundled requests (mitigated by caching and by newer full-bundle dev modes).

**Why prod is still bundled:** fewer round trips, tree shaking, minification, optimized chunking, and legacy-browser targets.


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.11, 3.4.2, 3.6.5.

**Why interviewers ask:** Almost everyone ships through a bundler but has only ever used one, so this is a test of whether you understand the machinery underneath your tool rather than memorising its config keys. The dev-versus-prod asymmetry is the sharpest version of it, and knowing why Vite skips bundling tells you more about your own codebase than knowing that a `define` field exists.

**Definition:** The build tool is the pipeline between authored source (TypeScript, JSX, CSS, assets) and deployable output: it resolves the module graph, transpiles to the browser's target syntax, bundles and optimises chunks, minifies, injects build-time configuration, and emits an HTML shell — plus a dev server that skips most of that work for speed.
**Questions**
1. What does a bundler do? Do we still need one with HTTP/2 and native ESM? *(Yes: tree shaking, minification, chunk strategy, asset handling, and avoiding deep import waterfalls.)*
2. Why is Vite's dev server faster than Webpack's? What's the trade-off?
3. Babel vs SWC vs esbuild vs tsc?
4. How does HMR work? *(A dev-server websocket tells the client which modules changed. The runtime re-imports them and calls accept handlers. React Fast Refresh preserves component state when only components changed.)*
5. How would you migrate a Webpack app to Vite? What breaks? *(Webpack-specific loaders, `require.context`, global `process.env`, CommonJS-only deps, aliases, and SVG/asset import conventions.)*


**Self-check:** Can you explain in 60 seconds why a Vite dev server starts faster than webpack, what that speed costs you in fidelity to the production build, and which of esbuild/SWC/Babel/`tsc` does which job?

**Resources:** Vite's "Why Vite" and "Going Production" guides; webpack's "Concepts" and loader docs; esbuild's plugin API docs; the SWC README and Next.js's compiler documentation; Rollup's and `tsup`'s docs on `external` and `dts` generation.

**Mini task:** Time `tsc --noEmit`, a full production build, and dev-server cold start on one app, then swap its transform step for plain `esbuild` and re-time — so you can state from your own numbers where the build cost actually lives.

**Mistakes:**
- **Treating dev as a proxy for production:** dev never minifies, never tree shakes, and in Vite never bundles, so `process.env` branches, CJS interop, and dead-code-only-in-prod bugs surface only at build time.
- **Adding a bundler plugin to do something `tsc` or a three-line transform already does,** inflating build time and config surface.
- **Choosing a target without a polyfill plan,** so a modern-looking build throws on `Promise` or `Array.prototype.at` in older browsers; `build.target` and `browserslist`/`core-js` have to be decided together.
- **Comparing tools on benchmark numbers instead of your own module graph,** since bundler cost is dominated by module count, `node_modules` shape, and plugin count.
- **Dropping source maps in production** (or publishing them), so the first real stack trace is unreadable.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Vite vs webpack:** Vite gives sub-second dev start and near-instant HMR by not bundling in dev, but it gives up most of webpack's escape hatches — exotic loaders, `require.context`, `Module Federation`, and long plugin ecosystems — so migration is real work, not a rename. Webpack stays right when you need Module Federation or depend on a plugin you can't replace.
- **esbuild vs SWC vs Babel:** esbuild bundles and transforms fast with a thin plugin surface; SWC is Babel-flavoured at Rust speed with occasional compatibility gaps; Babel is slow but is the only one that can run a plugin for a syntax that doesn't exist yet.
- **`tsc --noEmit` vs bundler-owned type checking:** a separate `tsc` pass catches everything and supports project references, at the cost of another slow step; a bundler "type check" is fast, partial, and usually wrong. Never let the production build be the only type check.
- **Unbundled dev vs bundling dev:** on-demand ESM starts instantly but sends thousands of requests and hides bundler-only bugs; webpack-style full dev bundling (and Turbopack's persistent cache) starts slower but makes dev and prod behave identically.
- **One all-in-one tool vs an explicit layered toolchain:** Parcel, Bun, and the framework defaults are fast to adopt and slow to escape; `tsc` plus a Rust transform plus Rollup is slower to configure and exactly tunable.

**Common wrong answers:** "Bundlers are obsolete since HTTP/2 and native ESM"; "Vite is fast because esbuild is fast" (it's fast because it doesn't bundle your source in dev); "esbuild replaces Babel" with no mention of type checking or the plugin surface; "webpack is slow because it's JavaScript" without naming the CommonJS resolution problem.

**Probe:** "Your app is instant on your laptop but a new joiner waits 40 seconds for `pnpm dev` to become usable." (Expect: the one-time `optimizeDeps` pre-bundle pass plus a cold `node_modules`; then check DevTools Network for module request count — if it's huge, `optimizeDeps.include` doesn't cover your heaviest CommonJS deps; if it re-optimises every start, the lockfile or config hash is changing, which happens when the config or `package.json` churns.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **A clear model of the pipeline** — resolve, transform, optimise, emit — and that dev and prod are genuinely different pipelines even inside one tool.
- **Understanding that ESM is what makes optimisation possible** (static `import`, no conditional `require`), and therefore why CJS dependencies need a pre-bundling pass.
- **That every "fast" tool pays for it somewhere:** Vite with prod/dev divergence, esbuild with a thin plugin surface and no types, SWC with Babel-compat gaps, Babel with raw speed.
- **Tools in the right roles:** `tsc --noEmit` for types, esbuild/SWC/Babel for transforms, Rollup/`tsup` for output, Rspack/Rolldown/Turbopack as the new bundler generation.

**Model answer, Q2:** "Vite's dev server doesn't bundle your source. It serves each `.ts`/`.tsx` file as a native ES module, transformed lazily when the browser asks for it, so startup is 'boot a Node server and answer requests' rather than 'walk the whole graph before serving anything'. Webpack has to build a complete dependency graph up front because CommonJS is inherently non-static — `require` can be conditional and resolved at runtime, so there's no way to know the graph without executing the code. Vite escapes that by pre-bundling dependencies with `optimizeDeps`, converting CJS to ESM once ahead of time so the graph the browser walks is static. HMR is cheap for the same reason: the dev server holds a module graph over websocket, so an edit only re-transforms and re-sends the changed module and its importers, and React Fast Refresh preserves component state when only component modules changed. The trade-off is that dev and prod run different machinery — dev is on-demand ESM with no minification and no tree shaking, prod is a Rollup build — so anything that depends on bundling to behave a certain way, like CJS/ESM interop, `sideEffects` shaking, or dev-only `process.env` branches, can pass locally and break in the build."
---

## 3.8.2 Configuration, Env Vars, Chunking, and Caching
🟡 → 🔴 · **Must-know**

```js
// vite.config.ts
import { defineConfig } from 'vite';
import react from '@vitejs/plugin-react';

export default defineConfig({
  plugins: [react()],
  resolve: { alias: { '@': '/src' } },
  server: { proxy: { '/api': { target: 'http://localhost:4000', changeOrigin: true } } },  // dev: no CORS needed
  build: {
    target: 'es2022',
    sourcemap: true,                       // upload to Sentry, don't serve publicly
    rollupOptions: { output: { manualChunks: { vendor: ['react', 'react-dom'] } } },
  },
});
```
- **Environment variables are inlined at build time.** In Vite, only `VITE_*` vars reach client code (`import.meta.env.VITE_API_URL`). In Next.js, `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`. **Anything in a client bundle is public**, so never put secrets there. Consequence: a client build is tied to its env values, so "build once, deploy many" needs runtime config (fetch `/config.json` at startup, or inject via a server-rendered script).
- **Content-hashed filenames** (`app.3f9a1c.js`) enable immutable caching. **Chunk strategy:** a stable vendor chunk survives app deploys, route chunks load on demand, and shared chunks avoid duplication. Watch for duplicate packages from mismatched versions (`npm ls`, `pnpm why`).
- **Source maps:** `hidden-source-map`/uploading to an error tracker gives readable stack traces without exposing source publicly.
- **Browserslist/targets:** controls transforms and polyfills (`core-js`). Modern targets mean smaller bundles.
- **Tree shaking requirements:** ESM, no side-effectful top-level code in the shaken module, and `"sideEffects": false` (or a list) in `package.json`. Marking CSS imports as side-effects matters.
- **Assets:** small assets inlined as data URIs (threshold), others hashed and emitted. CSS extracted and minified. SVG as components (SVGR) or URLs.
- **Common pitfalls:** huge barrel files, importing a whole library for one function, accidentally bundling server-only code (and secrets) in a client bundle, duplicate React copies (breaks hooks), "works in dev, breaks in prod" caused by CJS/ESM interop or dev-only behavior, and aliases configured for TS but not for the bundler.


**Prerequisites:** 3.8.1, 3.5.6, 3.4.2.

**Why interviewers ask:** "Works in dev, breaks in prod" and stale-assets-after-deploy are the two most common real defects in this area, and both live here. It's also where security shows up unprompted: the moment a secret goes into a `VITE_*` variable it is public, and a candidate who volunteers that before being asked is telling you something.

**Definition:** The configuration layer of a bundler — `vite.config.ts`, `webpack.config.js`, `next.config.js` — together with build-time environment-variable substitution, chunk-splitting strategy, and the content hashes and `Cache-Control` headers that decide how much of your app a returning user has to re-download.
**Questions**
1. How do environment variables work in a frontend build? Why can't they be secrets? How do you deploy one build to staging and prod?
2. What are content hashes and why do they matter?
3. Your production build works differently from dev. Where do you look?
4. How do you stop a server-only module from leaking into the client bundle? *(Framework boundaries like `server-only`/`use server`, separate entry points, `exports` conditions, and lint rules for forbidden imports.)*
5. What does `sideEffects: false` tell the bundler?


**Self-check:** Can you say which files must be `immutable`, which must be `no-cache`, what must happen to the previous release's hashed assets, and how you ship one build to staging and prod without a secret in the bundle?

**Resources:** Vite's "Env Variables and Modes" and "Static Asset Handling" guides; webpack's `DefinePlugin` and `optimization.splitChunks` docs; MDN's HTTP caching header reference; web.dev's "Caching best practices" guide; Rollup's `manualChunks` docs; `@arethetypeswrong/cli` for `exports` shape.

**Mini task:** Make one app's single build deploy to two environments: remove all `VITE_*` usage from client code, add a `config.json` fetched once at boot with a Zod-validated schema, then grep the emitted `dist/` assets to confirm no environment value appears in any of them.

**Mistakes:**
- **Putting a secret in a client bundle** via `import.meta.env`, `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`, or an inlined token — assume it shipped, rotate anything that ever did.
- **Caching the HTML entry for a long TTL,** so a deploy never reaches returning users, or serving new HTML next to deleted old chunks.
- **Deleting the previous release's hashed assets immediately,** 404ing anyone with an open tab, a bfcache entry, or an in-flight request.
- **Configuring an alias in `tsconfig` but not the bundler,** or the reverse, so either the editor or the build disagrees with the other.
- **Splitting too finely,** turning one payload into hundreds of tiny chunks and a request waterfall that costs more than the larger initial download saved.
- **Trusting a dependency's `sideEffects` claim,** which is a promise made by whoever published it and is wrong often enough to silently drop polyfills and break CSS order.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Build-time env vars vs runtime config:** inlining gives a statically analysable bundle with no startup request but ties each artefact to one environment; a `config.json` fetch or server-injected `window.__CONFIG__` gives one artefact for every environment at the cost of a request before boot or a dependency on whatever injects the config.
- **`manualChunks` vendor splitting vs automatic splitting:** a hand-maintained vendor chunk keeps framework code's cache lifetime independent of app deploys, but goes stale and duplicates packages when versions diverge; Vite/Rollup's automatic splitting follows the module graph and dedupes correctly, at the cost of less direct control over cache lifetime.
- **Public source maps vs uploaded `hidden-source-map`:** public maps give anyone readable production stack traces; uploading to Sentry gives you readable traces without publishing source, which is the only defensible default for closed-source apps.
- **Modern `build.target` vs broad targets:** `es2022` keeps the output small because no down-level transforms or `core-js` polyfills are needed, but drops older browsers; the legacy plugin buys that reach back for roughly double the JS.
- **Data-URI inlining vs emitted asset files:** inlining a small icon removes a request and helps LCP, but it can't be cached separately, it grows the JS parse cost, and an inlined background image blocks CSS paint.

**Common wrong answers:** "Env vars are encrypted in production"; "`.env.production` overrides at runtime"; "hashed filenames mean you don't need cache headers"; "`sideEffects: false` makes the bundle smaller" (it's a precondition — you still need ESM and clean top-level code); treating a server-only secret as safe because it wasn't prefixed.

**Probe:** "After yesterday's deploy, about 2% of users get a white screen and everyone else is fine." (Expect: they loaded an HTML document that references hashed chunks that either were never uploaded or were deleted — check the failing request in Network for a 404 on a hashed asset; fix by uploading assets before HTML, giving the HTML entry `no-cache`, and keeping the previous release's assets around for open tabs and bfcache.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Saying "anything in a client bundle is public" without prompting,** and proposing a real runtime-config mechanism for build-once-deploy-many rather than "rebuild per environment".
- **Cache reasoning tied to hashes:** hashed assets get `public, max-age=31536000, immutable`, the HTML entry gets `no-cache` so it revalidates, and the previous release's assets must survive the deploy — all three, or they haven't finished the thought.
- **A debugging *order* for dev/prod divergence** that starts at the artefacts — chunk list, resolved `exports` conditions, minification, `import.meta.env` — and uses `vite preview`, rather than "clear the cache and reinstall".
- **Understanding tree shaking as a contract** (ESM plus `sideEffects` plus side-effect-free module top level), including that CSS needs an explicit carve-out.

**Model answer, Q1:** "In a frontend build, env vars are not runtime lookups — the bundler does a literal text substitution at build time and the string ends up inside the shipped JavaScript. Vite only exposes prefixed vars through `import.meta.env`, so `VITE_API_URL` reaches client code; Next.js does the same for `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`; webpack does it with `DefinePlugin` against whatever keys you map. So the value is public the instant it ships — anyone can read the bundle off the network tab — and that's why no API key, signing secret, or internal-only hostname ever goes in one. For build-once-deploy-many I don't use build-time vars at all: I ship one artefact and fetch `/config.json` on boot, or have the server inject a `window.__CONFIG__` script tag at container start, and the app reads a typed config object with a Zod parse and defaults. If the app genuinely can't wait on that request, server-side injection is the fallback. The rule I hold to: prefixed vars for things that are safe to publish (API base URL, feature flag name, Sentry DSN), everything else stays on the server."
---

## 3.8.3 Monorepos: Workspaces, Turborepo, Nx
🔴 Advanced · **Good-to-know** (increasingly common at full stack companies)

- **Why:** share types, schemas, UI components, and config between web, API, and mobile. Atomic cross-package changes, one CI config, consistent tooling. **Costs:** tooling complexity, CI time without caching, and permissions/ownership.
- **Layers**
  1. **Package manager workspaces** (pnpm, npm, yarn): link local packages (`"@acme/ui": "workspace:*"`). pnpm's strict `node_modules` avoids phantom dependencies.
  2. **Task orchestrators:** **Turborepo** and **Nx** understand the package graph, run tasks in dependency order and in parallel, **cache** outputs by input hash (locally and remotely, so CI and teammates skip repeated work), and run only **affected** packages on a PR. Nx adds generators, a project graph, and module boundary lint rules.
  3. **TypeScript project references** or path/`exports` mapping to share types and build incrementally.
  4. **Versioning and publishing:** Changesets or semantic-release for libraries, or a single version for apps.
- **Typical layout**
```
apps/web (Next.js)      apps/api (Node)      apps/admin
packages/ui (components) packages/schemas (Zod, shared types) packages/config (eslint, tsconfig)
```
- **Internal packages:** either **build** them (compile to `dist`, with `exports`) or consume **source directly** (the app's bundler transpiles them, which is faster to iterate on but requires the bundler config to transpile workspace packages).
- **Pitfalls:** circular package dependencies, hoisting issues and phantom deps, mismatched dependency versions (use `syncpack`/catalogs), cache misses from undeclared inputs (env vars, files not listed in the task config), and giant CI jobs when everything depends on a core package.
- **Polyrepo vs monorepo:** polyrepo gives hard isolation and independent release cadence, but cross-repo changes and shared code are painful. Choose based on team size, coupling, and tooling maturity.


**Prerequisites:** 3.8.1, 3.4.2, 3.4.7.

**Why interviewers ask:** Full-stack roles increasingly live in monorepos, so this reveals whether you understand the consequences your code layout has on a build graph: which package depends on which, what goes into a cache key, and why "just move it into `packages/`" breaks CI in ways a polyrepo never would. It's also a test of whether you can advocate for a monorepo without romanticising it.

**Definition:** One repository holding multiple deployable apps and shared libraries, wired together by a package manager's workspace protocol (`"@acme/ui": "workspace:*"`) and driven by a task orchestrator — Turborepo or Nx — that reads the package dependency graph to order and parallelise tasks, cache outputs by input hash, and run only the packages a change affects.
**Questions**
1. Why use a monorepo? What problems does it introduce?
2. What does Turborepo/Nx caching do? What decides a cache hit? *(A hash of inputs: source files, dependencies, env vars, and task config.)*
3. How do you share TypeScript types and validation schemas between frontend and backend? 
4. What are phantom dependencies and how does pnpm prevent them?
5. How would you make CI fast in a large monorepo? *(affected-only runs, remote cache, parallelism, split jobs, caching installs)*


**Self-check:** Can you name what goes into a Turborepo cache key, what makes a task "affected", and the exact mechanism by which hoisting produces a phantom dependency — plus which tool makes that failure loud instead of silent?

**Resources:** Turborepo's "Core Concepts", "Remote Caching", and `turbo.json` reference pages; Nx's "What is Nx", `@nx/enforce-module-boundaries`, and generator docs; pnpm's "Workspaces" and "Why pnpm" pages plus `pnpm why`; Node's `package.json#exports` and `#imports` docs; Changesets' documentation.

**Mini task:** Stand up a pnpm workspace with `apps/web`, `apps/api`, and `packages/schemas`, add Turborepo and run `turbo build --graph`, then change a schema and confirm from the output that only the dependent apps rebuild and that a second identical run is a cache hit. Break it deliberately with an undeclared import and watch resolution fail.

**Mistakes:**
- **Undeclared inputs to a cached task** — an env var, a fetched schema, a file glob the task config doesn't list — producing a cache hit on stale output; list every input in `turbo.json` and pass env vars through explicitly.
- **Importing across package boundaries by relative path** (`../../packages/ui/src/...`) instead of the package name, which silently deletes the dependency edge so `affected` comes back empty and stale builds ship.
- **Accepting the build-everything default,** so one change to a core package rebuilds every app and CI takes 40 minutes; run affected-only on PRs and full builds on main.
- **One giant `packages/core` that everything imports,** so every leaf change invalidates every cache entry and no PR can use affected-only mode.
- **Circular package dependencies,** which no task graph can order; the shared piece has to move into a third package.
- **Skipping `--frozen-lockfile` in CI** (or using `npm install` in a pnpm repo), so a clean runner resolves different versions than the ones you tested.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Turborepo vs Nx:** Turborepo stays minimal — it reads your existing workspaces and `package.json` scripts, hashes inputs, caches remotely, and computes affected from the graph. Nx costs more setup (plugins, `project.json`, generators) but buys an explicit project graph, generators, and enforceable module-boundary rules. Pick Turborepo for a workspace you already understand; pick Nx when you want the graph to police architecture.
- **Consume internal packages as source vs build them:** pointing `exports` at `src` removes a build step and makes cross-package HMR instant, but requires every consumer's bundler to transpile the package and can leak dev-only code into production. Building to `dist` is the shippable option, paid for with a rebuild per change unless watch mode or `tsc -b -w` covers it.
- **Monorepo vs polyrepo:** atomic cross-cutting changes and shared config on one side; hard isolation, independent release cadence, and per-repo access control on the other. If the build got slow, fix the graph and caching before splitting the repo.
- **pnpm workspaces vs npm/Yarn workspaces:** pnpm's strict per-package `node_modules` surfaces bad packaging early and installs faster from a shared store; npm and Yarn PnP have broader tooling compatibility. Compatibility with a badly packaged dependency is the deciding factor.
- **Fixed versions vs Changesets:** pinning apps to one repo version removes version churn and makes deploys atomic; Changesets gives independent automated releases with changelogs and generated bumps, at the cost of release PRs and a compatibility matrix to maintain.

**Common wrong answers:** "Monorepos are faster because they avoid rebuilds" with no mention of what the cache key includes; "pnpm is fast because of its content-addressable store" as the whole answer to phantom dependencies; "just put the shared code in one folder and import it" (coupling with no package boundary, no dependency edge, nothing to rebuild); "Nx or Turborepo replaces the package manager" (they sit on top of workspaces).

**Probe:** "A commit passes on your machine but fails on a clean CI runner with `Cannot find module 'zod'`." (Expect: a phantom dependency — reproduce with a frozen-lockfile install in a fresh container, confirm with `pnpm why zod` that the importing package doesn't declare it, add it there, and add `import/no-extraneous-dependencies` so it can't come back.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Trade-shaped reasoning, not advocacy** — what atomic cross-package changes and shared config buy, and what they cost in CI time, dependency hygiene, version coupling, and access control.
- **That the orchestrator works off a declared graph,** and that a cache hit is a hash of inputs (source files, dependency versions, env vars, task config) — so an undeclared input is at best a cache miss and at worst a stale artefact.
