- **Hoisting as the mechanism behind phantom dependencies,** and pnpm's strict layout as the reason they fail loudly instead of silently.
- **A concrete CI plan:** affected-only runs, remote caching, cached install with a frozen lockfile, parallelism, and separate build and test jobs.

**Model answer, Q1:** "The payoff is atomicity plus one source of truth. When an API's Zod schema, the derived TypeScript types, and a UI package all change together, one commit and one PR carry the change end to end. In a polyrepo that's coordinated PRs across repositories, temporary version bumps, and release-ordering discipline, and the two halves are briefly incompatible in production. You also get a single CI config, one toolchain version, one lint rule set, and one place to put shared TypeScript, ESLint, and test config. What it costs is real: every package shares a dependency graph, so a version bump in one can break all consumers; CI time grows with graph size unless you cache and run affected-only; access control gets harder when different teams own different packages in one repo, since the default is that everyone can read everything; and a graph that's declared dishonestly turns into phantom dependencies and wrong cache hits. So the trigger for a monorepo is genuine coupling — shared types, shared runtime config, coordinated deploys — not team size. When coupling is low or teams need independent release cadences, a polyrepo is the better answer."
---

## 3.8.4 Building Libraries
🔴 Advanced · **Good-to-know**

- **Dual publish** ESM + CJS via `exports`: `{ ".": { "types": "./dist/index.d.ts", "import": "./dist/index.mjs", "require": "./dist/index.cjs" } }`. Put `types` first. Generate `.d.ts` (tsup `--dts`).
- **Externalize** peer dependencies (React) so they aren't bundled. Set `"sideEffects": false`. Preserve modules for better tree shaking.
- **Check packages** with `publint` and `arethetypeswrong`, and test the packed tarball (`npm pack`) in a sample app.

**Mini tasks**
1. Create a Turborepo or Nx workspace with `apps/web`, `apps/api`, and `packages/schemas`. Share a Zod schema and show the type error propagating to both apps when it changes.
2. Run the bundle analyzer on a Vite app, cut it by at least 30%, and document each change.
3. Configure a Vite app so one build deploys to staging and prod using runtime config.


**Resources:** Node's `package.json#exports` and `#imports` docs plus its ESM/CJS interoperability reference; TypeScript's "Packages" docs on `node16`/`nodenext` resolution and declaration emit; `tsup`, `tsdown`, and `unbuild` documentation; `publint` and `@arethetypeswrong/cli`; `arethetypeswrong.io`'s write-ups on the dual-package hazard; webpack and Rollup docs on `sideEffects` semantics.

**Mistakes:**
- **Inlining a peer dependency** (a `noExternal: ['react']`, or simply forgetting `external`) and shipping a second React to every consumer.
- **Ordering `types` after `import`/`require` in `exports`,** so `arethetypeswrong` reports "No types" and strict-mode consumers get implicit `any`.
- **Forgetting `./package.json` in `exports`,** which breaks bundlers, test runners, and anything that reads package metadata with an "unsupported" resolution error.
- **Ignoring the dual-package hazard** — ESM and CJS entries both loaded in one app, duplicating module state so singletons, `instanceof`, and pub/sub stores silently break.
- **No `files` field,** so `npm pack` ships your tests, fixtures, and source maps (potentially including source, via `.map` files with inline sources), or ships nothing usable because `dist` is gitignored.
- **Testing only against the workspace copy,** where aliases and transpilation are already configured, so the published tarball has never been installed anywhere.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Dual ESM + CJS publish vs ESM-only:** dual is universally compatible and sidesteps the dual-package hazard for most consumers; ESM-only halves the output and is right for modern bundlers and Node 18+, but breaks CJS consumers and older tooling. If you go ESM-only, declare only `import` in `exports` so a CJS consumer gets a clear error instead of a silent fallback.
- **`tsup`/`tsdown`/`unbuild` vs raw Rollup plus `tsc`:** the batteries-included tools get dual output and `.d.ts` generation from one config in minutes; raw Rollup with `tsc --emitDeclarationOnly` gives exact control over what's emitted, at the cost of a config you own forever.
- **Shipping `dist` vs shipping `src`:** `dist` is pre-transpiled to your target, needs nothing from the consumer, and is what a CDN like esm.sh can serve; `src` hands transpilation to the consumer's bundler, giving a smaller install and better tree shaking, but it requires their config to handle TypeScript, JSX, and path aliases and breaks in Node without a loader.
- **Blanket `external` vs selective `noExternal`:** externalising everything is simple and guarantees no duplicates, but pushes every dependency onto the consumer's resolver, which older bundlers sometimes can't do. A `noExternal` list lets you inline a CJS-only dependency and hand-write its `exports` shim, at the cost of guaranteeing exactly one instance of it.
- **Lockstep versions vs Changesets:** one version for the whole repo makes changes atomic and removes upgrade coordination; Changesets gives independent releases with generated changelogs, at the cost of release PRs and a compatibility matrix.

**Common wrong answers:** "Just add `main` and `module`"; "put React in `dependencies` so users get it automatically" (guarantees the duplicate-React bug); "if the build passes locally it's fine to publish"; shipping `src` and forgetting the consumer has to transpile it; `types` after `import` in `exports`.

**Probe:** "A consumer reports 'Invalid hook call' with your component library, but it works for every other user." (Expect: don't chase React versions first — run `npm ls react` for duplicates, then check whether your build inlined React instead of externalising it, whether their bundler pulled your package twice via both `main` and `exports` (the dual-package hazard duplicating React's dispatcher), and whether their project has two React copies from a transitive dep.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **A conditional `exports` map with `types` first,** and the reason — TypeScript matches conditions in declaration order and reads the wrong entry otherwise. Bonus for knowing `./package.json` needs exposing.
- **Correct dependency classification,** especially React as a peer *and* `external` in the build config, tied to the specific failure it causes.
- **That verification happens against the packed tarball in a real consumer,** with resolver modes named — `node16`/`nodenext` are where library bugs actually live.
- **Awareness of the dual-package hazard:** if ESM and CJS copies of your package both load in one app, module-level state is duplicated, so singletons, `instanceof`, and subscription stores break.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Two entry points plus a conditional `exports` map. I'd build with `tsup`, `tsdown`, `unbuild`, or Rollup to emit an ESM file (`.mjs`) and a CJS file (`.cjs`), generate declarations for both (`tsup --dts`), and point the map at them: `{ \"exports\": { \".\": { \"types\": \"./dist/index.d.ts\", \"import\": \"./dist/index.mjs\", \"require\": \"./dist/index.cjs\" }, \"./package.json\": \"./package.json\" } }`. `types` goes first because conditions are matched in declaration order and TypeScript honours that — if `import` comes first it matches, the `.d.ts` is never read, and consumers get implicit `any` under `strict`. I also expose `./package.json` explicitly, because `exports` turns the package into a black box and bundlers, test runners, and tooling all read it. And if the source is ESM I set `\"type\": \"module\"` so Node treats `.js` as ESM, with explicit relative extensions in the emitted imports."

**Questions**
1. How do you publish a library that works for both ESM and CommonJS consumers?
2. What belongs in a modern `package.json`, and why does `types` come first in `exports`?
3. `dependencies` vs `peerDependencies` vs `devDependencies` — where does React go?
4. How do you verify a published package is actually consumable?
5. How do you ship a breaking-ish change to a shared internal library without breaking every consumer?
6. When would you publish `src` instead of `dist`?

**Prerequisites:** 3.8.1, 3.8.2, 3.3.11.

**Why interviewers ask:** Library bugs are versioned and sticky — a wrong `exports` map or a missing `sideEffects` field ships to every consumer and can't be fixed by redeploying your app, only by a patch release and a reinstall. So this is a test of whether you think about the consumer's bundler and runtime, not just whether your own build goes green.

**Definition:** Producing an artefact other people can install: source compiled to both ESM and CommonJS behind a conditional `exports` map, declaration files emitted alongside, peer dependencies left external, and the result verified by packing the tarball and consuming it as a real dependency.
**Self-check:** Can you explain why Vite starts fast, how env vars end up in a bundle, and what makes a build cache hit, each in 60 seconds?

---

# 3.9 PWAs AND WEB APIs

## 3.9.1 Progressive Web Apps and Service Workers
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know (concepts)** / **Good-to-know (implementation)**

**Definition:** Web apps enhanced with a manifest, service worker, and HTTPS so they're installable, resilient to bad networks, and can re-engage users.

**Web App Manifest:** JSON linked via `<link rel="manifest">` with `name`, `short_name`, `icons` (192 and 512, with maskable), `start_url`, `scope`, `display` (`standalone`), `theme_color`, `background_color`, and optionally `shortcuts`, `screenshots`. Installability criteria vary by browser (HTTPS and a valid manifest are the core). Safari/iOS has its own quirks and storage limits.

**Service worker (SW):** a script that runs in a separate thread, **intercepts network requests** for its scope, and works with the **Cache API**. It requires **HTTPS**, has no DOM access, is event-driven, and can be terminated when idle (don't keep state in globals).

**Lifecycle**
```
register → install (precache) → waiting (if an old SW controls pages) → activate (clean old caches) → controlling clients → fetch/message/push events
```
- A new SW installs in the background but **waits** until all tabs using the old one close (or `skipWaiting()` is called). `clients.claim()` takes control of open pages immediately. **Updates:** the browser byte-compares the SW script (checked on navigation and about every 24h) and bypasses HTTP caching for it (so don't cache `sw.js` aggressively).
- **`event.waitUntil(promise)`** keeps the SW alive for async work. `event.respondWith(promise)` answers a fetch.

**Caching strategies**

| Strategy | Behavior | Use for |
|---|---|---|
| **Cache-first** | Cache, falling back to network | Versioned/hashed static assets, fonts |
| **Network-first** | Network, falling back to cache | HTML, frequently changing API data |
| **Stale-while-revalidate** | Serve cache now, update in the background | Avatars, non-critical API data |
| **Network-only / cache-only** | Self-explanatory | Payments / precached shell |

```js
// sw.js
const CACHE = 'app-v3';
const PRECACHE = ['/', '/offline.html', '/app.css', '/app.js'];

self.addEventListener('install', e => {
  e.waitUntil(caches.open(CACHE).then(c => c.addAll(PRECACHE)));
});
self.addEventListener('activate', e => {
  e.waitUntil(
    caches.keys()
      .then(keys => Promise.all(keys.filter(k => k !== CACHE).map(k => caches.delete(k))))
      .then(() => self.clients.claim()));
});
self.addEventListener('fetch', e => {
  const { request } = e;
  if (request.method !== 'GET') return;                       // never cache mutations
  if (request.mode === 'navigate') {                          // network-first with offline fallback
    e.respondWith(fetch(request).catch(() => caches.match('/offline.html')));
    return;
  }
  e.respondWith(caches.match(request).then(cached => {        // stale-while-revalidate
    const network = fetch(request)
      .then(res => { if (res.ok) { const copy = res.clone(); caches.open(CACHE).then(c => c.put(request, copy)); } return res; })
      .catch(() => cached);
    e.waitUntil(network);
    return cached || network;
  }));
});
```
```js
// registration (in app code)
if ('serviceWorker' in navigator) {
  navigator.serviceWorker.register('/sw.js').then(reg => {
    reg.addEventListener('updatefound', () => { /* show "New version available. Reload" UI */ });
  });
}
```
- **Use Workbox** (precaching with revisioned manifests, routing, strategies, expiration plugins) or `vite-plugin-pwa` instead of hand-rolling for production.
- **Capabilities:** offline support, **Background Sync** (Chromium only), **Web Push** (VAPID keys, user permission, subscription stored on your server, supported on Safari/iOS for installed web apps from 16.4), periodic sync (limited), and app badging.
- **Offline data:** IndexedDB for app data with a sync queue, conflict resolution strategy (last write wins, merge, CRDTs) for offline-first apps.
- **Pitfalls**
  - **Stale forever:** caching `index.html` cache-first traps users on old code. Always include a versioned cache and an update flow.
  - Caching authenticated/personalized responses in shared caches, caching opaque or error responses, and not cloning a response before using it twice (body streams are single-use).
  - SW scope is limited to its path (serve from the root for full scope).
  - Debug with DevTools → Application → Service Workers ("Update on reload", "Bypass for network", offline checkbox) and *Clear site data*.
  - **Kill switch:** keep a way to unregister a broken SW (ship a replacement SW that clears caches and unregisters).
  - Push needs careful permission UX (ask after a user action with context, not on page load).
- **When PWAs make sense:** content apps, internal tools, emerging markets with poor connectivity, and cost-constrained cross-platform needs. **Limitations:** weaker iOS capabilities historically, discoverability, and hardware access.


**Prerequisites:** 3.5.4, 3.5.5, 3.5.6

**Why interviewers ask:** A service worker is the sharpest test of whether you understand that the browser is a network plus a cache plus a process model, not just a DOM — the worker runs on its own thread, gets terminated when idle, has no DOM, and answers every request in its scope. Nearly every strong candidate has shipped one and been burned by it, so they ask about the update path and the cache-busting discipline, because that is where the real bugs live. It also opens into product questions (install, offline, push) that separate "I can build it" from "I can operate it".
**Questions**
1. What makes an app a PWA? What does a service worker do?
2. Explain the service worker lifecycle. Why might users not get your new version? *(waiting state, cache-first HTML, a cached `sw.js`)*
3. Which caching strategy for HTML? For hashed JS? For an API list? 
4. How does a service worker update?
5. How does push notification flow work end to end? *(Subscribe via `PushManager` with a VAPID public key → send the subscription to your server → the server sends via the push service → the SW's `push` event shows the notification.)*
6. Why must you `clone()` a response before caching it?
7. How would you build offline support for a notes app? *(App shell precache, IndexedDB for data, a mutation queue with background sync or retry, and a conflict strategy.)*
8. What are the risks of service workers? *(Stale content, hard-to-debug behavior, and security: an XSS plus a SW can persist an attacker's control, so keep scope tight.)*


**Mistakes:**
- **Serving `sw.js` with a long `max-age`:** the update byte-compare is throttled by your own caching, so users never even discover the new worker.
- **Reusing one cache name forever:** stale entries pile up until the origin hits its quota; version the cache and delete everything non-current in `activate`.
- **Reusing an already-consumed `Response`:** caching after `res.json()`, or after passing the response to the page, throws — `clone()` before both reads.
- **Keeping state in SW globals:** the worker is killed when idle, so anything you need must live in Cache Storage or IndexedDB.
- **Shipping no kill switch:** a worker that throws on every `fetch` keeps serving stale content or nothing at all until you push a replacement that clears caches and calls `self.registration.unregister()`.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Hand-rolled worker vs Workbox / `vite-plugin-pwa`:** hand-rolling teaches you the lifecycle and is fine for a single app shell; Workbox gives revisioned precache manifests, routing, expiration, and quota handling you will otherwise get wrong. Ship Workbox.
- **Precache the shell vs runtime-cache everything:** precaching is deterministic and works on the very first offline visit but fails the whole install if a single URL 404s (`addAll` is atomic) and it inflates the install; runtime caching is resilient but the first offline load of a route you never visited fails.
- **Network-first vs stale-while-revalidate for API data:** network-first is always fresh when online but adds latency and burns data; SWR is instant but can show stale values — fine for avatars and presence, wrong for prices and permissions.
- **`skipWaiting()` on deploy vs a user-prompted update:** automatic `skipWaiting()` swaps code under a live session and can break in-flight component state; a "new version available — reload" banner driven by `updatefound`/`controllerchange` is safer, but many users never click it.
- **Offline-first (IndexedDB as the source of truth) vs online-first with an offline fallback:** offline-first gives real offline UX but obliges you to build a mutation queue and a conflict-resolution policy; the fallback approach is a fraction of the work and covers "tunnel, not airplane".

**Common wrong answers:** "A service worker makes the app work offline, that's it" — no mention of scope, versioning, or the update flow. Or "the SW caches everything", which loses the interview outright, because caching mutations and personalised responses is the classic way to ship data leaks and ghost state.

**Probe:** "Your users are stuck on the previous deploy — the new worker installed but nothing changed. Walk me through why." (Expect: the new worker is in `waiting` while old tabs are open; or the HTML is being served from cache; or `sw.js` was HTTP-cached despite the spec's bypass; or two deploys reused the same cache name so old entries were never deleted.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **The three installability pillars and the scope/thread model,** not just "it lets me work offline".
- **Cache hygiene:** versioned cache names, purging non-matching keys in `activate`, precaching with revisions, and never caching non-`GET`, error, or opaque responses.
- **The update story:** `waiting` versus `skipWaiting()` and `clients.claim()`, plus why a cache-first `index.html` strands users on old code.

**Model answer, Q1:** "A PWA is a normal web app with three specific things: a valid web app manifest linked from the page, a registered service worker, and HTTPS. The service worker is a separate JS thread that intercepts same-origin requests in its scope and decides whether each one comes from the network or from Cache Storage — that's what buys offline and installability."
**Mini task:** make a small app installable and offline-capable with Workbox or by hand. Show an "offline" banner, an update-available prompt, and prove old-cache cleanup works across two deploys.


**Self-check:** "Explain, without notes, the exact sequence between a deploy and a user actually running the new code — including where `skipWaiting`, `clients.claim()`, and `controllerchange` each fit."

**Resources:** MDN's Service Worker API guide and the `ExtendableEvent` / `respondWith()` references; the Service Worker spec's "Service Worker Global Scope" section for lifecycle and termination; web.dev's "Learn PWA" and "Service worker caching and HTTP caching"; the Workbox docs, specifically `workbox-window`'s `messageSkipWaiting` recipe.
---

## 3.9.2 Fetch, Streaming, and Cancellation
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

```js
async function api(path, { method = 'GET', body, signal } = {}) {
  const res = await fetch(`/api${path}`, {
    method,
    headers: body ? { 'Content-Type': 'application/json' } : undefined,
    body: body ? JSON.stringify(body) : undefined,
    credentials: 'same-origin',
    signal: signal ?? AbortSignal.timeout(10_000),
  });
  if (!res.ok) {
    const err = new Error(`HTTP ${res.status}`);
    err.status = res.status; err.body = await res.text().catch(() => '');
    throw err;                               // fetch only rejects on network failure
  }
  return res.status === 204 ? null : res.json();
}
```
- **`fetch` resolves for 404/500**, and rejects only on network errors, CORS blocks, or aborts. Always check `res.ok`.
- **Body is a single-use stream**: read once (`json()`, `text()`, `blob()`), or `clone()`.
- **Streaming responses:** `res.body.getReader()` for incremental reads, or `for await (const chunk of res.body)` where supported. This enables streaming LLM output (SSE or chunked), large downloads with progress, and NDJSON.
- **Upload progress:** `fetch` has no progress events (request streaming has limited support), so use XHR `upload.onprogress` or chunked uploads with presigned URLs (Phase 5).
- **Cancellation and races:** `AbortController` per request, abort on unmount/new query, and ignore `AbortError`.
- **Headers/credentials:** `credentials: 'include'` for cross-origin cookies (needs CORS support). **Retries:** only for idempotent requests or with idempotency keys.
- **Request deduping and caching** are best delegated to TanStack Query/SWR (Phase 4).
- **`keepalive: true`** and `sendBeacon` allow requests to survive page unload.


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.7, 3.3.8, 3.5.4

**Why interviewers ask:** Nearly every React data bug they see in the wild traces to one of three things — forgetting that `fetch` resolves on a 404, letting superseded requests race to `setState`, or buffering a streaming response because nobody knew `res.body` was a stream. They ask this to see whether your data layer is defensive by default or patched after the race conditions ship.

**Definition:** The `fetch`-based data layer: request/response semantics that deliberately differ from XHR, `AbortController`-driven cancellation and timeouts, and incremental consumption of a response body as a `ReadableStream`.


**Self-check:** "Trace what the browser does between calling `fetch()` and your first `await` — when does it reject, when does it hand you a 500, and what happens if you try to read the body twice?"

**Resources:** MDN's `fetch()` reference and its guide to using `fetch` with streams; the Fetch Standard's "Body mixin" section for single-use semantics; MDN's `AbortController` and `AbortSignal.timeout` pages; the WHATWG Streams spec for `getReader()` and `TextDecoder`'s streaming mode.

**Mini task:** Write a 40-line `api()` helper that checks `res.ok`, attaches `AbortSignal.timeout(10_000)`, returns typed JSON (or `null` for 204), and throws an `ApiError` carrying `status` and body; then build a typeahead that consumes `res.body` with `getReader()` and a streaming `TextDecoder`, rendering tokens incrementally — and prove in the Network panel that superseded requests show as cancelled, not as five 200s.

**Mistakes:**
- **Leaving requests in flight after unmount:** setState-after-unmount warnings, wasted bandwidth, and stale flashes on the next mount.
- **Reading a body twice:** calling `await res.json()` twice, or `json()` then `text()`, throws `TypeError: body used already`.
- **Naive chunk splitting:** `chunk.split('\n')` on independently decoded chunks breaks UTF-8 at the seam; keep one streaming decoder and buffer the trailing partial line.
- **Over-sharing credentials:** `credentials: 'include'` cross-origin fails without `Access-Control-Allow-Origin` plus `Allow-Credentials`, and a wildcard origin is invalid alongside credentials.
- **Treating `keepalive` as an unload-safe channel:** it is capped at roughly 64KB; `navigator.sendBeacon` is the right tool for exit analytics and last-chance beacons.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`fetch` vs XHR:** `fetch` gives promises, `ReadableStream` bodies, `AbortSignal`, and `keepalive`; XHR is still the only way to get real upload `progress` events, and it's the only thing some corporate proxies tolerate. Reach for XHR specifically for upload progress.
- **A hand-rolled wrapper vs TanStack Query / SWR:** the wrapper is ~30 lines and forces you to understand every semantic; the library gives deduping, retry with backoff, stale-while-revalidate, and garbage collection for free. Build the wrapper once so you know what the library is doing for you.
- **Stream the body vs `res.json()`:** streaming enables token-by-token LLM output, NDJSON, and progress on a multi-gigabyte download; buffering is simpler and correct for small JSON — but once you stream, you cannot call `json()` afterwards and you own partial-UTF-8 reassembly.
- **Automatic retry vs failing loudly:** blind retries multiply load on an already-struggling backend and can double-charge a payment. Retry only idempotent verbs, or anything carrying an idempotency key, with exponential backoff and jitter.

**Common wrong answers:** Timing out with `Promise.race([fetch, sleep])` without noticing the request keeps running and can still resolve; or claiming `fetch` rejects on non-2xx. Both say "I've used it, I've never debugged it".

**Probe:** "Your typeahead fires six requests a second and results arrive out of order — what's wrong, and how do you fix it without a library?" (Expect: a fresh `AbortController` per keystroke that aborts the previous request, `AbortError` caught and dropped, plus a monotonically increasing sequence guard in case the abort lands too late to prevent the resolve.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Checking `res.ok` and mapping status to a typed error,** rather than assuming a rejected promise means "failed".
- **One `AbortController` per request,** wired to unmount and to superseded queries, with `AbortError` swallowed rather than surfaced as a user-facing failure.
- **Genuinely streaming** via `res.body.getReader()` plus one `TextDecoder` constructed with `{ stream: true }`, and awareness that `res.body` can be `null` and that splitting chunks on `"\n"` corrupts multibyte characters.
- **Honesty about `fetch`'s gaps:** no upload progress events, no automatic retry, single-use body.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Because the promise models the *exchange*, not the outcome — a rejection means it never completed: DNS, TCP, TLS, a CORS block that hides the response from script, or an abort. Once the response headers arrive the promise fulfils and the status code is just data you're handed, deliberately, because a 404 from a cache probe, a 304, and a 401 you want to render as a login prompt are all real responses you have to handle. So I check `res.ok` myself — true for 200 to 299 — throw a typed `ApiError` carrying `status` and the body text, and special-case 204 and 205 and `HEAD`, which carry no body, because calling `res.json()` on them throws `SyntaxError: Unexpected end of JSON input` and I want `null` instead."

**Questions**
1. Why doesn't `fetch` reject on a 404?
2. How do you timeout and cancel a fetch?
3. How do you show download or upload progress?
4. How do you consume a streaming response (e.g., from an LLM API)?
---

## 3.9.3 WebSockets, Server-Sent Events, and Alternatives
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** (full stack real-time, depth in Phase 5)

| | **WebSocket** | **SSE (EventSource)** | **Long polling** | **Short polling** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Direction | Full duplex | Server → client only | Simulated push | Client pulls |
| Transport | Upgraded TCP (`ws://`/`wss://`) | Plain HTTP stream (`text/event-stream`) | HTTP | HTTP |
| Reconnect | Manual | **Automatic** (with `Last-Event-ID` resume) | Manual | N/A |
| Proxies/firewalls | Occasionally problematic | Works anywhere HTTP does | Works | Works |
| Binary | Yes | Text only | Any | Any |
| Use for | Chat, collaboration, games, trading | Notifications, live feeds, dashboards, LLM token streaming | Legacy fallback | Low-frequency updates |

- SSE over HTTP/1.1 has a **per-domain connection limit** (about 6 across tabs), whereas HTTP/2 multiplexes many streams.
- **Production concerns:** reconnect with exponential backoff and jitter, **application-level heartbeats** (browser WebSocket API can't send protocol pings), authentication (cookies on the upgrade request, or a short-lived ticket, since custom headers aren't supported by the browser API), message ordering and **idempotent** handlers, resync after reconnect (fetch missed state), backpressure (`bufferedAmount`), scaling across servers (sticky sessions or a pub/sub backplane like Redis), and cleanup on unmount.
```js
function connect(url, onMessage) {
  let ws, attempt = 0, closedByUser = false, hb;
  const open = () => {
    ws = new WebSocket(url);
    ws.onopen = () => { attempt = 0; hb = setInterval(() => ws.readyState === 1 && ws.send('{"type":"ping"}'), 25_000); };
    ws.onmessage = (e) => onMessage(JSON.parse(e.data));
    ws.onclose = () => {
      clearInterval(hb);
      if (closedByUser) return;
      const delay = Math.min(30_000, 500 * 2 ** attempt++) * (0.5 + Math.random() / 2);   // backoff + jitter
      setTimeout(open, delay);
    };
  };
  open();
  return {
    send: (d) => ws.readyState === 1 && ws.send(JSON.stringify(d)),
    close: () => { closedByUser = true; ws.close(); },
  };
}
```
```js
// SSE
const es = new EventSource('/events');
es.addEventListener('order', (e) => update(JSON.parse(e.data)));
es.onerror = () => { /* browser auto-reconnects; show a "reconnecting" indicator */ };
```


**Prerequisites:** 3.3.8, 3.5.4, 3.9.2

**Why interviewers ask:** Real-time is where naive frontends fall over, because the protocol is the easy part and reconnection, resync, authentication, and backpressure are where the incidents are. They ask this to see whether you have shipped something that had to survive a flaky mobile network and a rolling deploy, or whether "open a WebSocket" is the end of your answer.

**Definition:** The three transports for server→browser live updates over HTTP — WebSocket's upgraded full-duplex connection, `EventSource`'s `text/event-stream` response, and the polling fallbacks — together with the reliability machinery they all require: authentication, reconnection, resync, and backpressure.
**Questions**
1. WebSocket vs SSE vs long polling? How do you choose?
2. How do you authenticate a WebSocket connection?
3. How do you handle reconnects without losing or duplicating messages? *(sequence numbers or last-event IDs, resync on reconnect, idempotent handlers)*
4. How would you scale WebSockets horizontally? 
5. Why use SSE for streaming LLM responses?


**Self-check:** "Describe exactly what my client does in the ten seconds after a network drop — who initiates the reconnect, how long the backoff is, what happens to messages I missed, and how do I know the socket is alive rather than silently dead?"

**Resources:** MDN's WebSocket API and `EventSource` reference pages; the HTML Living Standard's server-sent events section for `Last-Event-ID` and reconnection timing; RFC 6455 for the opening handshake and framing; web.dev's "Reconnect a WebSocket" pattern.

**Mini task:** Build a 60-line client with reconnect (exponential backoff plus jitter), an app-level ping every 25s and a liveness watchdog that force-closes a silent socket, and a `resync()` that fetches missed state on `onopen`; then verify it by toggling DevTools offline, killing and restarting the server mid-stream, and backgrounding the tab past the heartbeat interval.

**Mistakes:**
- **No `closedByUser` flag in cleanup:** unmounting runs `onclose`, the reconnect loop fires, and you leak a zombie socket for the life of the page.
- **Backoff with no jitter:** every client disconnected by the same tower reconnects on the same schedule and knocks the server over again on each wave.
- **No resync after reconnect:** there's a silent gap in the log, and the UI shows stale state with no error to signal it — the worst kind of bug.
- **Unbounded `send`:** a saturated socket grows `bufferedAmount` until memory blows up or the connection dies; check the queue and drop or coalesce non-critical frames.
- **Non-idempotent handlers:** delivery is at-least-once, so key updates on a server-assigned sequence or message ID and make replay safe.

**Trade-offs:**
- **WebSocket vs SSE:** WebSocket is full duplex and binary-capable but needs an upgrade that some proxies and load balancers break, and you own reconnection; SSE is plain HTTP, reconnects with `Last-Event-ID`, and is text-only, GET-only, one-directional. Default to SSE unless the client must talk.
- **WebSocket vs long polling:** long polling works anywhere, is trivially reproducible with `curl`, and costs one request per cycle; WebSocket is far cheaper per message but needs infra that tolerates long-lived connections and sticky sessions.
- **HTTP/1.1 vs HTTP/2 for streams:** HTTP/2 multiplexes many SSE streams over a single connection; HTTP/1.1 burns a connection per stream, so you're limited to roughly six per origin across tabs. Fan out from one multiplexed connection, or cap your `EventSource` count.
- **Application-level heartbeats vs protocol pings:** a real ping/pong keeps intermediaries from idling the socket out, but the browser can't send ping frames, so you ship an application `{"type":"ping"}` message plus a client-side liveness watchdog to compensate.
- **Hand-rolled client vs a library (`socket.io`, `SockJS`, `MQTT.js`):** a library gives rooms, binary, reconnection, and transport fallbacks for free; the 60-line hand-rolled version has no dependencies and total control. Use the library when you need presence and rooms, hand-roll when you need a plain custom protocol.

**Common wrong answers:** "The browser reconnects automatically" (true for `EventSource`, false for `WebSocket`, which only gives you an `onclose` event and nothing more); or using a WebSocket for a notification feed and then authenticating with a bearer token in the query string.

**Probe:** "Your socket silently stops delivering after a corporate proxy or a Wi-Fi handover, and the UI shows a stale board with no error anywhere. How do you find it?" (Expect: `onclose` never fires so the reconnect logic never runs; the fix is a liveness watchdog — if no heartbeat or message lands within a window, force-close and reconnect yourself — plus `readyState` checks before every send.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Choosing by directionality and infrastructure,** not by fashion: server→client means SSE, bidirectional means WebSocket, and either way there is a fallback.
- **Reliability specifics:** exponential backoff with jitter, an application-level heartbeat (the browser API cannot send protocol pings), `bufferedAmount` backpressure, and a resync fetch on reconnect.
- **Authentication realism:** cookies on the upgrade or a short-lived ticket, and an explicit statement that a header-based bearer token is impossible from the browser constructor.

**Model answer, Q1:** "SSE when the data only flows server to client — it's plain HTTP, it survives proxies, and `EventSource` reconnects for me with `Last-Event-ID`. WebSocket when the client genuinely talks back — chat, presence, collaborative editing, games — because it's full duplex and binary-capable. Long polling only as a legacy fallback for infrastructure that can't hold a stream open."
---

## 3.9.4 Files, Drag-and-Drop, and Binary Data
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

- **Objects:** `File` extends `Blob`. `FileReader` (async, callback-based) or modern `blob.text()`/`arrayBuffer()`/`stream()`. `URL.createObjectURL(blob)` for previews (**call `URL.revokeObjectURL` to avoid leaks**). `blob.slice()` for chunking. `canvas.toBlob` for image export. `<a download>` or `showSaveFilePicker` (File System Access, Chromium) for saving.
- **Drag and drop:** to make a drop zone, you **must `preventDefault()` on `dragover`** (and `drop`), or the browser opens the file. Read `e.dataTransfer.files`. For keyboard and accessibility, always offer a normal `<input type="file">` too (WCAG 2.5.7 requires non-drag alternatives).
```js
zone.addEventListener('dragover', e => e.preventDefault());
zone.addEventListener('drop', e => { e.preventDefault(); handleFiles([...e.dataTransfer.files]); });
```
- **Upload architecture:** small files via `multipart/form-data`. Large files via **presigned URLs direct to object storage** (the app server issues a signed URL, the browser uploads straight to S3, then confirms), **chunked/resumable uploads** (tus, S3 multipart), progress through XHR, concurrency limits, client-side validation (type, size) for UX **plus** server-side validation, antivirus scanning, and never trusting `file.type` or filename.
- **Images client-side:** resize/compress before upload with canvas or `createImageBitmap` (in a worker), strip EXIF for privacy, and preview via object URLs.
- **Binary APIs:** `ArrayBuffer`, typed arrays, `DataView`, `TextEncoder/Decoder`, `btoa/atob` (Latin-1 only, so encode UTF-8 first), `Blob` ↔ base64 data URLs (33% bigger, avoid for large data).


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.4, 3.1.10, 3.5.3

**Why interviewers ask:** Because file handling is where a frontend stops being a frontend and starts being a distributed-systems problem: chunking, per-part retry, progress, concurrency limits, and cleanup are all engineering decisions, and every candidate has shipped an uploader that leaked memory or trusted the client. It's also the cleanest screen for whether you distinguish "the browser told me" from "the server confirmed".

**Definition:** The browser's binary-data and file surface — `File`/`Blob` and their readers, `ArrayBuffer`/typed arrays/`DataView`, the drag-and-drop event model, and the upload architectures (single multipart POST, presigned direct-to-storage, chunked/resumable) that sit on top of them.


**Self-check:** "Trace a 2GB file from the user's disk into object storage in my design — which steps run on the main thread, which in a worker, how does it resume after a dropped connection, and what does the server re-check that the client merely claimed?"

**Resources:** MDN's `File`, `Blob`, and `FileReader` reference pages and its drag-and-drop guide (including `DataTransfer.setDragImage`); the File System Access API spec for `showSaveFilePicker` and `showOpenFilePicker`; the tus resumable-upload protocol spec; the S3 multipart upload reference for part numbering and ETags.

**Mini task:** Build a drop zone with a real `<input type="file">` fallback, pre-checks with human-readable rejection messages, object-URL previews revoked on unmount, and a chunked uploader (1MB parts, three concurrent, per-part retry, live progress, resume after a simulated disconnect) using XHR for upload progress; finish by taking a heap snapshot before and after 50 preview-mount cycles and confirming the object URL count returns to zero.

**Mistakes:**
- **Forgetting `URL.revokeObjectURL`:** every blob stays pinned for the document's lifetime; in a gallery view that's a visible leak on every page navigation.
- **Expecting `dataTransfer.files` during `dragenter`/`dragover`:** it's empty until `drop`; use `dataTransfer.items` for hover feedback and its `kind`/`type` for per-item checks.
- **Trusting `file.type` or the extension:** both are client-supplied metadata — a renamed binary arrives claiming `image/png` — so sniff the magic bytes server-side and scan for malware.
- **Reading binary as text:** `blob.text()` on a JPEG produces mojibake, and `new TextDecoder().decode(buffer)` is only meaningful for text; go through `arrayBuffer()` and typed arrays or `DataView`.
- **Using `btoa()` on non-Latin-1 strings:** it throws above U+00FF; encode UTF-8 with `TextEncoder`, convert to a binary string, then `btoa`.
- **Uploading `multipart/form-data` for huge files from `fetch`:** no progress events, no resumability, and body-size limits will reject it upstream.

**Trade-offs:**
- **`FileReader` vs `blob.text()` / `arrayBuffer()` / `stream()`:** `FileReader` is callback- and event-driven, works everywhere, and is the only clean way to get read `progress`; the promise-based Blob methods compose with `await` and streams and are far less error-prone. Modern code uses Blob methods and drops to `FileReader` only for progress or legacy support.
- **`URL.createObjectURL` vs a base64 data URL:** object URLs are effectively O(1), don't copy the bytes, and are revocable; a data URL inflates the payload by 33%, sits in memory until GC, and breaks on large blobs. Object URLs, always revoked.
- **App-server upload vs presigned direct-to-storage:** posting through the app keeps auth and validation in one place but doubles your egress and pins instances to huge request bodies; presigned PUTs scale and offload the bytes, at the cost of signing, bucket CORS configuration, and a finalise/confirm step whose failure leaves orphaned parts.
- **One request vs chunked/resumable:** a single `multipart/form-data` POST is simplest and perfectly fine under roughly 50MB on decent connections; chunking buys progress, bounded retry, and resume across flaky networks but demands an upload id, ordered part numbers, checksums, and server-side assembly plus a sweeper for abandoned parts.
- **Client-side resize/compress vs upload raw:** canvas or `createImageBitmap` in a worker can cut photo uploads by 70% and beat mobile bandwidth limits, but costs CPU, strips EXIF only if you ask, and never substitutes for server-side validation.

**Common wrong answers:** Building the drop zone without `preventDefault()` on `dragover`, so the browser opens the file and the app dies. Or reading `dataTransfer.files` during `dragenter` to show a preview — it's empty until `drop`. Also "I validate the MIME type client-side, so it's safe", which confuses UX with validation.

**Probe:** "A user drags a 4GB video and your uploader dies at 70% with a spinner and no error. What's the actual failure, and how do you redesign it?" (Expect: no chunking, so one long request hit a proxy or gateway timeout and the whole transfer was lost; move to a chunked or S3 multipart upload with a stable upload id, per-part retry, a concurrency limit, and resumability across reconnects.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Getting the DnD event model right:** `preventDefault()` on `dragover` and `drop` or the browser navigates to the file and the SPA unloads; reading `e.dataTransfer.files`; knowing `items` is available during drag while `files` is not.
- **Modern Blob APIs over legacy ceremony:** `blob.text()`, `blob.arrayBuffer()`, `blob.stream()`, and `createImageBitmap` rather than `FileReader` callbacks — with `FileReader` retained for progress events and legacy paths.
- **Upload architecture judgement:** size thresholds, presigned URLs to move bytes straight to object storage, chunking with a concurrency cap and checksums, and progress via XHR because `fetch` reports no upload progress.
- **Server-trust discipline:** `file.type` and the filename are attacker-controlled, so client checks are UX and the real validation happens server-side.

**Model answer, Q1:** "Four listeners — `dragenter`, `dragover`, `dragleave`, `drop` — and two rules. I `preventDefault()` on both `dragover` and `drop`, and I only read `e.dataTransfer.files` inside `drop`, because that list is empty until the drop fires. For hover styling I keep an enter/leave *counter* rather than a boolean, since `dragleave` fires as the pointer crosses child elements and a naive flag flickers the highlight off; while the drag is in flight I inspect `dataTransfer.items` and its `kind` and `type`, which are populated then. The instant I have the files I pre-check count, size and type for fast, human-readable feedback, then hand off to the chunked uploader with progress and a cancel button. And I always ship a real `<input type=\"file\">` beside the zone — dragging is unusable by keyboard and by screen reader, which is exactly what WCAG 2.5.7's drag-and-drop alternative is about."

**Questions**
1. How do you build a drag-and-drop uploader?
2. Why does the browser open the file if you don't call `preventDefault`?
3. How do you upload a 2GB file reliably?
4. Why use presigned URLs?
5. How do you avoid leaking memory with object URLs?
---

## 3.9.5 Device and Platform APIs
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

**General rules:** most require a **secure context (HTTPS)**, many need **user permission** or a **user gesture**, support varies (check MDN **Baseline**/caniuse and feature-detect), and failure must degrade gracefully.

| API | Notes |
|---|---|
| **Geolocation** | `navigator.geolocation.getCurrentPosition(ok, err, { enableHighAccuracy, timeout, maximumAge })`. Permission prompt, privacy-sensitive, explain *why* before asking, and handle denial |
| **Clipboard** | `navigator.clipboard.writeText()` / `readText()` (async, secure context, user gesture and permission for reads). Fallback `document.execCommand('copy')` is deprecated |
| **Permissions API** | `navigator.permissions.query({ name: 'notifications' })` to check state before prompting |
| **Notifications / Push** | Ask in context, never on load. Use the SW for push (3.9.1) |
| **Web Share** | `navigator.share({ title, text, url })`, mobile-oriented, needs a user gesture |
| **Page Visibility / Beacon** | Pause work when hidden. `sendBeacon` for exit analytics |
| **BroadcastChannel / `storage` event** | Cross-tab messaging |
| **Web Crypto** | `crypto.getRandomValues`, `crypto.randomUUID()`, `crypto.subtle` (hashing, AES-GCM, ECDSA). **Not for password storage** (do that server-side with argon2/bcrypt), and never roll your own crypto |
| **WebAuthn / Passkeys** | Phishing-resistant passwordless auth (Phase 7) |
| **Screen Wake Lock, Fullscreen, Vibration, Battery, Sensors** | Niche, check support (some are Chromium-only or restricted) |
| **WebRTC** | Peer-to-peer audio/video/data: needs signaling, STUN/TURN. Awareness level |
| **WebAssembly / WebGPU** | Near-native compute and GPU access: awareness, and where they fit (Phase 2.6.3) |
| **Payment Request, Credential Management** | Faster checkout and login flows |


**Prerequisites:** 3.5.4, 3.9.1

**Why interviewers ask:** These are the APIs where "it works on my machine" becomes a production incident, and the interesting part is never the API call — it's the permission lifecycle, the denial path, and the cleanup. A candidate with real mobile or cross-browser experience talks about why they asked late, what they do when the user says no, and which APIs don't exist in Safari, unprompted. They also use it to check that you know which of these are web standards and which are still Chromium-only.

**Definition:** The platform-integration surface — geolocation, clipboard, notifications and push, Web Share, wake lock, vibration, sensors, Web Crypto, WebAuthn, WebRTC, Payment Request — plus the cross-cutting rules governing all of them: secure context, transient user activation, permission state, Baseline support, and graceful degradation.


**Self-check:** "Which of these APIs can I call on a `file://` page, over plain HTTP, inside a Web Worker, and with no user gesture — and how do I determine that at runtime instead of guessing?"

**Resources:** MDN's Geolocation API and Permissions API guides; the W3C Permissions spec for `Permissions-Policy` and transient user activation; MDN's "Security and privacy" guidance for Web APIs plus its Baseline pages; RFC 8292 (VAPID) and the Web Push protocol spec.

**Mini task:** Build a complete permission flow for one feature (geolocation or notifications): a card that explains the value, a button that triggers the prompt from inside the click handler, a `navigator.permissions.query()` pre-check that skips the prompt entirely when state is already `denied`, a working fallback (manual ZIP entry, or an in-app inbox), and a runtime report that checks which of the section's APIs exist in your browser and flags any that require a secure context.

**Mistakes:**
- **Prompting without a user gesture:** browsers block or auto-deny, and iOS requires the prompt to be triggered by a tap on iOS 16.4+.
- **Leaving `watchPosition` or sensor streams running:** a live geolocation watch drains battery, keeps the location indicator on, and is a privacy incident; call `clearWatch` in cleanup.
- **Assuming `crypto.randomUUID()` is available everywhere:** it's secure-context-only and absent in older Safari; fall back to `crypto.getRandomValues` for tokens and IDs.
- **Dead-ending on denial:** an app that becomes unusable when a permission is refused is a support ticket and a one-star review; every permission needs an alternative path.
- **User-agent sniffing instead of feature detection:** UA strings lie and age badly; test for the member, not for a browser name.
- **Storing raw coordinates or clipboard contents** in analytics or logs, because these APIs return data that is personal by default.

**Trade-offs:**
- **Native APIs with feature detection vs a compatibility library:** native costs zero bundle and keeps behaviour predictable, but you maintain per-browser branches yourself; a library smooths the edges at the weight of a dependency and a layer of indirection. Native plus targeted fallbacks is the right default; a library only when you support genuinely old browsers.
- **Web Share API vs a custom share sheet:** Web Share gets you the OS-native sheet with the user's real apps and far better mobile ergonomics, but it needs a user gesture and cannot tell you the result; a custom sheet is required for share counters, deep links, and desktop coverage. Feature-detect with `navigator.canShare` before offering the button.
- **Web Push vs in-app realtime:** push reaches users who have closed the tab and costs you no always-on connection, but it needs VAPID keys, a subscription store, and a permission it can lose; in-app delivery is instant and on-brand but only works while the app is open. Push for re-engagement, SSE for in-session.
- **`crypto.subtle` vs server-side crypto:** `crypto.subtle` is fast, constant-time, and keeps plaintext in the page for things like encrypting a local draft before upload; the server is the only correct place for password hashing and key custody. Both, with an explicit boundary you can defend.
- **Asking once vs a progressive, non-blocking prompt:** blocking prompts maximise immediate opt-in but tank trust and get dismissed reflexively; a soft, contextual ask at the moment of value converts better and keeps the app fully usable for everyone who declines.

**Common wrong answers:** Requesting geolocation or notifications on page load; assuming `navigator.clipboard.writeText` works without a secure context and a user gesture; treating `navigator.vibrate`, `getBattery()`, or the Sensor APIs as universally available; using `Math.random()` for session tokens or idempotency keys.

**Probe:** "Your notification opt-in rate fell to 4% after a redesign. What are you changing first?" (Expect: they were prompting on load or burying the ask behind a settings screen; move it behind a direct user action with clear value, check `Notification.permission` first so you never re-prompt a denier, and offer an in-app channel for everyone who declines.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **Permission sequencing:** explain the value in context, trigger from a user gesture, pre-check with `navigator.permissions.query()`, handle denial with a genuinely usable fallback, and never prompt on page load.
- **Secure context and transient activation as hard prerequisites,** plus feature detection (`'clipboard' in navigator`, `navigator.canShare`) and Baseline data instead of guesses.
- **Crypto hygiene:** `crypto.randomUUID()` and `crypto.getRandomValues()` for tokens and IDs, `crypto.subtle` for client-side hashing and AES-GCM, and a clear line that password hashing is server-side with argon2 or bcrypt.
- **Cleanup and privacy discipline:** stop geolocation watches, release wake locks, detach sensor listeners, and stop collecting when the feature is idle.

**Model answer, Q1:** "I never prompt on page load. I ask in context, at the moment the permission pays for itself — the user taps 'find nearby stores' or flips a reminder toggle — and I put the prompt behind that gesture so it has user activation, with a sentence of my own copy saying what I'll do with the data and what they get out of it, because the browser's generic 'Allow this site to use your location?' gives a user nothing to decide on. Before I prompt I run `navigator.permissions.query({ name: 'geolocation' })`, and if the state is already `denied` I never call the API again — I degrade and route them to settings. Every permission gets a genuinely usable fallback, because an app that breaks when refused is a one-star review: manual postcode entry for geolocation, an in-app inbox for notifications, paste-a-link for share. I treat both outcomes the same way — thank them, don't lecture — and on iOS the prompt specifically has to be triggered from inside a tap handler."

**Questions**
1. How do you design a good permission-request flow?
2. What do \"secure context\" and \"transient user activation\" mean?
3. How do you feature-detect and polyfill?
4. How does `crypto.randomUUID` differ from `Math.random()` for IDs? *(CSPRNG vs predictable, so never use `Math.random` for tokens.)*
---

## 3.9.6 Internationalization (i18n) and Localization (l10n)
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know** (**Must** for global products)

- **i18n** is designing so a product *can* support many locales. **l10n** is adapting it for a specific one (translations, formats, imagery).
- **Locale-aware formatting** with `Intl` (3.3.15): dates, numbers, currency, plurals, lists, relative time, collation.
- **Plurals and grammar:** English has two plural forms, but other languages have up to six (Arabic) and gender/case. **Never concatenate strings** (`"You have " + n + " items"`), since word order differs. Use **ICU MessageFormat**: `"{count, plural, one {# item} other {# items}}"`. Libraries: `react-i18next`, **FormatJS/react-intl**, `next-intl`, Lingui.
- **Keys and workflow:** stable semantic keys, extract and manage translations with a TMS (Crowdin, Lokalise), provide context for translators, **pseudo-localization** (accented, longer strings) to catch hardcoded text and layout breakage, and CI checks for missing keys.
- **Layout:** text expands (German and Finnish about 30%+ longer), so don't hardcode widths. Support **RTL** (Arabic, Hebrew) with `dir="rtl"` and CSS logical properties (3.2.7), mirror directional icons (not logos or media controls), and set `lang` per document and per fragment.
- **Locale detection:** user preference (stored) > URL (`/fr/`) > `Accept-Language` header > default. Don't rely on IP geolocation alone. Make switching easy.
- **Routing and SEO:** locale-prefixed paths or domains, `hreflang` alternates, localized metadata, canonical per locale.
- **Dates and time zones:** store UTC, display per user zone (3.3.15). **Currency:** store minor units plus the currency code, format with `Intl.NumberFormat`.
- **Performance:** lazy-load locale bundles per route/locale, and keep translation JSON out of the main bundle.
- **Other pitfalls:** text in images, sorting that assumes ASCII, names (no first/last assumption), address and phone formats, calendars, and number input with decimal commas.


**Prerequisites:** 3.1.6, 3.2.7, 3.3.15

**Why interviewers ask:** For any product outside its home market this is a correctness problem rather than a feature, and it fails in ways that look fine in English: layouts that break at 130% string length, `1.234,50 €` read as a decimal by naive parsing, and dates that silently shift a day across a timezone boundary. A candidate who has shipped a translation loop talks unprompted about escaping, pseudo-localisation, and the cost of giving translators context — none of which you can pick up from a tutorial.

**Definition:** i18n is designing and engineering a product so language, number, date, currency, and layout rules can vary per locale; l10n is the per-locale adaptation itself — translations, formats, plural and gender rules, and culturally appropriate assets.
**Questions**
1. How would you add i18n to an existing React app? 
2. Why can't you build translated sentences by concatenation? What do you use for plurals?
3. How do you support RTL? 
4. How do you choose a user's locale?
5. How would you test that nothing is hardcoded in English? *(pseudo-localization, lint rules, and missing-key CI checks)*


**Mistakes:**
- **Persisting formatted strings:** storing `"$1,234.50"` or `"5 Jan 2026"` means you can never re-format for the next locale, the user's time zone, or a currency change — always store the primitive and format at render time.
- **Concatenating sentences from fragments:** even where it reads acceptably in English, it breaks in Japanese and Arabic, and building an `aria-label` by gluing raw user input is an injection-adjacent accessibility bug. Compose whole messages.
- **Hardcoding widths for text:** fixed `width: 200px` buttons, single-line headers, and `text-overflow: ellipsis` all break in German and Thai; size in `rem`/`ch`, allow wrapping, and test at +50% string length.
- **Sorting with `<` or a bare `localeCompare`:** ASCII ordering puts `Zebra` before `apple` and mishandles accents; use `Intl.Collator` with the right `sensitivity` and `{ numeric: true }` for version-like strings.
- **Omitting `dir` and `lang`:** an RTL locale without `dir=\"rtl\"`, or without per-fragment `lang`, breaks native control direction and screen-reader pronunciation.
- **Editing English copy in code to make it fit,** instead of giving translators context and fixing the layout.
- **Number inputs that only accept `.`:** European locales use decimal commas, so validate with a locale-aware parser and normalise before sending to the API.

**Trade-offs:**
- **ICU MessageFormat vs a library's richer DSL:** ICU is the lingua franca — every TMS understands it and it handles plurals, gender, and `select` — but it isn't type-safe by default. Libraries like FormatJS give typed keys and better DX at the cost of a runtime and functions a translator must be taught to read.
- **Semantic keys vs English text as keys:** semantic keys like `cart.itemCount` survive copy edits and let several locales share one key; English-as-key is quicker to start and breaks the instant anyone edits the source string, while scattering user-facing copy through your codebase. Semantic keys, with a lint rule to enforce them.
- **Locale-prefixed URLs vs per-locale subdomains:** `/fr/orders` is one codebase, one deploy, trivial cache-busting and clear `hreflang`; `fr.example.com` isolates failures and geo-routes at the edge but multiplies deployments, certificates, and DNS. Prefer prefixes unless you truly need edge isolation.
- **Pseudo-localisation in dev vs a full locale in CI:** pseudo-locale catches hardcoded strings and layout breakage in seconds on every machine but tells you nothing about real translation quality; a real locale in CI catches genuine quality and overflow but is slower. Run both — pseudo-locale on save, one real locale in CI.
- **Bundling every locale vs lazy-loading per locale:** shipping all locales is simpler and avoids any flash, but bloats the main bundle; dynamic import keeps it lean but needs the correct locale resolved server-side or the user sees the wrong language first.

**Common wrong answers:** "Pass the locale to `toLocaleString()` and ship translations as JSON" — that covers numbers and nothing else, missing plural categories, RTL, and key hygiene. Worse: forking the codebase per language, or concatenating strings because it works in English.

**Probe:** "Your German build is 35% longer and Finnish order totals are wrong above one million. Where do you look first?" (Expect: hardcoded pixel widths and hand-rolled number formatting instead of `Intl.NumberFormat`, plus the Finnish long-vowel system and grouping-separator conventions — and the fact that a single unformatted price string breaks the audit trail.)

**What they evaluate:**
- **ICU MessageFormat over concatenation,** and real awareness that plural categories differ per locale (`Intl.PluralRules('ar')` yields six) and that some languages need `select` on gender, not just counts.
- **`Intl` used properly:** `NumberFormat`, `DateTimeFormat` with an explicit `timeZone`, `RelativeTimeFormat`, `Collator` for sorting, `ListFormat`, `DisplayNames` — plus storing money as minor units plus a currency code and timestamps as UTC.
- **RTL treated as a layout problem,** not a mirror: `dir` on the root, logical properties throughout, `lang` attributes, and icon-directionality rules.
- **Process discipline:** semantic keys, pseudo-locale in dev, a missing-key CI gate, and a TMS workflow where translators get context and screenshots.

**Model answer, Q1:** "I'd pick a library that speaks ICU MessageFormat — `react-intl` or `i18next` — wrap the app in its provider, then sweep the codebase replacing hardcoded strings with stable semantic keys. From there: pseudo-locale in dev so every hardcoded string shows up immediately, a missing-key check in CI so a partial translation can't ship, `Intl` for every date, number, and currency, and locale bundles loaded dynamically so only the active language is in the initial payload."
**Mini task:** add `en`, `de`, and `ar` support to a small app: ICU plurals, `Intl` formatting, RTL layout via logical properties, a locale switcher that persists, and lazy-loaded locale files.


**Self-check:** "A user switches from `en-GB` to `ar-EG` at runtime — what changes in the URL, the `Intl` formatters, the document attributes, and the layout, and what would still be broken if I had only translated the JSON?"

**Resources:** MDN's `Intl` reference and its "Localization and internationalization" guide; the Unicode CLDR locale data that `Intl` is built on; FormatJS's ICU MessageFormat syntax documentation and the react-intl guides; the W3C `lang`/`hreflang` guidance and Google's SEO localisation documentation.

# PHASE 3.4-3.9 RAPID-FIRE (under 30 seconds each)

| # | Question | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Do TS types exist at runtime? | No, they're erased, so validate at boundaries |
| 2 | `any` vs `unknown`? | Unchecked vs must-narrow |
| 3 | `type` vs `interface`? | Merging and extends vs unions and mapped types |
| 4 | What does `satisfies` do? | Checks without widening |
| 5 | What's `never` for? | Exhaustiveness and impossible states |
| 6 | Discriminated union? | A shared literal tag enables narrowing |
| 7 | `Pick` / `Omit` / `Partial` / `Record`? | Keep keys / drop keys / all optional / key-value map |
| 8 | What's `infer`? | Capture a type inside a conditional type |
| 9 | Why Vite doesn't type check? | It only strips types, so run `tsc --noEmit` |
| 10 | Reflow vs repaint vs composite? | Geometry / appearance / layer assembly |
| 11 | Cheap animation properties? | `transform`, `opacity` |
| 12 | Layout thrashing? | Interleaved DOM reads and writes force sync layouts |
| 13 | `target` vs `currentTarget`? | Origin of the event vs the listener's element |
| 14 | What does `passive: true` do? | Promises no `preventDefault`, so scrolling isn't blocked |
| 15 | Same-origin definition? | Scheme + host + port |
| 16 | Who enforces CORS? | The browser, so it doesn't protect your API from curl |
| 17 | Why does JSON trigger a preflight? | Non-safelisted `Content-Type` |
| 18 | `*` with credentials? | Not allowed |
| 19 | Where to store session tokens? | `HttpOnly` `Secure` `SameSite` cookie, not `localStorage` |
| 20 | `no-cache` vs `no-store`? | Revalidate before use vs never store |
| 21 | Caching strategy for SPA? | Hashed assets immutable for a year, HTML `no-cache` |
| 22 | What does a 304 mean? | Validator matched, use your cached copy |
| 23 | When use a Web Worker? | CPU-heavy work that blocks input, with no DOM access |
| 24 | IntersectionObserver uses? | Lazy load, infinite scroll, viewability |
| 25 | Core Web Vitals? | LCP ≤ 2.5s, INP ≤ 200ms, CLS ≤ 0.1 |
| 26 | What replaced FID? | INP (March 2024) |
| 27 | Lab vs field data? | Diagnose in lab, prioritize and verify in field (RUM/CrUX) |
| 28 | Never lazy-load which image? | The LCP hero image |
| 29 | Why is JS costlier than images? | Parse, compile, and execute on the main thread |
| 30 | Break up long tasks? | Yield with `scheduler.yield` / `setTimeout`, workers, chunking |
| 31 | First rule of ARIA? | Use native elements first |
| 32 | Accessible name order? | `aria-labelledby`, `aria-label`, native label/content, `title` |
| 33 | Roving tabindex? | One tab stop, arrow keys move `tabindex=0` |
| 34 | Live region must? | Exist in the DOM before its text changes |
| 35 | What do automated a11y tools catch? | About 30-40%, and manual testing is still required |
| 36 | Why env vars aren't secret in frontends? | Inlined at build time into public bundles |
| 37 | Why is Vite dev fast? | Native ESM on demand and esbuild pre-bundling of deps |
| 38 | What decides a Turborepo cache hit? | Hash of inputs: files, deps, env, task config |
| 39 | Service worker update gotcha? | New SW waits until old tabs close, or `skipWaiting` |
| 40 | `fetch` and 404? | Resolves, so check `res.ok` |
| 41 | WebSocket vs SSE? | Duplex vs server-push with auto-reconnect over plain HTTP |
| 42 | ICU plurals purpose? | Correct plural forms and word order across languages |

# PHASE 3 (PARTS 1-3) FINAL READINESS CHECKLIST

**TypeScript**
- [ ] I can explain erasure, structural typing, and why runtime validation (Zod) is needed
- [ ] I know the `strict` family, `noUncheckedIndexedAccess`, and `@ts-expect-error`
- [ ] I can model state with discriminated unions and exhaustiveness checks
- [ ] I can write generics with constraints, and use `keyof`, indexed access, and utility types
- [ ] I can implement `Pick`, `Omit`, `ReturnType`, `Awaited`, and `DeepReadonly`
- [ ] I can type React props/events/refs and Express handlers, and share types across the stack

**Browser internals**
- [ ] I can walk the rendering pipeline and avoid layout thrashing
- [ ] I can explain event propagation, delegation, passive listeners, and cleanup with `AbortController`
- [ ] I can explain SOP and CORS (simple vs preflight, credentials) and fix real CORS bugs
- [ ] I can choose between cookies, localStorage, sessionStorage, IndexedDB, and the Cache API with security reasoning
- [ ] I can design a caching header strategy for a SPA and diagnose "users see the old version"
- [ ] I can use Web Workers and observers appropriately

**Performance**
- [ ] I know Core Web Vitals thresholds and the lab vs field distinction
- [ ] I can diagnose and fix LCP, INP, and CLS using their phase breakdowns
- [ ] I can shrink a bundle (analyze, split, tree shake, replace deps, tame third parties)
- [ ] I can run the "page is slow" playbook from field data to fix to regression guard

**Accessibility**
- [ ] I can explain accessible names, roles, and states, and when ARIA is appropriate
- [ ] I can build tabs, a modal, and a disclosure with correct keyboard behavior and focus management
- [ ] I can make SPA navigation and dynamic updates accessible (focus, live regions)
- [ ] I can test with axe in CI **and** manually with a keyboard and a screen reader

**Build tools and platform**
- [ ] I can explain bundling, HMR, tree shaking, code splitting, and why Vite starts fast
- [ ] I understand build-time env vars, content hashing, and source map handling
- [ ] I can set up and reason about a monorepo with workspaces and task caching
- [ ] I can build a service-worker caching strategy and handle updates safely
- [ ] I can use fetch correctly (errors, cancellation, streaming), and build reliable WebSocket/SSE clients
- [ ] I can design file uploads (presigned URLs, chunking) and a basic i18n setup (ICU, `Intl`, RTL)

# PHASE 3: Frontend Foundations

### Frontend Basics: HTML Fundamentals ✅
- ✅ Semantic elements, Accessibility standards, Form validation, Document structure, SEO best practices, Canvas API, Web components
- ➕ Meta tags and Open Graph, Responsive images (`srcset`, `picture`), `<dialog>`, `<details>`, Script loading (`async`/`defer`/modules), SVG, Shadow DOM and custom elements in depth

### CSS Styling ✅
- ✅ Box model, Flexbox, Grid, Responsive design, Preprocessors, Animation techniques, CSS modules
- ➕ Specificity and cascade, Stacking context and z-index, Positioning, CSS variables, Container queries, Logical properties, Modern CSS (`:has()`, nesting, `clamp()`), Transitions vs animations vs `requestAnimationFrame`, Centering techniques, BEM and CSS architecture, Dark mode, Tailwind CSS, CSS-in-JS trade-offs

### JavaScript Core ✅
- ✅ Variable declarations, Execution context, Scope chain, Closures, Prototypal inheritance, Event loop, Asynchronous programming
- ➕ `this` binding rules, Hoisting and TDZ, Promises (`all`, `allSettled`, `race`, `any`), async/await error handling, Microtasks vs macrotasks, ES6+ features, Modules (ESM vs CommonJS), Iterators and generators, Proxy and Reflect, Symbols, WeakMap/WeakSet, Currying, memoization, debounce, throttle, Deep vs shallow copy, Type coercion and equality, Immutability, Date/Intl, Regex, Memory leaks and garbage collection
- ➕ **Must-implement-from-scratch:** `bind`, `call`, `apply`, `Promise`, `Promise.all`, `debounce`, `throttle`, `curry`, `deepClone`, event emitter, `Array.map/filter/reduce` polyfills

### 🆕 TypeScript (🟡)
- Types vs interfaces, Generics, Utility types (`Partial`, `Pick`, `Omit`, `Record`), Union and intersection types, Type narrowing and guards, Discriminated unions, `unknown` vs `any` vs `never`, Mapped and conditional types, `tsconfig` and strict mode, Typing React and Express, Zod and runtime validation

### 🆕 Browser Internals (🔴)
- Critical rendering path, Reflow vs repaint, Compositing and GPU layers, JavaScript engine (V8), Event delegation and bubbling, Same-origin policy, Browser storage (cookies, localStorage, sessionStorage, IndexedDB), Browser caching (`Cache-Control`, ETag), Service workers, Web Workers, `IntersectionObserver` and `ResizeObserver`

### 🆕 Web Performance (🔴)
- Core Web Vitals (LCP, INP, CLS), Lighthouse and profiling, Bundle analysis, Code splitting and tree shaking, Lazy loading, Image and font optimization, Critical CSS, Prefetch/preload, Virtualization of long lists, CDN and compression (gzip, Brotli)

### 🆕 Accessibility in Depth (🟡)
- WCAG basics, ARIA roles and when not to use them, Keyboard navigation and focus management, Screen reader testing, Color contrast, Accessible forms and modals, Automated tools (axe)

### 🆕 Build Tools and Tooling (🟡)
- Vite, Webpack, esbuild/SWC, Babel, Monorepos (Turborepo, Nx), Module federation, Environment configs, Source maps

### 🆕 Progressive Web Apps and Web APIs (🟡)
- PWA manifest and offline, Push notifications, Fetch/AbortController, WebSockets and SSE, File API and drag-and-drop, Geolocation, Clipboard, i18n and localization
